Abstracts for NAPCON 2025

Abhishek D. Sabale1, S. Phadnis1, D. Ghorpade1, S. Madas1, S. Bhalare1, M. Lopez2, S. Nair2, V. Naik2, J. Gogtay2, S. Salvi1

1Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Cipla Ltd, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Background: AIR combines inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) with fast acting bronchodilators like salbutamol or formoterol and are beneficial for reducing exacerbations and improving compliance as compared to traditional SABA relievers. However, there is limited empirical evidence from India regarding its usage and the factors influencing its adoption by healthcare professionals.

Objective: To understand doctors’ prescribing patterns and preferences for anti-inflammatory relievers and their role in asthma management.

Methods: Clinicians (pulmonologists, general physicians and paediatricians) in India were randomly selected and invited to participate in this study. Data was collected through a structured study instrument, using both self-administered questionnaires and telephonic interviews with doctors.

Results: Among the 404 doctors who participated in this study, 52.0% were pulmonologists, 35.6% were physicians and 12.4% were paediatricians. On an average, 73% of the asthma patients received a reliever medication. AIR was prescribed to an average of 72.5%, while SABA was prescribed to 27.5% asthmatic patients. 29.2% of doctors prescribed only AIR as reliever while 70.3% doctors prescribed SABA or AIR. Table 1 shows the prescribing patterns of different AIR combinations. 85.1% of doctors opined that efficacy of AIR therapy was better than SABA alone.

AIR combinations used by different practitioners

Conclusion: A total of 29.2% doctors prescribed only AIR as reliever therapy. Budesonide-Formoterol combination was the most commonly prescribed AIR. The concept of AIR is well-accepted in India, however, there is scope to further improve the uptake.

Acknowledgment: This study was supported by Cipla Ltd. India.

Asmita Gaikwad1, M. Barne1, S. Madas1, V. Das2, M. Dhawale1, A. Shaikh1, P. Shinde1, A. Sabale1, S. Salvi1

1Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Chest Research and Training Pvt. Ltd., Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: India has a population of 1·3 billion people of which 15% lives in slums as per a report by committee on slum statistics/census. India accounts for 18% of the global population but contributes to a disproportionate 30.2% of the global burden of chronic respiratory diseases. We aimed to study the prevalence and risk factors associated with respiratory symptoms in the urban slums in Pune City.

Materials and Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional, interviewer administered, questionnaire-based study among men and women >18 years of age in the urban slums of Pune City. The questionnaire included questions on respiratory symptoms and the factors that were associated with them.

Results: We screened 3862 (M: F = 39%: 61%), age > 18 yrs, 93 % from urban slums. Prevalence of current respiratory symptoms in this population is as per the Figure 1. The factors that were associated with respiratory symptoms are depicted in Table 1.

Prevalence of current respiratory symptoms

Factors that were associated with respiratory symptoms in our study

OR: Odds ratio, CI: Confidence interval

Conclusion: Our study shows that dhoop, agarbatti, loban, mosquito coil, use of biomass fuel, passive smoker in the house, smoker (self), family or past history of asthma or TB were associated with a high risk of respiratory symptoms. Respiratory symptoms were also significantly associated with presence of anaemia, hypertension, diabetes and obesity. We also found that presence of a separate kitchen and presence of one or more window in the house were negatively associated with respiratory symptoms.

Keywords: Poor indoor air quality, respiratory symptoms, separate kitchen

Benin Rajesh, Hariprasad Kalpakam, Rohit Vadala, Sameer Bansal, Anmol Thorbole, Ravindra Mehta

Vaayu Chest and Sleep Specialists, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Background: Vehicular traffic emission is one of the largest sources of air pollution in metros. Progressive deterioration in air quality leads to significant morbidity and mortality by affecting multiple organ systems, exponentially increased in high-risk populations. Specifically, occupational exposure to vehicular pollution is a significant health risk to traffic policemen, and affects lung function and cardiovascular health.

Aim: This cross-sectional point-prevalence study aimed to screen traffic police personnel (TPP) in Bengaluru city for respiratory and general health.

Methods: A comprehensive respiratory health screening project for TPP was done in Bengaluru, from 1-12-23 to 30-8-24. Symptoms, mask usage, smoking history, prior co-morbidities, BMI, vitals, random blood sugar and spirometry were assessed.

Results: 2450 TPP were screened over 10 months, 91% males. Mean age was 38.84 years (range 26-59; SD-8.62). The participants had an average of 13.45 years of service (range 3-28; SD-7.82). Majority of them did not use masks (n = 1370, 55%) during working hours. The average air quality index (AQI) estimated during the study period was 115. There were 5% active smokers (n = 131). The average BMI was 25.8 kg/m2, and 47% were overweight (n = 891), 11% obese (n = 206). Diabetes mellitus was noted in 14% (n = 343), 50% of them with uncontrolled sugars. Hypertension was noted in 15% (n = 367), 25% uncontrolled. Cough was the most commonly reported symptom (13%). Spirometry showed early lung function impairment (Preserved Ratio with Impaired Spirometry-PRISM) in 28% (n = 513) and obstructive pattern in 1% (n = 20).

Conclusions: This is the largest occupational health screening study of TPP’s in the Indian literature, and one of the largest in global literature. Significant respiratory and general health issues were noted, and likely reflects the situation in other major metros of the country. There is an imperative need for interventions to protect the health of TPP’s in Bengaluru. Implementing protective measures such as use of face masks, regular screenings, promoting a healthier lifestyle, and comprehensive follow-up of affected individuals are major interventions required at an individual and policy level.

Acknowledgements: Shri MN Anucheth, IPS, Joint Commissioner of Police, Traffic, Bengaluru City. Mrs. Nandita Sanghani, Dr. Nanda Sanjay, Mrs. Roopa Chillamkurti and other volunteers at Vaayu who helped selflessly in the initiative.

Keywords: Lung function, occupational health, pollution, traffic police personnel

Chittal Palaniappan, Somanath Dash, Raghavendra Rao, Madhu Harsha

Department of Respiratory Medicine, GSL Medical College, Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Kartagener’s syndrome is an autosomal recessive genetic disorder with triad of Situs Inversus, Chronic sinusitis & Bronchiectasis. In this syndrome, there is defective movement of cilia leading to recurrent chest, ENT infections & infertility. We hereby report five cases of this syndrome & two among them were associated with Pulmonary Tuberculosis (PTB).

Methods: All patients with Dextrocardia who came to our institute on 2024 with history of cough with sputum were included in this study. On the basis of clinical & radiological findings, Kartagener’s syndrome (KS) was diagnosed.

Results: Five cases included in this study, 3 males & 2 females. Among females, one is adult of age 48 years & another is 22 years. Among 3 males included, one is adult of age 53 years & other two are of ages 20 years & 22 years. Two cases were associated with PTB. Semen analysis done for two male patients, one was infertile & other was fertile. Whole Genome Sequencing done for one patient showing homozygous mutation in DNAI1 gene.

Conclusion: KS exists as cases of chronic recurrent sinopulmonary infections. PTB with secondary bacterial infections can be the presenting manifestation in them. Initial sputum AFB may be negative but repeat sputum examination following control of secondary infection may be rewarding in diagnosis of PTB. Diagnosis are delayed as there is no reliable non-invasive diagnostic test for KS. Genetic counselling & fertility issues should be addressed once diagnosed, though Infertility is not a universal phenomenon in KS

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, Kartageners syndrome, sinusitis, situs inversus, tuberculosis

Disha Ghorpade1, S. Bardapurkar2, H. Vhora3, V. Singh4, P. Bhattacharyya5, R. Diwan6, S. Bardapurkar2, U. Singh4, S. Chauthmal7, S. Madas7, R. Swami7, A. Sable7, M. Lopez8, J. Gogtay8, S. Salvi7

1Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Shree Nursing Home, Aurangabad, Maharashtra, India, 3Vhora Chest Clinic, Baramati, Maharashtra, India, 4Asthma Bhawan, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India, 5Institute of Pulmocare and Research, Chakpachuria, West Bengal, India, 6Jyotirling Hospital, Kolhapur, Maharashtra, India, 7Chest Research and Training, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 8Cipla Ltd, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Background: Asthma is an inflammatory disease which affects all portions of the airways, whereas COPD affects only the small airways. Small Airways Disease (SAD) asthma is emerging as an important phenotype of asthma that has distinct clinical manifestations. SAD asthma is reported in up to 50% of asthmatics, highlighting its prevalence and the importance of identifying it as a distinct phenotype. The recognition of SAD in asthma has significant implications for treatment, as it may require targeted therapy aimed specifically at the small airways to manage symptoms effectively and improve patient outcomes. Physiological tests such as spirometry FEF25-75% and more recently lung oscillometry is emerging as an important sensitive diagnostic test for SAD asthma.

Methods: Pulmonologists from 5 centers across India, who were members of the International Network for Lung Oscillometry Research (ARISE) took part in the study. Oscillometry was performed using the Antlia FOT (iCALTECH, Bangalore. India) and spirometry was performed using devices meeting ATS/ERS standards, among asthmatic patients visiting their clinics. SAD was defined using cut-off of ≥1 for R5-R20 or ≥65% for FEF25%-75%. In addition, the prevalence of PRISm (preserved ratio impaired spirometry), Obstructive Airways Disease (OAD) and Restrictive Lungs Disease was also identified using spirometry classification, amongst the SAD patients.

Results: Out of data obtained from 1969 asthmatic patients, 1334 were eligible for the final analysis, of which 1069 (80.1%) had SAD. Considering the phenotypic differences, asthma presents with more breathlessness, wheeze, and chest tightness than non-SAD asthma as shown in Table 1. The prevalence of PRISm and OAD by spirometry amongst asthmatics has been shown in Table 2.

Phenotypic differences between small airways disease and nonsmall airways disease

BMI: Body mass index, SAD: Small airways disease, SD: Standard deviation, FEV1: Forced expiratory volume in 1 s

Spirometry classification amongst small airways disease and nonsmall airways disease asthmatic patients

SAD: Small airways disease, FEV1: Forced expiratory volume in 1 s, FVC: Forced vital capacity

Conclusion: The early detection of SAD, particularly mild cases, is better achieved with lung oscillometry. Understanding these differences is crucial for implementing appropriate therapies that address the specific needs of patients with SAD asthma.

Dhruv Gupta, Varuna Jethani, Shahnwaz Saifi, Ankit Batra, Rakhee Khanduri, Sushant Khanduri

Department of Respiratory Medicine, HIMS, SRHU, Jollygrant, Dehradun, India

Introduction: Lung cancer remains a leading cause of cancer-related mortality worldwide, characterized by diverse clinical presentations and genetic heterogeneity. This study aims to analyze the epidemiology, clinical patterns and mutational profiles of lung cancer patients treated at a tertiary care hospital.

Methods: The study was conducted in the Department of Respiratory Medicine at the HIMS, Dehradun after written informed consent. The approval of institutional ethics committee of Swami Rama Himalayan University was taken before starting the study. Demographic and anthropometric indices, socioeconomic status were recorded. As per radiological features patients were further subjected for diagnosis of lung cancer by bronchoscopy, Computed tomography guided Fine needle aspiration or Biopsy and thoracoscopy and further mutational analysis was done.

Results: Majority of patients were males. The most common symptom was shortness of breath. A significant majority were smokers, with history of 10-20 pack years. Upper lobe was the most frequently involved area. Adenocarcinoma was the most common pathological type. Over half of the patients had mutations, with Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) being the most common. Among EGFR mutations, Exon 19 was the most prevalent.

Out of the total 71 patients, 52.11% exhibited mutations, while 47.89% did not present any mutations. In the study conducted by Kaler A. K. et al, only 38.67% of patients tested positive for any mutation. Similar findings were observed in the study by Veldore et al., where mutations were detected in 40% of cases. In our study, EGFR mutations were the most prevalent at 29.58%, followed by TP53 at 11.27%, and KRAS at 7.04%. Singh V et al. reported a higher prevalence of EGFR mutations

Conclusion: This highlights the critical intersections between lifestyle factors, genetic predisposition, and cancer development, underscoring the urgent need for integrated prevention, early detection, and tailored therapeutic strategies.

Keywords: Immunohistochemistry, lungcancer, mutational analysis

Divya Bhojwani, Rithik, Sagar Bhagat, Saiprasad Patil, Hanmant Barkate

Global Medical Affairs, Glenmark Pharmaceuticals Limited, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Glycopyrronium provides rapid bronchodilation within five to fifteen minutes of administration and has a good safety profile. This study evaluated the use of nebulized glycopyrronium (Neb G) in hospitalised patients and its impact on clinical outcomes.

Methods: Medical records of adult patients treated with Neb G for acute exacerbations of OAD in hospital were reviewed. The study was conducted at 400 centers in India after approval from an independent ethics committee.

Results: Out of 4,000 medical records, 2,780 were analysed. The mean patient age was 59.56 ± 9.72 years, with 2,084 males (74.96%). COPD was the primary diagnosis in 1,804 (64.89%) patients followed by asthma in 523 (18.81%). A total of 1,558 (56.04%) patients had a history of exacerbations, and 1,156 (41.58%) had previous hospitalizations. Nearly half, 1,390 (51.55%), received Neb G as monotherapy, while combination therapy with Nebulized ICS and LABA was preferred for others on first day of hospitalization. The preferred dosage was 25 mcg twice daily for an average of 13.45 days. Patients stayed in the ICU for 3.15 ± 2.93 days and in the ward for 4.96 ± 3.03 days. SABA was required in 1492 (53.66%) patients, mostly needing them on the first or second day 1390 (50%), while 714 (25.68%) required oral corticosteroids. Oxygen support, in the form mechanical ventilation or NIV, was needed in 564 (20.28%) patients. Clinical resolution and discharge occurred in 2497 patients (89.82%). Adverse events occurred in 56 patients (2.01%), with dry mouth reported in 3 (0.11%).

Conclusion: Nebulised Glycopyrronium, as monotherapy or in combination with ICS/LABA, offers an effective, reliable option for managing OAD exacerbations in hospital settings.

Keywords: Bronchodilators, exacerbation, glycopyrronium, nebulisation

Divya Bhojwani, Rithik, Sagar Bhagat, Saiprasad Patil, Hanmant Barkate

Global Medical Affairs, Glenmark Pharmaceuticals Limited, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Acute exacerbations of obstructive airway diseases (OAD) require prompt medical intervention. While nebulized (Neb) short-acting bronchodilators (SABA/SAMA) are commonly used, they often necessitate multiple doses. Glycopyrronium (GLY), however, offers a rapid & longer duration of action. A questionnaire survey was conducted to evaluate its use in hospitalized patients.

Methods: A structured 10-question survey was administered to physicians managing OAD exacerbations from last 10 years. The survey was validated by a panel of four senior pulmonologists specializing in OAD management.

Results: Of the 120 physicians invited to participate in the survey, 101 physicians actively participated. For managing exacerbations, most physicians preferred Neb bronchodilators (SABA + SAMA) as the first line therapy, Neb GLY with Neb ICS/LABA being the secondary choice for severe exacerbations [Table 1]. Opinions varied regarding the timing of therapy transitions, particularly in severe exacerbations [Table 2]. After stabilization in Emergency Room (ER)/Intensive Care Unit (ICU) to the ward, 44% of physicians preferred Neb GLY based open triple therapy, while 26% opted for GLY-based triple therapy (pMDI/DPI). This trend continued at discharge. The majority (84%) of respondents supported using Neb GLY, either alone or Neb ICS/LABA, for hospitalized patients. Of these, 59% initiated treatment within the first two days and 48% continuing therapy until discharge, extending it for 7-15 days’ post-discharge.

Preferred initial therapies for obstructive airway diseases exacerbations

DPI: Dry-Powder-Inhaler, ICS: Inhaled corticosteroids

Therapy transition preferences in severe exacerbations

Conclusion: Nebulized glycopyrronium is widely preferred for hospitalised OAD patients, particularly in ERs/ICUs and wards. Its use alongside open triple therapy is favoured throughout the hospital stay and during follow-up care. Our findings highlight the role of Neb GLY’s in managing OAD exacerbations, especially in severe cases.

Keywords: OAD exacerbation, nebulized glycopyrronium, survey

Bhaumik Kamdar, Shahid Patel, Girija Nair, Lavina Mirchandani, Manas Bhanushali, Deepika Ughade

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, DY Patil School of Medicine, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Objective: Pulmonary function tests (PFTs) are critical in diagnosing and categorizing lung pathologies. This study aims to compare the diagnostic utility of Forced Expiratory Volume in 1 second (FEV1) and Forced Expiratory Flow at 25–75% of vital capacity (FEF25-75) across various lung pathologies, including obstructive, restrictive, and mixed ventilatory defects.

Methods: A prospective analysis of 200 PFTs was conducted, encompassing patients diagnosed with a spectrum of lung diseases, including asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), interstitial lung disease (ILD), and mixed-pattern pathologies. Data on FEV1, FEF25-75, and other spirometric parameters were extracted. Comparative analysis was performed to evaluate the sensitivity, specificity, and overall diagnostic performance of FEF25-75 relative to FEV1 in characterizing airway obstruction, small-airway dysfunction, and disease progression. Statistical significance was determined using appropriate tests, and correlations between these indices and clinical diagnoses were analyzed.

Results: FEV1 demonstrated robust sensitivity in detecting airflow limitation in obstructive and mixed-pattern diseases. However, FEF25-75 exhibited greater sensitivity for identifying early small-airway dysfunction, particularly in asthma and early-stage COPD. FEF25-75 showed higher variability but provided additional information to FEV1, particularly in cases with borderline FEV1/FVC ratios. In restrictive pathologies, both parameters were less discriminatory but still informative for mixed-pattern differentiation.

Conclusion: While FEV1 remains the cornerstone of spirometric analysis, FEF25-75 offers valuable additional insights, particularly in detecting early small-airway dysfunction and in cases with equivocal FEV1 findings. Integrating both indices enhances diagnostic accuracy and provides a more nuanced understanding of lung pathology, particularly in diseases involving heterogeneous airway involvement.

Keywords: FEF25-75, FEV1, obstructive lung disease, PFT, restrictive lung disease, spirometry

Gaurav Mishra, Rahul Tyagi

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Armed Forces Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Bronchoscopy is a valuable tool in managing critically ill patients, particularly in intensive care settings, due to its diagnostic and therapeutic potential with a low incidence of complications. However, in patients with severe hypoxemic respiratory failure, bronchoscopy especially bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL), poses risks such as hypoxemia. Despite these risks, timely diagnosis is crucial as undetermined causes of acute hypoxemia are associated with poor outcomes. This study investigates the diagnostic yield, clinical outcomes, and complication rates of bedside bronchoscopy in hypoxemic patients.

Objectives: (a) To determine the frequency of a change in the primary diagnosis following bedside bronchoscopy in patients with hypoxemic respiratory failure, (b) To assess the incidence, types, and severity of adverse effects associated with bedside bronchoscopy in patients with hypoxemic respiratory failure.

Methods: This retrospective observational study was conducted from December 2023 to December 2024 at a tertiary respiratory care center. 40 patients aged >14 years with hypoxemic respiratory failure with moderate to severe hypoxemia (FiO2 requirement >40%) underwent bedside bronchoscopy. Medical records were analyzed for changes in diagnosis, treatment modifications, and adverse events. FiO2 requirements before, during, and after the procedure were recorded. Patients were followed until discharge or death.

Results: In a cohort of 40 patients with hypoxemic respiratory failure (mean age: 51.5 years) who underwent videobronchoscopy with bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL), an etiological diagnosis was achieved in 26 patients (65% of the total cases). The BAL findings led to a change in treatment in 20 patients (50% of total cases), of whom 10 patients (50%) had a favorable outcome. In contrast, among the 14 patients where no diagnosis was established, only 4 patients (28%) had a favorable outcome. Overall, 14 patients (35%) survived, while 26 patients (65%) succumbed to their illness, highlighting the potential impact of BAL in guiding treatment decisions and improving outcomes in respiratory failure patients. BAL was crucial in diagnosing conditions like PCP pneumonia, fungal pneumonia, strongyloides, DAH and PAP, which would have been easily missed. The procedure was well-tolerated, with no significant complications other than transient increases in oxygen requirements which was effectively managed with increasing the oxygen support.

Conclusion: Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) in acute respiratory failure, though technically challenging, facilitates the identification of atypical diagnoses and can significantly improve patient outcomes.

Keywords: Bedside bronchoscopy, clinical outcomes, bronchoalveolar lavage, diagnostic yield, hypoxemic respiratory failure

M. Harshavardhana, C. Nandish, B. Vidyasagar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Bapuji Hospital, JJM Medical College, Davanagere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Cardiovascular disease is a major cause of mortality in COPD. Microalbuminuria (MAB) is a sensitive marker of cardiovascular risk and kidney disease. The presence of MAB is associated with arterial stiffness and worse cardiovascular outcomes. MAB has a stronger association with cardiovascular events and death than CRP. MAB is believed to reflect a state of generalised endothelial dysfunction, and therefore could be a promising biomarker to identify patients with COPD COR PULMONALE at increased cardiovascular risk, without any comorbidities.

Aim of the Study: To evaluate the association between presence of MAB and outcomes of patients hospitalised with acute exacerbation of COPD (AECOPD) IN COR-PULMONALE.

Methods: (1) Study population: This is a prospectively collected data from patients admitted for aecopd to the respiratory medicine department of bapuji hospital in J. J. M. M. C, davanagere. Patients with history of respiratory disorders other than COPD, other comorbidities, inability or unwillingness were excluded. (2) Study design: Patients demographics, clinical parameters were recorded on admission, including vital signs, level of dyspnoea (mMRC and 6MWT), and abg (pH, PaCO2, PaO2). Standard laboratory measurements (CBC, serum creatinine and CRP) collected from each patient at the time of admission prior to initiation of any treatment. Patients with GFR < 60 mL/min/1.73 m2 were excluded. Patients were classified in four COPD groups A–D as defined by GOLD criteria. Study investigator recorded the patients vital status and the numbers of new AECOPD (defined as need for antibiotics and/or oral corticosteroids, visits to the emergency department and/or hospitalisations) based on self-reporting of patients and was confirmed by hospital records. MAB was defined as a urinary albumin excretion of 30–300 mg/24 hours, when measured in a 24-hour urine collection using a standard spectrophotometric method. (3) Statistical analysis: AECOPD and hospitalisations are both reported as the proportion of patients undergoing an event as well as the mean (SD) number of events per patient per year. Categorical values were analysed using the χ2 test or Fisher’s exact test (χ2) as appropriate and were reported as n (%). Continuously measured variables were compared by Student’s t-test. Continuous parameters with normal distribution are reported as means±SD. Level of statistical significance was set at p value < 0.05. All statistical analyses were performed using SPSS for Windows software.

Results: Patients with MAB required a more prolonged hospitalisation days and had higher rates of AECOPD.

Conclusion: MAB on admission for AECOPD was associated with a higher risk of AECOPD at 1-year follow-up. Patients with MAB required more prolonged hospitalisation, were frequent exacerbators, had more severe COPD according to the GOLD classification, more severe airflow obstruction, more comorbidities, lower BMI, more severe dyspnoea, lower 6MWT, more impaired oxygenation, higher PaCO2, higher use of LTOT and NIMV prior to admission. This suggest a possible role for MAB in the identification of patients with COPD at increased risk of adverse outcomes that may need early intensive interventions and management.

Jaya Tak1, D. Rai1, P. Adwika1, B. Pattnaik1, S. Bangaru1, N. Bhatraju2, R. Guleria1, A. Agrawal2, G. Yadav3, R. S. Dhaliwal3, T. Srivastva4, A. Mohan1

1Department of Pulmonary, Critical Care and Sleep Medicine, AIIMS, New Delhi, India, 2CSIR-IGIB, New Delhi, India, 3ICMR, New Delhi, India, 4Department of Genetics, University of Delhi (South Campus), New Delhi, India

Background: Lung cancer and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) are major health concerns with overlapping risk factors like tobacco smoke. Differential expression of microRNAs (miRNAs) in these conditions suggests their potential as biomarkers. Exhaled breath condensate (EBC) provides a non-invasive method for miRNA detection, which could aid in distinguishing between lung cancer and COPD.

Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional study with 25 healthy controls (mean age: 60.07 ± 8.3 years), 25 COPD patients (mean age: 61.6 ± 8 years), and 25 lung cancer patients (mean age: 57.6 ± 10 years). Exhaled breath condensate (EBC) was analyzed for miRNA expression using microarray and quantitative PCR (qPCR). The statistical analysis involved global mean normalization, ΔCt value calculation, and unpaired t-tests to compare miRNA levels between the groups.

Results: Among the recruited subjects, the number of current smokers was 7 in the healthy control group, 21 in the COPD group, and 18 in the lung cancer group. The average BMI was 25.9 for healthy controls, 23.6 for COPD patients, and 20.1 for lung cancer patients. We identified 36 miRNAs distinguishing COPD from healthy controls and 43 miRNAs separating lung cancer from healthy controls. Additionally, 18 miRNAs were specific to differentiating lung cancer from COPD, with 15 upregulated and 3 downregulated.

Conclusion: miRNA profiling in EBC demonstrates significant potential as a non-invasive tool for differentiating lung cancer from COPD. The promising results highlight its utility in diagnosis and disease differentiation. However, to fully establish its clinical value, additional validation and functional studies are essential.

Razak Khan

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: SARS-CoV and MERS-CoV are emerging coronaviruses with mortality rates of 10% and 35% respectively. Research suggests that hematological tests may help identify need for additional diagnostics for SARS-CoV-2 infection. While many studies focus on RT-PCR for diagnosis, there is gap in research on hematological markers in patients with atypical pneumonia who test negative by RT-PCR. This study aims to explore how hematological parameters can guide diagnosis and treatment.

Methods: It was hospital based descriptive epidemiological study. Cross-sectional in design. Study was planned for 12 months from August, 2020 to July, 2021 till sample size is achieved. Study population were patients presenting in wards, ICU and OPD of RNT medical college with atypical pneumonia, testing negative for both COVID-19 on RT-PCR and H1N1.

Results: In our study of 151 patients, 127 (84.1%) had low platelets, and 5 (3.3%) had high platelets. Of 127 with low platelets, 37 (97.4%) died, and 90 (79.6%) were discharged. Elevated D-Dimer levels were seen in 97.37% of the deceased and 80.54% of the discharged. CRP levels were elevated in 94.74% of the deceased and 84.96% of the discharged. IL-6 levels were elevated in 78.95% of the deceased and 67.26% of the discharged.

Conclusion: This study provides valuable insights for future researchers and clinicians, enhancing their understanding of atypical pneumonias and enabling identification of such patients based on hematological parameters during a pandemic, even in presence of negative diagnostic test results.

Keywords: Atypical pneumonia, CRP, D-dimer, IL-6 levels, platelet count, severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus

Rishab Rampradeep

Saveetha Medical College Hospital, Saveetha Institute of Medical and Technical Sciences, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Accurately distinguishing exudative from transudative pleural effusions is essential for guiding treatment. While Light’s criteria remains a diagnostic standard, it is prone to misclassification, particularly in heart failure. This study explores the potential of a novel sonographic scoring system using thoracic ultrasound to determine the exudative or transudative nature of pleural effusions.

Methods: This prospective study involved 74 patients with pleural effusions, categorized based on clinical diagnosis. Thoracic ultrasound was performed to assess laterality, loculations, fluid echogenicity (anechoic or echogenic), and pleural thickening (≥3 mm). These parameters were integrated into a sonographic scoring system, where each positive finding contributed one point to a total of 4. The diagnostic performance of the scoring system was compared to Light’s criteria in terms of sensitivity and specificity.

Results: Thoracic ultrasound findings correlated significantly with exudative effusions. The sonographic scoring system achieved a sensitivity of 98.4% and specificity of 40% in distinguishing exudative from transudative effusions.

Conclusion: The proposed sonographic scoring system could serve as a simple point of care, non-invasive bed side screening tool which could aid physicians in determining the nature of the pleural effusions.

Keywords: Pleural effusion, sonographic score, thoracic ultrasound

Ruby Swami1, Nagrajan Srinivasan2, K. S. Arora3, Deepak Shukla4, Indranil Haldar5, Joydeep Ganguly6, K. Madhu7, Rennis Davis8, S. K. Awasthi9, Sandeep Raj Bharma10, Vikram Jaggi11, Sapna Madas1, Sujata Chauthmal1, Abhishek Sabale1, Rushika Shah12, Meena Lopez12, Senthilnathan Mohanasundaram12, Jaideep Gogtay12, Deesha Ghorpade1, Sundeep Salvi1,13

1Chest Research and Training Pvt Ltd (CREST), Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Hindustan Hospital, Coimbatore, Tamil Nadu, India, 3Kharak Singh Arora Multispeciality Hospital, Alwar, Rajasthan, India, 4Dr. Deepak Shukla Chest and Allergy Care, Jabalpur, Madhya Pradesh, India, 5The Lung Care Clinic, Hooghly, West Bengal, India, 6J. Ganguly Chest Clinic, Berhampore, West Bengal, India, 7Apollo BGS Hospital, Mysore, Karnataka, India, 8Dr. Rennis Davis’s Clinic, Thrissur, Kerala, India, 9Matra Chaya Chest and Allergy Centre, Kanpur, Uttar Pradesh, India, 10Chest Medical Center, Hyderabad, Telangana, India, 11Asthma Chest and Allergy Centres, Delhi, India, 12Cipla Ltd, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 13Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Spirometry is crucial for diagnosing various lung diseases and is considered the gold standard for lung function measurement. However, it remains a challenging test for many patients, requiring significant effort and cooperation.

Objective and Methods: In this cross-sectional, observational, point prevalence study, we evaluated the challenges faced by spirometry technicians and pulmonologists referring patients for spirometry, particularly those undergoing the test for the first time. The study was conducted across 10 centers (pulmonologist clinics) in India, located in Uttar Pradesh, South Delhi, Telangana, Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Karnataka, Madhya Pradesh, West Bengal (2 centers), and Rajasthan.

Results: The study included 12 experienced technicians (average experience of 6.9 years) and 316 patients [Mean age: 44.39 ± 17.23, range18-96 years; M: F = 57.9%: 42.1%]. One patient refused to perform spirometry. Of the 315 who underwent spirometry, all completed 3 blows, except one who was unable to perform due to old age. The average spirometry trials performed were 4 and the average time taken was 23 minutes (range: 6-60 mins) The primary reasons for spirometry testing were obstructive airway disease (50%), breathlessness (23%), and cough (18%). According to the pulmonologists, the use of spirometry influenced management in 27.85% and diagnosis in 25.32% of patients. The spirometry technicians reported that 29% of patients found it challenging to perform the test (74.7% due to breathing difficulties, 9.9% due to inability to follow instructions, and 15.38% due to other reasons). Furthermore, 34.9% of patients themselves reported challenges with the test, primarily breathing issues (58.2%), instructional difficulties (22.7%), and mouthpiece–related problems (12.73%). Approximately 34.81% of patients indicated a preference for a simpler alternative lung test to spirometry.

Conclusion: Despite the involvement of well-trained technicians, a significant proportion of patients (around one-third) found spirometry difficult to perform. This highlights the ongoing need for simpler, more accessible alternatives to traditional spirometry for lung function assessment.

Keywords: Alternative lung test, diagnostic tool, lung function test, obstructive airway disease, patient challenges, pulmonary function, spirometry, technician experience

S. Shilpa, P. Thomas George

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Government Medical College, Thrissur, Kerala, India

Background: The anterior mediastinum is a site of diverse pathological entities ranging from benign and malignant neoplasms to infectious and inflammatory conditions each presenting unique diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. Fungal infections of the mediastinum are rare, they can occur in the context of immunosuppression or as an unusual presentation of systemic fungal disease. These infections are often misdiagnosed due to their nonspecific clinical and radiological manifestations, leading to potential delays in appropriate treatment.

Case Study: 39 year old male presented with h/o cough of 4 months duration, followed by left sided pleuritic chest pain for 3 months, hoarseness of voice, loss of weight and loss of appetite, features of superior vena caval obstruction like dilated veins over chest and left upper limb. Past history revealed the blackish discolouration and induration of left palm for the past 4 years. CT Thorax showed non enhancing soft tissue lesion in prevascular space encasing arch of aorta, pulmonary vessels, left main bronchus and causing erosion of 2nd and 3rd ribs. Blood investigations including serum beta HCG, AFP, LDH within normal limits. USG guided transthoracic biopsy revealed suppurative granulomatous inflammation with giant cell reaction with fungal organisms, morphology favouring phaeohypomycosis. Patient was started on oral antifungals. After 1 month of therapy, patient showed significant improvement with no features of SVCO and xray showed significant reduction in size of lesion.

Conclusion: The case highlights the unique presentation and the importance of maintaining a broad differential diagnosis when evaluating an anterior mediastinal mass, emphasizing its rarity and clinical significance

Shraddha Acharya, N. Huliraj, Deepak Kumar, Basava S. J., G. K. Shankar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Kempegowda Institute of Medical Sciences, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis is defined as the abnormal, irreversible dilatation of the bronchi along with airway inflammation, where patient presents with cough, sputum production and recurrent respiratory tract infections. Proteolytic enzymes play a pivotal role in bronchiectatic distortion of the airway, and are therefore considered valuable prognostic markers. Bronchiectasis is also associated with impaired ventilatory function of the lungs. Hence this study highlights the relationship between the sputum neutrophil elastase ratio and the spirometry severity changes in bronchiectasis patients.

Methods: It is a cross sectional observational study conducted in group of 30 patients, over a duration of 9months at KIMS Hospital, Bangalore. The study was done on whom HRCT thorax confirmed bronchiectasis. Exclusion criteria included, patients aged below 18 years, bronchiectasis due to other causes including interstitial lung disease, old pulmonary tuberculosis and lung malignancy.

Results: The sputum neutrophil elastase (NE) readings by ELISA increased with the severity of obstruction in bronchiectasis. The value of neutrophil elastase levels were significantly lower, i. e. mean NE value of (34.2 +/- 1.27) ng/ml in mild obstruction, as compared to individuals with severe obstruction, with mean NE (82.23 +/- 22.38) ng/ml according to spirometry severity classification.

Conclusion: A significant correlation is found between higher sputum NE levels with lower FEV1 values, suggests that NE could be employed as a diagnostic and prognostic tool to assess the disease severity of bronchiectasis and monitor treatment protocol in clinical practice.

Keywords: Biomarker, bronchiectasis, neutrophil, spirometry

Shraddha Acharya, Basava S. J., N. Huliraj, M. K. Raghavendra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Kempegowda Institute of Medical Sciences, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Background: Multiple myeloma and plasmacytoma are disorders characterised by abnormal proliferation of plasma cells. Plasmacytoma is typically a localised tumour composed of plasma cells, whereas Multiple Myeloma is marked by the uncontrolled proliferation of plasma cells in the bone marrow, leading to widespread systemic damage affecting various organs. In our case study, we highlight this unique overlap of plasmacytoma with Multiple Myeloma.

Case Study: A 77 year old man presented with right side shoulder pain, chest pain and exertional breathlessness that had persisted for one month. High resolution computed tomography (HRCT) Thorax revealed a heterogeneous enhancing lesion in the right upper lobe. A CT-guided biopsy was performed, and histopathological examination showed CD-138 positive plasma cells, suggestive of Plasmacytoma. Serum Protein electrophoresis revealed the presence of a M spike and elevated beta-2 microglobulin levels. A bone marrow aspiration was performed, which had features consistent with Multiple Myeloma. Chemotherapy was initiated based on the working diagnosis of multiple myeloma with plasmacytoma.

Conclusion: The case report underscores the importance of early evaluation of patient with unexplained shoulder pain, as the final diagnosis may be Multiple Myeloma. Early diagnosis and prompt initiation of chemotherapy, along with supportive care can significantly improve the prognosis and quality of life for patients with Multiple Myeloma.

Shradha Bajan, Girija Nair, Lavina Mirchandani

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D. Y. Patil Hospital, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS) has revolutionized bedside diagnostics, offering clinicians with a rapid, non-invasive tool for evaluating and managing patients. In pulmonary medicine, POCUS has become indispensable, enabling real-time assessment of lung pathologies extremely fast with high accuracy, minimal cost, and no radiation exposure.

Case Reports: In our case series of 5 patients, POCUS provided quick and accurate diagnosis. Case 1: A patient after violent coughing becomes acutely breathless. Pneumothorax: Absence of lung sliding, “barcode sign.” on M-mode and presence of a lung point and lung pulse. Case 2: A case of SLE and hypoalbuminemia has progressive breathlessness over 2 months and decreased air entry on the right side. Pleural Effusion: Visualization of anechoic fluid collections in the pleural space with presence of the sinusoidal sign and the waving hand pattern. POCUS also helped this patient to quantify and tap the Right pleural effusion without causing a pneumothorax. Case 3: A 70 year old diabetic smoker, also a K/C/O dilated cardiomyopathy. Pulmonary Edema: Detection of B-lines and absence of A-lines indicating interstitial fluid accumulation. A plethoric poorly collapsible IVC and a poor EF 25% was also found. Case 4: 20 year female with post covid fibrosis develops fever, productive cough and acute breathlessness. Pneumonia: dynamic air bronchogram giving a Shred sign and a Starry Night sign S/O consolidation found. Case 5: A 20 year morbidly obese patient after a long travel from the US, presents to the ED in hypotension and hypoxia. DVT with Pulmonary Embolism: D sign, Mc Connells sign, Tricuspid Regurgitation, and plethoric non collapsible IVC. Thrombus noted in the Femoral vein. In emergency and critical care, POCUS enables swift diagnosis and guides interventions like thoracentesis, chest tube placement, and monitoring of disease progression. The advent of portable ultrasound devices and artificial intelligence integration has further expanded its accessibility and diagnostic capabilities.

Conclusion: POCUS has transformed pulmonary diagnostics by providing clinicians with a powerful, rapid, accurate bedside imaging modality that enhances patient diagnosis and care.

Shubham Agarwal1, Anant Mohan1, Vidushi Rathi Ish1, Karan Madan1, Vijay Hadda1, Saurabh Mittal1, Randeep Guleria1, Milind Hote2, Manoj Sahu2, Sandeep Seth3, Aarti Vij4, Maroof A. Khan5

1Department of Pulmonary, Critical Care and Sleep Medicine, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India, 2Department of CTVS, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India, 3Department of Cardiology, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India, 4Organ Retrieval and Banking Organisation, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India, 5Department of Biostatistics, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India

Introduction: Pulmonary rehabilitation (PR) in chronic respiratory diseases improves symptoms, quality of life, and exercise capacity. Lung Transplantation (LT) is the treatment of choice if disease progresses despite optimal management. As poor performance status (PS) has adverse peri-operative outcomes, improving PS via protocolised rehabilitation may increase chances of getting waitlisted.

Aims: Primary objective was to study effect of a protocolised PR programme on performance status using Karnofsky Performance Status (KPS) score. Secondary objectives were ECOG status, quality of life, symptom severity, sarcopenia, spirometry (PFT and DLCO), six-minute walk distance, and eligibility for LT waitlisting.

Methods: Prospective, single arm, interventional study on patients with end-stage lung disease, meeting referral criteria for LT. A protocolised 12-week hybrid hospital and home-based PR intervention was conducted, and all outcomes were assessed at baseline, and within 1 month of completion of the intervention.

Results: 75 patients were enrolled, and intervention completed by 51 patients (68%). 35 patients met LT listing criteria, 27 being “unfit” for LT at baseline, 18 of whom completed the intervention. Significant improvement seen in KPS, ECOG, SGRQ score, visual analogue scale (VAS) score for cough and dyspnoea, and sarcopenia for all 51 patients. Of the 18 patients unfit for waitlisting, 12 became fit, and 7 were waitlisted for LT.

Conclusion: Patients eligible for LT benefit from a hybrid PR programme, with improvement in KPS, quality of life and sarcopenia, and eligibility for LT waitlisting.

JJM Medical College, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Mercury is a heavy metal known to cause harmful effects on various organ systems. It exists in 3 forms-Elemental mercury, Organic mercury, and Inorganic mercury compounds. Mercury toxicity is hazardous when inhaled, intravenously administered or ingested and can result from both occupational and non-occupational exposure. The common manifestations are fever, gastrointestinal distress, respiratory complications, neurological abnormalities, nephrotoxicity and dermatological manifestations.

Case Report: A 38 years old female came with complaints of non-productive cough for 10 days with no significant past history and comorbidities. On examination Vitals-WNL, RS-B/L rhonchi. ChestXray showed high density opacity in all over lung fields. Patient reassessed with history and gave history of taking medications for infertility from the local traditional folk practitioner two tablets twice daily with betelleaf juice and cowmilk from past 1 months. Composition of medications showed Swarna (Gold), PARADA (Mercury), Gandhaka (Sulphur) in 1:8:16 ratio. HRCTthorax showed high density opacity with classical diffraction. CBC, RFT, LFT – WNL. Blood mercury level 46mcg/L, Urine mercury level – 14mcg/L increased. A multidisciplinary approach made to rule out other systemic manifestation but no obvious findings. Patient started with Dimercaprol injection 150 mg twice daily im for 11 days and repeat blood and urine mercury level monitored showed improvement.

Conclusion: This case report demonstrates the diagnostic and therapeutic challenges in managing a patient with mercury poisoning. The patients’ recovery relied on an interdisciplinary approach and timely medical interventions, highlighting the significances of coordinated care in managing toxicological emergencies.

P. Smitha, Akshay M. Hiremath, B. Vidyasagar

JJM Medical College, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Obstructive sleep apnea syndrome (OSAS) is frequent partial (hypopnea) or complete (apnea) upper airway collapse during sleep leads to oxygen desaturation, increased respiratory effort, arousal and sleep fragmentation. OSAS is considered independent risk factor for hypertension and has association with heart failure, arrhythmias, coronary disease, metabolic syndrome and type 2 diabetes. Although due increasing prevalence of OSAS, overnight technician attended polysomnography performed in sleep laboratory remains gold standard for diagnosis, its high cost, long waiting list and limited accessibility have made it impractical in OSA screening. This study evaluated the value of Stop BANG Questionnaire (SBQ) to screen for OSA and compared it with the oxygen desaturation index (ODI) and their combination. Objectives: 1. Evaluating the effectiveness of diagnostic value of individual and the combined SBQ and ODI with AHI in diagnosing OSA. 2. Assessing the individual and combined validity of the ODI and SBQ alongside the AHI in diagnosing OSA.

Methods: One year cross sectional study conducted among 80 patients who are clinically risk for OSA based on screening SBQ in comparison to the objective assessment who underwent standard overnight attended polysomnography (PSG level-1. PHILIPS RESPIRONICS SLEEPWARE G3) in Bapuji Hospital, attached to JJM Medical college, Davangere from May 2023 to May 2024.

Results: Of 80 screened patients 53 % had OSA. All the patients reported at least one suspected OSA symptom, the most common were snoring (72%) and tiredness (57%). SBQ showed increased sensitivity than specificity to screen OSA. As severity of OSA increased, the sensitivity increased and specificity decreased for both measurements. ODI achieved an increased specificity and could correctly diagnose OSA which was better than SBQ. For all severities of OSA, ODI alone displayed a larger AUC than SBQ and similar AUC to their combination.

Conclusion: The ODI demonstrated higher specificity and AUC compared to the SBQ. Additionally, combining ODI with SBQ did not enhance diagnostic accuracy. Thus, ODI may be a more effective and user-friendly option for initial OSA screening compared to SBQ

S. G. Sowmitha

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Saveetha Medical College and Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is a heterogenous lung condition characterised by chronic respiratory symptoms (dyspnea, cough, sputum production and/exacerbation) due to abnormalities in airways (bronchitis, bronchiolitis) and/or alveoli (emphysema) that cause persistent, often progressive, airflow obstuction. The chronic inflammation of COPD is due to recurrent colonization or due to recurring inflammatory process as an aftermath of bacterial colonization. WBC and its subtypes are well known markers of inflammation. During periods of stress the neutrophil levels go up and the lymphocyte levels come down, this is the basis behind calculating the NLR ratio. There are other inflammatory markers which have been under scrutiny including ESR, CRP, which need to be correlated with NLR. Measurement of FEV1 is a reliable marker to assess the degree of obstruction and disease severity. But this value does not signify the level of inflammation. Moreover, pulmonary function testing and exercise related tests cannot be performed in many COPD patients who are in exacerbation, hence at these junctures, biomarkers can help in risk and severity assessment and treatment.

Aim: To analyse the Neutrophil-Lymphocyte Ratio in blood among COPD patients as a marker of disease severity. The objectives of the study included:

1. To analyse the neutrophil-lymphocyte ratio in blood among COPD patients, 2. To find out the correlation of NLR ratio with severity of obstruction, RDW and see if NLR can be used to assess disease severity comparing with BODE index.

Methods: In a cross sectional design, 80 patients were recruited. Patients with acute exacerbation of COPD admitted in the respiratory medicine ward and also stable COPD patients attending the Respiratory Medicine OPD were chosen as study subjects. Data like MMRC grade, BMI, PFT, 6MWT, ESR, CRP, RDW, Absoute Neutrophl count and absolute lymphocyte count are collected. Also a chest xray is taken to rule out other pathologies.

Results: NLR has positive correlation with MMRC, RDW and BODE index. Thus NLR can be used as a marker of disease severity in COPD. According to ROC curve analysis of NLR, with 4.1 as cut off, NLR can be used to predict an exacerbation in COPD patients. NLR and RDW are increased among the exacerbated COPD patients compared to the stable patients. Hence both these markers can be used to denote the severity of the disease and also an exacerbated state. Also RDW is positively correlated to NLR, hence both these simple, cost effective markers can be used in routine practice to grade the severity of COPD. CRP, another acute phase reactant protein is also found to have a statistically significant association with COPD and is found to be elevated more in the exacerbated COPD patients compared to the stable ones.

Conclusion: NLR is a cost effective, easy, quick marker derived from routine CBC. It can be used as a marker of disease severity among COPD patients, and also to predict an exacerbation.

Sujata Chauthmal1, Suhas Bardapurkar2, Harshvardhan Vhora3, Virendra Singh4, Parthasarathi Bhattacharya5, Ragini Diwan6, Shreehas Bardapurkar2, Udaiveer Singh4, Sapna Madas1, Ruby Swami1, Abhishek Sabale8, Meena Lopez7, Jaideep Gogtay7, Sundeep Salvi1,8, Deesha Ghorpade1

1Chest Research and Training, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Shree Nursing Home, Aurangabad, Maharashtra, India, 3Vhora Chest Clinic, Baramati, Maharashtra, India, 4Asthma Bhavan, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India, 5Institute of Pulmocare and Research, Chakpachuria, West Bengal, India, 6Jyotirling Hospital, Kolhapur, Maharashtra, India, 7Cipla Ltd, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 8Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Small airways dysfunction (SAD) asthma is an emerging phenotype of asthma which is associated with more severe form of the disease, uncontrolled asthma, greater night-time awakenings, obesity and exercise-induced asthma. This phenotype of asthma has been shown to affect more than 50-60% of asthmatics globally. However, there is limited data on the prevalence of SAD in the Indian asthmatic population.

Aim: We aimed to study the prevalence of SAD among Indian asthmatics using lung oscillometry and spirometry.

Methods: Pulmonologists from five centres across India, who were members of the International Network for Lung Oscillometry Research (ARISE), were invited to participate in this study. The study involved asthmatic patients visiting their clinics, where lung oscillometry and spirometry were performed along with a questionnaire that captured data on demographics, risk factors, symptoms and co-morbid conditions. Oscillometry was conducted using the Antlia FOT device (iCALTECH, Bangalore, India), while spirometry was performed using devices that met the standards set by the American Thoracic Society (ATS) and the European Respiratory Society (ERS)-2019. SAD was defined using specific criteria: R5-R20 value of ≥1 cmH2O/l/sec or FEF25%-75% ≤ 65%.

Results:

Out of data obtained from 1969 asthmatic patients, 1334 were eligible for the final analysis. The prevalence of SAD using cut-off values of ≥ 1, > 0.9, > 0.8 and > 0.7 cmH2O/L/sec for R5-R20 were reported to be 60.6%, 65.8%, 72.2% and 77.4% respectively, whereas FEF25%-75% <65% was reported to be 61.8%. The prevalence of SAD amongst males and females was reported to be 77% and 82.4%, respectively. In SAD asthmatics, 50.7% of males and 53.5% of females were obese, while 18.8% and 15.7% were overweight, respectively. SAD was significantly more common among obese and overweight females (p = 0.002), and a similar trend was observed amongst SAD asthmatic males (p = 0.007).

Conclusion: The burden of SAD-Asthma among 1334 asthmatics visiting clinics across 5 centers in India was 60% using the cut-off of R5-R20 of ≥1 cmH2O/L/sec.

S. V. Suman, Ravi Tejraj Mehta, Ashok Jaiswal

Medical and Regulatory Affairs, Zydus Healthcare Limited, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Most of the currently available LABA/LAMA combinations are twice-daily therapies. Once-daily inhalers may provide better adherence & symptom control in COPD compared to twice-daily therapies. This study evaluated efficacy & safety of world’s first pMDI of vilanterol combined with glycopyrronium (Gly/Vt) in patients with COPD.

Methods: Prospective, randomized, double-blind, multicentre, phase III clinical trial. Patients of either gender (n = 288), 40-65 years, current/ex-smokers with moderate-severe COPD, CAT-score ≥ 10 with clinically stable COPD within 4weeks prior to screening were randomized to receive either Gly/Vt 25/12.5 mcg pMDI 2-puffs once-daily or Glycopyrronium/Formoterol (Gly/For) 9/4.8 mcg pMDI 2-puffs twice-daily for 12 weeks.

Results: Primary endpoint i. e., Change in trough FEV1 from baseline to week-12 was numerically more with Gly/Vt (LSM ± SD = 204.15 ± 21.77 ml with Gly/Vt & 178.37 ± 21.62 ml with Gly/For, mean difference 25.77 ml, p = 0.401). Trough FVC improved by 165.94 ± 385.72 ml with Gly/Vt & 154.07 ± 304.47 ml with Gly/For (p = 0.7719). Post-bronchodilator FEV1 improvement was 307.78 ± 416.22 ml & 252.46 ± 319.58 ml with Gly/Vt & Gly/For respectively, p = 0.2065. Post-bronchodilator FVC change from baseline was 180.98 ± 364.16 ml with Gly/Vt & 158.41 ± 297.92 ml with Gly/For (p = 0.5651). CAT score reduced by 11.20 ± 6.88 & 10.71 ± 7.04 in Gly/Vt & Gly/For group respectively, p = 0.5487. No significant difference in exacerbations were observed (Gly/Vt-13, Gly/For-15, p=0.7195; all exacerbations were mild). Both the treatments were well-tolerated, most common adverse events were cough & headache. No serious adverse events were reported.

Once-daily Gly/Vt improved FEV1, FVC with reduction in CAT & exacerbations similar to twice-daily Gly/For.

Conclusion: In patients with moderate-severe COPD, once-daily Vilanterol/Glycopyrronium significantly improved lung-function, symptom-control with good safety profile & was non-inferior to twice-daily Glycopyrronium/Formoterol.

Keywords: COPD, formoterol/glycopyrronium, glycopyrronium, vilanterol

Introduction: Human metapneumovirus (hMPV), a single-stranded RNA virus identified in 2001, primarily impacts high-risk populations. Its clinical presentation often overlaps with other respiratory pathogens, complicating diagnosis. This study seeks to characterize hMPV’s clinical and radiological features to enhance better understanding of this viral illness.

Methods: This retrospective study analyzed patients with hMPV detected via Filmarray Biofire (January 2023–November 2024) and had lower respiratory involvement as seen on computed tomography (CT). The clinical and radiological findings were reviewed to assess disease patterns.

Results: A cohort of 10 patients (mean age 57, with a predominance of females) was included in the study. 5 patients had diabetes and 3 were immunosuppressed. Avirup Maiti, Arup Halder, Raja Dhar, Shyam Krishnan, Beauty Biswas

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: The pre-test probability of obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) is assessed by several questionnaires. However, the validity of these questionnaires hasn’t been well verified in Indian population. The present study assesses whether the predictability of STOP BANG changes with adding extra parameter like waist circumference.

Objective: To identify the optimal predictors for OSA (mild, moderate, and severe) in Indian patients undergoing evaluation for sleep disorders.

Methods: We designed a questionnaire which aimed at looking at a new scoring system which might be more valid for an Indian population. The patients were selected based on a polysomnogram that was done and the study was carried out on successive patients over a one-year period.

Results: Waist circumference showed the strongest association with OSA severity (p < 0.001). Neck size >40 cm was also significantly associated with OSA severity, (p < 0.001). BMI >35 was associated with OSA severity but less prominently compared to waist circumference and neck size (p < 0.001). Waist circumference and BMI equally predicted ODI. For predicting T <90%, waist circumference outperformed neck circumference and BMI. The STOPBANG model (Odds Ratio: 2.83, AUC: 0.849, AIC: 164.2) outperformed the STOPBAWG model (Odds Ratio: 2.577, AUC: 0.819, AIC: 167.66). Including waist circumference as a categorical predictor as STOP BWANG improved AUC to 0.88, indicating a significant enhancement in model performance. Replacing neck circumference with waist circumference led to a slight reduction in predictive performance, but the models still demonstrated strong associations with OSA severity.

Conclusion: When we used the STOP-BWANG instead of the STOP-BANG, it is truly the best tool for predicting OSA in an Indian population.

Keywords: OSA, STOP BANG, STOP BAWG, STOP BWANG

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Background: Post-intensive care syndrome (PICS) is increasingly recognized among ICU survivors. This study aims to investigate the prevalence of PICS following discharge from a respiratory ICU and its relationship with ICU parameters, severity of illness, and overall health-related quality of life (HRQL).

Methods: A prospective observational study was conducted involving 122 ICU patients. Forty-four patients were excluded due to mortality or readmission within six months of discharge. The remaining 78 patients were assessed three months post-discharge using the Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale (HABC) and the Short Form-36 (SF-36) survey. Clinical and biochemical parameters, including APACHE II scores, PaCO2 levels, and p/f ratios at admission, were analyzed in correlation with PICS indicators.

Results: The average HABC score was 7.10, with 25% of the cohort displaying moderate PICS symptoms. The functional domain of HABC was most significantly affected. A strong positive correlation (p = 0.00001) was observed between APACHE II scores (mean: 10) and HABC scores, highlighting the influence of illness severity on PICS outcomes. Admission PaCO2 and p/f ratios also demonstrated significant relationships with HABC scores. Furthermore, an inverse correlation was identified between HABC scores and SF-36 results, emphasizing the negative impact of PICS on HRQL.

Conclusion: Approximately 20% of ICU survivors in this cohort experienced moderate PICS, with functional impairments being predominant. Key ICU metrics, including APACHE II scores and admission blood gas parameters, were associated with PICS development. The inverse relationship between HABC and SF-36 highlights the detrimental impact of PICS on quality of life, underlining the need for targeted interventions and rehabilitation strategies for ICU survivors.

Diptendu Dey, Shyam Krishnan, Nirmalya Banerjee, Raja Dhar

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Rapid intraoperative diagnostic methods are essential for confirming sampling accuracy and providing quick pathological insights. Frozen section (FS), a well-established technique, involves tissue freezing and microscopic examination, requiring specialized equipment. Imprint cytology (IC) offers a simpler alternative, involving pressing tissue on the slides for immediate cytological assessment. This study compares FS and IC in pulmonary biopsies to evaluate IC as a rapid diagnostic tool.

Methods: This study analysed 119 biopsy specimens (55 endobronchial, 56 pleural, and 8 transbronchial) from May 2023 to November 2024 at a tertiary care hospital in Eastern India. Specimens were evaluated using IC, FS, and final histopathology. Sensitivity, specificity, and procedure time were calculated for each technique.

Results: FS demonstrated an overall sensitivity of 89.74% and specificity of 90.24%, compared to 58.44% sensitivity and 92.86% specificity for IC. For endobronchial specimens, FS sensitivity and specificity were 92.30% and 87.50%, respectively, while IC achieved 64.10% sensitivity and 93.75% specificity. Procedure times averaged 28.4 minutes for FS and 11.2 minutes for IC. Both methods showed comparable positive predictive values (PPV) for malignant and tubercular lesions: FS at 94.11% and 100%, and IC at 92.5% and 100%, respectively.

IC exhibited significant specificity for both endobronchial and pleural specimen. PPV of IC almost equal to FS. Procedure time is significantly shorter in IC.

Conclusion: IC offers a reliable, cost-effective and rapid diagnostic alternative.

Keywords: Endobronchial, frozen section, imprint cytology, pleural

Divya Sree Kalakota, Swapnil S. Kulkarni

KEM Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Obstructive sleep apnea is defined by presence of repetitive episodes of upper airway obstruction during sleep with an AHI of atleast 5 events/hour and with persistent complaints of snoring, excessive day time somnolence, apneic spells, unrefreshing sleep and fatigue.

Methods: This retrospective study was conducted at a tertiary hospital in Pune using SACS score. Study group consisted of 80 patients out of which 50 patients were positive and 30 patients were negative for AHI, diagnosed at our hospital on the basis of history, probability scores and polysomnography was analyzed. Inclusion criteria-more than 40 years and both the genders. Subjects were categorised into 3 groups based on their SACS score (S-snoring, A-apnea, C-neck circumference in centimeters, S-systemic hypertension).

Results: A total of 80 patients were included in study. Patients were classified as per their SACS score as follows:

Irrespective of OSA, patients with moderate to high SACS score were found to have one of the following pre-existing cardiovascular diseases (systemic hypertension, Ischemic heart disease, arrythmias).

Conclusion: SACS score is a good predictor for diagnosis of OSA which is a quick and reliable method that can be used in OPD basis and most of patients with moderate to severe SACS score have OSA. Further studies are needed for better evidence to support diagnosis of OSA.

Niraj Dhakare, Radha Munje, Gyanshankar Mishra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Indira Gandhi Government Medical College, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB), particularly drug-resistant tuberculosis (DR-TB), continues to be a major global health threat, especially in India, where the emergence of drug resistance complicates treatment. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) to TB medications further challenge treatment compliance and patient outcomes. Understanding ADR profiles is critical for optimizing treatment strategies.

Objectives: This study aimed to evaluate the incidence, causality, severity, and risk factors associated with ADRs in DR-TB patients undergoing treatment, and to assess treatment compliance in those affected by ADRs.

Methods: An ambispective cohort study was conducted at a tertiary care institute from January 2023 to September 2024. The study included 174 DR-TB patients receiving treatment under the Programmatic Management of Drug-Resistant TB (PMDT). Data were analyzed using SPSS, and associations with ADR occurrence were assessed using the Chi-square and t-test. Inclusion criteria involved DR-TB patients aged ≥18 years, registered under PMDT-NTEP.

Results: Out of 174 patients, 79% (138/174) experienced ADRs, with common reactions being skin discoloration (60.34%), peripheral neuropathy (52.87%), and joint pain (28.74%). Most ADRs were mild (52.90%) or moderate (40.58%). Causality assessment classified 92.03% of ADRs as probable. Male patients and those with pulmonary TB or co-morbidities, such as diabetes and hypertension, were more likely to develop ADRs. No significant association was found between ADRs and HIV status or addiction history. Bedaquiline was found to be relatively cardio-safe.

Conclusion: This study highlights the need for proactive ADR management, the development of less toxic drugs, and enhanced monitoring strategies to improve treatment adherence and outcomes in DR-TB patients. The findings support the expanded use of Bedaquiline in all-oral regimens and advocate for therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) to optimize treatment.

Keywords: Adverse drug reactions, bedaquiline, drug-resistant tuberculosis, pharmacovigilance, treatment adherence

Introduction: Syndrome Z is the coexistence of two chronic diseases obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) and metabolic syndrome (MS). Metabolic syndrome and OSA share a similar pathophysilogic milieu that increase the risk of cardiovascular events. Metabolic syndrome include central obesity, hypertension, insulin resistance and hyperlipidaemia. The objective is to investigate the prevalence and severity of syndrome Z at tertiary care center.

Methods: A prospective study was conducted in 30 patients in tertiary care center who met the screening criteria for OSA using STOPBANG Questionnaire and the Epworth Sleepiness Scale. All patients underwent overnight level 2 polysomnography using Alice PDx Philips machine. Parameters of MS and OSA were noted using National Cholesterol Education Program-Adult Treatment Panel (NCEP-ATP) and American Academy of Sleep Medicine guidelines. We did a comparative study using metabolic syndrome parameters and AHI level in PSG in study group patients.

Results: Mean age of participants was 51.6 years, 72% were male, Mean BMI and waist circumference were 30.4kg/m2 and 110.6cm respectively. Almost 73% of participants were k/c/o DM. 83.5 % of hypertension and 62.8% of Dyslipidemia. The prevalence of OSA in the entire group was 93.0%. 10 patients who fulfilled all the criteria for diagnosis of MS had severe OSA.

Conclusion: The prevalence of Syndrome Z in study individuals was high. OSA may represent an important risk factor for development of MS. Therefore, it is essential for clinicians to systematically evaluate the presence of metabolic abnormalities in OSA patients and vice versa.

Introduction: Interstitial lung diseases (ILD) represent a heterogeneous group of lung conditions characterized by dyspnoea, hypoxia, restrictive ventilatory defects, and diffuse pulmonary infiltrates on radio imaging. Objectives: This study aimed to evaluate the incidence, causality, severity, and risk factors for cardiovascular disorders associated with ILDs.

Methods: A cross-sectional study was conducted at a tertiary care centre from October 2022 to September 2024. The study includes 73 ILD patients attending OPD/IPD of centre. Results: Out of 73 patients, 26 (35.6%) had normal ECG, 8 (10.9%) had RBBB pattern, and 6 (8.22%) had p-pulmonale. No significant difference in gender and different age groups. 46% of CTD-ILD, 69.2% Of IPF, 60% Of HP, and 100% of CPFE had PH.

Conclusion: The increase prevalence of PH can exacerbate symptoms of breathlessness and despite optimal disease specific treatments and management of secondary infections, patients may not experience satisfactory symptomatic improvement. Thus, this study highlights the need for proactive cardiac evaluation and their management in ILD patients.

Introduction: Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) is a complex allergic disorder caused by hyper-immune reactions against the offending fungus, Aspergillus fumigatus. HRCT and Chest X-ray play crucial roles in diagnosing and monitoring ABPA. The ISHAM criteria for ABPA diagnosis involve a combination of serological markers and radiological findings. However, these criteria are not always used for diagnosing ABPA.

Aim: This study aimed to investigate the radiological findings in Allergic Broncho Pulmonary Aspergillosis (ABPA) patients across multiple centers in India.

Methods: An observational study was conducted by pulmonologists from 23 centers registered with the Respiratory Research Network (RRN). ABPA patients underwent chest X-rays and high-resolution computed tomography (HRCT). Based on the International Society for Human and Animal Mycology (ISHAM) 2013 criteria, patients were classified into three groups:

Group 1: Fully meeting ISHAM criteria.Group 2: Partially meeting ISHAM criteria (positive HRCT or serological results in two parameters).Group 3: Diagnosed by a physician but not meeting ISHAM criteria.

Group 1: Fully meeting ISHAM criteria.

Group 2: Partially meeting ISHAM criteria (positive HRCT or serological results in two parameters).

Group 3: Diagnosed by a physician but not meeting ISHAM criteria.

Results: Of the 802 patients, 37.2% (n = 299) were in Group 1, 50.2% (n = 403) in Group 2, and 12.4% (n = 100) in Group 3. HRCT was performed in 84% of the patients, and 81% of those (n = 544) showed findings consistent with ABPA. Key differences between Group 1 and Group 2 in abnormal HRCT findings included bronchiectasis (P = 0.002), high-attenuation mucus (P = 0.047), centrilobular nodules (P = 0.006), tree-in- bud appearance (P = 0.04), and mosaic attenuation (P = 0.03).

Chest X-rays were performed in 717 patients, with 64% (n = 458) displaying abnormalities [Table 1]. Co-morbidities such as hypertension and diabetes were most prevalent in Group 2.

Radiological features in the 3 patient groups

HRCT: High-resolution computed tomography

Conclusion: ABPA patients who fully met ISHAM criteria exhibited significantly distinct HRCT and chest X-ray findings compared to those not meeting the full criteria. These findings underscore the importance of ISHAM guidelines in ensuring accurate diagnosis of ABPA.

Keywords: Imaging Sciences in Pulmonology, Chest Infections
