Background: Adherence to inhaler devices play an important role in the treatment of Asthma and Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD). Our recent study (Adherence to Inhalers in COPD and Asthma-AICA) showed that, 61.8% patients had poor adherence to inhalers. In this post-hoc analysis, we aimed to determine the factors influencing the adherence to inhalers among these patients.

Methodology: AICA was a multi-centric, observational study in which we collected data regarding adherence to inhalers using the Test for Adherence to Inhalers (TAI) from 390 Asthma and COPD patients across India. In this study, we analysed individual components from the TAI scale for the patients who had poor adherence (n = 241).

Results: Out of the 241 Obstructive Airway Disease (OAD) patients with poor adherence (63.1% Asthma and 36.9% COPD), 59.8% reported that they sometimes forgot to take their inhalers. A higher proportion of patients reported they stopped (42.7% sometimes and 22.8% always) using inhalers after feeling well. Patients also reported (51.9% sometimes and 14.1% always) using fewer inhalations than prescribed. 25.3% and 8.3% patients reported stopping inhalers sometimes and always due to side effects, respectively. Only 14.1% mentioned that cost was sometimes a barrier to inhaler use, whereas, 81.7% patients stated that cost was never a barrier. Subgroup analysis of individual components for asthma and COPD patients revealed better adherence among COPD patients with fewer participants reporting forgetting or stopping inhaler use after feeling well as compared to asthma patients [Figure 1].

Individual component analysis of TAI for poor adherence. (a) Individual component analysis for asthma patients (b) Individual component analysis for COPD patients

Conclusion: Factors like forgetting to take inhalers, discontinuing use when feeling better and using fewer inhalations than prescribed were the major contributors that resulted in poor adherence to inhalers. Fear of side effects was not found to be a major concern as two-third of the participants reported never stopping their inhalers due to this reason. Individual component analysis revealed that COPD patients had better adherence as compared to asthma patients, perhaps due to the severity of the condition and the need for persistent use of inhaler. These findings highlight the importance of raising awareness and providing training to OAD patients about the need for consistent adherence to inhaler therapy.

Keywords: Poor adherence, TAI, COPD, Asthma, Inhalers.

Yogita Hatmode, Sameer Bansal, T. V. Sagar, Prerana Abhisheki, Ravindra Mehta

Department of Pulmonary Rehabilitation, Vaayu Chest and Sleep Specialists, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: PR delivery in India has limited data, in the face of exponentially rising chronic respiratory diseases. Awareness, acceptance, adherence and economics are barriers to effective PR. The 3T’s of time/travel/traffic are a challenge in most metros. To surmount this issue, we formed a unique model of OOHH (onsite/online/hybrid and home), to enhance PR delivery. This study describes the preliminary results of this approach, focusing on relevant outcomes.

Methods: PR done with standard guidelines was carried out in VAAYU Pulmonary Rehab center, an outpatient facility with the OOHH model-we report findings of the 1st 15 months. All patients were initially offered onsite PR, and then hybrid models were adopted for the 3T issues. Pure online rehab was done for out of town patients, and home PR was offered for patients who could not be brought to the PR center. Baseline characteristics, patients preferences and outcomes were noted and analysed.

Results: 842 patients were referred for the PR program, of which 390 patients (46%) were eventually enrolled.189/390 (48%) were males, mean age 63.6 yrs. A total of 5421 PR sessions were conducted in 15 months. PR delivery modes were onsite, online, hybrid and home-based in 71%, 16%, 9%, 4% respectively. Most common indications for PR were ILD, COPD, severe bronchial asthma, bronchiectasis, severe lung infection sequelae and others in 33%, 22%, 14%, 10%, 7%, 14% patients respectively. Overall adherence to the PR program was 77%. Improvements were seen in 6-minute walk distance in 84% patients, quality of life scores (CAT AND SGRQ) in 93.4% patients and fatigue scores in 91% patients at the end of the PR program (minimum 6 sessions).

Conclusion: This unique delivery model in a large series of patients in an urban metro setting, shows a high engagement and completion rate. High adherence (77%) and significant improvements in exercise capacity, quality of life, and fatigue were observed. This is the first study to show that the ‘OOHH model’ as an innovative PR delivery mechanism can surmount practical issues with better outcomes. A flexible PR delivery system can be a tremendous value addition to enhance accessibility, adherence and effectivity.

Keywords: Chronic obstructive pulmonary diseases, interstitial lung disease, pulmonary rehabilitation

Vinay C. Dharmadhikari

S. S. Institute of Medical Sciences and Research Centre, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Electronic cigarettes are battery powered electronic devices which aerosolize liquid that contains nicotine, humectants and flavours. E-cigarette smoking is becoming a trend among young adults and gaining wide acceptance as they are considered a less harmful and safer alternative to conventional cigarette smoking. Numerous studies have shown that E-cigarettes are not emission free and presence of nicotine increases levels of NO exhaled from consumers and hence marked airway inflammation. Increased oxidative stress, vascular endothelial damage, impaired endothelial function and changes in vascular tone have all been reported in different human studies on vaping. E-cigarettes are expanding nicotine market by attracting youth who are likely to consider vaping as a cooler alternative to conventional smoking. Most of the people still remain unaware about e-cigarettes and the harmful effects caused by them. Hence the study was conducted among medical undergraduates and interns to assess their knowledge and understanding about E-cigarettes.

Methods: A Questionnaire based study among 200 medical undergraduates and interns of a tertiary care hospital in Davanagere, regarding E-cigarettes. The questionnaire consisted of three parts-Sociodemographic details, Knowledge, Beliefs, and Attitude about E-cigarettes and comprised of 13 questions. Quantitative data was expressed as mean+/-SD. Qualitative data was expressed as numbers and percentages. Software SPSS-22 was used for statistical analysis.

Results: The mean age of study population was 20.37 years with+/-2.12 years. Majority of the study population were males (63%). Among 126 males, 15 were smokers, none of the 74 females were smokers.123 out of 200 had heard about E-cigarettes. 81 knew a little about E cigarettes, 36 had reasonable knowledge, whereas 6 had substantial knowledge about E-cigarettes.

More than 60% of medical undergraduates and interns knew about E-cigarettes, more than 50% believed that they are safer than conventional smoking, helpful in smoking cessation and opined that they have to be regulated at public and workplaces.

Conclusion: Comprehensive awareness programs may be needed in further educating undergraduates and interns about E-cigarettes hence can help in educating patients in future.

Keywords: Awareness, conventional moking, electronic cigarettes, questionnaire

Vishal Bhise, Radha Munje, Gyanshankar Mishra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Indira Gandhi Government Medical College and Hospital, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS) syndrome is a severe hypersensitivity reaction linked to various drugs, including antitubercular therapy (ATT), though rarely reported. This case highlights a rare Rifampicin-induced DRESS syndrome, emphasizing early recognition and management to prevent complications.

An 18-year-old female was diagnosed with drug-sensitive pulmonary tuberculosis and initiated on a shorter regimen under programmatic research: two months of daily Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Pyrazinamide, Ethambutol, and Moxifloxacin (2HRZEM), followed by two months of Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Ethambutol, and Moxifloxacin (2HREM). After 26 days, she developed drug-induced hepatitis, necessitating temporary discontinuation of ATT. Following recovery, the regimen was restarted. Subsequently, the patient presented with fever, maculopapular rash, eosinophilia, and liver enzyme abnormalities. A diagnosis of DRESS syndrome was confirmed using RegiSCAR criteria. Rifampicin was identified as the causative drug through rechallenge protocols. To manage the adverse reaction, the ATT regimen was modified to exclude Rifampicin. The revised regimen included Streptomycin, Levofloxacin, Isoniazid, and Ethambutol during the intensive phase (2 months), followed by Levofloxacin, Isoniazid, and Ethambutol during the continuation phase (4 months). The patient tolerated the revised treatment well, with resolution of symptoms and successful completion of therapy.

Conclusion: DRESS syndrome, though rare with ATT, must be considered in patients with rash, eosinophilia, and systemic involvement. Drug rechallenge under close monitoring is critical for identifying causative agents. Early diagnosis, drug withdrawal, and therapy modification ensured a positive outcome, highlighting the importance of individualized care.

Vishwambar Shridhar Khadekar, Jairaj P. Nair, Sonal Karpe, Swapnil Thorve, Siddharth Waghmare

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Lokmanya Tilak Medical College and General Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Depression is known to be more prevalent in patients with tuberculosis (TB). This issue is particularly pronounced in patients with multi-drug resistant TB (MDR-TB), where the complexity of the disease, extended treatment regimens, and increased mortality rates contribute to higher depression rates.

Methods: Fifty patients with drug-resistant TB receiving treatment were interviewed using the PHQ-9, for measuring the severity of depression this was a cross sectional point prevalence study to assess the prevalence and severity of depression among these patients.

Results: This was observed that 94% of MDR TB patients had depression. Depression was present in all age group but the levels of depression varied across the ages. In patients aged <30 years, 35% exhibited minimal depression and 25% mild depression, with no cases of severe depression. For those aged 30–60 years, 39.1% reported moderate depression, particularly males, while 34.8% had minimal depression. Among patients >60 years, minimal and mild depression were equally common (42.9% each), with moderate depression observed in 14.3%. Gender differences were also notable, as males in the 30–60 years’ group experienced higher rates of depression, whereas females in the <30 years’ group exhibited depression. Across all age groups, minimal depression was most prevalent, followed by mild depression. Moderate depression was more frequent in males aged 30–60 years, while severe depression was absent.

Conclusion: As Depression is commonly prevalent the need for integrated mental health care in MDR-TB management is necessary to improve treatment adherence and quality of life

Limitation: Etiology of depression needs to be studied with a larger sample size as the disease severity duration of treatment, pill load and drug like cycloserine in the regimen of treatment cannot be determined.

Keywords: Depression, MDR-TB, PHQ-9, Tuberculosis

Veeravalli Varun Teja, Somanath Dash

Department of Respiratory Medicine, GSL Medical College, Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Drug-induced lupus erythematosus (DLE) is a rare response that arises following drug exposure, resulting in systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) symptoms in the absence of pre-existing lupus.

Case: A 42-year-old patient, with a diagnosis of pulmonary tuberculosis which is sputum CBNAAT detected, started on Anti-tubercular therapy (ATT), he came for a follow-up after 3 months, where he developed dark patches on his face, upper and lower extremities, and scalp, photosensitivity, scarring alopecia, and malar rash and ANA result found to be positive. The patient was found to have drug-induced Discoid Lupus Erythematosus (DLE) and was prescribed 200 mg of hydroxychloroquine tablets for treatment.

Conclusion: DLE is an idiosyncratic reaction in which drug exposure triggers SLE-like features in individuals without pre-existing lupus. Patients with DLE are believed to have a genetic predisposition, and an alternative theory suggests a link with slow acetylators of INH.

Keywords: Anti nuclear antibody, discoid lupus erythematosus, isoniazid, tuberculosis

Department of Respiratory Medicine, TNMC and BYL Nair Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Haemoptysis remains a critical and often life-threatening presentation in respiratory medicine. It is a diagnostic and therapeutic challenge, particularly in endemic regions where tuberculosis (TB) is prevalent. TB, with its potential to cause cavitary lung lesions, is a major risk factor for secondary infections like chronic pulmonary aspergillosis (CPA). CPA often develops in pre-existing lung cavities, creating a complex interplay between these conditions.

Case Report: A 28-year male presented with complaints of cough with expectoration, haemoptysis and fever for 5 days. he was evaluated with sputum studies which came MTB not detected and CTPA suggested no evidence of pulmonary thromboembolism and right upper lobe fungal ball [Figure 1]. Clinico-radiologicaly weight adjusted empirical ATT and antifungal was started in view of past inadequate att. Clinically, the patient improved and discharged. Four months later, the patient presented with recurrent massive haemoptysis and hemodynamic compromise. The patient inotropic support, blood transfusions. The patient was re-evaluated with sputum was negative and CTPA suggestive of chronic pulmonary aspergillosis with fungal ball [Figure 2]. Patient underwent a Bronchial artery embolization. The patient was stabilised and discharged on anti-fungal medications and referred for surgical lobectomy.

Right upper lobe fungal ball

Chronic pulmonary aspergillosis with fungal ball

Conclusion: This case underscores the importance of a structured diagnostic approach, timely intervention, and individualized therapy in managing haemoptysis in patients with a history of TB and chronic pulmonary aspergillosis. Collaborative, multidisciplinary management remains the cornerstone of care.

Haemoptysis due to chronic pulmonary aspergillosis can be managed with medical, interventional and surgical line of management.

Shanmugapriya K.1, Preethi Evangeline2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, SLMCH, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India, 2SLMCH, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Breast tuberculosis, also known as tuberculous mastitis, is a very uncommon type of tuberculosis commonly presented in young women. It accounts for <0.1% of all breast illnesses and <2% of TB cases. Even in TB-endemic nations like India, tubercular mastitis is frequently misdiagnosed as pyogenic breast abscess or breast cancer. We present a case of tuberculous mastitis, which presented as a breast abscess with no history of tuberculosis.

Case Report: A 20 year-old girl presented with a lump in her right breast for two months that ruptured the day before her hospital visit, associated with discharge of pus and intermittent pain. She also noted a palpable lump in her right axilla. Investigations were all normal except for erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 100 mm/hr and positive Mantoux test of 20 x 20 mm induration with vesiculation. Chest X-ray was not suggestive of tuberculosis. Family screening of TB was negative. Breast ultrasound revealed mastitis with an abscess in the right breast and lymphadenopathy in the right axilla. Fine needle aspiration cytology (FNAC) was performed, which revealed sheets of lymphocytes mixed with aggregates of epithelioid histiocytes reflective of granulomatous inflammation, suggesting a case of tuberculous mastitis. The patient was started with empirical antituberculosis therapy (ATT). After completion of intensive phase of ATT, she reported back with a swelling in the left axilla and a lump in the left breast of one month duration. Abscess was drained. Acid-fast staining of the discharge demonstrated acid-fast bacilli (AFB). Gene-Xpert was done, which reported the presence of Mycobacterium tuberculosis and rifampicin resistance was not detected. On follow-up after 3 months, the enlarged lymph node had regressed, the abscess had resolved and the patient was asymptomatic.

Conclusion: There are very few cases of breast TB reported. Most commonly, women of reproductive age group, including multiparous and pregnant women are affected. In our case, an adolescent girl presenting with bilateral tuberculous mastitis makes the diagnosis challenging. Concomitant axillary lymphadenopathy is common in this form of mastitis. A trial of ATT combined with routine clinical evaluation is strongly recommended if there is a high clinical suspicion of TB.

Keywords: Anti-tubercular therapy, axillary lymphadenopathy, breast TB, tuberculous mastitis

Razak Khan

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Miliary tuberculosis refers to haematogenous spread of the tubercular bacilli resulting in dissemination of the disease. It might be associated with multiple atypical manifestations that result in a delayed diagnosis, often a missed diagnosis. One such manifestation is that of pancytopenia which is a rare entity in miliary tuberculosis.

Case Report: Here is a case of 30 year old female who came with complaint of generalised weakness since 3 months, low grade fever since 3 months, cough with minimal mucoid expectoration (whitish in colour) since 2 months, Patient also complaint of shortness of breath (grade-1) which worsened over 2 months to breathlessness at rest which is the time when patient presented to the OPD. CBC was done which showed pancytopenia. Chest x ray revealed multiple millet sized nodules spread over B/l lung fields, following which HRCT – THORAX was done which also revealed multiple nodules less than 3 mm in size and a diagnosis of miliary tuberculosis was made. Peripheral blood smear and bone marrow biopsy was planned. Bone marrow biopsy revealed well formed granulomas with reduced cellularity. Patient was started on anti-tubercular treatment with methylprednesolone and Filgrastim. With initiation of treatment patient started showing improvement in CBC values with clinical improvement. Patient was discharged on ATT and after 3 months patient came for follow up with normal CBC values.

Conclusion: It is imperative to start anti-tubercular therapy as early as possible in cases of Miliary Tuberculosis to ensure a better prognosis

Introduction: Intra cranial tuberculomas are rare and represent a serious form of tuberculosis caused by hematogenous spread of mycobacterium tuberculosis

Case Report: A 15 yr. old male presented with complaints of fever, loss of weight since 3 months, one episode of tonic clonic seizure 4 days back, headache and left sided weakness since 1 day. Past h/o: no significant past history. Chest x-ray shows cavities at right lower lobe and left upper lobe. Ct chest shows few consolidatory patches seen involving right lower lobe and left upper lobe, multiple fibrocavitory lesions with changes in superior segment of right lower lobe and apico posterior segment of left upper lobe. MRI BRAIN: T2 hypointense focus is noted at left frontal lobe with mild perifocal edema in periphery

Conclusion: The combination of MRI findings with clinical features can enhance the overall diagnostic accuracy for intra cranial tuberculomas. Diagnosing and treatment with anti tubercular theraphy with corticosteroids and anti epileptics are corner stones.

K. S. Vishnu

B. J. Government Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Musculoskeletal tuberculosis, although less common, is one of the prevalent form of extrapulmonary tuberculosis. In rare instances, tuberculosis can affect the chest wall, leading to rib destruction. Typically, such cases are accompanied by lesions in the pleuro-pulmonary region. Here, we present a case of tubercular osteomyelitis of the rib, resulting in its destruction, without any associated lung parenchymal involvement.

Case Report: A 15-year-old female presented to the outpatient department with a 4-month history of left-sided chest pain, weight loss over the past 3-4 months, and intermittent episodes of fever for the past month. The patient was referred from an outside hospital for a Transthoracic Needle Biopsy based on the findings from an outside CECT thorax report. On examination, the patient was vitally stable, and no palpable swelling of the chest wall was noted. CECT Thorax showed a well-defined, heterogeneously enhancing soft tissue mass located at the posterior aspect of the left second rib, with underlying bony erosion. The mass extends into the muscular plane of the left posterior chest wall and is seen abutting the first left rib superiorly. Mediastinal necrotic lymphadenopathy is also noted. These findings are indicative of a neoplastic origin, likely originating from the bone, such as Ewing’s sarcoma. A CT Guided Trans Thoracic Needle Biopsy was performed from the lesion. The histopathology report of the biopsy taken from the soft tissue lesion at the posterior aspect of the second left rib indicates chronic granulomatous inflammation, suggestive of tuberculosis, and there was no evidence of malignancy. Treatment included the Pediatric AKT regimen. After 6 months of AKT, the patient was followed up and reported significant improvement in symptoms. A repeat CT was performed to assess radiological resolution, which showed a significant reduction in the size of the lesion.

Conclusion: The findings in this case highlight the importance of maintaining a high suspicion of tuberculosis in patients presenting with destructive rib cage lesions, especially in a country like India where tuberculosis is prevalent.

Although not common, Musculoskeletal Tuberculosis should be considered in the differential diagnosis of any patient presenting with nonspecific chest pain and radiological evidence of rib cage bony erosion.

P. Arunan, L. Muthukumaran, N. Meenakshi, R. Sridhar

Chettinad Hospital and Research Institute, Kelambakkam, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) remains a global health challenge, with early diagnosis and treatment ensuring a high cure rate. Medical interns, as frontline healthcare providers, play a crucial role in TB management. Enhancing their knowledge, attitude, and behaviour (KAB) is vital for improving TB control. This experimental study evaluates the impact of an educational intervention on the KAB of medical interns towards TB and the National Tuberculosis Elimination Program (NTEP).

Methods: A one-group pretest-post-test design was used to assess the effectiveness of the intervention among 110 medical interns at Chettinad Medical College. Data were collected using a WHO-based KAB questionnaire administered before and after the intervention. The intervention consisted of educational sessions focusing on TB pathophysiology, NTEP guidelines, and tools like GeneXpert and Nikshay. Paired t-tests and Wilcoxon Signed Ranks tests were used to analyse pretest and post-test differences.

Results: Knowledge - Mean scores increased from 42.85% to 81.33%, with 70% achieving adequate knowledge post-intervention (p < 0.001). Attitude - Scores improved from 43.64% to 77.73%, with adequate attitude rising from 6.4% to 43.6% (p < 0.001). Behaviour - Mean scores increased from 40.73% to 74.00%, with adequate behaviour improving from 13.6% to 58.2% (p < 0.001).

Conclusion: This study demonstrates the effectiveness of a structured educational intervention in improving the KAB of medical interns. Greater emphasis on clinical exposure is essential to translate knowledge and attitudes into practical behaviour.

Keywords: Educational intervention, experimental study, medical interns, NTEP, TB

D. Archanaa, Shanmugapriya K.

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Sri Lalithambigai Medical College and Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: The Dual Burden of Diabetes and Extrapulmonary Tuberculosis: A Growing Public Health Concern. The intersection of diabetes and tuberculosis is becoming an emerging public health challenge, with a notable rise in cases of extrapulmonary tuberculosis (EPTB) among diabetic patients. Diabetes, known for its role in weakening the immune system, is now recognized as an independent risk factor for tuberculosis. This bidirectional relationship between the two conditions poses significant health risks, as each condition can exacerbate the other. Given the growing prevalence of diabetes worldwide, understanding its impact on EPTB is crucial. This study aims to investigate the incidence and key predictors of extrapulmonary tuberculosis among diabetic patients. Insights from this research could inform targeted interventions and guide the development of integrated healthcare strategies to manage the dual burden of diabetes and tuberculosis.

Methods: This institutionally-based retrospective cohort study was conducted among 76 diabetic patients at Sri Lalithambigai Hospital between January 2024 and July 2024. All eligible diabetic patients who met the inclusion criteria were included in the study. Data were collected and entered using EpiData Version 4.6 and analyzed using STATA Version 17. The survival time of diabetic patients was estimated using the Kaplan-Meier survival curve, and differences in survival times across various categorical variables were assessed using the log-rank test. Both bivariable and multivariable Cox proportional hazards regression models were fitted to identify independent predictors of tuberculosis among diabetic patients.

Results: Incidence of EPTB in 76 Patients:

Observed cases: Approximately 76/500×17≈2.6. This can be rounded to 2 or 3 cases in the smaller cohort.Incidence rate: The incidence rate remains consistent at 1.5 per 100 person-years.

Observed cases: Approximately 76/500×17≈2.6. This can be rounded to 2 or 3 cases in the smaller cohort.

Incidence rate: The incidence rate remains consistent at 1.5 per 100 person-years.

Person-Years of Follow-Up: To calculate the follow-up time for 76 patients: Follow-up time = 76/500 × 1101.5PY = 167.83. Thus, the total follow-up time for 76 patients is approximately 168 person-years.

Factors Associated with Increased EPTB Risk:

Age: Older diabetic patients remain at significantly increased risk (AIR: 4.8, 95% CI: 1.2–20.7, p = 0.03).

Diabetic medication: Certain medications increase the risk (AIR: 1.4, 95% CI: 1.24–16, p = 0.03).History of PTB: Having had pulmonary tuberculosis before diabetic follow-up increases risk (AIR: 1.5, 95% CI: 3.2–6.9, p = 0.01).Alcohol consumption: History of alcohol use significantly increases risk (AIR: 4.0, 95% CI: 1.2–13, p = 0.02).

Diabetic medication: Certain medications increase the risk (AIR: 1.4, 95% CI: 1.24–16, p = 0.03).

History of PTB: Having had pulmonary tuberculosis before diabetic follow-up increases risk (AIR: 1.5, 95% CI: 3.2–6.9, p = 0.01).

Alcohol consumption: History of alcohol use significantly increases risk (AIR: 4.0, 95% CI: 1.2–13, p = 0.02).

Protective Factor:

Normal BMI (18.5–25): Associated with a reduced risk of developing EPTB (AIR: 0.22, 95% CI: 0.06–0.76, p = 0.02).

Normal BMI (18.5–25): Associated with a reduced risk of developing EPTB (AIR: 0.22, 95% CI: 0.06–0.76, p = 0.02).

Implications for 76 Patients:

For a smaller cohort of 76 diabetic patients, the findings suggest:

Targeted interventions are essential for individuals with the above risk factors.Prevention strategies, such as maintaining a healthy BMI, should remain a focus to reduce the burden of EPTB in diabetic populations.

Targeted interventions are essential for individuals with the above risk factors.

Prevention strategies, such as maintaining a healthy BMI, should remain a focus to reduce the burden of EPTB in diabetic populations.

While the absolute numbers are scaled down, the significance of these findings is proportionally relevant and emphasizes the need for vigilant monitoring in smaller diabetic cohorts.

Conclusion: In this study, we found a high rate of extra-pulmonary tuberculosis among diabetic patients. Factors significantly linked with increased risk of extra-pulmonary tuberculosis included age, using insulin as a hypoglycemic medication, having a past history of PTB before diabetic follow-up initiation, and a history of alcohol consumption, while BMI was associated with a rate reduction of EPTB. Early screening and treatment for extra-pulmonary tuberculosis is highly recommended at diabetes mellitus follow-up for patients with the above risk factors.

Keywords: Diabetes mellitus, extra-pulmonary tuberculosis, predictors of extra-pulmonary tuberculosis

Himanya Tatineni, S. Chandrasekar, Kaleeshwari, L. Muthukumaran, R. Sridhar, N. Meenakashi

Department Respiratory Medicine, Chettinad Hospital and Research Institute, Kelambakkam, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: Tuberculosis (TB) remains a global health challenge. The WHO’s End TB Strategy aims to reduce TB incidence by 90% between 2015 and 2030. India’s National TB Elimination Programme (NTEP) provides tuberculosis preventive treatment (TPT) to household contacts (HHCs) of pulmonary TB patients aged <5 years and HHCs aged >5 years without TB infection.

Objective: To assess the knowledge and attitude of HHCs towards TPT, evaluate adherence and reasons for discontinuation, identify programmatic obstacles, and assess adverse drug reactions.

Methods: A cross-sectional study using a structured questionnaire via telephone calls was conducted with data from Nikshay, targeting HHCs of active pulmonary TB cases from 2024 in Thiruporur TU. Descriptive analysis was performed on knowledge, attitude, adherence, adverse effects, and barriers.

Results: Of 260 registered, 214 participated. 97% had positive knowledge about TPT, with 98% recognizing its preventive role and 100% informed by health workers. Attitude was positive in 86%, with 80% strongly agreeing on TB prevention benefits. Adherence was 75%, while 25% discontinued treatment. Awareness of side effects was universal, and all participants acknowledged free medication availability and clear staff communication. Barriers included difficulty visiting health centers (4%). Older individuals (>55 years) faced difficulty like missing the dose and loosing interest.

Conclusion: While knowledge and attitude towards TPT were high, adherence remains suboptimal, especially among older participants. Addressing age-related challenges and logistical barriers is essential for improving adherence and achieving NTEP goals

Siramsetti Manoj Sreevatsav, Somanath Dash

Department of Respiratory Medicine, GSL Medical College, Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Evans syndrome is a rare autoimmune disorder characterized by simultaneous or sequential autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA) and immune thrombocytopenia. Co-occurrence of pulmonary tuberculosis (PTB) in such a setting, compounded by chronic liver disease (CLD), poses significant diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. This report details the intricate interplay of these conditions in a middle-aged female, emphasizing the need for multidisciplinary management.

Case Report: A 41-year-old housewife presented with shortness of breath, chronic cough with expectoration, fever, and loss of appetite for a month. She was a known case of AIHA (ANA-positive) diagnosed two years ago and managed with transfusions. Additionally, she had diabetes (3 months) and hypothyroidism (3 years) on regular medication. Biomass fuel exposure history suggested a predisposition to chronic respiratory disease. Imaging revealed extensive pulmonary pathology: patchy consolidations, cavitations, pleural thickening, and ground-glass opacities indicative of PTB. Concurrent liver findings (hepatosplenomegaly, fatty liver, hepatic hemangiomas, portal hypertension, ascites) confirmed CLD. Laboratory investigations showed anemia, thrombocytopenia, elevated D-dimer and CRP, deranged liver function, and albuminuria. PTB diagnosis was confirmed by sputum TRUNAAT positivity. Managing TB in CLD required initial pyrazinamide cessation, later reintroduced in an adjusted regimen. On follow-up, the patient clinically improved on anti-tubercular therapy (ATT).

This case highlights the critical importance of individualized care in complex cases involving rare autoimmune disorders like Evans syndrome, TB, and CLD. Early identification and tailored treatment prevent further complications, underscoring the role of vigilant multidisciplinary approaches.

Conclusion: Evans syndrome co-existing with PTB and CLD necessitates a comprehensive evaluation and cautious therapeutic strategy to avoid drug toxicity and optimize outcomes. A coordinated team effort is essential for managing such intricate cases effectively.

Sarthak Jindal

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Overall, 40–60% of cases suspected clinically and radiologically for PTB yield negative-sputum smear results for acid-fast bacilli. Research indicates that over 50% of these patients would require chemotherapy within 12 months if untreated. Fiberoptic bronchoscopy combined with bronchoalveolar lavage can be employed to diagnose smear-negative PTB. The BAL sample can undergo Ziehl–Neelsen staining, microbial cultures, and molecular analysis.

Methods: Patients admitted with clinical and radiological indications of pulmonary tuberculosis who had no prior history of receiving antitubercular treatment from any source were included in study. In these individuals, one spot and one overnight sputum sample were collected and those negative for AFB staining and CBNAAT were included. After administering appropriate pre-medications, fiberoptic bronchoscopy was performed and bronchoalveolar lavage was obtained and sent for ZN staining, CBNAAT and culture.

Results: Of the 82 bronchoalveolar lavage samples, the Ziehl-Neelsen smear, CBNAAT, and culture were positive in 48 (58.5%), 58 (70.7%), and 60 (73.1%) patients, respectively. When comparing BAL culture using BACTEC MGIT, sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, and Negative predictive value of BAL AFB smear are 73.3%, 81.8%, 91.6%, and 53%, respectively. In contrast, sensitivity, specificity, PPV, and NPV of BAL CBNAAT relative to BAL culture are 93.3%, 90.9%, 96.5%, and 83.3%, respectively.

Conclusion: FOB-guided BAL samples are highly effective for the rapid and definitive diagnosis of sputum-examination negative patients who have a strong clinical and radiological suspicion of pulmonary tuberculosis.

Introduction: TB caused by M. tb strains that are resistant to both H and R with or without resistance to other first-line anti TB drugs is called Multidrug-resistant TB (MDR-TB). Infection of the female genital tract caused by Mycobacterium Tuberculosis is known as female genital tuberculosis. Endometrium is involved in approximately 50%-60% of women with genital tuberculosis.

Case Report: A 32 year old female with complaints of menstrual irregularity and infertility presented to the clinic for evaluation of infertility and under routine evaluation, endometrial biopsy was done and sent for CBNAAT and the result showed RR-TB. Endometrial biopsy sample should be sent for CBNAAT, culture along with HPE other wise MDR-TB can be missed.

Swaroop Godbole, Amita Athavale, Pratibha Singhal, Vijay Khatri, Bhumin Patel, Tanya Athavale, Gaurav Jain, Dhiraj Atram

Department of Pulmonary Medicine and EPRC, Seth GSMC and KEM Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pulmonary tuberculosis (TB) often mimics malignancy, posing diagnostic challenges, especially in high TB-prevalence areas. This case emphasizes the need for a high suspicion of TB, even when malignancy is suspected based on clinico-radiological basis.

Case Report: A 54-year-old male with ischemic heart disease, diabetes, hypertension, and a history of alcohol and tobacco use, presented with a 5-month history of fever, chills, shortness of breath, and weight loss. Chest X-ray showed right-sided pleural effusion (resolving) and right upper zone opacity. CECT revealed a lobulated soft tissue lesion with a cavity in the right upper lobe, associated pleural effusion and thickened pleura, raising concern for malignancy. Pleural aspiration revealed exudative lymphocytic fluid with low ADA and negative GenExpert for MTB. A WB-PETCT scan showed metabolically active lesions in the right middle lobe, pleura, and subcarinal lymph nodes, suggesting malignancy. Bronchoscopy revealed inflammation, mucosal irregularity, and thick filamentous material in the right upper lobe. GenExpert on bronchial washings was positive for MTB with Rifampicin resistance.

Conclusion: ADA levels in TB effusion can be low and thus low ADA in pleural fluid does not exclude TB. While imaging initially suggested malignancy, resolving pleural effusion on serial Chest X-rays, “tree-in-bud” nodules, and absence of bronchus cut-off sign on CT raised suspicion for TB. Bronchoscopy findings were consistent with endobronchial TB, particularly the hyperemic type.

A high index of suspicion for TB is crucial, even with atypical radiological findings, to ensure timely diagnosis and appropriate management

Introduction: All suspects of pulmonary tuberculosis are subjected initially to sputum CBNAAT. CBNAAT is sensitivity in 86%. In few subjects CBNAAT can be negative, due to dry cough or improper sample collection. Fibreoptic bronchoscopy is an alternative method to obtaining sample in such cases which aids in early diagnosis and initiation of treatment thus, reducing mortality and morbidity.

Methods: Clinico-radiologically suspected subjects of pulmonary tuberculosis who are either sputum negative or not getting adequate sputum sample were identified and those fit for bronchoscopy were subjected to procedure and bronchi washings were obtained. Smear and CBNAAT examination of fluid was done. Data recorded and results statistically analysed.

Results: In this study out of total 60 cases, 36 (60%) subjects were males while 24 (40%) subject’s females. Out of the 60 subjects, 24 subjects (40%) were BAL positive, among BAL positive 4 subjects (16.6%) Rifampicin resistant, and 8 (33.3%) subjects among BAL positive previously used ATT.

Conclusion: In subjects with strong clinical and radiological suspicion of tuberculosis and in whom sputum CBNAAT is negative, FOB could be used as a tool for further assessment to confirm tuberculosis microbiologically and also to know drug resistance patterns.

Sahu Gaurav, Nair Shreeja, Potdar Pradeep

Department of Respiratory Medicine, MGM Hospital, Kamothe, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) is primarily a pulmonary disease, but it can disseminate to other organs, including the bone marrow. Bone-marrow involvement in TB is exceedingly rare and typically occurs in the context of disseminated or miliary TB. The case explores the potential mechanisms behind this rare manifestation and the implications for management.[1]

Case Report: We present a case of 55-year-old female with a history of successfully treated pulmonary TB 15 years ago presented with progressive fatigue, easy bruising, and bleeding gums. She had recently started empirical antitubercular therapy (ATT) for suspected TB reactivation, prompted by respiratory symptoms and abnormal chest X-ray findings. Her sputum and Bronchoalveolar Lavage (BAL) was negative for TB. A complete blood count revealed pancytopenia. Bone-marrow aspiration and biopsy demonstrated granulomatous inflammation with numerous AFB on Ziehl-Neelsen stain, confirming Mycobacterium tuberculosis involvement in the bone-marrow. HIV and hepatitis screen were negative. Bone-marrow involvement by TB is extremely rare and typically occurs in disseminated TB or immunocompromised patients. This case challenges the notion that TB reactivation only affects the lungs, suggesting that latent TB can later disseminate to the Bone-marrow. The pancytopenia may have resulted from both disseminated TB and hematologic side effects of ATT, known to occasionally cause Bone-marrow suppression. The patient’s ATT regimen was adjusted to address Bone-marrow involvement. She received supportive care, including blood transfusions and growth factors to manage pancytopenia. Over the following months, her blood counts gradually normalized, and she showed clinical improvement. Follow-up imaging demonstrated stabilization of pulmonary findings, and no further hematologic complications were noted. This case highlights the importance of considering Bone-marrow involvement in patients with a history of TB, particularly those starting empirical ATT. While rare, bone-marrow TB should be considered in patients presenting with unexplained pancytopenia, even years after the initial TB diagnosis.

Conclusion: Clinicians should be aware of the potential complications of ATT in patients with prior TB and monitor for hematologic abnormalities. Further research is needed to explore the pathophysiology of bone-marrow TB and to refine management strategies for these rare, complex cases.

Keywords: Acid-fast bacilli, ATT complications, bonemarrow involvement, disseminated TB, empirical antitubercular therapy, granulomatous inflammation, pancytopenia, reactivation, tuberculosis

Reference

1. Kuo PH, Yang PC, Kuo SS, Luh KT. Severe immune hemolytic anemia in disseminated tuberculosis with response to antituberculosis therapy. Chest 2001;119:1961-3.

Introduction: A 12 year old girl with fever, weight loss, diagnosed as tuberculosis, clinicoradiologicaly, started on atd but symptomps not improved, discontinued ATD by herself, visited different clinics, finally re-evaluated. Now she is attending her school regularly and engaged in her regular activities. Mohit Kondisetti, Nikhil Sarangdhar, Deepika Ughade, Girija Nair, Shahid Patel, Tanay Sinha

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D Y Patil University, School of Medicine, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Lemierre’s syndrome is a Septic Thrombophlebitis of Internal Jugular Vein, the common causes of which include Fusobacterium, Bacteroides, other anaerobes and Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus. We describe an unusual presentation of Lemierre’s syndrome where Mycobacterium Tuberculosis was identified as the etiological agent in a young female.

Case Report: A 26-year-old lady presented with fever, left sided painful neck swelling since 3 months associated with odynophagia, pain and swelling over left cheek, productive cough, loss of weight and appetite and low grade fever since 3 months. Neck Sonography revealed bilateral cervical and axillary lymphadenopathy (L > R) of which FNAC was inconclusive. CT thorax revealed presence of a thrombus in the Left Internal Jugular Vein, consolidation of right middle lobe and mediastinal, cervical and axillary lymphadenopathy. Her USG Neck Vein Doppler revealed Bilateral Internal Jugular Venous (IJV) thrombosis with significant luminal compromise (L > R) with a conglomerated lymph node compressing the Left IJV. Deep Vein Thrombosis scan of bilateral lower limbs showed no Thrombus. Hemogram and coagulation profile were normal except for the presence of microcytic hypochromic anemia and leukocytosis. Broncho Alveolar Lavage and the CBNAAT of the BAL washings detected the presence of Mycobacterium Tuberculosis sensitive to Rifampicin. Patient was started on Anti-TB Therapy of Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Pyrazinamide, Ethambutol (HRZE) from NTEP and Low Molecular Weight Heparin for 5 days, switched to oral Rivaroxaban, to which the clinical improvement was observed in terms of reduction in cervical lymph node swelling, resolution of fever and cheek pain, increased appetite and she was discharged with advice for follow-up.

Conclusion: Tuberculosis is widely prevalent in the Indian Subcontinent and unusual and bizarre clinical presentations are often encountered at tertiary care centers as seen in this case of Disseminated Tuberculosis with Septic Thrombophlebitis of Bilateral Internal Jugular Veins with Pulmonary and Extrapulmonary Tuberculosis

Sharmin Mukadam, Manas Bhanushali, Girija Nair, Deepika Ughade, Tanay Sinha, Nikhil Sarangdhar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, DYPMCH, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Objective: To analyse the clinical profile, demographic trends, and treatment outcomes of patients diagnosed with tuberculosis (TB) at a tertiary care centre in Navi Mumbai over a five-year period, providing insights into the epidemiological changes in TB management.

Methods: A retrospective study was conducted on TB patients managed at the Department of Pulmonary Medicine, DYPMCH, from 2020 to 2024. Data were obtained from TB registers, the Medical Records Department (MRD), and the NIKSHAY portal. Both indoor and outdoor patients were included, with variables such as age, gender, type of TB (pulmonary/extrapulmonary), comorbidities, treatment regimens, and outcomes analysed.

Results: A total of [2043] patients were reviewed. The majority of cases were pulmonary TB (65%), with extrapulmonary TB accounting for 34. The male-to-female ratio was 2:1, with the most affected age group being [20-30yrs ]. Treatment success rates were observed in 95% of cases. The trends showed decrease in smear positive cases during year 2020 and 2021 due to covid-19 effect.

Conclusion: This five-year retrospective analysis highlights changing demographic and clinical trends in TB cases, emphasizing the need for early diagnosis, tailored management, and strengthened follow-up systems. The findings underscore the importance of continuous epidemiological monitoring to improve TB control strategies in tertiary care settings.

Keywords: Clinical profile, demographics, retrospective , tertiary care, treatment outcomes, tuberculosis, analysis, navi mumbai

Tanisha James, B. L. Shashi Bhushan, K. U. Vivek

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, BMCRI, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Lower lung field tuberculosis without concomitant upper lobe disease involvement occurs in 7% or less with active pulmonary tuberculosis, results from transbronchial perforation of hilar lymph node with spread into adjacent lung. Lower zone opacity on chest x-ray often masquerades as pneumonia, aspiration, atelectasis.

Case Report: 70 year old male, fruit vendor by occupation presented with complaints of cough with expectoration, whiteish in colour, non-foul smelling and non-blood tinged and complaints of fever since 2 weeks, low grade intermittent, not associated with evening rise of temperature. No associated history of loss of weight or appetite. Patient had no know co-morbidities. Known alcoholic and smoker. Chest Xray showed right lower zone consolidation. Sputum AFB smear and CBNAAT was negative. HIV screening was negative, CECT thorax was done which reported patchy areas of consolidation in right lower lobe with few non enhancing areas in right lower lobe likely necrotizing pneumonia. Patient was initially treated on Ceftriazone, on no clinical improvement diagnostic bronchoscopy was performed, bronchoalveolar lavage and post bronchoscopy sputum samples were sent for gene Xpert and analysis. BAL CBNNAT was negative however post bronchoscopy sputum CBNAAT was positive for PTB and patient was started on first line antitubercular drugs, Patient symptomatically showed improvement and was discharged home with ATT medication.

Conclusion: Post primary tuberculosis usually affects upper lung field, occurrence in lower lung fields is rare via transbronchial perforation of hilar lymph node. Tubercle bacilli maybe challenging to detect on smear and culture, bronchial sampling or post bronchoscopy sputum may reveal tubercle bacilli. Tuberculosis should be considered as a potential diagnosis in lower lung field pneumonia in individuals with cough, fever and evening spikes of temperature, in high TB burden countries such as India.

Mycobacterial infection in the oral cavity liberates the mycobacterium that ascends into the salivary gland via its duct or pass to its associated lymph nodes via lymphatic drainageThe second pathway involves haematogenous or lymphatic spread from a distant primary lung focus.

Shreeja Nair1, Tanuja P. Barve2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, MGM Medical College, Kamothe, Maharashtra, India, 2MGM Medical College, Kamothe, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Healthcare workers (HCWs) who tend to patients of active drug-sensitive and multidrug-resistant tuberculosis are more likely to become infected due to greater exposure than the public. This study was done to assess the challenges and quality of life among HCWs working in a medical college in Raigad district of Maharashtra, which houses the district TB centre. We also calculated the prevalence of TB among these HCWs. Our aim also included promotion of prevention strategies, raising awareness about national guidelines, treatment options, and to address social stigmas associated with TB.

Methods: This was a prospective, observational, cross-sectional study done over two months in a tertiary care center in Maharashtra. 250 HCWs (doctors, nurses, laboratory technicians, workers from housekeeping department, security staff, clerks) were interviewed for the study with three validated questionnaires and results were calculated with appropriate statistics methods. This study was an STS project with grant by ICMR in 2024.

Results: From the study we found a prevalence of TB among HCWs to be 6.4%, a slightly higher figure as compared to previous studies. 44% of these HCWs were doctors, 25% were housekeeping staff and 12.5% nurses. 75% cases had pulmonary TB and 19% had MDR TB. A dismal 73% of HCWs had adequate awareness of TB. 31% were smokers but none had significant comorbidities. 96% had good quality of life. None of the patients faced any stigma related to TB during treatment.

Conclusions: This study reiterates the fact that TB is highly prevalent among health care workers and we must take strict measures and implement strategies to reduce its spread in hospitals. Awareness of the disease is equally important and emphasis must be given on training HCWs on TB. We hope to make efficient changes in the infection control policies of our hospital with this study.

Keywords: Healthcare workers, prevalence, tuberculosis

Niraj Dhakare, Radha Munje, Gyanshankar Mishra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Indira Gandhi Government Medical College, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB), particularly drug-resistant tuberculosis (DR-TB), continues to be a major global health threat, especially in India, where the emergence of drug resistance complicates treatment. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) to TB medications further challenge treatment compliance and patient outcomes. Understanding ADR profiles is critical for optimizing treatment strategies.

Objectives: This study aimed to evaluate the incidence, causality, severity, and risk factors associated with ADRs in DR-TB patients undergoing treatment, and to assess treatment compliance in those affected by ADRs.

Methods: An ambispective cohort study was conducted at a tertiary care institute from January 2023 to September 2024. The study included 174 DR-TB patients receiving treatment under the Programmatic Management of Drug-Resistant TB (PMDT). Data were analyzed using SPSS, and associations with ADR occurrence were assessed using the Chi-square and t-test. Inclusion criteria involved DR-TB patients aged ≥18 years, registered under PMDT-NTEP.

Results: Out of 174 patients, 79% (138/174) experienced ADRs, with common reactions being skin discoloration (60.34%), peripheral neuropathy (52.87%), and joint pain (28.74%). Most ADRs were mild (52.90%) or moderate (40.58%). Causality assessment classified 92.03% of ADRs as probable. Male patients and those with pulmonary TB or co-morbidities, such as diabetes and hypertension, were more likely to develop ADRs. No significant association was found between ADRs and HIV status or addiction history. Bedaquiline was found to be relatively cardio-safe.

Conclusions: The high prevalence of ADRs in DR-TB treatment underscores the need for improved pharmacovigilance and tailored management strategies. The findings support the expanded use of Bedaquiline in all-oral regimens and advocate for therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) to optimize treatment.

This study highlights the need for proactive ADR management, the development of less toxic drugs, and enhanced monitoring strategies to improve treatment adherence and outcomes in DR-TB patients.

Keywords: Adverse drug reactions, bedaquiline, drug-resistant tuberculosis, pharmacovigilance, treatment adherence

Sowmya Mandula, Raghu Srikanti

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Guntur Medical College, Guntur, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Inguinal lymph node MDR TB is rare.

Case Report: A 38-year-old male presented with left side groin swelling for one month not associated with pain, fever and cough with expectoration for 10 days. History of rheumatoid arthritis for 12 years and on regular treatment. No previous history of anti-tubercular treatment and other comorbidities. Chest X-Ray and CT chest are normal. FNAC from left inguinal lymph node send for CBNAAT shows MTB positive, rifampicin resistance detected and Sputum CBNAAT negative. Patient was initiated on short oral Bedaquiline regimen and is on follow up.

Conclusions: Here we reported a unique case of unilateral isolated inguinal MDR TB because of increase in prevalence of extra pulmonary MDR TB. CBNAAT is crucial for the early diagnosis of drug sensitive and resistant tuberculosis and its management in India like endemic countries.

All lymph node FNAC should be sent for CBNAAT along with histopathological examination.

Eesh Goyal, Vishal Chopra, Kranti Garg

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, GMC, Patiala, Punjab, India

Introduction: Subphrenic abscess is relatively common, but a tubercular subphrenic abscess in the absence of coexisting tubercular abdomen is extremely rare and often confused with pyogenic or amoebic liver abscess. A tuberculous abscess is usually associated with foci of infection either in the lung or GIT, or with an immunocompromised state.

Case Report: A 16-year-old female presented with complaints of right-sided chest pain. The dull, aching pain was located in the right infraaxillary area and radiated to the back. On systemic examination, dullness on percussion was noted in the right 6th and 7th intercostal space in the mid-axillary area, and reduced breath sounds were heard in the right infraaxillary area. On abdominal examination, tenderness was present in the right hypochondrium. The patient’s CBC was within normal limits; however, the CXR showed a raised right hemidiaphragm with angle blunting. An USG of the abdomen revealed a cystic lesion in the subdiaphragmatic region and hepatomegaly. A CECT of the whole abdomen showed a moderate-to-large-sized, thick, irregularly-walled, multiseptated, multiloculated collection within the subdiaphragmatic space, with extensive areas of necrosis. The patient was managed with drainage and antibiotics. Pus was sent for aerobic culture, sensitivity testing, CBNAAT, and liquid culture for MTB. CBNAAT showed MTB detection with no resistance to rifampicin. The patient was started on ATT. A repeat ultrasound of the abdomen showed complete resolution of the abscess.

Conclusion: Isolated tubercular subphrenic abscesses, though rare, can be confused with pyogenic and amoebic liver abscesses. Therefore, any isolated subphrenic abscess should be evaluated for a tubercular etiology, as misdiagnosis can lead to serious complications.

Keywords: Amoebic liver abscess, CBNAAT, subphrenic abscess

Utkarsh Singh, Medha Bragaje, Pramod Chaudhari, A. Anita, Anubhuti Singh

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Bharati Vidyapeeth (DTU) Medical College and Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Inroduction: TB is one of the leading cause of mortality among infectious disease and has a enormous impact on many countries. India has highest TB burden country accounting for the 1/5th of the total global incidences. ATT induced ADR includes hepatitis, gastritis, arthralgia, haematological disorders, skin related disorders. Adverse events due to drug-sensitive as well as resistant TB treatment are one of the most important reasons for treatment interruption and nonadherence.

Methods: A descriptive longitudinal study which was conducted over period of 8 months from May 24 to December 24 in pulmonary medicine department of Bharati hospital and research institute, Pune. 50 patient were registered under RNTCP which include both pulmonary and extra pulmonary tuberculosis. All patients above 18 years of age who are diagnosed with Drug sensitive tuberculosis and who developed ADR during the intensive phase of treatment.
