Nirupam Rajanish, P. L. Meshram, R. R. Hegde, S. A. Agale

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Grant Government Medical College and Sir J. J. Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) is a significant global health issue, with millions of new cases annually. Managing TB involves assessing bacterial load and immune response, as these affect disease severity and treatment. The Neutrophil-to-Lymphocyte Ratio (NLR) is a notable immune marker, indicating systemic inflammation and disease progression in TB and other infections. NLR shows the balance between neutrophils, elevated during acute inflammation, and lymphocytes, key in adaptive immunity. The study involved using the Sputum GeneXpert system to explore the correlation between the Neutrophil-to-Lymphocyte Ratio (NLR) and bacterial load in tuberculosis (TB) patients.

Methods: This prospective hospital-based study, conducted over a month at a tertiary care center, involved 65 patients with pulmonary tuberculosis. Elevated NLR suggests an inflammatory response, as neutrophils are part of the acute inflammatory response, while lymphocytes are involved in the adaptive immune response, and GeneXpert provides results in about 2 hours, significantly faster than traditional methods like culture (which may take weeks). This quick turnaround is crucial for initiating early treatment and controlling TB spread.

Results: The average neutrophil-lymphocyte ratio (NLR) ranges from approximately 3.12 (low bacterial load) to 5.75 (high bacterial load). The average NLR for medium bacterial load is approximately 4.71. The average NLR for very low bacterial load is approximately 5.67. The very low bacterial load category, with an average NLR of 5.67, contradicts the typical trend that lower bacterial loads should correspond to lower NLRs. This anomaly suggests that the relationship between bacterial load and NLR is not as straightforward as the figures presented and requires more nuanced analysis to account for the multitude of variables at play.

Conclusion: This study aimed to correlate NLR with bacterial load in TB patients by leveraging the GeneXpert MTB/RIF system for bacterial detection. This study shows that NLR can vary independently of bacterial load, as it is influenced by several factors such as the patient’s immune response, underlying comorbidities, or treatment interventions. The findings could shed light on how systemic inflammation correlates with TB severity, offering a potential avenue for better patient management, especially in settings where quick and simple diagnostic tools are needed.

Keywords: Neutrophil lymphocyte ratio, sputum genxpert

Srushti Bonde, P. L. Meshram, R. R. Hegde, S. A. Agale

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Grant Government Medical College, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Background: The National Tuberculosis Elimination Programme (NTEP) in India regularlyupdates its guidelines for the treatment of drug-sensitive and drug-resistant tuberculosis. In 2020, a significant change was introduced with the replacement of injectable kanamycin with bedaquiline in multi-drug resistant tuberculosis (MDR-TB) treatment regimens. This study aimed to compare the treatment failure rates between these two approaches.

Methods: Data was collected from the Nikshay portal, focusing on freshly diagnosed MDR-TB patients treated with kanamycin-based regimens (2018-2019) and bedaquiline-based regimens (2020-2021). Treatment failure rates werecalculated for both groups, and the influence of various factors, includingdemographics, comorbidities, and specific treatment regimens, was analyzed.

Results: The study found no significant difference in treatment failure rates between kanamycin and bedaquiline-based treatments. Within the bedaquiline group, the shorter MDR/RR-TB regimen showed a slightly higher, though not statistically significant, failure rate compared to the longer oral regimen. Similarly, in the kanamycin group, the regimen for MDR with fluoroquinolone resistance had a slightly higher failure rate than the conventional MDR regimen. Factors such as diabetes, HIV co-infection, and previous TB treatment history did not significantly impact treatment failure rates. The comparable treatment failure rates between kanamycin and bedaquilinebased regimens suggest that the shift to bedaquiline maintains treatment efficacy while eliminating the challenges associated with injectable medications. The slightly higher failure rates observed with specific regimens within both groups warrant further investigation to optimize treatment strategies.

Conclusions: This study supports the NTEP’s transition to bedaquiline-based regimens for MDR-TB treatment. Continued monitoring and analysis of treatment outcomes are crucial to ensure the long-term success of TB control efforts.

Keywords: Bedaquiline, India, kanamycin, MDR-TB, NTEP, treatment failure, tuberculosis

Anita Saibannavar, Harshita Mittal

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, R. C. S. M. G. M. C., Kolhapur, Maharashtra, India

Background and objectives: Pulmonary tuberculosis classically presents with cavitation which is a hallmark radiological feature. It usually manifests as single cavitary lesion mostly in the upper lobe (apical and posterior segments). This upper lobe predilection is attributed to decreased blood supply, higher oxygen tension and impaired lymphatic drainage. Later in HIV era, pulmonary TB presentation have diversified with lower lobe involvement and diffuse pulmonary disease becoming more common due to profound immunosuppression and low CD4 counts.

However, with the growing prevalence of comorbidities such as diabetes, malnutrition and alcoholism, unusual and diffuse radiological patterns are also seen in Non-HIV patients. This case series aims to highlight such unusual radiological presentations in Non-HIV patients with mild immunosuppression.

Methods: A retrospective case series was conducted on five Non-HIV patients with mild immunosuppression who were diagnosed with pulmonary TB at a tertiary care center. Clinical data, comorbidities, and radiological findings were reviewed and analyzed for deviations from classical TB presentations.

Results: All patients exhibited unusual radiological findings, notably bilateral multiple cavitary lesions. These radiological lesions resembled those seen in bronchopneumonia, septic emboli and fungal infections. None had profound immunosuppression or HIV, but all had underlying comorbidities such as diabetes, malnutrition and alcoholism.

Conclusion: Pulmonary TB can present with unusual radiological findings in Non-HIV patients with mild immunosuppression, mimicking other infectious and inflammatory pulmonary conditions. Awareness of such presentations is crucial for timely diagnosis and management, especially in the context of rising comorbidities.

Keywords: Unusual presentation, multiple cavitary lesion, comorbidities, upper lobe predilection, mild immunosuppression

Raja Dhar1, Rakesh K Chawla2, Moumita Rahaman3 Aditya K Chawla2, Gaurav Chaudhary2, Ashutosh Gautam4, Rajat Singal4

1Birla Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India, 2Chawla Clinic, Rohini, New Delhi India, 3RMO, Birla Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India, 4Mankind Pharma Ltd, Okhla Phase III, New Delhi, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and asthma profoundly affect patients’ quality of life (QoL), yet traditional severity measures often fail to capture this dimension. While GOLD guidelines recommend mucolytic agents to reduce exacerbation risk and enhance QoL in COPD, data on the combined assessment of QoL and lung function changes, especially from the Indian subcontinent, remain limited.

Objective: To assess the efficacy and safety of the combination of N-acetylcysteine and Acebrophylline (Combination named Abiways), in patients with moderate to severe COPD and Asthma.

Methods: In this non-randomized, interventional, prospective, single-arm, post-marketing surveillance study, participants were administered Abiways for 90 days. The primary endpoint was Quality of Life, which was evaluated using the COPD Assessment Test (CAT) and Asthma Control Test (ACT) scores. Secondary endpoints included mean FEV1 and FVC changes and adverse event occurrence.

Results: Of 102 recruited patients with moderate to severe obstructive airway disease, 97 completed the study (76 COPD and 21 Asthma patients respectively; mean age 57.9 ± 8.1 years; 33 females, 64 males). Overall, FEV1 improved significantly from 1.287 L to 1.484 L (197 mL; p < 0.001) with similar statistical improvements in COPD (1.237L to 1.414L; p = 001) and asthma (1.477 L to 1.747 L; p = 0.004) subpopulations. COPD patients showed statistically significant improvements in CAT scores (17.18 ± 1.02 to 10.58 ± 0.9, p = 0.0001); however, such significance was not observed in the ACT scores for asthma patients. FVC remained unchanged in all subgroups [Figure 1]. No severe adverse events occurred.

QoL and lung functional parameters improvement in COPD and asthma patients

Conclusion: The combination of N-acetylcysteine and Acebrophylline improves CAT scores in moderate to severe COPD patients, and FEV1 in both COPD and asthma patients with a favorable safety profile. The combination appears safe and effective for obstructive airway disease management.

Keywords: ABIWAYS, acebrophylline, asthma, CAT score, COPD, FEV1, N-Acetylcysteine

Muthirevula Archana Reddy, Adheep B. Amberker, B. Vidyasagar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, JJM Medical College, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: In COPD patients, impaired gas exchange and hypercapnia trigger various hormonal and endothelial responses that can lead to water retention and hyponatremia. This condition can cause central nervous system dysfunction, reversible cardiac conduction defects, secondary renal insufficiency, and potentially death. Hypokalemia often accompanies hyponatremia in COPD patients, either independently or together. Hypokalemia can result from respiratory acidosis, metabolic alkalosis, or prolonged steroid use, and β2 agonists may also contribute. Acute respiratory failure with hypokalemia is associated with high mortality rates, often due to cardiac arrhythmias or impaired nerve-muscle conduction.

Objectives: To evaluate the extent of electrolyte imbalance during acute COPD exacerbations and to use these imbalances as prognostic indicators for COPD management.

Methods: Primary data was collected from 130 COPD patients with acute exacerbations admitted to Bapuji Hospital, Davangere, from May 2023 to May 2024. Diagnosis was based on history, clinical examination, pulmonary function tests, and the modified medical research council dyspnea grading. Age and sex-matched healthy controls were recruited from the general population. Serum electrolyte levels were measured at admission and discharge. Data were analyzed using MS Excel and SPSS Software, with categorical values reported as numbers and percentages, and numerical values as means and standard deviations. Statistical significance was determined with ANOVA and unpaired t-tests, considering p < 0.005.

Results: The study included 130 patients (78 males, 52 females) with a mean age of 56.85 years, compared to 58.33 years for controls. Sixty percent of both cases and controls were male, and forty percent were female. Fifty-five percent of cases and sixty percent of controls were smokers. At admission, the mean SpO2 of cases was 86.14%, which was statistically significant compared to controls. The mean FEV1 for cases was 51.55% versus 81.42% for controls, and the FEV1/FVC ratio was 56.1 for cases compared to 71.24 for controls. Serum sodium levels in cases were 130.45 mEq/L versus 138.2 mEq/L in controls, while serum potassium levels were 3.24 mEq/L in cases compared to 4.4 mEq/L in controls. Hyponatremia and hypokalemia worsened with advancing age. Males had a mean sodium level of 130.4 mEq/L and potassium of 3.3 mEq/L, while females had 131.8 mEq/L and 3.4 mEq/L, respectively. Longer illness duration correlated with greater electrolyte imbalances. Eighty-five percent of cases had wheezing, forty-three percent had crepitations, and eighteen percent had SpO2 below 70%. Hyponatremia and hypokalemia were significantly associated with lower oxygen saturation. Patients with both emphysema and chronic bronchitis had a mean sodium of 128.70 mEq/L and potassium of 3.1 mEq/L. Lower sodium and potassium levels were observed in patients with reduced more severe MMRC and GOLD stages. Duration of hospital stay and frequency of exacerbations correlated with electrolyte imbalances. Patients requiring mechanical ventilation had mean sodium and potassium levels of 126 mEq/L and 3.03 mEq/L, respectively.

Conclusions: Hyponatremia and hypokalemia are common in acute COPD exacerbations, and there is a significant correlation between serum electrolytes and severity indicators such as the oxygen saturation, MMRC grading, pulmonary function tests, GOLD index. Reduced SpO2 at admission and advanced age are assoventilation severe hyponatremia and hypokalemia, often necessitating mechanical ventilation.

Sanjana Shastri1, Meena Lopez1, Manish Jain2, Priti Meshram3, Akash Balki4, Himanshu Pophale5, Sharad Tikkiwal6, Sandesh Sawant1, Abhijit Vaidya1, M. Senthilnathan1, Jaideep Gogtay1

1Medical Department, Cipla Ltd, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 2Maharaja Agrasen Superspecialty Hospital, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India, 3Grant Government Medical College and Sir J. J. Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 4Shree Hospital and Critical Care Centre, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India, 5AMAI Charitable Trust’s Ace Hospital and Research Centre, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 6Asthma Bhawan, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: GOLD-2024 recommends triple therapy in frequent exacerbators. Evidence is emerging on triple therapy benefits in patients without a recent exacerbation; however, data is lacking in Indian patients. In an Indian Phase-IV trial, Budesonide/Glycopyrronium/Formoterol (BGF)(400/25/12mcg) FDC given through Dry-Powder-Inhaler (DPI) was well-tolerated and improved lung-function, symptoms and HRQoL. The aim of this post-hoc analysis was to assess impact of BGF on symptom-scores, lung-function and HRQoL in patients with and without a recent exacerbation.

Methods: The phase-IV open-label, prospective, non-comparative, 24-weeks clinical trial evaluated safety and efficacy of BGF-DPI twice-daily in moderate-severe COPD. Patients 40-75 years, current/ex-smokers, with spirometry-diagnosed COPD (GOLD-2020 guidelines), CAT>10 and able to use DPI were included. Outcomes evaluated in this post-hoc-analysis were change at 24 weeks from baseline in CAT, FEV1, SGRQ & mMRC in patients with (Group 1) and without (Group 2) documented history of exacerbation prior to 1-year from screening.

Results: Of 194 patients in Phase-IV trial, exacerbation history data was available for 106 (74-Group1; 32-Group 2). Mean CAT score improved (p < 0.001) by 24-weeks in both groups (6.639 + 4.136-Group1; 6.233 + 5.070-Group2). Trough FEV1 improved in Group 1 (0.053 + 0.188 L, p = 0.024) at 24 weeks; improvement in Group 2 was not significant (0.044 + 0.294 L). Mean SGRQ improved (p < 0.001) by 21.819+16.471 in Group1 and 15.967 + 17.365 in Group2. Improvement (p < 0.001) of 0.417 + 0.645 units in mMRC was seen in Group 1; improvement was non-significant in Group 2 (0.167 + 0.531). In patients with and without a recent exacerbation history, at the end of 24 weeks, CAT and SGRQ scores improved significantly. Trough FEV1 and mMRC saw significant improvement in patients with a recent exacerbation history and numerical improvement in those who did not have an exacerbation in the year prior. No significant inter-group differences were seen in CAT, trough FEV1 and SGRQ measures.

Conclusion: Budesonide/Glycopyrronium/Formoterol FDC significantly improved symptoms and HRQoL in Indian COPD patients, irrespective of exacerbation history.

Keywords: Budesonide/glycopyrronium/formoterol, COPD, exacerbation, triple therapy

Siddhartha Chakraborty, Manu Chopra, Rahul Tyagi, Kislay Kishore, Aseem Yadav, M. C. Jyothis

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Army Institute of Cardiothoracic Sciences, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Therapeutic bronchoscopy plays a pivotal role in management of non-malignant endobronchial lesions such as carcinoid, leiomyoma and hamartoma, with minimal side effects and saving the patients from life-threatening surgeries.

Case Reports: Four patients underwent therapeutic bronchoscopy using cryotherapy with repeated freeze thaw cycles, argon plasma coagulation and electrocautery for debulking and ablation of lesion. A 34 year old male with soft tissue density mass lesion in superior segment right lower lobe was diagnosed as a case of pulmonary hamartoma on endobronchial biopsy. He underwent multiple sessions of debulking of mass using cryofreeze and thaw technique with near complete resolution of endobronchial lesion. Another 29 year old male with mass lesion in left main bronchus and proximal left upper lobar bronchus with distal near total collapse of left upper lobe was diagnosed as a case of pulmonary hamartoma using a novel procedure tunnelled endobronchial biopsy. He underwent multiple sessions of electrocautery fulguration resulting in complete resolution of lung mass. Another patient diagnosed as a case of leiomyoma, an extremely rare tumour especially in male, in the right upper lobe bronchus underwent multiple sessions of electrocautery fulguration and debulking using cryotherapy with repeated cycles of freeze thaw. There was significant resolution of the lesion, avoiding pneumonectomy. A case of carcinoid right lower lobe, diagnosed on endobronchial biopsy, underwent debulking using cryotherapy and electrocautery leading to near complete resolution.

Conclusion: Therapeutic bronchoscopy using cryotherapy, electrocautery and argon plasma coagulation led to positive outcomes and resolution of non-malignant lesions, saving the patients from critical surgeries such as pneumonectomy

Ravi Tejraj Mehta, Ashok Jaiswal

Medical and Regulatory Affairs, Zydus Healthcare Limited, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Single inhaler triple therapies, combining LABA/LAMA/ICS are promising treatment for COPD, the second-leading cause of deaths due to non-communicable diseases in India. This study aimed to assess the effectiveness and safety of Formoterol/Glycopyrronium/Budesonide (FGB) administered as SITT in COPD patients in real-world clinical setting in India.

Methods: This is a 24-week, prospective, multicentric, real-world observational study involving adults with COPD prescribed a single inhaler triple therapy [FGB 12/25/400 mcg DPI (n = 168) or 4.8/9/160 mcg pMDI (n = 127)] at the discretion of the treating physician. Effectiveness outcomes include change in FEV1, FVC, COPD Assessment Test (CAT) and Clinical COPD Questionnaire (CCQ). Adverse events are also assessed. Interim analyses (n = 295) of patients who have completed 24-week follow-up are presented here.

Results: FEV1 improved from 1.16 ± 0.50 litres at baseline to 1.33 ± 0.58 litres by week 4 (mean change 0.17 ± 0.39; P < 0.0001), and 1.49 ± 0.73 litres at week 24 (mean change 0.33 ± 0.64; P < 0.0001). FVC improved from 1.90 ± 0.70 litres at baseline to 2.06 ± 0.76 litres at week 4 (mean change 0.16 ± 0.45; P < 0.0001) and 2.24 ± 0.98 litres at week 24 (mean change 0.35 ± 0.87; P < 0.0001). CAT score reduced from 21.81 ± 10.04 at baseline to 11.17 ± 6.63 at week 24 (P < 0.0001), and CCQ from 4.08 ± 3.05 at baseline to 1.90 ± 1.54 at week 24 (P < 0.0001). FGB was well-tolerated, most common adverse events being palpitations (1.7% patients) and dryness of mouth (1.4%). No serious adverse events were reported.

Conclusions: This large ongoing real-world study enrolling patients across India highlights the high symptom burden and poor lung functions of COPD patients eligible to receive triple therapy. SITT with FGB is a well-tolerated therapeutic option which effectively improves lung function early within a month leading to relief from symptoms and improvement of QoL at 6 months.

SITT with FGB significantly improves lung function and QoL in COPD patients with good safety profile.

Keywords: Budesonide, COPD, formoterol, glycopyrronium, quality of life, single inhaler triple therapy

Gareema Narwal, Vipul Kumar, Kanishka Choudhary

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Pt BD sharma, PGIMS, Rohtak, Haryana, India

Introduction: Acute exacerbation of COPD (AECOPD) is a frequent complication of COPD that may result in hospitalization requiring NIV. Patients with COPD and diabetes mellitus are more prone to infections and subsequent exacerbation of COPD. In present study we have tried to decipher the effect of glycemic control with regards to outcome of NIV therapy in AECOPD patients with T2DM.

Methods: A prospective cross sectional study was conducted in tertiary care centre from January 2023 to April2024.Patients of AECOPD with or without diabetes mellitus requiring NIV therapy were enrolled. Group A-50 patients of AECOPD without T2DM, GroupB-90 Patients of AECOPD with T2DM. Parameters such as ABG values at start of NIV therapy, subsequently at interval of 12 hrly, at discharge from hospital, length of hospital stay were used to assess the response to NIV therapy.

Results: Diabetic patients have a significantly lower pH, lower (PaO2) oxygen levels (41.6+/-12.77), higher PaCO2 (83.8+/-15.31) compared to non-diabetic patients suggesting more severe acidosis, worse oxygenation and worse hypercapnia. 37.78% of patients with diabetes had a stay of more than 6 days. Hospital stay was found to be 4.33 +/- 0.718 days in those who had mean random blood sugar (RBS) below140 mg/dl, 6.19 +/- 1.14 days in those who had RBS above 140. No association was found between hospital mortality and status of diabetes mellitus.

Conclusions: We observed that AECOPD patients with T2DM require longer duration of NIV, improvement in acidosis and oxygenation status is more gradual with NIV therapy when compared to non diabetic patients. Effective glycemic control plays a critical role in improving the outcomes of NIV therapy in patients with AECOPD and diabetes mellitus.

Kanishka Choudhary, Vipul Kumar, Gareema Narwal

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Pt.BD Sharma, PGIMS, Rohtak, Haryana, India

Introduction: Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) is now one of the top three causes of death worldwide and 90% of these deaths occur in low-and middle-income countries (LMICs). NIV is the standard of care for most patients with AECOPD but some patients are intolerant to NIV due to various factors. In this prospective observational study we have studied the role of HFNC in patients of AECOPD patients who are intolerant to NIV.

Methods: 80 patients who presented to emergency department with AECOPD without any other comorbidity were enrolled. All patients having respiratory acidosis(pH<7.35 and paCO2>50mmHg), an exacerbation of dyspnea with evidence of accessory respiratory muscle use and persistent hypoxemia despite oxygen therapy were included. All patients were given NIV therapy and various parameters such as arterial blood gases, improvement in paCO2 and paO2 levels for 12hrs, 24 hrs and 48 hrs and 72 hrs) were used to assess the level of improvement. Patients who couldn’t tolerate NIV were given a trial of HFNC(60 L/min) and results were assessed.

Results: Twenty patients intolerant to NIV were switched to HFNC; 4 improved, while the rest required invasive mechanical ventilation (MV). Improvement criteria included decrease in paCO2 levels, length of hospital stay, improvement in respiratory rate and paO2 levels.

Conclusion: This study highlights the role of High Flow Nasal Cannula (HFNC) oxygen therapy as a potential alternative in patients with acute exacerbation of COPD who are intolerant to Non-Invasive Ventilation (NIV). While HFNC showed limited overall effectiveness, with only 4 patients meeting the improvement criteria, it demonstrated some benefit in improving parameters such as arterial blood gases, respiratory rate, and oxygenation levels and hence helped in avoiding the MV along with complications. However larger randomized controlled trails are needed to know the exact place of HFNC in management of AECOPD.

Shradha Bajan, Shahid Patel, Girija Nair, Abhay Uppe, Nikhil Sarangdhar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D. Y. Patil University, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Breast carcinoma is increasingly affecting lower age groups over the last decade giving an age adjusted rate for Indian females of 25.8/ lakh women. Breast carcinoma can metastasise to the following organs in the frequency of bones-51%, lungs-17%, brain-16%, and liver-6%. Cancers other than breast carcinoma that can metastasize to the lungs include colorectal, testicular, thyroid malignancies and osteosarcoma.

Case Report: A 40 year female, with left breast carcinoma underwent MRM. Histopathology-immunohistochemistry showed TNM stage pT2N3a with ERpositive, PRpositive and HER2positive. Treated with 8 cycles of chemotherapy - Endoxan, Doxorubicin and Steroids (no targeted therapy given), 15 cycles of 3DCRT radiotherapy and was discharged on tamoxifen. Two years later, presented with bilateral ovarian complex cysts. CA125/ROMA values negative. Underwent trans-abdominal hysterectomy, bilateral salpingo-oopherectomy and left ovarian cystectomy. Histopathology ruled out malignancy and was suggestive of inactive endometrium consistent with tamoxifen therapy with chronic papillary endocervicitis.

After seven months, presented with an acute cough, breathlessness and mild fever at D.Y.PATIL HOSPITAL. With a differential-diagnosis of fungal/bacterial LRTI, tuberculosis and metastatic lung disease, bronchoscopy performed. BAL negative for cytology, KOH mount, Gramstain and GeneExpert. Serial chestxrays showed rapid worsening, suggestive of infectious etiology. HRCT showed bilateral mild pleural effusions and consolidation. Appropriate antibiotics and antifungals administered, with little improvement. PET-CT showed lung and bone metastasis, mediastinal lymphadenopathy, bilateral pleural effusion and consolidation in the lungs indicating metastatic lung disease with concomitant lung infection. Started on targeted chemotherapy with paclitaxel and trastuzumab. Serial chest xrays showed improvement.

Conclusion: (1) Early detection by spreading awareness of self breast examination is mandatory. (2) Metastatic disease in CA breast reduces the 5 year survival rate from 96% to a horrific 21%. (3) HER2+: Targeted therapy with Monoclonal Antibodies (Trastuzumab and Pertuzumab) + IV Hyaluronic acid prevents metastasis.

“Being Proactive and Not Just Reactive” helps prevent progression and metastasis.

Mohd Hamza Omer, Anita Saibannavar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, R.C.S.M. G.M.C., Kolhapur, Maharashtra, India

Background: Rheumatoid arthritis is a systemic autoimmune disorder that primarily affects the joint but can also lead to extra articular manifestation including pleuropulmonary involvement. One such complication is Bronchiectasis, a condition characterized by permanent dilation and damage to the airways and recurrent respiratory infection. This case study highlights the co-occurrence of bronchiectasis in a middle age female patient with rheumatoid arthritis.

Case Report: Here we present a case of a middle age female, case of rheumatoid arthritis presented with respiratory symptoms without any prior history of respiratory disease since childhood. Rheumatoid arthritis was confirmed by RA factor and Anti CCP antibody test. On HRCT thorax she was diagnosed as bilateral bronchiectasis. With detailed history and examination, we ruled out other causes of bronchiectasis. Her respiratory symptoms improved with antibiotics and chest physiotherapy and Rheumatoid arthritis was managed as per Rheumatologist opinion. Regular follow up was advised to monitor disease progression.

Conclusion: This case highlights the importance of recognizing pleuropulmonary complications like Interstitial lung disease or Bronchiectasis in patients of rheumatoid arthritis particularly those with persistent respiratory symptoms. Early diagnosis and multidisciplinary approach to treatment are essential for improving outcomes and to prevent further pulmonary sequelae in them.

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, lung and autoimmune disorder, rheumatoid arthritis

Sanat S. Verenkar, Gajanan S. Gaude, Bhagyashri B. Patil, Jyothi Hattiholi, S. Gautam, Kirankumar Pujar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, KAHER Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Background: Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) is a progressive lung condition that often leads to significant weight loss and malnutrition, exacerbating disease severity and disability. Malnutrition in COPD patients is underrecognized despite its strong association with respiratory muscle dysfunction and clinical outcomes. The Mini Nutritional Assessment (MNA) is a validated tool offering a comprehensive evaluation of nutritional status, surpassing the limitations of Body Mass Index (BMI). This prospective observational study aims to assess the prevalence of malnutrition in COPD patients using MNA and to compare its efficacy with BMI.

Methods: A total of 100 COPD patients were recruited from the Respiratory Medicine OPD of KLE Dr. Prabhakar Kore Hospital, Belagavi, over one year. Assessed using MNA, anthropometric measurements, mMRC dyspnea scale, CAT score, and spirometry. Nutritional and clinical correlations were analysed.

Results: Over 50% of the participants were either malnourished or at risk of malnutrition based on MNA scores. The study found that BMI alone failed to identify many at-risk patients who were flagged by MNA. Malnutrition prevalence correlated strongly with worsening spirometric indices (FEV1), higher CAT scores, and advanced GOLD stages. Notably, even patients with normal or high BMI exhibited nutritional deficiencies detected by MNA. Furthermore, psychological factors, reduced food intake, and prolonged smoking histories were common contributors to malnutrition.

Conclusions: The findings confirm that malnutrition is prevalent among COPD patients and often goes undetected using BMI alone. MNA proved to be a more comprehensive tool, offering insights into the multifaceted aspects of nutritional status, such as dietary intake and psychological health.

Routine application of MNA for COPD patients could enhance early detection of malnutrition and facilitate timely interventions, potentially improving outcomes and reducing hospitalizations.

Keywords: BMI, COPD, malnutrition, MNA

Vedamurthy Basavaraja Janglera, T. Prasannakumar, D. Pragati Rao

Department of Respiratory Medicine, M S Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis is a chronic inflammatory disease. In India, bronchiectasis is a significant public health concern due to various factors, such as the high prevalence of pulmonary infections, including tuberculosis (TB) and lower respiratory tract infections. Following anatomical damage in the bronchi, exacerbations tend to increase due to the progressive deterioration of pulmonary physiology. HALP score is an indicator of nutritional status and systemic inflammation and that has been used to predict bronchiectasis exacerbation. The HALP score is a comprehensive index that measures immune health as well as nutritional status.

Methods: 86 patients with HRCT Thorax confirmed bronchiectasis were included in the study. HALP score is determined by the following formula: Hb (g/L) x albumin (g/L) x lymphocytes (/L)/platelet (/L). Spirometry was performed, FEV1 And FVC were recorded. Compared HALP scores and other metrics between the exacerbation and stable groups. Continuous data was represented as mean and standard deviation. Mann-Whitney U Test/Independent t-Test was used for Comparative analysis between groups. Spearman/Pearson correlation analysis between other variables. P-value<0.05 was considered as statistically significant.

Results: HALP scores were significantly lower during exacerbation periods compared to stable periods (p < 0.001) suggesting reduced nutritional and inflammatory resilience. Mean HALP Scores of 12.30 during Exacerbation and 18.76 during stable period. Lower lung function ratios (FEV1/FVC) and FEV1 in the exacerbation group compared to the stable group (r=0.214r). HALP scores did not differ significantly between genders (p = 0.228).

Conclusions: The study highlights the significant utility of the HALP score as a biomarker to differentiate between clinical states (exacerbation and stable) in patients with Bronchiectasis reflecting systemic inflammation and nutritional status.

The HALP score in Bronchiectasis could be a non-invasive, cost-effective tool for monitoring patient status and predicting exacerbations.

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, cost effective, exacerbation and stable period, HALP score

Cheshtha Singla, Vishal Chopra, Kranti Garg

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, GMC, Patiala, Punjab, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) remains a global public health burden. COPD is a heterogenous lung condition characterised by a persistent, often progressive airflow limitation. A physiological variable – FEV1 (% predicted) is used to grade the severity of COPD. Multidimensional scales like the BODE, ADO, DOSE index have been used to determine the prognosis of COPD. Our study aims to compare these indices with GOLD grading and determine their correlation for determining the severity of COPD.

Methods: It was a prospective cross-sectional study on 100 COPD patients. The patients enrolled in the study were classified as per their GOLD grading and ABE assessment tool. Indices like BODE index, ADO index, DOSE index were calculated for each patient at the time of enrolment. These prognostic scores were used to determine if any statistically significant correlation exists between these indices and GOLD grading.

Results:

A significant positive correlation exists between BODE index and GOLD grading (expected Pearson r > 0.6). ADO index correlates moderately with GOLD grading (expected Pearson r ~ 0.4-0.6). DOSE index shows weaker or inconsistent correlation (expected Pearson r<0.4).

Conclusions: Multidimensional indices complement GOLD grading by integrating physiological and symptom-based measures. Indices like BODE are valuable for tailoring interventions and monitoring progression.

BODE index is a stronger predictor of COPD severity compared to ADO and DOSE. DOSE index may excel in identifying symptom burden but not airflow limitation.

Keywords: ADO, BODE, COPD, DOSE, GOLD

Vaishnavi Handral, Lavina Mirchandani, Girija Nair, Shahid Patel, Nikhil Saranghdhar, Manas Bhanushali

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D Y Patil University School of Medicine, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Bronchial Asthma is a common airway disorder with exposure to environmental allergens being a known risk factor. Skin prick test is a valuable diagnostic tool for assessing allergen sensitization and in customizing the management of Bronchial Asthma. Aims and objectives of our study are to document the various food and aero allergen sensitization with skin prick test and their correlation with exacerbation of bronchial asthma.

Methods: A prospective cross-sectional observational study was conducted among 85 patients of diagnosed bronchial asthma aged 18-60 years that are willing to undergo skin prick test. Patients aged less than 18 years and more 60 years were excluded. A diagnosis of bronchial asthma confirmed with careful history and examination along with routine investigations and with a standard spirometry criteria. With consent of patient, skin prick test was performed. A wheal more than 3mm in diameter was considered positive. Statistical tests were applied to find out correlation between patients with history of exacerbation of bronchial asthma in past year and sensitization to aero and food allergens.

Results: In our study majority of the patients were more than 50years (36.5%) and 64.7% were female. Sensitization to most common allergens among each allergen group (dust, mites, pollens, epithelia, insect, food, fungus) are described. 20 individual (23.5%) sensitized to house dust. 21 individuals (24.7%) were sensitized to mites d. pteronyssinus. 12 individuals (14.1%) sensitized to cassia siamea, 11 individual (12.9%) sensitized to pigeon feather, housefly, cockroach, rice weevil all were sensitized in 20 individuals (23.5%). 30 individuals (35.3%) tested sensitive to Aspergillus fumigatus. In the study population, 45 individuals had at least one exacerbation in the past year, of which 7 patients required ICU. Mites sensitization was statistically significant (p-0.03) in the patients that required ICU.

Conclusion: There is significant correlation with mite sensitization and exacerbation of Bronchial Asthma. Eliminating allergen exposure and Allergen Immunotherapy guides management.

Anna George, Lavina Mirchandani, Girija Nair, Shahid Patel, Manas Bhanushali

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Dr. D. Y. Patil Medical College, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Asthma and COPD are distinctly different in terms of clinical presentation, radiological and spirometry findings. However, there exists considerable overlap causing diagnostic dilemma, impacting their management. Sputum cellularity is being used to differentiate obstructive airway phenotypes. In this study, we assessed certain objective inflammatory indices and correlated them with pulmonary function tests- FEV1 and FEV1/FVC ratio.

Methods: A total number of 77 patients were taken diagnosed with asthma (n = 43) and COPD (n = 34) between June 2024 to December 2024.The patient’s pulmonary function test were conducted and routine blood samples were analyzed for inflammatory indices. The levels of these indices were compared between the two groups, and their relationship with FEV1 and FEV1/FVC ratios was assessed.

Results: The majority of the participants were male (51.95%). The mean age of the study participants was 47.17 ± 17.96 years. 25.97% of the participants were smokers, and 7.7% of them had a history of tuberculosis. The mean FEV1 and mean FEV1/ FVC ratio were not significantly different between the 2 groups. Similarly, no significant difference was found in mean platelets and eosinophil count or median Eosinophil Neutrophil ratio (ENR), Eosinophil Basophil Ratio (EBR), and Eosinophil Lymphocyte Ratio between the groups. However, the median Neutrophil Lymphocyte Ration (NLR), Systemic immune -inflammation Index (SII), and Systemic Inflammation Response Index (SIRI) were significantly higher in the COPD group, while the median Lymphocyte Monocyte Ratio was higher in the asthma group (p < 0.05), with Mann-Whitney U test. The FEV1/FVC ratio correlated positively with SIRI in both groups, though this was not statistically significant using Spearman’s correlation test.

Conclusion: The results of this study show inflammatory indices can serve as biomarkers to distinguish patients with asthma and COPD. Larger multicentric trials will help to further explore their clinical relevance and predictive value.

Heritage Institute of Medical Sciences, Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh, India

Background: When a Doctor makes a Diagnosis of Asthma in people older than age 20, it is known as adult-onset. Usually, asthma symptoms appear early in life. However, some people develop asthma symptoms for the first time in adulthood; their clinical status is called LOA, defined as: “asthma with onset of symptoms in adult life in a patient with no pre-existing, persistent respiratory symptoms,” but the definition of age has not been clarified. A better understanding of all the aspects of LOA can help clinicians make more precise guided treatment decisions using existing or new treatment approaches and to devise optimal personalized management of the disease.

Swapna Nair1, Subhashis Roy2, Srinivas Jakka3, Vaishali Naik1, Meena Lopez1, M. Senthilnathan1, Jaideep Gogtay1

1Department of Medical Affairs, Cipla Ltd., Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 2Shaishab Clinic, Kolkata, West Bengal, 3Ankura Hospitals, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Guidelines recommend using a combination of inhaled corticosteroids and long acting beta2-agonist (ICS-LABA) for children whose asthma is not controlled with ICS alone. The usage patterns of ICS-LABA among Indian children with asthma however remain unclear. This study therefore aimed to examine the prescription trends of ICS-LABA in managing asthma in children in India.

Methods: A digital cross-sectional survey was conducted among clinicians managing pediatric asthma.

Results: 454 clinicians (55.7% respiratory physicians and 44.3% pediatricians) participated in the survey. Pediatricians reported seeing more asthma patients/month as compared to respiratory physicians (62.7% pediatricians and 24.11% respiratory clinicians saw >20 asthmatics/month,p<0.001). The usage of ICS-LABA for management of pediatric asthma was similar across specialties with 64.03% of respiratory physicians and 74.13% of pediatricians reporting that up to 50% of their patients wereprescribedthe combination. Being uncontrolled on low-dose ICS (53.96%), ICS+leukotriene inhibitors (49.78%), PRN-SABA (44.93%) or medium-dose ICS (41.85%) were cited as the most common reasons for prescribing ICS-LABA combination. Respiratory physicians cited reduction in daytime (32.41%) and night-time (27.27%) symptoms as the most important reasons for preferring ICS-LABA, whereas pediatricians cited reduction in night-time symptoms (34.33%) and emergency clinic visits (32.34%) as the most important benefits. Budesonide-formoterol was the most preferred ICS-LABA by both respiratory physicians and pediatricians (78.66% vs 61.69%, p<0.001) followed by fluticasone-salmeterol (11.86% vs 27.36%). 83% respiratory physicians vs. 63.68% pediatricians (p<0.001) preferred using the combination as maintenance and reliever therapy (MART). pMDI with spacer (80.18%) was the preferred device for managing asthma in children.

Conclusion: The findings of the survey indicate that ICS-LABA is well-accepted for the treatment of pediatric asthma in India, with budesonide-formoterol being the favored combination. ICS-LABA was reported to provide benefits such as decreased symptoms and fewer emergency clinic visits in children.

Introduction: Allergic bronchopulmonary mycoses are complex pulmonary disorders caused by immune reactions mounted against fungi, most often Aspergillus fumigatus, which colonize the airways of patients with chronic lung disease, most commonly asthma or cystic fibrosis (CF). Patient is currently stable with no active hemoptysis and symptomatic relief.

Conclusions: In cases of lung mass, ABPA HAM is also to be considered as a differential diagnosis and thorough workup is required to differentiate between infectious causes and malignancy.

S. Ashok Kumar, Mahavir Modi

1Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Ruby Hall Clinic, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Pulmonary aspergillosis, primarily caused by Aspergillus fumigatus, manifests in various forms, from acute infections to chronic lung diseases. Clinical presentations depends on the patient’s immune status and underlying health conditions.

Objective: To elucidate the clinical presentations of pulmonary aspergillosis, including acute invasive aspergillosis, chronic pulmonary aspergillosis and allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis. Understanding these variations is crucial for accurate diagnosis, effective treatment and improving outcomes.

Case Studies: (1) A 23-year-old male with recent influenza presented with fever and dry cough. CT chest revealed bilateral ground-glass opacities. Bronchoscopy showed diffuse white plaques, and BAL culture grew Aspergillus fumigatus. He was diagnosed with acute bronchopulmonary aspergillosis and responded well to antifungal treatment. (2) A 38-year-old male with chronic sinusitis presented with fever, cough and breathlessness. CT chest showed bilateral cavitating lesions and sputum culture grew Aspergillus fumigatus, confirming chronic pulmonary aspergillosis. He improved with antifungal therapy. (3) A 55-year-old male with lung cancer and asthma presented with cough and expectoration. CT chest revealed proximal bronchiectasis and serology and sputum were positive for Aspergillus. He was diagnosed with allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis and managed with steroids and antifungals.

Conclusion: The diverse presentations of pulmonary aspergillosis highlight the need for heightened clinical awareness and comprehensive diagnostic strategies. Effective management requires prompt identification and a personalized approach, underscoring the need for ongoing research and vigilance.

Keywords: Aspergillus fumigatus, aspergillus lung disease, invasive aspergillosis, pulmonary fungal infection

S. Sriram, S. Yuvarajan, R. Praveen, Cherukumalli Navya, Sagana

Sri Manakula Vinayagar Medical College and Hospital, Puducherry, India

Introduction: Candida is an opportunistic fungus which colonizes the oral cavity and can be considered as a commensal when it is seen in the sputum. Candida in pleural fluid is rare and it needs utmost attention as it is a sterile fluid. It can result from invasive candidiasis or by direct inoculation of the pleural space after thoracic or abdominal surgeries.

Case: A 75-year-old male who is a known case of diabetic, coronary artery disease and chronic kidney disease, with irregular treatment adherence presented to the hospital with one-month history of cough with expectoration and breathlessness. Investigations, including blood culture, sputum culture, acid-fast bacillus (AFB) smear, and fungal smear (KOH), were negative. CT thorax revealed left-sided empyema. Diagnostic thoracentesis was performed, the pleural fluid fungal smear revealed yeast with pseudohyphae, which was suggestive of Candida. Fungal culture confirmed the growth of Candida albicans.The patient underwent therapeutic thoracocentesis and was started on intravenous fluconazole for antifungal treatment.

Conclusions: Candida in pleural fluid is a rare but severe condition characterized by fungal growth in pleural fluid, often following pneumonia, abscesses, or esophageal rupture, and is associated with high mortality, especially in immunocompromised patients. Mortality rates in Candida empyema vary but can reach up to 30%, with factors like sepsis and diabetes contributing to poor outcomes. Despite antifungal therapy, this condition is often fatal, particularly in cases linked to surgery or esophageal rupture. Diagnosis requires isolation of the same fungus from pleural fluid and other specimens, along with signs of infection like fever and leukocytosis. Treatment typically involves thoracocentesis and antifungal therapy. Carry home message Early recognition of fungal empyema is crucial for successful management. Prompt application of diagnostic methods, including imaging and microbiological cultures, is vital for accurate diagnosis. Initiate targeted antifungal therapy without delay and maintain regular follow-up to assess treatment response and prevent potential complications.

P Jayakar Reddy, N. Bhaskara Rao

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Guntur Medical College, Guntur, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Aspergillomas are formed when colonized fungal organisms lining innersurface of pre-existing cavity spread into the cavity itself the fungi proliferate in the form of fungal ball inside the cavity.

Case Report: A 23 year old female with allegedly consumption of paraquat poisoning 6months back immediately developed vomitings and loose stools was treated in hospital and later sent home within one month developed SOB,revisited the hospital,was treated,sent home with LTOT which she used for 1month,chest radiography shows fibrotic strands later went to hospital again for fever,chest pain,increased with cough.Multiple visits to hospital i/v/o SOB,headache,fever.Now Xray shows a cavity in left upperlobe,so started ATT clinicoradiologically.After using ATT for 15 days she developed DILI&LFT shows elevated bilirubin and enzymes,so ATT stopped.Referred to pulmonology opd i/v/o left upperlobe cavity and h/o ATT usage and with complaints of cough,sob,pleuritic chestpain,followup Xray shows soft tissue density within the cavity with fibrotic changes in left mid&lower zones.HRCT done which confirmed aspergilloma.Serum aspergillus specific IgG done was elevated and aspergilloma confirmed.Patient started on itraconazole and now is on followup Carry home message: Pulmonary cavity although very rare cannot be overlooked in case of Paraquat ingestion which is an extremely toxic herbicide, which can cause severe damage to lungs, liver, kidney, etc.,with no specific antidote.

Conclusion: This case highlights the very rare occurrence of cavity& aspergilloma within cavity as sequelae of paraquat poisoning and emphasizes need for prompt recognition and management.

Elsa Rachel Abraham, Raghu Srikanti

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Guntur Medical College, Guntur, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Hydatid disease, a worldwide zoonosis is caused by the larval stage of Echinococcus. Lung are the second most common location (20%) in Cystic echinococcosis after the liver (80%). Most pulmonary cysts are located in lower lobes and unilateral in 80% of pulmonary echinococcosis.

Case Report: 53 years old female cook by occupation presented with chief complaints of shortness of breath, cough, abdominal pain since 3 months. Her chestxray and CTchest showed right sided loculated pleural effusion for which she was started on ATT empirically.Upon detailed history taking it was revealed that patient had complaint of pain abdomen in right hypochondriac and umbilical area 20 years back and underwent surgery. She was then sent for USG abdomen which revealed cystic lesions in sub diaphragmatic region.CT Abdomen revealed small multiple hepatic,sub hepatic,sub diaphragmatic cysts.CT Chest showed multiple cysts in right lung with water lily sign which is pathognomonic of Pulmonary hydatid disease. Based on the radiological evidence and past history, she was diagnosed as Pulmonary Hydatid Disease and started Albendazole 400mg BD.Albendazole was continued beyond 6 months as her symptoms were not resolved.Chestxray done after 2 years revealed 80% resolution& patient had significant clinical improvement.

Carry Home Message: Prolonged medical management beyond 6 months has shown significant clinical improvement and improvement in the quality of life.

Conclusion: Hydatid cysts most often occur as a single unilocular cyst, with only 38% occuring as multiple unilocular cysts.The most important diagnostic tool is radiology.Good medical response and improvement in the quality of life is seen even in cases of multiple lung hydatid cysts after prolonged therapy beyond 6 months.

P. R. Sreelatha, S. Niveditha

TDMCH, Alappuzha, Kerala, India

Background: Influenza is a highly contagious viral infection primarily caused by Influenza A, known for its potential to cause both epidemics and pandemics. The clinical presentation and severity can vary with each outbreak, with children being the most commonly and severely affected. Early detection and timely initiation of antiviral therapy are essential to reduce morbidity, mortality, and overall disease severity.

Aim: This study aims to examine the clinical profile of microbiologically confirmed Influenza A H1N1 cases among inpatients aged 28 days to 12 years during the period from April to September 2024.

Methods: A descriptive study was conducted in the Pediatrics Department of Government Medical College, Alappuzha, Kerala, South India. Children aged 28 days to 12 years who tested positive for Influenza A H1N1 via polymerase chain reaction (PCR) from nasopharyngeal swabs during the study period were included. Data were collected from medical records using a semi-structured proforma, with prior approval from the Institutional Research Committee, Ethics Committee, and Medical Superintendent.

Results: A total of 85 pediatric cases tested positive for Influenza A H1N1 PCR. The gender distribution was nearly equal (male to female ratio 1:1.1). Age distribution showed a predominance of cases between 1 and 10 years, with fewer cases in children younger than 1 year or older than 10 years. The peak incidence occurred in June and July, coinciding with the monsoon season. Fever was the most common symptom, followed by rhinitis and cough. Constitutional symptoms such as lethargy and anorexia were also frequent. Neurological symptoms, including seizures, delirium, and headache, were observed, with seizures being more common in children under 5 years and delirium more frequent in older children. Gastrointestinal symptoms such as vomiting, abdominal pain, and diarrhea were also reported. Blood tests revealed a leukocyte count ranging from 1,500 to 18,400, with about one-third showing leucopenia, particularly on day 5 of illness. A neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio of less than 2 was found in three-quarters of the cases. Platelet counts ranged from 5,6000 to 67,5000 with 23.5% of patients showing thrombocytopenia, especially between days 3 and 5 of illness. Elevated SGOT levels (51–350 IU/L) were seen in half of the patients, most commonly in those aged 6 to 10 years, with a threefold rise predominantly in girls. Liver enzyme elevation (SGOT and SGPT) was noted in 12% of cases. CSF analysis in 11 patients and EEG in 4 revealed normal results, except for one patient showing transient slowing on EEG. The length of hospital stay ranged from 2 to 12 days, with a median of 5.5 days, except for two children with severe acute malnutrition. Nineteen percent of patients required intensive care unit (ICU) admission. Eighty percent of children received oseltamivir, which was initiated between day 1 and day 8 of illness. No correlation was found between the timing of antiviral therapy initiation and the duration of hospital stay. About one-third of the patients were also treated with antibiotics.

Conclusion: This study on 85 pediatric cases of microbiologically confirmed H1N1 Influenza A during the 2024 monsoon epidemic highlights the occurrence of transient encephalopathy in older children and significant transaminasemia, particularly in girls. No correlation was found between the timing of antiviral therapy initiation and the length of hospital stay.

Keywords: Clinical profile, influenza A H1N1, pediatrics

Medishetty Abhigna, A.Vinay Kumar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Chalmeda Anand Rao Institute of Medical Sciences, Karimnagar, India

Background: Pertussis in older adults often presents as chronic cough, commonly misdiagnosed as other LRTI , GERD,asthma, COPD.It can also lead to severe outcomes such as pneumonia in infants and older adults, necessitating hospitalization. Pneumonia associated with pertussis in adolescents and adults often results from secondary bacterial infections. This case highlights the complex interplay of bacterial and viral pathogens contributing to severe respiratory failure in a patient with chronic respiratory disease.

Case Study: A 66-year-old male , chronic smoker presented to the emergency department with a 1 year history of an intermittent productive cough, which had worsened over the past 7 days , difficulty breathing since 3 days , history of 3 days of fever 5 days prior to admission. Routine investigations showed leukocytosis, elevated C-reactive protein (CRP), and elevated D-dimer levels. A chest X-ray revealed bilateral interstitial infiltrates and consolidations, more prominent on the right side. ABG indicated Type 1 respiratory failure. Sputum culture revealed Bordetella pertussis and further culture testing identified multidrug resistant Klebsiella pneumoniae. Nasopharyngeal tested positive for COVID-19 via RT-PCR.

Discussion: This case underscores the challenges posed by co-infections in the setting of chronic pulmonary conditions and smoking. The patient’s chronic airway inflammation likely predisposed him to secondary bacterial infections, while COVID-19 exacerbated the inflammatory and immune dysregulation. The multidrug-resistant Klebsiella pneumoniae posed therapeutic difficulties, limiting antibiotic options. Co-detection of Bordetella pertussis is rare in adults but highlights the need for vigilance in atypical respiratory infections. Additionally, COVID-19 positivity further complicated management, contributing to hypoxemia and increased risk of poor outcomes.

Conclusion: Pertussis should be considered in the differential diagnosis of persistent cough or atypical pneumonia in older adults .Co-infections with resistant bacterial pathogens and viral agents, including SARS-CoV-2, underscore the need for timely diagnostic workup and integrated management strategies.

Key words: Pertussis, klebsiella, Covid 19, respiratory failure, chronic cough

Archit Gupta, Deepika Ughade, Manas Bhanushali, Tanay Sinha, Girija Nair, Nikhil Sarangdhar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D.Y Patil University School of Medicine, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Sheehan’s syndrome occurs as a result of ischemic pituitary necrosis due to severe postpartum hemorrhage. The coexistence of aspergillosis with Klebsiella in a patient of Sheehan’s is an unusual occurrence in medical literature.

Case Report: A 55 year old female came with complaints of cough, breathlessness & fever since 1 week with history of Sheehan’s syndrome with hypothyroidism, on thyroxine & low dose of oral prednisolone. Chest radiograph showed right upper lobe collapse. HRCT thorax s/o consolidation with right upper lobe collapse. Patient was admitted and broncoscopy was advised. Bronchoalveolar lavage taken from all the segments of both lungs with biopsy from posterior segment of right upper lobe. BAL c/s shows klebsiella sp. and lung tissue HPE revealed aspergillosis. Patient started on antibiotics as per drug sensitivity and on Tab Itraconazole.

Early clinical suspicion coupled with prompt diagnosis and multidisciplinary interdepartmental discussion often helps to resolve diagnostic and treatment dilemmas, besides offering a good standard of care and a better chance of survival.

Vamshi Goud1, Anita T. Anokar1, Anuradha Tolpadi2, Sheetal Vernekar1, Meera Modak2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Bharati Vidyapeeth (DTU), Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Microbiology, Bharati Vidyapeeth (DTU), Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Influenza poses a significant public health burden in India. The re-emergence of H1N1 influenza underscores the need to understand the post -COVID dynamics of this virus so as to guide the surveillance and prevention strategies accordingly.

Methodology: A single centre retrospective study is carried out in Pune, Maharashtra, to analyse changes in seasonal trends, mortality and age distribution of H1N1 positive cases in last 2 years (January 2023 to December 2024). The demographic, clinical and outcome data was collected and analysed.

Results: In this study, total 137 H1N1 PCR positive cases were included, out of which 135 were hospitalised. Total 68 were in children <18years. The age distribution of cases indicates a higher concentration of cases in the 0-10 and 40-50 year’s age group. Males comprised a larger proportion of H1N1 cases (59.9%) compared to females (40.1%). In the year 2023, the month-wise distribution showed peak of cases in September. The number of positive cases increased throughout the year in 2024, total 110 cases with two peaks, first in late winter and second largest peak during monsoon season in August. Mortality in the admitted cases was 14.28%. The mortality rate is notably higher in the 0-5 and >50 years age groups compared to other age groups. The vaccination among the hospitalised adults with Influenza was 2.9%.

Conclusion: Rising H1N1 influenza cases with higher mortality and poor adult vaccination is a major public health concern. Considering potential shifts in seasonality and intensity is crucial for informing public health interventions, vaccination and preparedness strategies in India.

Key words: Influenza, H1N1, Vaccination

Yash Kedia

Department of Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine, VMMC and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India

Introduction: Oxygen desaturation index (ODI) has a significant correlation with the development and progression of cardiovascular diseases (CVD) in patients with obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). Continuous positive airway pressure therapy (CPAP) improves the hypoxic burden in OSA but has not been shown to reduce cardiovascular morbidity conclusively. We evaluated if optimal CPAP titration aimed at reducing the apnea hypopnea index (AHI), also optimally reduced the hypoxic burden, as assessed using ODI.

Methods: This was a retrospective observational study in 40 patients of OSA who underwent a split-night level 1 sleep study and achieved optimal CPAP titration based on the AHI index. We calculated the AHI and ODI during the diagnostic portion and at the optimal CPAP titration pressure and compared the results between patients with and without CVD.

Results: 55% of the patients had CVD, including hypertension, dyslipidemia, or coronary artery disease. During the diagnostic study, the AHI and ODI had significant positive corelation (r = 0.90, p = 0.001). During the CPAP titration study, despite achieving an AHI of < 5 events per hour in supine REM sleep, 50% of the patients had an ODI of more than 5 events per hour. At the optimal CPAP titration pressure, the mean ODI was significantly higher in patients with CVD (p=0.026).

Conclusion: Despite optimal CPAP titration, the hypoxic burden as evaluated using ODI was persistently higher in patients with CVD. This under correction of the hypoxic burden despite optimal CPAP titration might contribute to the progression of CVD in OSA.

Ann Elizabeth John, Bhagyashri Patil, Gajanan S. Gaude, Jyothi Hatiholli, S. Gautam, Kiran Kumar Pujar, Ningappa Karalingannavar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Rapid eye movement (REM) sleep related obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) represents a distinct subtype of OSA characterised by apnea and hypopnea episodes predominantly occurring during REM sleep. These events result in significant hypoxemia and sleep fragmentation. The purpose of this study was to assess the prevalence of REM related OSA among patients with OSA, explore its association with the Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS) and analyse related demographic and polysomnographic parameters.

Aim: Determine the prevalence of REM OSA in patients with obstructive sleep apnoea. Assess the correlation of Epworth sleepiness scale with overall AHI, REM OSA.

Methods: The study included fifty-eight patients who underwent polysomnography (PSG) at K.L.E Dr.Prabhakar Kore Hospital, Belagavi. REM-related OSA was defined based on previously established broad and strict criteria (REM apnea-hypopnea index [AHI]/non REM [NREM] AHI ratio ≥2 and REM AHI >5 with NREM AHI <5, respectively) and its association with socio-demographic parameters and polysomnographic features were analysed using appropriate statistical measures.

Results: Prevalence of REM related OSA in the study was 39.66% (strict criteria) and 43.1% (broad criteria). There is no significant correlation between REM AHI and ESS (Epworth sleepiness scale) or overall AHI and ESS. Patients with REM related OSA also exhibited: (1) Higher BMI (31.28 ± 1.17 vs 30.39 ± 1.98; p=0.038), (2) Larger neck circumference (41.8 ± 1.68 vs 39.94 ±2.95, p= 0.004), (3) Poorer sleep efficiency (82.32 ± 2.41 vs 84.00 ± 2.32; p=0.009), (4) Greater oxygen desaturation (lowest spo2:77.48 ± 1.94% vs. 81.58 ± 5.04%; p<0.001), (5) Longer apnea events during REM sleep (35.96 ± 3.12 vs 31.33 ± 6.01 seconds; p<0.001) were observed.

Conclusion: REM related OSA is associated with obesity, severe oxygen desaturation and significant contribution to overall AHI. This study aims to highlight the importance in identifying patients with REM OSA and their subsequent management to mitigate its cardiovascular and neurocognitive risks.

Keywords: Epworth sleepiness scale, obstructive sleep apnea, polysomnography, rapid eye movement sleep related apnea

P. Dhinesh, Jyothi Hattiholi, Gajanan S. Gaude, Bhagyashri B. Patil, S. Gautam, Kirankumar Pujar

Department of Respiratory, Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, KAHER University, Belgaum, Karnataka, India

Background: Sleep-disordered breathing, including obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), involves interrupted or reduced breathing during sleep, often due to airway blockage. Diagnosis is made through polysomnography. The prevalence of pulmonary hypertension (PH) in OSA varies widely (15% to 75%), with most patients having normal pulmonary artery pressure (PAP) or mild PH. Severe PH, leading to right heart issues, is more common in those with additional lung conditions like COPD or obesity.

Aim/Objective: The study aims to determine the prevalence of PH in OSA, assess the correlation between the severity of PH and OSA severity, and identify other risk factors associated with both conditions.

Method: All patients undergoing polysomnography will be included. Informed written consent will be obtained, with parental consent for those under 18. Detailed patient histories will be recorded, and polysomnography will be classified into mild, moderate, or severe categories. Pulmonary artery pressure will be evaluated using 2D echocardiography. The correlation between polysomnography severity and PAP will be analysed.

Results: The study involved 60 patients (65.4% male, 34.6% female), with 76.4% being obese. The distribution of OSA severity was 16.3% mild, 25.5% moderate, and 58.2% severe. Pulmonary artery pressure findings were 21.9% mild, 9.1% moderate, 5.4 % severe, and 63.6% normal, with an overall prevalence of PH in OSA patients at 36.4%.

Conclusion: Routine screening by 2D echocardiography for PH in OSA patients is recommended, regardless of the presence of severe comorbidities, as lung disease is not a prerequisite for PH in OSA.

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D Y Patil University of Medicine, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Thyroid disorders may directly cause sleep-related breathing disorders or act as contributing factors through metabolic syndrome. Various studies have been done to establish an association between sleep apnea and hypothyroidism or thyroid disorders; however, the relationship remains unclear. As such, recognition and management of both SDB and thyroid disorders and the effect each has on the patient are essential.

Methods: An observational study of 50 patients with deranged thyroid profile or already diagnosed with thyroid disorders, with a STOP BANG of 3 or more. Patients were evaluated for sleep-disordered breathing using level 1 polysomnography at the sleep lab in D Y Patil Hospital, Navi Mumbai.

Results: Among the 50 subjects, Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) was diagnosed in 52%, with OHS in 10% and mixed sleep apnea in 4%, while 34% of patients had no sleep-related breathing disorders. The chi-square test indicated a significant association between thyroid disorders and sleep apnea diagnoses (P value = 0.013), with 73.1% of those with obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) hypothyroidism and 41.2% with subclinical hypothyroidism.

Conclusions: Our study underscores a significant link between thyroid disorders and sleep apnea, specifically OSA. In patients with thyroid disease, there was a positive correlation between AHI and weight, BMI, and STOP-BANG.

This study used polysomnography to investigate the prevalence and characteristics of sleep apnea in patients with thyroid diseases. Our findings reveal significant associations between thyroid dysfunction and sleep apnea, contributing to the growing evidence of the interplay between these conditions.

Keywords: Apnea–hypopnea index, hypothyroidism, obesity, obstructive sleep apnea, polysomnography

Simren Faizal

Baby Memorial Hospital, Kozhikode, Kerala, India

Introduction: Mesothelioma is the most common primary malignant tumour of the pleura, with 70% of cases linked to asbestos exposure. The lifetime risk of developing mesothelioma in asbestos workers is 8-13%.

Case Report: A 76-year-old retired marine engineer with intermittent asbestos exposure and a history of diabetes, hypertension, and coronary artery disease presented with a 4-month history of progressive dyspnoea, right-sided chest pain, and dry cough. Examination revealed decreased chest wall movement, tactile fremitus, stony dullness on percussion, and diminished breath sounds on the right side. A diagnosis of moderate right-sided pleural effusion was made. Chest X-ray showed right-sided opacity with convex upper border and superior mediastinal widening. Thoracentesis revealed exudative pleural fluid with lymphocytic predominance. CT thorax showed lobulated soft tissue density involving the right pleura, extending to the mediastinal, costal, and diaphragmatic surfaces, with pleural effusion and underlying lung collapse, suggesting a malignant etiology, likely metastatic involvement or mesothelioma. Thoracoscopy revealed multiple exophytic nodules on the costal and mediastinal pleura. Cytology showed atypical cells, and pleural biopsy revealed neoplastic cells in papillae and cords within desmoplastic stroma. Immunohistochemistry showed positivity for D240 and WT-1, consistent with epithelioid mesothelioma. PET-CT staging revealed T4N2M0, indicating stage 4 disease.

Conclusions: Given the patient’s history of intermittent asbestos exposure, the diagnosis of epithelioid mesothelioma emphasizes asbestos as a significant risk factor.

This case highlights the strong link between asbestos exposure and malignant mesothelioma, underscoring the need for early diagnosis and appropriate staging for effective management and prognosis.

Kaushik Bhowal

Department of TB and Respiratory Diseases, Institute of Medical Sciences, BHU, Uttar Pradesh, India

Introductions: Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia (TPE) is a clinical manifestation of a parasitic infection caused by filarial nematodes (Wuchereria bancrofti, Brugia malayi and Brugia timori) caused by immune hyper-responsiveness to microfilaria trapped in lungs. Commonly occurs in young adults and transmitted by mosquito vectors and humans being the definitive hosts. Imaging studies in TPE can be easily misdiagnosed with other differential diagnosis. Here we present a case of Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia.

Case: 27 years old male presented with complaints of dry cough for 2 months without any H/O hemoptysis, dyspnea for around 2-3 months- insidious onset, non-progressive, Fever around 2 months back which subsided after 15 days with medications. There was no history of chest pain. There was no H/O any known comorbidities. The patient had no prior H/O anti tubercular drug intake or contact with TB patient. He has no H/O smoking or alcohol intake and is a college student. The patient had no allergic history to any substance. Chest auscultation revealed wheeze in the Left suprascapular area. CXR was done which revealed a miliary pattern in B/L lung fields without any other significant finding. To confirm the finding, HRCT chest was done which confirmed it to be miliary pattern and reported as small randomly nodular lesions in B/L lung parenchyma - likely miliary Koch’s. The patient was advised with routine blood investigations along with fibre optic bronchoscopy. CBC revealed elevated TLC with marked eosinophilia (36%) with other blood parameters within normal limits. Routine examination of BAL fluid also confirmed similar findings (eosiniphilia) Later on, Total serum IgE was found to be elevated (14539 kUA/l). BAL c/s, fungal KOH and fungal c/s showed sterile reports. BAL CBNAAT was reported as MTB not detected. Serum Microfilaria antigen was sent and showed to be negative but serum antifilarial IgG was seen to be markedly elevated. A diagnosis of Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia was made and patient was started with diethylcarbamazine for 21 days, antibiotics and other supportive medications including bronchodilators. Patient showed significant improvement in symptoms and chest radiograph showed resolution in lesions. Currently, patient is asymptomatic and without any ongoing medications.

Conclusions: Imaging studies in TPE can mimic miliary tuberculosis, the presence of marked eosinophilia is an important distinguishing feature and further management is thereafter differ drastically and has a significant impact on patient overall.

Shivam Gurme, Siddharth Waghmare, Jairaj P. Nair, Sonal Karpe, Swapnil Thorve

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Lokmanya Tilak Medical College and General Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Alveolar silicoproteinosis is a rare form of silicosis that develops rapidly following intense exposure to respirable crystalline silica. It leads to alveolar accumulation of lipoproteinaceous material, causing bilateral lung consolidation, patchy ground-glass opacities, and a characteristic “crazy-paving” appearance on imaging.

Case Report: A 27-year-old male stone cutter with five years of silica exposure presented with progressive dyspnea and intermittent cough with whitish sputum over two years. He reported diffuse bilateral chest pain, non-radiating and unrelated to palpitations or sweating. He had a history of sputum-positive pulmonary tuberculosis, treated with anti-tubercular therapy (ATT) for six months, but self-discontinued a second course initiated after symptom recurrence. On examination, SpO2 was 98%, with bilateral crepitations. Chest X-ray showed dense bilateral opacities sparing the upper zones. CT revealed multiple consolidative masses with air bronchograms, centrilobular nodules, fibrotic changes, and mediastinal lymphadenopathy with egg-shell calcifications. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid was milky, negative for infectious agents, and showed PAS-positive macrophages. The diagnosis was silicoproteinosis with progressive massive fibrosis.

Conclusions: Silicoproteinosis is a pathologic variant of acute silicosis, resembling pulmonary alveolar proteinosis (PAP). It results from silica-induced macrophage dysfunction and type II pneumocyte hyperplasia, leading to proteinaceous alveolar accumulation. Imaging features include centrilobular nodules, consolidations, and lymph node calcifications. The patient’s history of tuberculosis complicated the diagnosis, underscoring the overlap between silicosis, tuberculosis, and silico-tuberculosis in occupations with high silica exposure, such as stone cutting. This case highlights the importance of early recognition and differentiation of these entities to guide appropriate management.

Introduction: Medical thoracoscopy also known as pleuroscopy today remains the gold standard technique for diagnosing and managing undiagnosed exudative pleural effusions.
