Nitesh Goyal, T. G. Ranganath, Ajoy Kumar Behera

Department of Pulmonary, Critical Care and Sleep Medicine, AIIMS, Raipur, Chhattisgarh, India

Introduction: Lung cancer, a leading cause of global cancer mortality, necessitates timely and accurate diagnostic methods. Transthoracic needle biopsy (TTNB) is pivotal for obtaining tissue from peripheral thoracic lesions for histopathology. Ultrasound-guided TTNB (USTTB) offers real-time imaging, cost-efficiency, and safety advantages over CT-guided methods, particularly for immobile or vulnerable patients. This study evaluates the diagnostic efficacy and safety of USTTB for peripheral thoracic lesions.

Methods: A retrospective study at AIIMS Raipur analysed 97 patients undergoing USTTB between October 2022 and March 2024. Data on demographics, clinical presentation, radiological findings, and histopathology were assessed. The Bard Max-Core™ biopsy instrument was utilized. Statistical analysis employed IBM SPSS Version 20.0.

Results: The study cohort had a mean age of 54.9 ± 15.9 years, with 74.23% males. Cough (77.32%) and chest pain (62.89%) were the most common symptoms, while 67.01% patients reported weight loss. Ultrasound findings revealed heterogeneous echotexture in 81.44% of lesions, with intervening pleural effusion in 9 patients. The biopsy procedure involved 4–6 passes, yielding high-quality cores for histopathological evaluation. Diagnostic yield observed was 91.75%, with lung adenocarcinoma (44.94%) and squamous cell carcinoma (17.98%) being the commonest diagnoses. Rare conditions such as pulmonary alveolar proteinosis and diffuse alveolar damage were also identified. Complications included hemoptysis (2.06%), pneumothorax (8.25%) and chest pain (21.65%), all managed conservatively without requiring invasive intervention. Histopathological assessment confirmed adequate tissue sampling in all cases, demonstrating the robustness of the biopsy technique.

Conclusions: USTTB demonstrated high diagnostic accuracy with minimal complications, consistent with prior studies. Its bedside feasibility, cost-effectiveness, and safety profile make it suitable for vulnerable patients and resource-limited settings. Limitations include the retrospective design and lack of a comparative cohort.

USTTB is a safe and effective diagnostic tool for peripheral thoracic lesions. Its integration into diagnostic workflows is recommended, particularly in settings requiring real-time, minimally invasive techniques.

Keywords: Diagnostic yield, lung cancer diagnosis, minimally invasive procedures, peripheral thoracic lesions, safety profile, transthoracic needle biopsy, ultrasound-guided biopsy

Shital Patil1, Ganesh Narwade1, Gajanan Gondhali2, Deepak Patil2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Venkatesh Chest Hospital and Critical Care Center, Latur, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Internal Medicine, MIMSR Medical College, Latur, Maharashtra, India

Background: Non-resolving pneumonia (NRP) has significant impact on cost of care, mortality and morbidity due to incorrect diagnosis due to inadequate samplings, inappropriate and or adequate antibiotics use and impaired host defenses. Tuberculosis is the predominant etiological diagnosis in NRP cases in high burden settings. Bronchoscopy is the most appropriate invasive technique in these cases due to pathological site-oriented samplings with high yield with rapid molecular based Gene Xpert MTB/RIF test.

Methods: Prospective, observational study, included 794 non-resolving pneumonia cases (NRP) (defined as pneumonia with slow or incomplete resolution after twelve weeks in spite of adequate and appropriate antibiotic treatment during hospitalization) admitted in indoor unit during 2015 to 2023. All cases were having inconclusive etiological profile including induced sputum smear AFB and Gene Xpert MTB/RIF tests. Covariates recordings such as Age, gender, smoking history, diabetes mellitus, alcoholism and nutritional status were done. HRCT thorax imaging done in all cases for defining exact pathological site. Bronchoscopy guided BAL has been sent for bacterial, fungal and tuberculosis and malignancy workup. Tuberculosis samplings were processed for Gene Xpert MTB/RIF and Liquid (MGIT 960) culture methods. Statistical analysis by Chi-test and Anova Test.

Results: Etiological diagnosis of 794 NRP cases as TB in 398 (50.12%) cases, Lung malignancy in 156 (19.64%), Bacterial pneumonia 112 (14.10%), fungal pneumonia in 100 (12.59%) and foreign bodies in 16 (2.01%) & undiagnosed in 12 cases. Total TB diagnosis is confirmed in 398 cases, by MTB documentation in Gene Xpert MTB/RIF and or liquid culture yields mycobacterium tuberculosis. BAL Gene Xpert MTB/RIF Positive (very low cycle threshold) in 68 cases and confirmed TB by culture in 42 (61.76%) cases. BAL Gene Xpert MTB/RIF Positive (low to high cycle threshold) in 318 cases and conformed TB by culture in 316 cases. TB diagnosis by BAL cycle threshold (very low and low to high) and Liquid culture methods has showed significant association (p<0.00001). Sensitivity of BAL CBNAAT (in consideration with Culture) is 89.94% in our cases with negative initial induced sputum results. Covariates analysis has observed significant association with TB diagnosis by BAL cycle threshold (very low and low to high) and Liquid culture methods (p<0.00001).

Conclusion: Tuberculosis is the very common and underestimated cause of NRP and usually missed with conventional diagnostic techniques. Bronchoscopy is the gold standard test in workup of NRP and provides etiological diagnosis in near all cases. BAL gene Xpert MTB/RIF is sensitive and specific test to diagnose tuberculosis. Semiquantitative analysis with cycle threshold has given important clues towards to start treatment directly. We recommend for cautious workup in cases with very low MTB load in Gene Xpert MTB/RIF test and these cases should undergo liquid culture techniques to prevent overtreatment.

Shivam Suresh Gurme, Siddharth Waghmare, Jairaj P. Nair, Sonal Karpe, Swapnil Thorve

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Lokmanya Tilak Medical College and General Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Post-tuberculosis obstructive airway disease (post-TB-OAD) is a common sequelae associated with pulmonary TB. Acute exacerbation of post-TB-OAD is characterized by acute worsening of symptoms. Hypoxia and respiratory fatigue are indications for non-invasive ventilation in these cases.

Methods: Sixty patients of Post TB OAD who received NIV were enrolled for HACOR score. HACOR score was calculated 1 hour after NIV application. NIV success was defined as clinical improvement, correction of respiratory acidosis, and no subsequent need for intubation. The need to shift from NIV to the invasive mode of ventilation was defined as NIV failure. A cuffoff score of >5 was considered predictive of NIV failure. Using contingency tables the sensitivity, specificity, negative predictive value and the positive predictive value was calculated.

Result: 60 patients were enrolled in the study. NIV failure was seen in 18 patients. The mean HACOR score in this group was 7. The sensitivity was 77.77%, and the specificity was 28 % for a score of >5. The positive predictive value and negative predictive value were 31% and 75 %, respectively.

Conclusions: In our study, we tried to assess the effectiveness of the HACOR score in predicting NIV failure in patients with post-TB-OAD. Previous study conducted by Duan et al revealed sensitivity of 72.6% and specificity of 90.2%. Another study by Waghmare SR et al had Sensitivity 89.47% and Specificity of 87.09%. Compared to these our study showed comparable sensitivity (77.77%), however specificity was low.

We Found a score more than 5 had a higher NPV and sensitivity in predicting NIV failure.

Keywords: HACOR, NIV, post TB OAD

Sanjay Kumar, Pranjali Atrish

1Sanjay Gandhi Post Graduate Institute of Medical Sciences, Lucknow, Uttar Pradesh, India

Introduction: Desmoplastic small round cell tumor (DSRCT) is a rare and highly aggressive malignancy, predominantly affecting adolescents and young adults. It arises from serosal surfaces, with common primary sites including the abdomen, ovary, and orbit. The tumor is driven by the EWS-WT1 fusion gene, leading to oncogene upregulation.[2] Treatment typically includes chemotherapy, radiation, and cytoreductive surgery, but severe complications, such as hematologic toxicity and pulmonary complications, are common. This case highlights the successful management of a 3-year-old child with DSRCT who developed pulmonary edema and hemorrhage following chemotherapy, treated with high-frequency positive pressure ventilation (HFPPV), corticosteroids, and antifibrinolytic agents.

Case Series: A 3-year-old child with no significant past medical history presented with abdominal distension, constipation, and intermittent fever. Imaging revealed a mass in the right lumbar region, confirmed as DSRCT through biopsy. The tumor involved the perimuscular connective tissue, serosal surface, and lymph nodes. Following exploratory surgery and mass resection, the patient underwent chemotherapy (VDC-IE regimen). Ten days later, the child developed pancytopenia and febrile neutropenia, requiring blood transfusions. Respiratory distress, tachypnea, and tachycardia led to ICU admission, where pulmonary edema was diagnosed via ultrasound and chest X-ray. Despite initial treatments, including diuretics and transfusions, the patient progressed to respiratory failure and required intubation, from which he was successfully weaned and extubated in 3 days. On day 2 post extubation, the patient experienced a worsening respiratory condition, requiring reintubation due to massive pulmonary hemorrhage. High-frequency positive pressure ventilation was initiated to maintain ventilation. The ventilatory settings were adjusted to pressure control with a respiratory rate of 90/min, a pressure control of 30 cmH2O, a positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) of 10 cmH2O, and 100% FiO2. Tranexamic acid and Methylprednisolone (20 mg/kg/day) was initiated and slowly tapered both of which helped resolve pulmonary hemorrhage and successfully weaning the child from ventilator and ultimately being extubated.

Conclusions: This case emphasizes the challenges in managing DSRCT complicated by pulmonary edema and hemorrhage. The use of HFPPV, corticosteroids, and tranexamic acid played a key role in stabilizing the patient’s respiratory function and enabling subsequent treatment. Early intervention with this approach significantly improved the patient’s prognosis, highlighting the importance of tailored management strategies for pediatric oncology patients with severe complications.

Aditya Padmakar Adhikari, L. Muthukumaran, R. Sridhar, N. Meenakshi

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Chettinad Hospital and Research Institute, Kelambakkam, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: Invasive mechanical ventilation (IMV) is a life-saving intervention for critically ill ICU patients but is associated with high morbidity and mortality depending on underlying conditions. Understanding clinical outcomes in these patients aids in identifying prognostic factors and guides improvement in ICU care.

Objective: To evaluate of Clinical Outcome of Mechanically Ventilated Patient in Tertiary Care Hospital.

Methods: A retrospective study of ICU patients at Chettinad Hospital requiring IMV. Adults with inadequate respiratory drive or unresponsive to oxygen therapy were included. Patients extubated and transferred to the ward were classified as having a “good” outcome. Those who died or were discharged against medical advice without extubation were categorized as “adverse.”

Results: 110 patients were analyzed (mean age 61.01±14.32 years, 71.8% male). The mean ICU stay was 9.00±5.35 days, ventilation duration was 6.00±3.96 days, and hospital stay was 18.00±8.89 days. Respiratory issues were the leading cause of IMV (44.10%), followed by cardiac (20.20%), neurological (15.80%), non-cardiac (14.50%), and sepsis (9.40%). Of the patients, 65.40% had a good outcome, while 34.60% had adverse outcomes. ICU mortality was 20%, with 14.6% dying post-transfer. There was statistically significant difference in outcome of patients with respiratory causes as compared to other causes.(p=0.03). In multivariable regression analysis concerning medical diagnoses, multi-organ dysfunction syndrome were statistically positively correlated with mortality, with a p-value of less than 0.05.

Conclusions: Mechanical ventilation is a life-saving intervention; prompt detection of problems and appropriate treatment significantly reduce morbidity. A considerable proportion of patients admitted to the ICU necessitate breathing assistance through invasive mechanical ventilation. with mortality being highest in those suffering from multi-organ dysfunction syndrome.

Early intervention and targeted care for high-risk groups improve outcomes, highlighting the importance of continuous ICU practice evaluation to reduce mortality.

Gaurav Mishra, Rahul Tyagi

Department of Respiratory Medicine, AFMC, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Weaning from prolonged invasive mechanical ventilation (> 7 days of IMV) presents significant clinical challenges, particularly in patients with complex comorbidities. This case series evaluates the clinical management and outcomes of 40 patients requiring prolonged IMV, with a focus on survival, challenges encountered, and strategies employed in a tertiary respiratory centre in western Maharashtra.

Methods: A retrospective analysis of 40 patients aged 58–87 years requiring prolonged IMV was conducted. Underlying conditions included bronchiectasis-COPD overlap, severe pneumonia with ARDS, massive pulmonary embolism, and chronic structural lung diseases. Data regarding patients who underwent tracheostomy or managed with multidisciplinary interventions, including physiotherapy, nutritional support, antimicrobial therapy, and psychosocial care were recorded and analyzed with their final outcome.

Results: Of the 40 patients requiring prolonged mechanical ventilation, 12 (30%) survived and were successfully decannulated. Out of 12 patients who were successfully weaned off, 07 (17.5%) are still on OPD follow up. The average hospital stay was 48 days (range: 30–72 days), with ventilator support lasting an average of 14 days and tracheostomy duration averaging 42 days (range: 18–95 days). Key interventions included managing multidrug-resistant infections, such as MDR Klebsiella, Pseudomonas, and Burkholderia, through culture-guided antibiotic therapy, addressing electrolyte imbalances like hypokalemia/hyponatremia, and providing continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) for acute kidney injury. Survivors benefited from structured weaning protocols, including early transition to non-invasive ventilation, T-piece trials, physiotherapy-driven ambulation, employment of relatives for early psychological support and early oral feeding initiation, and. These strategies were critical in facilitating recovery and achieving decannulation.

Conclusions: Prolonged ventilation remains a significant clinical challenge, with a survival rate of 37.5% in this cohort. Successful weaning necessitates a multidisciplinary approach involving early identification of complications, infection control, robust rehabilitation, and psychosocial support. This series highlights the need for individualized care pathways to optimize outcomes in prolonged IMV cases.

Keywords: Multidisciplinary care, prolonged mechanical ventilation, weaning and decannulation

Introduction: Pulmonary AV malformations are rare abnormal dilated vessels leading to abnormal connections between pulmonary arteries and veins right to left shunt. They are direct high flow, low resistance fistulous connection. Here we present a young female patient with avm with chronic hemoptysis refractory to conservative management.

Case Report: Using 6FR catheter vascular access was made and H1 catheter was used to cannulate right EIV and common iliac vein. Later right bronchial artery was superselectively embolized and cyanoacrylate was injected into AVM.

Patient was stable and she did not develop further hemoptysis after procedure on follow up.

Conclusions: BAE reduces the recurrence of hemoptysis 70-90%. In massive hemotysis, chronic hemoptysis refractory to conservative treatment prompt decision has to be made to treat with bronchial artery embolization.

BAE has to be considered as a mainstay of treatment in patient of AV malformation, refractory hemoptysis, massive hemoptysis.

Kumar Doshi1, Salil Bendre1, Kavita Mehta1, Anjali Kamath1, Sameer Shah2, Hardik Shah2

1Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Nanavati Max Superspeciality Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Gastroenterology, Nanavati Max Superspeciality Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Transarterial Radioembolization (TARE) is an effective intra-arterial treatment for liver cancers, especially hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), that employs radioactive microspheres containing yttrium-90 (90Y). However, TARE has possible side effects, such as radiation-induced lung damage, frequently observed in patients with a large hepatopulmonary shunt, higher than 20%.[2] However, cases involving lower lung shunt fractions remain rare and poorly understood.

Case Report: A 69-year-old male, case of Hepatocellular Carcinoma with portal hypertension, presented with complaints of breathlessness and dry cough for 20 days. His lung shunt fraction was 9%. The patient had undergone Transarterial Chemoembolization (TACE) with 50mg Adriamycin 30 days ago followed by TARE. His symptoms began five days after TARE. He denied fever or chest pain and was a known diabetic. Prior to treatment, the patient’s HRCT thorax showed no parenchymal lesions. However, a repeat imaging revealed ground-glass opacities in both the upper lobes, middle lobe, and lingula, along with smooth interstitial septal thickening in upper lobes. Based on clinico-radiological findings, the patient was started on intravenous steroids, initially with methylprednisolone 40mg. Despite treatment, his oxygen requirement increased. Repeat imaging showed an increase in the ground-glass opacities. With ongoing clinicoradiological deterioration, the steroid dose was escalated to 80mg/day, and a diagnostic bronchoscopy was performed which showed no growth in lavage cultures. Pulse high dose steroid therapy was initiated and patient showed significant improvement in clinical parameters.

Conclusion: This case highlights that radiation-induced lung injury from TARE can occur even with a lung shunt fraction as low as 9%, which is below the accepted threshold of 20% for significant risk. This underlines the importance of closely monitoring patients undergoing TARE, as lung toxicity may develop despite a relatively low risk profile based on traditional shunt fraction thresholds. Early recognition and timely intervention are crucial for mitigating the severity of pulmonary complications in these patients.

Yash Kedia1, Nitesh Gupta1, Pranav Ish1, Rohit Kumar2, Ankur Jain3

1Department of Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine, VMMC and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India, 2Department of Pulmonary Medicine, VMMC and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India, 3Department of Hematology, VMMC and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India

Pulmonary Langerhans cell Histiocytosis (PLCH) presents as a cystic lung disease, causing bilateral bizarre shaped cysts, which often rupture and cause pneumothorax. PLCH can present with progressive lung disease leading to declining lung functions and worsening quality of life. Treatment options include smoking cessation, long term oral corticosteroids and use of chemo toxic drugs like vinblastine, cladribine and cytarabine. However, there is no consensus for the use of one drug over the other. We here present a case of a 22-year-old non-smoker male, who presented with secondary spontaneous pneumothorax and was diagnosed to have PLCH along with diabetes insipidus. He was treated with oral corticosteroids and subcutaneous cytarabine 5 days per month for one year. Follow up at one year was suggestive of preserved lung functions (on spirometry and six-minute walk test) along with regression of cysts on computed tomography scans of the thorax.

G. Mohan Siva Sundaram, Girish Dandagi, Sandeep Dandin, Santosh Honnavar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Belagavi Institute of Medical Sciences, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Granulomatosis with polyangitis (GPA) is a rare ANCA- associated vasculitis, with a prevalence of about 3 per 100,000 individuals. It is characterized by necrotizing granulomatous inflammation usually involving respiratory tract, and necrotizing vasculitis affecting small and medium sized vessels. GPA can affect individuals of any age, frequently involving upper airways, lungs and kidneys, though it can affect any organ.

Case Report: A 19 yr old male, software employee trainee by occupation, with no co- morbidies presented with chest pain, fever and dry cough since 15 days. Investigations revealed positive C- ANCA with CXR showing multiple nodules with cavitations, HRCT shows multiple areas of lobular, subsegmental consolidation with ground glass opacities diffusely involving both lungs with multiple areas of internal cavitation and lung biopsy showing chronic inflammatory changes. He was treated with prednisolone and methotrexate, leading to clinical and radiographic improvement.

Conclusions: This case underscores considering GPA as differential diagnosis in patients with respiratory symptoms with B/L cavitatory changes. Timely diagnosis and treatment are crucial for managing GPA effectively, highlighting the need for vigilance in recognizing this rare but serious condition.

Granulomatosis with polyangitis should be considered in differential diagnosis of patients presenting with respiratory symptoms. Early diagnosis is critical, as high-dose steroids and cytotoxic agents is essential for improving patient outcomes and managing this potentially life threatening condition.

1Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Ruby Hall Clinic, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Cavitary form of cryptogenic Organizing Pneumonia (COP) is a type of idiopathic interstitial pneumonia characterized by the formation of granulation tissue within the lung’s airspaces and/or interstitium. Only 4 cases have been published so far, ours being 5th.Thick-walled cavitary form is still rarer and which may mimic malignancy.

Case Report: A 62-year-old male, ex-smoker presented with persistent symptoms of dry cough and breathlessness of 6 months duration, denied history of fever, haemoptysis and weight loss. Chest x ray showed a thick-walled cavity in right upper zone. PET CT showed FDG avid (SUV max 20.2) heterogeneously enhancing cavity lesion in right upper lobe with wall thickness of 20 mm, likely of neoplastic pathology. CT guided biopsy was done from thick wall of the cavity. Histology was suggestive of Organizing pneumonia. Other causes of COP like GPA, sarcoidosis, RA, eosinophilic pneumonia was excluded. Our final diagnosis was cavitary form of COP.

Conclusion: Thick walled Cavitary form of COP can mimic malignancy, particularly when wall thickness is more than 5 mm. Organizing pneumonia presenting as thick wall cavity is rare. It should be considered as differential diagnosis of lung cavitary lesions.

Keywords: Cavitary form of cryptogenic organizing pneumonia, CT guided biopsy, neoplastic, wall thickness

Vedamurthy Basavaraja Janglera, D. Pragati Rao

Department of Respiratory Medicine, M S Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Cryptogenic Organising Pneumonia (COP) is a critical diagnosis due to its clinical and radiological similarities with infectious pneumonia, COP is often misdiagnosed. An accurate diagnosis of COP is essential because the treatment approach differs significantly from other forms of pneumonia.

Case Report: A 63year old lady known case of diabetes, hypertension and hypothyroidism presented with 20days history of low-grade intermittent fever and cough. On examination vitals were stable and left infra scapular crepitations heard. Chest x-ray and HRCT thorax showed bilateral lower lobe consolidation (L>R). In view of persistent fever spikes and non-resolving pneumonia further underwent bronchoscopy and BAL. BAL gram stain, culture, MGIT and fungal elements were negative. For further work up, patient underwent CT guided biopsy which showed many fibrotic plugs filling the alveolar sacs and ducts (Masson bodies), PAS and GMS stains were negative for fungal elements, ZN stain negative for AFB and no features of malignancy. A diagnosis of COP was made and started on Oral Corticosteroids (0.5mg/kg) and tapered on follow up. On follow up after 1 month a repeat HRCT was done showed complete radiological resolution.

Conclusion: Prompt recognition of COP is essential given its nonspecific symptoms and potential for misdiagnosis. Our patient demonstrated a favourable response to corticosteroid therapy. Long-term follow-up will be crucial to monitor for disease recurrence or complications.

Mili Gupta, Pramod Chaudhari, Anubhuti, Makarand Sanpurkar, Tushar Aneja

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Bharati Vidyapeeth (DTU) Medical College and Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: PAVMS are rare but crucial in the differential diagnosis of pulmonary issues like dyspnea, hemoptysis, hypoxemia and nodules. Complication include life- threatening conditions like stroke, brain abscess, and severe hemoptysis.

Case Report: A 58-year-old female patient was admitted with seizure, frothing at the mouth, and transient left hemiparesis. MRI identified a brain abscess and conservative treatment with cephalosporins and antiepileptics was started. Despite this, the patient had another seizure and was referred to our center. CXR revealed a well- defined opacity in the right lower lobe. Subsequent CT pulmonary angiography confirmed pulmonary arteriovenous malformations with aneurysms in the right middle and lower lobes. A 2D echocardiogram excluded valvular vegetations and atrial septal defect while bubble contrast study verified right to left shunting, confirming the presence of PAVMS. Dermatological examination found multiple asymptomatic ecchymotic lesions and past history of recurrent epistaxis was suggestive of hereditary hemorrhagic telangiectasia (HHT), also known as Osler -Weber – Rendu syndrome. The patient underwent successful embolization of three PAVMS using amplatzer vascular plugs and coil embolization using catheter, which stopped blood flow.

Conclusions: PAVM symptoms typically appear in middle aged adults. Studies show only 40% are symptomatic, primarily with dyspnea and hemoptysis. When associated with HHT, it can manifest as epistaxis and telangiectasias. They should be suspected in patients with unexplained dyspnea, hypoxemia, nodules, or a history of stroke or brain abscess.

Understanding the potential for neurological complications, including strokes and brain abscesses due to paradoxical embolization, is critical. Routine screening of all patients with brain abscess for ACM will improve outcomes in this patient population.

Shubham Lohade

Vedantaa Institute of Medical Sciences, Dahanu, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia (LIP) is a rare pulmonary complication of Sjogren’s syndrome (SS), characterised by diffuse infiltration of lymphocytes in the pulmonary interstitium. This case highlights a patient with SS presenting with significant respiratory symptoms, emphasising the diagnostic and therapeutic challenges.

Case Report: A 56-year-old female with a known diagnosis of Sjogren’s syndrome presented with breathlessness of MMRC grade 3 and productive cough for two weeks. She denied fever, chest pain, pedal oedema, or hemoptysis. On examination, the patient was tachypneic with oxygen saturation of 90%. Systemic examination was unremarkable, but bilateral crackles and wheeze were noted on auscultation. Routine blood work, sputum cultures, and other infectious workups were negative. High-resolution computed tomography (HRCT) revealed bilateral ground-glass opacities (GGOs), multiple thin-walled cysts, diffuse interstitial thickening, and sub-pleural consolidation. These findings, combined with the clinical presentation and absence of alternative causes, led to a diagnosis of LIP secondary to Sjogren’s syndrome. The patient was managed with a combination of antibiotics, oral corticosteroids, inhalational bronchodilators, and oxygen therapy. Clinical improvement was noted, and she was discharged in stable condition with outpatient follow-up arranged.

Conclusions: Lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia should be considered in patients with Sjogren’s syndrome presenting with unexplained respiratory symptoms. Prompt diagnosis using clinical, radiological, and laboratory findings is critical for appropriate management and improved outcomes.

This case underscores the importance of recognising LIP as a potential manifestation of Sjogren’s syndrome. HRCT is instrumental in diagnosis, and a multidisciplinary approach is crucial for optimal management. Early intervention with corticosteroids and supportive therapy can result in favourable outcomes.

Deepak Muthreja1, Shital Patil2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, American Oncology Institute, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Respiratory Medicine, Venkatesh Chest Hospital and Critical Care Center, Latur, Maharashtra, India

Background: Interstitial lung disease (ILD) encompasses a diverse group of chronic pulmonary disorders characterized by progressive scarring of lung tissue, leading to impaired gas exchange and respiratory function. These conditions can significantly impact quality of life, often resulting in considerable morbidity and mortality. Early and accurate diagnosis is essential for initiating appropriate treatment and improving patient outcomes. However, ILD diagnosis is frequently delayed due to its nonspecific symptoms, which often overlap with more common respiratory conditions, such as asthma or chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). The delay in diagnosing ILD can result in advanced disease progression at the time of diagnosis, limiting therapeutic options and contributing to poorer prognosis. Factors contributing to these delays may include patient-related issues, such as delayed healthcare- seeking behavior; healthcare system challenges, such as inadequate access to specialized diagnostic services; and clinician-related barriers, including limited familiarity with ILD and its atypical presentations.

Methods: Prospective, observational study in two tertiary care centers, American Oncology Institute, Nagpur & Venkatesh Chest Hospital and critical care center, Latur India, included 100 cases of interstitial lung diseases diagnosed by HRCT thorax cases. The protocolized medical history & covariates such as age, gender, smoking history, occupation, history of malignancy, or chemotherapeutic drug exposure and alternative diagnosis such as COPD, Asthma, Tuberculosis and pneumonia made before final diagnosis were recorded.

Results: In a cohort of 100 patients with ILD, the average time to diagnosis was observed to be 2.2 years following the onset of symptoms. Notably, the most common initial misdiagnosis were bilateral pneumonia (40%), COPD/asthma (30%), and tuberculosis (30%). These findings highlight the diagnostic challenges posed by ILD, as its early symptoms are often mistaken for more prevalent respiratory conditions.

Conclusion: Understanding the duration and causes of diagnostic delays in ILD is crucial for developing strategies to enhance early recognition and timely intervention. This study aims to further investigate the time elapsed between symptom onset and ILD diagnosis, as well as to identify key factors contributing to these delays. By addressing these gaps, the findings of this study may guide efforts to improve diagnostic pathways and ultimately optimize care for individuals with ILD.

Jigneshkumar M. Patel, Anisha Rajvanshi, Nisha Chandra, Zarir F. Udwadia

Department of Respiratory Medicine, P. D. Hinduja Hospital and Medical Research Center, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Antisynthetase syndrome (ASyS) is a rare autoimmune disease that commonly presents as interstitial lung disease (ILD), myositis, and arthritis. Disease progression, particularly in ILD, significantly affects morbidity and mortality, requiring timely diagnosis and personalized treatment, including novel therapies like rituximab.

Case Report: A 28-year-old female presented with a history of small and large joint pain at age 18, which resolved spontaneously. At age 22, she developed papular rashes on her forearms and nodular lesions on her fingertips, initially misdiagnosed as eczema and treated with steroids. At age 23, she reported myalgia, low-grade fever, dry cough, and joint pain. Investigations showed nonspecific interstitial pneumonia (NSIP) ILD on HRCT. Autoimmune markers were positive for ANA, anti-PL7, and anti-Ro52, with elevated inflammatory markers and CPK. She was diagnosed with ASyS and started on steroids and azathioprine, which was switched to tacrolimus due to intolerance within a few weeks. At age 24, due to worsening joint symptoms and muscle pain, methotrexate was added. At age 25, she presented to our hospital with worsening symptoms, increased GGO on HRCT, and declining FVC and DLCO, prompting the addition of mycophenolate mofetil and discontinuation of tacrolimus. At age 26, with persistent breathlessness, fibrotic NSIP on CT, and further decline in FVC and DLCO, nintedanib and rituximab were initiated. Three doses of rituximab (1 g each) were administered: the first two doses two weeks apart, and the third dose after 6 months. This led to symptom improvement, highlighting its potential in refractory ASyS-ILD.

Conclusion: Rituximab offers a promising option for patients with refractory ASyS-ILD, particularly when standard therapies fail. Its timely initiation in this case stabilized disease progression and improved symptom control, underscoring its importance in managing refractory cases.

Sweta Das, Divya Balan

Department of Respiratory Medicine, KMC, Manipal, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Pancoast Tobias Syndrome is a rare condition primarily caused by bronchogenic carcinoma which accounts for less than 5% of all bronchogenic carcinomas. Small cell carcinoma at this site is particularly rare. These tumours arise in the lung apex, invading nearby structures including brachial plexus and sympathetic nervous system. The syndrome is characterized by symptoms including ipsilateral shoulder and arm pain, muscle weakness, paraesthesia, and Horner’s syndrome.

Case Report: A 70-year-old male with smoking index of 350 with a 10-year history of gradually progressive dyspnoea and cough with new onset right upper limb pain and weakness for the past two months. Examination showed grade 2 clubbing, bilateral inspiratory rhonchi, and right-hand muscle wasting, reduced power and reduced sensation. He had right eye partial ptosis and ipsilateral miosis. CT showed right upper lobe lesion with invasion of surrounding structures including the brachial plexus, ribs, vertebrae, and subclavian vessels. Biopsy suggestive of poorly differentiated neuroendocrine tumour.

Conclusions: Pancoast tumours, though rare, should be suspected in patients with upper limb pain, weakness, and Horner’s syndrome, particularly in heavy smokers. Early diagnosis and a multidisciplinary approach are key to improving outcomes.

Pancoast tumours typically present with neck and upper limb pain due to brachial plexus involvement. Risk factors include smoking, male gender, and elderly. Diagnosis requires imaging and biopsy. Treatment includes chemoradiation and surgery, with palliative care for advanced cases.

Keywords: Bronchogenic carcinoma, neuroendocrine tumour, pancoast tumour

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Lokmanya Tilak Medical College and General Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Drowning is a leading cause of accidental death worldwide. However, the presence of underlying pathologies, such as lung malignancies, can complicate the clinical presentation and management of drowning This case highlights the rare but critical coexistence of lung malignancy and drowning.Non-small cell lung carcinoma (NSCLC) is the most common type of lung cancer, accounting for approximately 85% of all cases. Case Report: 62 yr old male transferred from pvt hospital after 7 days of A/H/O fresh water drowning under influence of alcohol with DOE grade II -III since drowning and low grade fever and cough since 3 days patient was intubated in pvt hospital for two days.k/c/o DM and HTN.patient is bidi smoker 1 bundle /day since 30 yr. sputum c/s Ecoli grew.appropriate antibiotics and NIV support given.after 10 days of treatment no resolution of consolidation on cxr so CECT chest done suggestive of B/L consolidation with thrombus in SVC. Conclusion: This case report highlights the incidental discovery of lung cancer in a patient with drowning. Bronchoscoy TBLB revealed the presence of an undiagnosed lung malignancy, which was not clinically suspected during the patient’s lifetime.this emphasizes the need for increased awareness of lung cancer and it’s screening that may be asymptomatic or overlooked until advanced stages.

Nemi Chand Garg

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Patients presenting with lung abscess may have an associated malignancy in 7-36% cases.There should always be a high index of suspicion for lung cancer whenever there is a radiographic diagnosis suggesting lung abscess especially in patients with associated risk factors(higher age,smoking,wall thickness >15 mm,irregular speculated margin) and unresponsive cases to ensure early diagnosis and initiation of treatment.

Case Report: Here is a case of 58 yr old male who came with the following complaints- right sided chest pain radiating towards back dull aching in nature since 1year, cough with mild expectoration (whitish in colour) since 9 months. Patient had multiple history of hospital admission first in October 2023 where bronchoscopy was done which revealed no endobronchial growth, following which patient was again admitted in December 2023 and a diagnosis of tubercular lung abscess (clinically diagnosed) with superadded infection with staphylococcus aureus. Patient was started on antitubercular therapy and sensitive antibiotics for staph aureus (cefepime and linezolid). Patient was reassessed and diagnosis felt more in favour of malignancy therefore a repeat FNAC was done which revealed well differentiated squamous cell carcinoma and the patient was referred to the department of radio-oncology for further management.

Discussion: Malignancy can itself lead in abscess formation in multiple ways. (1) Lung cancer can cause endobronchial obstruction which may result in post obstructive pneumonia which may progress to abscess formation. (2) Squamous cell carcinoma is well known to undergo cavitation which may be followed by abscess formation.

Conclusion: Thus physicians should have a high suspicion for lung cancer when there is a radiographic diagnosis of suspicious lung abscess. Proper diagnostic evaluation and repetitive cytology is necessary so as not to delay an underlying diagnosis of lung cancer.

Yadlapalli Vasmitha Chowdary, A.Vinay Kumar

Department: Pulmonary Medicine, Chalmeda Anand Rao Institute of Medical Sciences, Karimnagar, India

Background: Nocardia, a genus of Gram-positive bacteria, thrives in aerobic conditions. Infection typically occurs through inhalation into the respiratory tract. Often referred to as “the great masquerader,” Nocardia has a remarkable ability to mimic tuberculosis in both clinical symptoms and imaging characteristics. The situation becomes even more complex when co-infection with Nocardia and MTB occurs, presenting a heightened challenge that demands careful attention and prompt intervention.

Case Study: A 60-year-old male presented with a cough with sputum and SOB for the past 3 months, along with a headache for the last 2 days. The patient has a history of pulmonary tuberculosis and was on ATT for 18 months. He has been using inhalers for 3 years. chest revealed bilateral fibronodular opacities. An MRI of the brain showed multiple conglomerate ring-enhancing lesions in the left occipital lobe, hippocampus, and right temporo-occipital lobe, with perilesional edema. Bronchoscopy was performed, BAL sent for modified ZN stain revealed Nocardia species and CBNAAT positive for MTB.

Discussion: Approximately two-thirds of all primary Nocardia spp. Infections affect the lungs .In our case, the patient initially presented with respiratory symptoms, followed by the onset of headache. Inhalation was likely the route of infection. CNS is the second most frequent site of infection, typically through dissemination from the primary pulmonary focus. The importance of microscopic examination cannot be overstated, as the presence of branched, unevenly colored Gram-positive bacilli is strongly indicative of Nocardia sspp. Advanced diagnostic techniques like MALDI-TOF MS and 16S rRNA gene analysis are instrumental in confirming the presence of Nocardia. Given that clinical signs are often non-specific, distinguishing pulmonary nocardiosis from tuberculosis can be challenging. A confirmed diagnosis of tuberculosis helps eliminate other potential pathogens.

Conclusion: Patients face higher morbidity and mortality following opportunistic infections caused by pulmonary pathogens like Nocardia and MTB. Consequently, the early detection of these pathogens, coupled with the prompt selection of appropriate antimicrobial therapy, can drastically improve patient outcomes and prognosis.

Keywords: Disseminated Nocardia, MTB, immunocompromised, Microbiological isolation.

M. Anjali, Banani Jena

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, IMS and SUM Hospital, Bhubaneswar, Odisha, India

To report a rare case of paraskeletal plasmacytoma manifesting as a chest wall mass and to emphasize its clinical presentation, diagnostic approach, and treatment challenges.

Methods: A case report approach was used to document the clinical history, diagnostic imaging (chest radiograph, CECT), laboratory results, and histopathological evaluation of a chest wall mass. Treatment included a triplet chemotherapy regimen (bortezomib, lenalidomide, and dexamethasone).

Case Report: A 60yr old male presented with right-sided chest pain and a localized chest wall swelling. Imaging revealed a homogenously enhancing soft tissue mass associated with rib fractures and lytic skeletal lesions [Figures 1 and 2]. Histopathology confirmed plasmacytoma with immature plasma cells [Figure 3]. Diagnostic studies also showed monoclonal gammopathy and extensive bone marrow involvement. Despite initiating chemotherapy, the patient succumbed to the disease after one cycle.

Chest radiograph showing homogenous opacity involving right mid and lower zones

CECT of the thorax (axial image) showing a homogenously enhancing soft issue density lesion measuring 8.7 cm × 6.1 cm in the right anterior chest wall along with pathological fracture in the anterior aspect of the fifth rib

Haematoxylin and eosin stained biopsy specimen from chest wall mass revealing the presence of neoplastic plasma cells having immature chromatin and atypical mitosis. Few plasma cells are binucleated. The cells arranged in nests and sheets intervened by blood vessels (original magnification ×400)

Conclusion: Paraskeletal plasmacytoma is a rare manifestation of multiple myeloma with poor prognosis. Differentiating paraskeletal plasmacytoma from extramedullary disease is essential for treatment planning. This case highlights the challenges of managing aggressive presentations of multiple myeloma.

Niladri Palit, Soumya Bhattacharjee

Department of Respiratory Medicine, R G Kar Medical College and Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: In bronchogenic carcinoma with pleural effusion cases, presentation and clinical features are different [in terms of severity and grading] in case of cytologicaly positive and cytologically negative cases.There are some paramalgnant causes of pleural effusion, in a diagnosed carcinoma case, where cause of effusion is not malignancy itself, we tried to find out those causes.

Methodology: This is a descriptive observational study conducted in patients with newly diagnosed bronchogenic carcinoma with pleural efussion admitted in department of respiratory medicine, rgkar medical college in alloted time frame.

Results: In 60% cases pleural fluid malignant cell positive. Among cytologicaly positive 5% were trasudative rest exudative, 70% heamorrhagic efussion. Among cytologicaly negative, 3 cases (5%) were later diagnosed as malignancy by thoracoscopic biopsy. CYTOLOGICALY positive cases presented with relatively large efussion and more severe symptomps, than cytologically negative cases. In cytologicaly positive cases there were underlying visible mass in 65% cases and enlarged mediatinal node in 35% cases on chest radigraphy, which is relatively higher than cytologicaly negative cases, 25% and 5% respctively. In 45% of malignant cell positive efussion cases, there was distant metastasis, predominantly in liver (90%). Cytologicaly negative cases later diagnosed with tissue biopsy in 40% cases, 15% cases were diagnosed by fob guided biopsy. adenocarcinoma (40%), squamous cell carcinoma (30%) were the leading cause. In 40% of cytologicaly negative cases, cause of efussion found to be paramalignant, among which 15% were due to associated pulmonary oedema, 15% were due to liver dysfunction, 35% cases were pulmonary infections (consolidation with parapneumonic efussion), 10% were due to lymphatic bockage, 5% due to ckd, in rest of cytologicaly negative cases, causes remain undiagnosed.

Conclusion: Pleural efussion in bronchogenic carcinoma cases is not always due to malignancy itself. in this study we have seen melignant cell can only be demostrated in about half of the malignancy cases with pleural efussion. But when malignant cell is found in pleural fluid, disease is more severe. Among half of the cytolgicaly negative pleural efussion in bronchogenic carcinoma cases causes of efussion are paramalignant like, pneumonia with parapneumonic efussion> pulmonary oedema>liver dysfuntion>lymphatic obstruction>CKD.

Keywords: Bronchogenic carcinoma with pleural effusion, malignant cells, paramalignant effusion

N. Nagpavan, D. Pragati Rao, F. Tanisha Saleem

Department of Respiratory Medicine, M S Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Sarcomatoid carcinoma of the lung is a rare, high-grade, poorly differentiated, non-small cell cancer. The incidence of sarcoma is 0.3% to 1.3% of all lung cancers. According to the WHO classification, there are five histological variants of sarcomatoid carcinoma, among them is spindle cell carcinoma of the lung with rare histological subtypes.

Case Report: A 30 year old female who presented with one month of breathlessness on exertion, cough and left-sided chest pain. Contrast chest CT unveiled a large, heterogeneously enhancing left lung mass with massive pleural effusion, suggestive of neoplasm. Smaller lung lesions hinted at metastasis. CT guided lung biopsy showed features are of spindle cell neoplasm. Known case of Left Lower Limb sarcoma status post radiotherapy.

Conclusions: Sarcomatoid carcinoma of the lung, though rare, should be considered in patients presenting with aggressive pulmonary symptoms and masses, particularly in those with a history of sarcoma or prior radiotherapy. Its high-grade and poorly differentiated nature underscores the importance of early recognition, accurate histological diagnosis and a multidisciplinary approach to management to improve outcomes.

Pulmonary spinal cell carcinoma is a rare and aggressive malignancy. Initial presentation may be chest pain, cough, shortness of breath or hemoptysis. Treatment involves a multidisciplinary approach, including surgery, radiation therapy and chemotherapy. Prognosis can be grim due to its propensity for metastasis and resistance to treatment. Research into targeted therapies and immunotherapy is ongoing, offering hope for improved outcomes in the future. Vigilant surveillance, early intervention and personalized treatment plans are essential in managing this challenging disease.

Umesh Kulkarni

B. J. Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Systemic sclerosis (Ssc) is chronic multisystem disease characterized by wide spread vascular dysfunction and progressive fibrosis of skin and internal organs. The diagnosis of SSc is based primarily upon the presence of characteristic clinical findings and supported by specific serologic abnormalities. SSc is usually associated with pulmonary manifestations like Pulmonary Hypertension, Interstitial Lung disease and less commonly malignancy.

Case Report: A 30year old female came to OPD with complaints of Fever and Hip pain for the past 15 days. Patient was diagnosed as a case of clinically diagnosed Pulmonary tuberculosis from outside hospital and received 5 months of Anti-Tubercular Therapy. Patient had history of difficulty in opening mouth, dyspnoea on exertion and dry cough with constitutional symptoms for past 7 months. On clinical examination Clubbing (Grade 2) was present, digital ulcers were present, along with Fish mouth appearance and pinched nose. PET scan revealed pleural based soft tissue mass in right lower lobe. ANA-IF had 4+ speckled pattern. CT guided biopsy of right iliac region s/o metastatic deposits of adenocarcinoma.

Conclusion: SSc is one of the common autoimmune condition that has a high predisposition for malignancy. Patients with rheumatic disease should receive age and sex appropriate screening for malignancy as well as screening appropriate to individual risk factors that are present This case highlights the importance of microbiological confirmation of Tuberculosis before treatment initiation even in high prevalence country like India.

Madamanchi Yamini Sri Varun Teja, Vishak Acharya

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Kasturba Medical College, Manipal Academy of Higher Education, Mangalore, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Visualization of pleura on bronchoscopy often indicates severe structural compromise, thinning out erosion and destruction of lung parenchyma and bronchial walls, due to multiple etiologies varying from infections, inflammations, trauma or even malignancies. Squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) of the lung is a common histological subtype of non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), frequently associated with smoking. Bronchoscopic evaluation plays a vital role in diagnosing SCC, offering direct visualization of endobronchial abnormalities and facilitating tissue sampling.

Case Report: A 62-year-old male, ex-smoker and known case of chronic kidney disease, diabetes mellitus, dysthymia, and ischemic heart disease, presented with complaints of exertional breathlessness, cough with occasional hemoptysis, and intermittent fever for two weeks. A chest X-ray revealed a cavity in the right lower lobe. The patient underwent bronchoscopy, which demonstrated distorted architecture in the right lung with visualization of the visceral pleura. A biopsy from the surrounding tissue confirmed squamous cell carcinoma.

Conclusion: This case report illustrates a rare presentation of squamous cell carcinoma of the lung with a cavitary lesion and visualization of the visceral pleura on bronchoscopy. The findings emphasize the critical role of a systematic diagnostic approach, including advanced imaging, bronchoscopy, and histopathology, in managing complex lung pathologies.

Himanya Tatineni, L. Muthukumaran, S. Chandrasekar, S. Sridhar, N. Meenakshi

Chettinad Hospital and Research Institute, Kelambakkam, Tamil Nadu, India

Background and Objectives: Tuberculosis (TB) and lung cancer pose significant global health challenges, often presenting with overlapping clinical features that complicate diagnosis. While TB is prevalent in endemic regions, lung adenocarcinoma is increasingly diagnosed in elderly populations. The coexistence of TB and lung cancer is rare but clinically significant, requiring a multidisciplinary diagnostic and therapeutic approach. This report discusses a 73-year-old female with chest pain, breathlessness, and pleural effusion, ultimately diagnosed with Stage IV lung adenocarcinoma and microbiologically confirmed pulmonary TB.

Case Study: A 73-year-old female with biomass fuel exposure, pet exposure, and tobacco snuff use presented with chest pain, breathlessness, appetite loss, and significant weight loss. Imaging revealed a heterogeneously enhancing lesion in the right lower lobe, pleural nodular thickening, and moderate effusion. Bronchoscopy showed narrowed lumen with no intraluminal obstruction and thoracoscopy findings included hemorrhagic fluid, sago-grain plaques and pleural thickening. Histopathology confirmed adenocarcinoma of lung, and Pleural tissue CBNAAT detected Mycobacterium tuberculosis. Immunohistochemistry showed TTF-1, CK7, and Napsin-A positivity. The patient was started on weight-based anti-tubercular therapy (ATT) and immune checkpoint inhibitor therapy.

Conclusion: Dual pathology of TB and lung adenocarcinoma is rare but clinically significant. A multidisciplinary approach using imaging, microbiology, and histopathology is essential for accurate diagnosis. Early ATT initiation and personalized cancer therapy are crucial for improving outcomes in such complex cases. Further research is needed to better understand the interaction between TB and lung cancer and optimize management strategies.

Department of Respiratory Medicine, GSL Medical College, Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Pseudo-meigs syndrome is defined as pleural effusion, ascites and ovarian tumour other than fibroma.

Case Report: A 47yr old female presented with complaints of cough, breathlessness, abdominal pain and distension since 10 days. On examination decreased breath sounds on right hemithorax and xray shows right sided pleural effusion. Thoracocentesis done and pleural fluid sent for biochemical, cytological analysis.USG ABDOMEN showing solid cystic lesion suggesting malignancy and ca125 is elevated. Patient underwent laparotomy and resection of tumour and ovary has been done.Post resection pleural effusion and ascites was resolved confirming the diagnosis of pseudo-meigs syndrome.

Conclusions: The etiology of fluid accumulation remains unclear, most likely being filtration of interstitial fluid in peritoneum through tumour capsule and diffusion into pleural space through diaphragmatic lymphatic vessels and apertures.

Pseudo-meigs syndrome is rare entity. The management is symptomatic relief, exploratory laparotomy and definitive surgery.After surgery further management and prognosis depends on type of tumour.

Shital Modak, Shahid Patel, Girija Nair, Sanket Patel, Nikhil Sarangdhar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, D. Y. Patil University School of Medicine, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Non-resolving pneumothorax with persistent bronchopleural fistula is a dreaded complication of underlying lung disease and it may not resolve by Conventional methods of treatment. We describe an unusual case of a young female, previously treated for drug-resistant pulmonary tuberculosis in childhood who developed pneumothorax with bronchopleural fistula on the contralateral side 9 years after treatment completion and was successfully treated with autologous blood patch.

Case Report: A 22-year-old female was referred with pneumothorax and ICD in situ. Physical examination was normal except for tachycardia and tachypnea. Chest Radiograph and CT revealed right sided pneumothorax with a destroyed left lung. The patient underwent fiberoptic bronchoscopy (FOB), right sided pleurodesis using autologous blood patch. Previous history was of MDR-TB in 2014 with resistance to Rifampicin and Isoniazid and susceptibility to Kanamycin, PAS, and Ofloxacin. Bronchoscopy was performed with sequential balloon occlusion of each segment of the right lung. Subsequently 2 autologous blood patch pleurodeses with 25 ml blood were performed was and the ICD was unclamped and post-procedure chest radiograph showed full expansion of right side of lung and patient was discharged. The pneumothorax resolved successfully without recurrence during follow-up.

Conclusions: Autologous blood patch pleurodesis provides an effective, cost-efficient alternative to surgical interventions for pneumothorax in patients with compromised pulmonary reserve. The fibrin seal created by autologous blood facilitates pleural adhesion and prevents further air leaks.

Pallavi Jaybhaye, Radha Munje, Gyanshankar Mishra, Sanjay Gour, Kapil Mate

Indira Gandhi Government Medical College, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Lymphangioleiomyomatosis (LAM) is a rare cystic lung disease primarily affecting women of reproductive age. It occurs in two forms: tuberous sclerosis complex-associated LAM (TSC-LAM) and sporadic LAM, the latter arising due to spontaneous genetic mutations. LAM often presents with progressive dyspnea, recurrent pneumothorax, or chylous effusions, making early recognition crucial.

Case Report: A 41-year-old female farmer presented with sudden-onset breathlessness for seven days, initially Modified Medical Research Council (mMRC) Grade 2, progressing to dyspnea at rest over three days. She also reported right-sided chest pain and dry cough. There was no history of pulmonary tuberculosis, smoking, inhalational therapy, trauma, or prior hospitalization. She had undergone intercostal chest drain (ICD) insertion for a right-sided pneumothorax at a private hospital two days prior. On admission, vital signs were stable (pulse rate: 84/min, respiratory rate: 22/min, blood pressure: 104/70 mmHg, oxygen saturation [SpO2]: 96% on room air, random blood sugar: 100 mg/dL). Chest radiograph revealed a left-sided pneumothorax with the right-sided ICD in situ, necessitating left-sided ICD placement. High-resolution computed tomography (HRCT) of the thorax showed gross left-sided pneumothorax, minimal right-sided pneumothorax, and multiple thin-walled cysts in both lungs, suggestive of cystic lung disease—LAM. Laboratory investigations were unremarkable except for elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) (403 U/L). Histopathology from transbronchial lung biopsy (TBLB) confirmed LAM, with human melanoma black-45 (HMB-45) and smooth muscle actin (SMA) negativity. Vascular endothelial growth factor-D (VEGF-D) testing was recommended.

Conclusions: LAM should be considered in women of reproductive age presenting with spontaneous pneumothorax, especially when HRCT reveals multiple thin-walled lung cysts. Early diagnosis is crucial for appropriate management.

Pallavi Jaybhaye, Radha Munje, Gyanshankar Mishra, Kapil Mate

Indira Gandhi Government Medical College, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Empyema is a serious pediatric complication of pneumonia, often requiring early diagnosis and intervention. When associated with skeletal abnormalities, conditions like osteogenesis imperfecta (OI) should be considered, as they may predispose patients to infections and fractures. This case highlights the diagnostic challenge of empyema in a child with suspected OI.

Case Report: A 13-year-old male presented with fever for 10 days, right-sided chest pain, breathlessness, and dry cough for 7 days. His history included recurrent leg fractures, bilateral congenital clubfoot, and walking difficulties since birth. On admission, vital signs were: pulse rate (PR): 134/min, respiratory rate (RR): 40/min, blood pressure (BP): 100/70 mmHg, oxygen saturation (SpO2): 99% on room air, temperature: 102°F. Clinical examination revealed acute respiratory distress, right-sided chest tenderness, and diminished breath sounds. Chest X-ray and thoracic ultrasound showed a moderate right-sided pleural effusion, and diagnostic thoracentesis revealed frank pus, confirming empyema. Laboratory findings included anemia, leukocytosis, elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), and high pleural fluid adenosine deaminase (ADA) and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) levels. Pleural fluid analysis identified methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) sensitive to linezolid, cotrimoxazole, doxycycline, and chloramphenicol. Further evaluation revealed blue sclera on ophthalmic examination, raising suspicion of OI as an underlying connective tissue disorder. The patient was treated with MRSA-sensitive antibiotics and supportive care, leading to clinical improvement.

Conclusions: Children with osteogenesis imperfecta have an increased risk of recurrent respiratory infections and pleural effusion. Early recognition of underlying skeletal disorders is crucial for timely intervention and better clinical outcomes.

Kommera Akshay, A. Vinay Kumar

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Chalmeda AnandRao Institute of Medical Sciences, Karimnagar, Telangana, India

Background: Pancreatic pleural effusion often unrecognized clinical syndrome which results from an internal pancreatic fistula and usually presents as an exudative effusion of unknown cause. The effusion frequently occurs without clinical evidence of pancreatitis, but occasionally it may be associated with a pseudocyst of the pancreas. Chronic massive pancreatic pleural effusion is usually recurrent and characterized by very high levels of amylase in the pleural fluid.

Case Report: 37 year old male patient with history of chronic alcohol usage and previous episodes of pancreatitis presented with complaints of productive cough since 1 month, Difficulty breathing since 1 month which was gradually progressing. chest Xray reviled a right gross pleural effusion thoracocentesis was done dark red colour fluid was drained & Fluid analysis showed educative picture i/v/o respiratory distress a chest tube was placed. Sr. Amylase and lipase are elevated, pleural fluid cytology was negative,Pleural fluid analysis for amylase and lipase are elevated, cect abdomen revealed chronic pancreatitis features leading to a diagnosis of pancreatic pleural effusion.

Conclusion: Chronic pancreatic pleural effusion is a rare and complex condition that poses significant diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. Our case highlights the importance of considering this condition in patients with recurrent or persistent pleural effusion, particularly in those with a history of pancreatic disease. The management of chronic pancreatic pleural effusion requires a multidisciplinary approach, involving gastroenterologists, pulmonologists, and surgeons. While thoracocentesis and ERCP with stenting can provide temporary relief, surgical intervention may be necessary in some cases to address the underlying pancreatic disease.

Vishak Acharya, Madhav Kamath, Saraswathi Ram Mohan, Aquilin Sara Alias

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Kasturba Medical College, Mangalore, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Mediastinal cysts are true congenital cysts and are commonly found in the middle mediastinum, comprising of 12-25% of the mediastinal mass.Bronchogenic cysts are the most common (40-50%) followed by esophageal duplication cysts. They are usually incidental findings on computed tomography, typically appearing as a round fluid filled lesion. Esophageal duplication cysts are present in early childhood, with an incidence rate of 1 in 8200, with twice as likely to occur in males compared to females.

Case Report: 52 year old female presented to the outpatient department of Kasturba Medical College, with complaints of on and off fever for 2 months, with chest x-ray showing gross right sided pleural effusion, which warranted further evaluation. On physical examination, there was decreased breath sounds over the right interscapular, infrascapular, mammary, infra-axillary areas. Blood tests revealed elevated total counts, Pleural fluid showed lymphocyte predominance and culture grew methicillin resistant staphylococcus aureus, with GeneXpert being negative. Imaging: USG Chest showed right sided gross pleural effusion, with cholelithiasis. MRCP was done, showed cholelithiasis, loculated collection indenting the left lobe of liver, left hepatic vein with suspicion of mediastinal cyst. PET scan -Duplication cyst in the right hemithorax with no FDG uptake he was taken up for right sided thoracotomy with excision biopsy.Histopathology showed features consistent with esophageal duplication cyst. Esophageal duplication cysts should be considered as a rare but possible differential diagnosis in patients with recurrent right sided pleural effusion.Esophageal duplication cysts are commonly seen in children. They are usually asymptomatic and are found incidentally.

Conclusions: The case underscores the importance of considering congenital anomalies like esophageal duplication cysts in the differential diagnosis for patients presenting with atypical chest symptoms. Early diagnosis is essential to prevent complications of infection, compression of adjacent structures. Surgical excision remains the definitive treatment.
