B. Bavithra, Raghu Srikanti

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Guntur Medical College, Guntur, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Pneumothorax is abnormal presence of air in the pleural cavity. It can be spontaneous and traumatic. Spontaneous can be primary or secondary. Primary is without underlying lung disease. Secondary is due to airway disease, connective tissue disorder, malignancy, etc.

Case Report: A 13 year old girl brought to hospital with complaints of sudden onset breathlessness with left sided chest pain since 2 days. Patient is on Anti-tubercular treatment since 5 months under sputum basis. Previous history of right sided pneumothorax for which Intercostal drain insertion done in outside hospital 1 week back followed by resolution. On examination positive wrist / thumb sign, arachnodactyly, high arched palate, suggestive of marfanoid Habitus. Chest Xray showed Right upper lobe hyperlucency, Left sided Pneumothorax with bilateral cystic lucencies. High-resolution computed tomography chest showed Left sided pneumothorax, right upper lobe bulla, Bilateral cystic lucencies with surrounding consolidation. Patient had seizure episode suggestive of hypercoaguable state. Magnetic Resonance Imaging Brain showed cerebral venous sinus thrombosis. Clinical and radiological picture is in favour of marfans syndrome, which is responsible for Secondary spontaneous pneumothorax.

Conclusions: Recurrent spontaneous pneumothorax (RSP) is a clinically significant condition that presents unique challenges in diagnosis and management. It not only occurs due to pulmonary consequences but also due to systemic manifestations either acquired or genetic predispositions.

By early diagnosis and treatment of multisystem involvement in marfan’s syndrome increases the life expectancy by 30 years.

Sahu Gaurav, Nair Shreeja, Potdar Pradeep

Department of Respiratory Medicine, MGM Hospital, Kamothe, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Psoriatic arthritis (PsA) is an inflammatory autoimmune condition that primarily affects the joints but can also involve various extra-articular manifestations, including the lungs. While pulmonary complications in PsA are less common, bronchiectasis/cystic/ bullous lung disease has been documented, and secondary spontaneous pneumothorax (SSP) may complicate pre-existing lung disease. However, the association between PsA, cystic lung disease and SSP is rarely described in the literature. We present a 19-year-old malnourished male with Pierre-Robin syndrome, PsA, bullous lung disease, and bronchiectasis.

Case Report: He presented with acute right-sided chest pain, dyspnea, and hypoxia. Imaging revealed a large right-sided pneumothorax, which was managed with thoracostomy tube placement. However, a bronchopleural fistula was slow to heal. Bronchoscopy was attempted but complicated by maxillary and mandibular hypoplasia. The fistula, located in the posterior segment of the right upper lobe, was identified using dye instillation. Broncho-alveolar lavage revealed Enterobacter and Acinetobacter species, and antibiotics were adjusted accordingly. The patient improved and was discharged after 30 days with follow-up for PsA and bronchiectasis. This case highlights the rare but important consideration of secondary spontaneous pneumothorax in asyndromic patient with psoriatic arthritis and bronchiectasis with bullae. Chronic pulmonary diseases, such as cystic lung disease and bullous lung disease, increase the risk of pneumothorax due to airway damage and lung tissue fragility.[1] Clinicians should be aware of this potential complication in patients with autoimmune diseases and chronic lung conditions, particularly when they present with acute respiratory symptoms. Pulmonologists should anticipate difficulty that may arise when attempting bronchoscopy in patients with poor mouth opening and narrowed airway for management of Bronchopulmonary fistulae.

Keywords: Autoimmune disease, bronchiectasis, chest tube, cystic lung disease, Pierre Robin’s syndrome, psoriatic arthritis, respiratory distress, secondary spontaneous pneumothorax1

Reference

1. Cooley J, Lee YC, Gupta N. Spontaneous pneumothorax in diffuse cystic lung diseases. Curr Opin Pulm Med 2017;23:323-33.

Gopika Ramesh, M. Anand, Safreena Muhamed

Government Medical College, Kozhikode, Kerala, India

Introduction: Interstitial lung disease (ILD) comprises of a heterogeneous group of diseases characterized by varying degrees of pulmonary inflammation and fibrosis. Pulmonary rehabilitation is an important component in the treatment of patients with ILD.

Methods: A prospective cohort study of 60 patients with ILD were studied at Government Medical College, Kozhikode. Patients were recruited into 2 groups. One group received PR program of 8 weeks and the other group received PR program of 20 weeks. After collecting baseline data, patients were recruited for pulmonary rehabilitation program (PR). They were followed up at 3,6,9 months and assessed pulmonary functions, 6MWD and other relevant data.

Results: Out of 60 patients with ILD, 38 were females and 22 were males. They were equally recruited into 2 groups. The long term pulmonary rehabilitation group showed a significantly greater increase in 6 Minute Walk Distance (6MWD) 380m compared to standard pulmonary rehabilitation group 260m (p value <0.001). There was no significant change in pulmonary function test (FEV1, FVC, FEV1/FVC). Long term pulmonary rehabilitation group showed substantial reduction in SGRQ scores, but not statistically significant. Dyspnea score was improved in long term pulmonary rehabilitation group, but not statistically significant. Anxiety and depression scores improved significantly in the long term pulmonary rehabilitation group compared to standard pulmonary rehabilitation group.

Conclusion: Long term pulmonary rehabilitation provides significant benefits over standard pulmonary rehabilitation program in patients with ILD, including improved exercise capacity, quality of life along with reduced rates of hospitalisations and exacerbations.

Keywords: 6MWD, interstitial lung disease, pulmonary rehabilitation

T. V. Sagar, Yogita Hatmode, Sameer Bansal, Anmol Thorbole, Ravindra Mehta

Department of Pulmonary Rehabilitation, Vaayu Chest and Sleep Specialists, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Background: Guidelines recommend a minimum 6-week comprehensive Pulmonary rehabilitation (PR) program for patients with chronic respiratory diseases (CRDs), to see desired outcomes. However, acceptance, adherence, logistics and economics is a challenge in the global and Indian setting for the recommended duration. We hypothesized that a lesser duration may also meet desired outcomes, and assessed the interim efficacy/outcomes at the 3-week milestone in an outpatient PR program.

Methods: A retrospective analysis was conducted at a 3-week interim period in patients undergoing outpatient PR for various CRDs, at an outpatient PR facility in Bengaluru (VAAYU Chest and Sleep Services). 2 supervised sessions were conducted every week.

Results: 380 patients were enrolled for PR program of varying durations (3 to 12 weeks). 182/380 (48%) patients completed a 3-week program, and were assessed for standard outcomes. The mean age was 64.3 yrs. (±15). The most common diagnoses were Interstitial Lung Disease, Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease, Asthma, Bronchiectasis and others in 40%, 23%, 17%, 12% and 8% patients respectively. Average 6 Minute Walk Distance (6MWD) was 252m (±103) at baseline. Mean increase after 6 sessions was 33.6 [p - 0.16]. Mean change in Fatigue severity scale (FSS) was -7.4 [p<0.01] from a baseline mean score of 38. Mean change in CAT score was -4 [p<0.01] from a baseline mean of 23, while change in SGRQs was -8.9 [p<0.01] from baseline average score of 48.6. MCID was reached for all parameters after a 3-week (6 sessions) PR program, except for 6MWD. Of note, there was a high re-enrolment rate (62%) for longer duration PR program after completion of 6 sessions.

Conclusion: The earliest significant improvement is seen at 3 weeks in an outpatient PR program, which may represent the minimal effective duration that leads to significant improvement of functional and quality of life parameters. This milestone also encourages patients to re-enrolment for longer PR programs, overcoming challenges mentioned above.

Keywords: Chronic respiratory diseases, pulmonary rehabilitation

Dipika Koli, Unnati Desai, Ketaki Utpat

Department of Respiratory Medicine, TNMC and BYL Nair Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Spirometry is essential for diagnosing respiratory diseases like COPD and post-infectious obstructive airway disease, particularly in assessing airflow obstruction through FEV1 (Forced expiratory volume in 1 second) and FVC (Forced vital capacity) measurements. It also helps detect Upper Airway Obstruction (UAO), which can complicate these conditions. In patients with COPD and post-infective airway disease, the presence of UAO may exacerbate symptoms and alter spirometric patterns, making it essential to evaluate both lower and upper airway components. This study focuses on using spirometry to identify Upper Airway Obstruction (UAO) and assess its impact on lung function in these patients.

Methods: This is a retrospective observational study, conducted over two years at a tertiary care center, including spirometric testing of COPD and PIOAD patients. Measurements included FEV1, FVC, PEF, Empey’s index, ratio of the flow at the midpoint of the forced expiratory manoeuvre to the flow at the midpoint of the forced inspiratory manoeuvre FEF50/FIF50, and flow-volume loops (FVLs). The various spirometry indices and FVLs were analyzed to identify distinct patterns of airway obstruction, focusing on variations in peak expiratory flow and loop shape, which may reflect differences in the underlying pathology of airflow limitation.

Results: The study included 193 patients, with 80 diagnosed with COPD and 113 with PIOAD. The mean age was 56.3 years (±12.95), with 126 males and 67 females. Visual assessment of flow-volume loops (FVLs) showed 120 patients with normal loops (48 COPD, 72 PIOAD). Inspiratory loop flattening was observed in 17 patients (5 COPD, 12 PIOAD), and expiratory loop flattening in 42 patients (22 COPD, 20 PIOAD). Both inspiratory and expiratory loop flattening was seen in 5 patients (3 COPD, 2 PIOAD). Variable intrathoracic UAO was present in 53 COPD and 49 PIOAD patients, while extrathoracic UAO was seen in 3 COPD and 26 PIOAD patients. Fixed UAO occurred in 2 PIOAD patients.

Conclusion: The study found that upper airway obstruction can occur in COPD and PIOAD patients, further limiting airflow and worsening FEV1 reduction, altering flow-volume loops, and lowering the FEV1/FVC ratio. The added resistance from upper airway narrowing complicates spirometry, exacerbating the obstructive pattern and worsening lung function.

Keywords: Chronic obstructive pulmonary diseases, flow-volume loop, post-infectious obstructive airway disease, upper airway obstruction

K. Shanmugapriya 1,2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Sri Lalithambigai Medical College and Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Dr. M. G. R. Educational and Research Institute, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: Spirometry is a diagnostic tool commonly used to treat chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). The impulse oscillometry system (IOS) allows for the calculation of respiratory impedance indices, showing promise for early COPD detection.

Methods: This prospective comparative study was performed at a tertiary care hospital. Consecutive sampling was employed to select the participants. We recruited participants into two groups: non-smokers and smokers (n=30 in each group). All participants underwent comprehensive evaluation, including spirometry and IOS assessments. These measurements provided important data for comparative analysis of respiratory function within the studied groups.

Results: Non-smokers were outnumbered in all categories except for the 21-30 group. The proportion of smokers was 23.3%, compared to 16.7% of non-smokers. The highest proportion of non-smokers was found in the 41-50 age group (33.3%), while the lowest was in the 21-30 group (16.7%). The highest proportion of smokers was found in the 21-30 age group (23.3%), and the lowest was in the 41-50 age group (13.3%). Smokers and non-smokers showed similar distributions in weight categories: normal weight (36.7%), overweight (53.3%), and obese (10%). No significant differences in demographic characteristics were observed. Significant differences emerged in spirometry parameters (FEV1, FVC, FEV1/FVC, and FEF) and IOS parameters (R5, R5-R20, and AX) between smokers and non-smokers. Among smokers with normal spirometry results, 53% had airway obstruction in the IOS.

Conclusion: The IOS is effective in assessing airway obstruction in patients with limited cooperation. Furthermore, it acts as a sensitive screening tool for early detection of bronchial obstruction.

A. Nikhil Krishnan, E. Jyothi

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Government Medical College, Kerala University of Health Sciences, Kozhikode, Kerala, India

Background: Effective airway clearance, anti-infective treatment, and preservation of airway patency have long been considered crucial therapeutic modalities for bronchiectasis. The role of pulmonary rehabilitation has been extensively studied in patients with chronic respiratory disorders. While most of the research associated with pulmonary rehabilitation has been done in patients with COPD, there is limited data about its efficacy in patients with bronchiectasis. This study aims to investigate the effects of respiratory rehabilitation and limb exercise training on pulmonary function, sputum clearance and quality of life in patients with bronchiectasis.

Methods: Primary observation indexes include patient’s sputum volume, 6MWD, spirometry values and St George Respiratory Questionnaire. Data will be collected on first visit after taking written informed consent and enrolled into the program. Supervised respiratory training combined with limb exercises will be given to the patient for a total period of 3 months at a frequency of 3 times a week. A typical session will be a 45 minute supervised training. It includes diaphragmatic and pursed lip breathing techniques, deep breathing with chest wall expansion, endurance training and limb exercises. Sample size – 70.

Results: After the programme, sputum volume increased from 23±5.2 to 35 ±8.8 ml/day; FEV1 increased from 1.27±0.38 to 1.46±0.18L; FVC increased from 1.53±0.26L to 1.70±0.27L; 6MWD improved from 282+/-48 to 321m+/- 55; SGRQ also showed improvement.

Conclusion: The study results suggest that pulmonary rehabilitation significantly improved the quality of life, pulmonary function and sputum clearance in patients with bronchiectasis.

R. Usmanali, Dipika Koli, Ketaki Utpat, Unnati Desai

Department of Respiratory Medicine, TNMC and BYL Nair Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Spirometry is currently the commonly performed lung function test in clinical practice for diagnosis of obstructive airway diseases. However, it requires forceful expiratory and inspiratory maneuver and good patient co–operation. Spirometry cannot be done in pediatric population who are unable to understand complex commands. Forced oscillometry technique (FOT)is a non-invasive method to assess lung function. In 1956, DUBOIS et al described the forced oscillation technique by using sinusoidal sound waves of single frequencies generated by a loud speaker and passed into the lungs during tidal breathing. The output of a measure of respiratory impedance (z), which included the respiratory resistance (R) and respiratory reactance (X) measured over a range of frequencies (usually from 3 to 35Hz). FOT has advantages being effort independent for diagnosis of multiple respiratory conditions.

Case Report: A 9-year-oldfemale child, 4th standard student was referred to the OPD. She was symptomatic since 4 yr with complaints of episodic dyspnea and episodic cough, seasonal and diurnal variation of symptoms,allergic rhinitis on exposure to dust and cold. No family history of bronchial asthma. The clinical examination was normal. Chest X-Ray was normal. The patient did not cooperate for a PFT. Hence a FOT was performed [Figure 1]. The FOT showed an increase in resistance at 5 hertz (R5), normal resistance at 19 Hertz (R19), increase in resistance of the small airways ( R5-19), The reactance was more negative (X5) and the an increased FRES. All these findings were suggestive of a small airway obstruction.

FOT of the patient

Conclusion: FOT diagnosed small airway obstruction in this pediatric patient.

FOT is a non-invasive test which can be easily done in children, elderly and disabled patients.

Introduction: Chest ultrasound (USG chest) is a non-invasive, bedside imaging tool enabling rapid evaluation of respiratory conditions in emergency settings. This study assesses its diagnostic utility by comparing clinical history, USG chest findings, and final diagnoses in pulmonary pathologies.

Methods: Design: Prospective analysis of 50 patients with respiratory distress. Protocol: Diagnoses were established based on: (1) Clinical history and symptoms, chest X ray. (2) USG chest findings using BLUE protocols. (3) Final diagnoses confirmed by clinical, radiological, and laboratory data. Conditions Studied: (1) Pulmonary oedema, (2) COPD/Asthma, (3) Lung consolidation/pleural effusion, (4) Interstitial lung diseases (ILD).

(3) Consolidation/ pleural effusion. (1) Pulmonary Oedema and Consolidation/Pleural Effusion: USG chest demonstrated superior accuracy compared to history, clinical evaluation, and chest X-ray, achieving 100% sensitivity and specificity for consolidation/pleural effusion. (2) COPD/Asthma: Combining USG chest with clinical history improved diagnostic accuracy. (3) ILD: USG chest showed high diagnostic precision even for less common conditions.

Conclusion: USG chest is a highly accurate, reliable, and indispensable tool for diagnosing pulmonary conditions, particularly pulmonary oedema and lung consolidation. Its bedside utility, especially in resource-limited emergency settings, significantly improves diagnostic precision and patient outcomes.

Shital Patil1, Ganesh Narwade1, Gajanan Gondhali2, Deepak Patil2, Manojkumar Bhadake2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Venkatesh Chest Hospital and Critical Care Center, Latur, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Internal Medicine, MIMSR Medical College, Latur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Radiological outcomes are initially interpreted as post covid lung fibrosis in short term follow-up of recovered COVID-19 cases in post covid care settings and during long term evaluation these abnormalities are defined as post covid sequalae’s. In present study, post covid lung abnormalities were evaluated as ‘Residual Lung abnormalities (RLAs)’ as radiological outcomes phenotypes.

Methods: Prospective, observational study, initially included 1000 COVID-19 RT-PCR confirmed cases with lung involvement documented and radiological severity phenotypes categorized on HRCT thorax as mild, moderate and severe as per lung segment involvement bilaterally at entry point. Final radiological outcomes were defined by performing radiological assessment done at one year of discharge from hospital in cases with specified inclusion criteria. Radiological final outcomes phenotypes were defined as radiological lung abnormalities (RLAs) and categorised as resolving, persistent and progressive types documented in HRCT imaging at one year of follow up. Finally, we have included 209 cases with RLAs. Covariates recordings of age, gender, comorbidity, laboratory parameters and interventions such as oxygen support and oxygen plus ventilatory support requirement during hospitalization were done. Statistical analysis is done by using Chi square test.

Results: RLAs were documented in 20.73% (209/1000) cases. Final radiological outcomes were documented as resolving 72.24% (151/209), persistent 19.61% (41/209) and progressive 8.13% (17/209). Significant association was documented between RLAs and duration of illness at entry point [p<0.00001], laboratory parameters at entry point (D-dimer, CRP, IL-6) [p<0.00001], radiological CT severity phenotypes [p<0.00001] and interventions required in indoor unit [p<0.00001]. Significant association observed between RLAs with covariates such as age, gender, diabetes mellitus, IHD, Hypertension, COPD, Obesity and lung functions assessment by spirometry at one year follow-up. [p<0.00001].

Conclusion: Radiological lung abnormalities (RLAs) are new radiological classification of post covid lung sequelae and categorized as resolving, persistent and progressive. Final outcomes phenotypic classification showed an important role in further workup and treatment plannings of these RLAs during follow up. Importantly, the proportionate number of progressive phenotypes have underlying rheumatological predisposition, and the large number of cases in resolving phenotypes doesn’t need any treatment; while, few cases in the persistent category need protocolized follow up.

Department of Respiratory Medicine, IPGMER and SSKM Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Being the chief respiratory muscle, the function of diaphragm has a direct impact on the prognosis and quality of life in COPD patients. Here, we assessed the structural and functional status of diaphragm by Ultrasonography in stable COPD patients.

Methods: This is a cross-sectional observational study done in 100 stable COPD patients attended the OPD of Department of Respiratory Medicine, IPGME&R and SSKM Hospital. Basic clinical details, spirometric assessment, CAT SCORING and BODE INDEX were collected for each patient. Diaphragm structure and function were measured in terms of thickness, thickening ratio and fraction, excursion separately for both right and left hemi-diaphragms and the values are compared with progressive worsening of COPD.

Results: Out of 100 patients, 58 patients were male and 42 were female. The mean age of the patients was 62.94±6.49. The outcomes were compared with mild, moderate, severe obstructive groups. There were significant differences in diaphragmatic thickening fraction- Right side: 63.57±9.32, 60.96±10.23, 48.71±10.43 (p<0.001) and Left side: 66.29±8.63, 60.77±8.64, 53.42±7.98 (p <0.001). There were significant differences in thickening ratio- Right: 1.63±0.09, 1.60±0.10, 1.48±0.10 (p <0.001) and Left: 1.67±0.11, 1.61±0.08, 1.53±0.08 (p <0.001). There were significant differences in excursion during deep inspiration- Right: 4.20±0.40, 3.92±0.46, 3.29±0.41 (p <0.001) and Left: 4.23±0.36, 4.02±0.46, 3.37±0.40 (p <0.001). Both thickness ratio (Right: r -0.701, Left: -0.634; p <0.001) and excursion (Right: r -0.784, Left: r -0.768; p <0.001) were significantly correlated with the number of exacerbations.

Conclusion: Diaphragm ultrasound parameters can be used along with pulmonary function tests for the evaluation of severity in COPD patients.

Keywords: COPD, diaphragm excursion, diaphragm thickness

Geet Doshi

Doshi Chest Clinic & Hospital, Baramati, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The diagnosis of peripheral lung lesions is challenging as the yield of Sputum cytology & flexible bronchoscopy is relatively low. For peural/peripheral lung parenchymal lesions the diagnostic accuracy of Ultra-Sonography(USG) guided biopsy is similar to that of CT guided biopsy.

Case Report: A 45/M presented with chronic dry cough,breathlessness,Left sided chest pain & constitutional symptoms since 6 months. Patient was admitted & all routine investigations were done. Chest X-Ray (CXR) was suggestive of left sided multiple well circumscribed homogenous opacities. A contrast enhanced CT Thorax was obtained to look for vascular involvement of the lesions; which suggested non-vascular lesions. The largest sub-pleural lesion was selected and USG guided lung biopsy was done with Bard’s Biopsy Gun under local anaesthesia. The lesion was located by scanning the intercostal spaces & Colour Doppler helped identify the absence of blood vessels in the path of biopsy. Biopsy with caseous material was collected in 2 containers – 1)Normal Saline-(Truenat) 2)Formalin-(Histopathology-HPE). Post procedure USG screening showed no evidence of Pneumothorax. Post procedure CXR showed no evidence of pneumothorax. Truenat reported MTB detected, Rifampicin Resistance not detected. HPE was suggestive of acute on chronic inflammatory infiltrates composed of neutrophils, eosinophils & macrophages with large areas of necrosis. Patient was started on Antitubercular drugs 4FDC 4-tablets/day (Weight 52Kg) under NTEP(National Tuberculosis Elimination Program) and was discharged.

Conclusion: USG remains underutilized as a diagnostic technique despite its high diagnostic yield, real time guidance, no radiation exposure. USG is portable, light-weight, affordable with excellent imaging, readily available & its use in interventional procedures should be encouraged.

Shivam Gurme, Jairaj P. Nair, Sonal Karpe, Swapnil Thorve, Siddharth Waghmare, Anjali Kamath

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Lokmanya Tilak Medical College and General Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Recurrent hemorrhagic pleural effusion is often caused by tuberculosis, malignancy, or collagen vascular diseases. However, thoracic endometriosis (TES), an extra-abdominal manifestation of endometriosis, should also be considered in women of reproductive age. Thorax is the most common site for TES, which requires a high degree of clinical suspicion for diagnosis. We present a rare case of recurrent pleural effusion due to TES in a young female with infertility.

Case Report: A 33-year-old female presented with 6 months of exertional dyspnoea (MMRC grade 2) and occasional dry cough. She was treated for tubercular cervical lymphadenopathy and had undergone three therapeutic thoracenteses. Her menstrual history was significant for menorrhagia and dyspnoea since menarche. She underwent hysteroscopy and work up for infertility was inconclusive. Clinical examination and chest xray revealed moderate right-sided pleural effusion. Thoracentesis showed hemorrhagic fluid. Pleural fluid analysis ruled out tuberculosis and malignancy. Medical thoracoscopy revealed hemorrhagic fluid and small raised erythematous area on the parietal pleura. Biopsy of this area showed endometrial stroma, confirmed by immunohistochemistry (PAX8 and CD10 positive), diagnosing pleural endometriosis. The patient underwent talc slurry pleurodesis and started on monthly subcutaneous GnRH analogue injections for 3 months. Follow-up chest X-ray after 3 months showed no recurrence of pleural effusion.

Conclusion: Awareness and early recognition of thoracic endometriosis can prevent unnecessary treatments, reduce delays in diagnosis, and improve patient outcomes through targeted, multidisciplinary management.

Arun V. Joy, Vani Viswanath

Department of Pulmonology, MGM Muthoot Hospitals, Kozhencherry, Kerala, India

Case Report: A 73-year-old man, with history of cough with scanty hemoptysis, anorexia and weight loss for 3 months. Clinical examination demonstrated reduced breath sounds, fine crepitations on right infrascapular areas and no enlargement of the superficial lymph nodes.

Chest x ray showed heterogenous lesion right lower zone. CECT chest showed focal heterogeneously enhancing lesion right lower lobe posterobasal and medial basal segment measuring 5x5x8 cm.Post contrast enhancing lesion with hypoenhancing areas suggestive of necrotic components with segmental bronchus cut off and multiple enhancing mediastinal lymphnodes (largest-1 cm). Whole body PET showed metabolically active lesion in right lung lower lobe (moderate FDG uptake-SUV-7.2).No definite PET evidence of hypermetabolic lesion elsewhere in the body [Figure 1].

CT and PET images

He underwent relevant investigations, which found ESR of 92mm/h, C-reactive protein of 166 mg/L, LDH-386 mg/dl. Antinuclear antibody quantitative determination, ANCA profile results were all negative. Bronchoscopy and lavage taken from right lower lobe bronchus and sent for bacterial culture,cytology,cellblock, CBNAAT, AFB culture,fungal culture and all reported to be negative. CT guided biopsy of right lower lobe lung mass was performed; histology was negative for malignancy and showed severe chronic interstitial inflammation with fibrosis and old haemorrhage. IHC showed numerous positive IgG4 plasma cells (more than 40 positive cells per higher power field in both samples), which was highly suggestive of IGG4-RD.\

Lung biopsy tissue. H&E staining, original magnification ×40. The immunohistochemistry shows IgG4/IgG-positive plasma cell ratio of >40% [Figure 2].

IHC of the biopsy sample

Therapy with corticosteroids has been shown to be effective in both pulmonary and extrapulmonary lesions, although there is no consensus on the regimen for steroid maintenance therapy. The guidelines of Japanese consensus for the treatment recommend ‘an initial prednisolone dosing of 0.6 mg/kg/day for 2–4 weeks, tapered every 1–2 weeks by 5 mg/day, based on biochemical markers, clinical improvement and imaging’. A maintenance dose of 2.5–5 mg is recommended, which should be discontinued after 3years of treatment, depending on improvement after increase in dosage, considering readministration in case of relapse. Following that recommendation, treatment was initiated with oral prednisone 60mg/day, with a slow tapering plan over the two following months.

Prednisone therapy relieved our patient’s symptoms and pulmonary lesions. Currently, the patient is still under corticosteroid therapy. A chest x-ray and CT chest was performed 2 months after treatment, which showed remission of the right lower lobe mass [Figure 3]. Patient has been lost to follow up since he is asymptomatic. It is accepted that IgG4-RD must be followed up with serum levels of IgG4 in order to evaluate relapsing (up to 50% of patients show high levels of it before relapsing). For patients who are steroid refractory, immunosuppressants can be considered, including rituximab.

Followup radiologic imaging

Conclusions: Our case fulfilled the diagnostic criteria for IgG4-RD, with lung involvement, therefore, the diagnosis was made. First-line treatment is based on immune-modulating drugs, namely corticosteroids, while the use of immunosuppressors should be considered only after the failure of first-line treatment. Surgery did not seem to play any role in the treatment of the disease, and it is uniquely related to diagnosis confirmation; in fact, IgG4-RD is often diagnosed only after the unexpected results of a biopsy for a suspected lung malignancy due to the rarity of the disease and the lack of specific signs or symptoms.

Sidhant Narang, Hafiz Deshmukh, Ashish Deshmukh, Sunil Jadhav, Shivprasad Kasat

Introduction: Pancreatic pseudocyst, a common complication of acute or chronic pancreatitis, in rare instances may also extend to the mediastinum and the pleural space. Here is an unusual presentation of a pancreatic pseudocyst communicating with the thoracic cavity via esophageal hiatus and presenting as a case of right sided pseudocyst of lung.

Case Report: A 51 year old male came with complaints of abdominal pain and abdominal fullness since 6 months along with breathlessness, cough and right sided chest pain since 1 month. Patient is an alcoholic and was admitted as a case of acute on chronic pancreatitis,6 months prior. Examination of respiratory system revealed stony dullness on percussion and diminished breath sounds on auscultation over the right hemithorax. Abdominal examination showed mild tenderness over the entire abdomen Haemogram, KFT, LFT were within normal limits. Chest radiograph revealed a homogenous opacity over the right hemithorax with contralateral shift of mediastinum. Pleural fluid showed high amylase and lipase concentration. CECT chest was suggestive of large pseudocyst in right hemithorax causing right lung collapse and contralateral mediastinal shift, communicating with the pseudocyst of lesser sac via esophageal hiatus

Conclusion: Usually pancreato-pleural effusions present as left sided effusions. We could only find 3 documented cases of right sided pancreatic pleural effusions with only 1 of them communicating via esophageal hiatus. A detailed history, lab investigations and radiological evaluation should be considered in cases of both right and left sided effusions which is crucial in identifying the primary pathology and guiding further workup.

Kailas Chakote, Ketaki Utpat, Unnati Desai

Department of Respiratory Medicine, TNMC and BYL Nair Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Lung agenesis is a rare and complex congenital malformation characterized by the complete absence of one or both lungs. It is a developmental anomaly that typically results from the failure of lung formation during early embryogenesis, often leading to significant respiratory and developmental challenges. While unilateral lung agenesis is more common, bilateral lung agenesis is extremely rare and generally fatal shortly after birth due to the absence of essential respiratory function.

Case Report: A 17-year-old female presented with dyspnea. She had complaints of dyspnea, occasional chest pain since 4years. She had no significant past medical surgical or family history.

Her vitals were normal. Her general examination was normal. On respiratory system examination, she had decreased breath sounds on left hemithorax. On CVS examination apex beat was on the extreme left side of the chests, which indicated left sided mediastinal shift.

She was evaluated with sputum studies for CBNAAT, which was MTB, not detected. Her Chest X ray showed a left opaque hemi thorax with tracheal shift towards left [Figure 1]. A computed tomography of chest showed there was complete mediastinal shift towards left and hyperinflation of right lung noted. Tracheal shift towards the left with left main bronchus not visualized [Figure 2]. Bronchoscopy showed tracheal web with tracheal lumen severely compromised, around 2 cm distal to vocal cords, scope could not be negotiated further [Figure 3].

Chest X ray showing a left opaque hemi thorax with tracheal shift towards left

Computed tomography of chest showing complete mediastinal shift towards left and hyperinflation of right lung noted

Tracheal web with tracheal severely compromised lumen on bronchoscopy

The differential diagnosis of left opaque hemi thorax with left tracheal shift includes bronchial obstruction by a foreign body, endobronchial mass, pulmonary agenesis versus aplasia, hypoplasia, pneumonectomy.

Conclusions: Pulmonary agenesis/aplasia, while rare, should be considered in patients presenting with unexplained respiratory symptoms and radiological findings of lung absence. Early, accurate diagnosis with counseling is crucial for unnecessary workup.

Shanmukha Venkat Sai Kiran Kona, Somanath Dash

Department of Respiratory Medicine, GSL Medical College, Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Drug-induced Interstitital lung disease is a large and very heterogeneous group of adverse drug reactions, ranging from mild to progressive and life threathening disease.

Case Report: A 71 yr old female presented with complaints of shortness of breath and dry cough aggrevated sine 10 days. Past h/o recurrent urinary tract infections for which she used NITROFURANTOIN for 8 yrs. Chest xray shows bilateral reticular opacities in lower regions. Ct chest shows diffuse reticular and ground glass opacities, interlobular septal thickening and few fibrotic bands noted in bilateral lung fields suggestive of INTERSTITIAL LUNG DISEASE – NSIP pattern. Nitrofurantoin was stopped, patient gradually improved clinically and radiologically.

Conclusion: The diagnosis can be particularly challenging when multiple potentially pneumotoxic drugs are used for treatment of diseases that frequent cause pulmonary complications. Acute pneumonitis generally develops within one to two weeks of nitrofurantoin use and presents with fever dyspnea and cough. In subacute and chronic disease, the most common presenting symptoms are dyspnea and cough, which develop after atleast one month of treatment. Identification and discontinuation of the culprit drug is the cornerstone of treatment.

S. Ashok Kumar1, Mahavir Modi2

1Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Ruby Hall Clinic, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Swyer-James-MacLeod Syndrome (SJMS) is a rare, acquired pulmonary disorder characterized by unilateral hyperlucency of the lung, often diagnosed incidentally through imaging. First described in the 1950s, SJMS results from childhood bronchiolitis obliterans, leading to alveolar overdistension and reduced vascular perfusion in the affected lung. The hallmark radiologic feature is a hyperlucent lung with reduced vascular markings, typically unilateral. Clinical presentation varies, ranging from asymptomatic cases to recurrent respiratory infections, chronic cough, and exertional dyspnea. Pulmonary function tests may reveal obstructive or restrictive patterns. Diagnosis relies on chest X-ray, CT scan, and ventilation-perfusion scans, which demonstrate ventilation-perfusion mismatch. Treatment focuses on symptom management, infection control, and, in severe cases, surgical resection of the affected lung. This review discusses the pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnostic approaches, and management strategies for SJMS, highlighting its relevance in differential diagnoses for hyperlucent lung syndromes.

Case Report: A 65-year-old female presented with complaints of breathlessness. Clinical examination revealed decreased breath sounds on the right side.

Investigations:

Chest X-ray (CXR): Unilateral hyperlucency on the right sideArterial Blood Gas (ABG): Type II respiratory failure2D Echocardiography (Echo): Severe pulmonary hypertensionCT Pulmonary Angiography (CTPA): No evidence of pulmonary thromboembolism (PTE), but findings consistent with Swyer-James Syndrome (SJS).

Chest X-ray (CXR): Unilateral hyperlucency on the right side

Arterial Blood Gas (ABG): Type II respiratory failure

2D Echocardiography (Echo): Severe pulmonary hypertension

CT Pulmonary Angiography (CTPA): No evidence of pulmonary thromboembolism (PTE), but findings consistent with Swyer-James Syndrome (SJS).

Swyer-James Syndrome presenting with severe pulmonary hypertension and Type II respiratory failure.

Conclusion: Swyer-James Syndrome (SJS) remains a rare but important diagnosis in patients presenting with unilateral hyperlucent lung and respiratory symptoms. Early recognition through imaging and clinical correlation is essential for appropriate management and to prevent recurrent infections and long-term complications. Although the condition is irreversible, symptomatic treatment, infection control, and pulmonary rehabilitation can significantly improve quality of life. Continued awareness and research are needed to enhance diagnostic accuracy and refine therapeutic approaches for this underrecognized syndrome.

Keywords: Bronchiolitis obliterans, hyperlucent lung, pulmonary disorder, rare lung disease, respiratory failure, Swyer-James-MacLeod Syndrome.

Siddhartha Chakraborty1, Manu Chopra1, Rahul Tyagi1, Kislay Kishore1, Aseem Yadav1, M. C. Jyothis1, Ankush Gupta2

Introduction: We present a rare case report of an adolescent male, product of a consanguineous marriage, who had severe pulmonary hypertension and worsened on Anti-PAH therapy. He was diagnosed as a case of Peripheral Veno-occlusive disease based on right heart catheterization and whole genome sequencing.

Case Report: A 17 year old male presented with progressive breathlessness to a peripheral hospital. Two-dimensional echocardiography showed severe pulmonary hypertension. He was started on anti-pulmonary hypertension medications. However, his condition worsened while on medications and he reported to our center with severe hypoxemia. Computed tomography pulmonary angiogram showed multiple ground glass nodules with mediastinal lymphadenopathy. Right heart catheterization revealed pre-capillary pulmonary hypertension. This case report highlights the importance of need for awareness about this rare disease as early diagnosis and referral for lung transplantation can be lifesaving.

Conclusions:

Introduction: Anterior mediastinal masses encompass a diverse group of neoplastic and nonneoplastic lesions located within the mediastinum, presenting a diagnostic challenge due to their varied etiologies and potential for serious complications. Tuberculosis presenting solely as an isolated mediastinal mass, as observed in our patient without parenchymal lesions, is rare.

Case Report: A 25-year-old male presented with dry cough and right sided chest pain for the past 6 months. On initial evaluation the patient had a palpable mass of 3x2 cm below the clavicle. Chest radiograph shows ill-defined heterogeneous opacity over the right middle and lower zone silhouetting the right heart border. Computed tomography showed hypo dense soft tissue density in anterior mediastinum on right side obscuring right heart border and ascending aorta. USG guided anterior chest wall biopsy showed caseous necrosis with adjacent fibrocollageous tissue showing epithelioid granuloma and gene Xpert indicated very low Mycobacterium tuberculosis detection without rifampicin resistance. He was started on Anti-tubercular therapy and currently is on follow up.

Conclusions: Anterior mediastinal mass in this case exemplifies the diverse and often enigmatic nature of thoracic pathologies, requiring comprehensive and multidisciplinary diagnostic approach. The atypical manifestation challenges conventional diagnostic paradigms, highlighting the necessity for a high degree of clinical vigilance. The integration of advanced imaging modalities, coupled with histopathological and microbiological evaluation, remains paramount in establishing accurate diagnosis. Early recognition and prompt initiation of targeted therapy are crucial in mitigating potential complications and ensuring favorable outcomes.

Dhiraj Atram1, Amita Athavale2, Vijay Khatri2, Tanya Athavale2, Gaurav Jain2

1Seth GS Medical College and KEM Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Pulmonary Medicine and EPRC, Seth GS Medical College and KEM Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Cystic lung diseases are a heterogeneous group of conditions characterized by multiple cystic lesions in the lung parenchyma. These conditions can stem from infections, genetic disorders, autoimmune processes, or neoplasms, often presenting diagnostic challenges due to overlapping clinical and radiological features. The complexity increases with systemic involvement, such as endocrine dysfunctions or extrapulmonary lesions. A thorough evaluation involving multiple disciplines is crucial to determine the underlying cause and guide effective management.

Case Report: A 31-year-old male with recurrent bilateral pneumothorax and progressive dyspnea underwent bilateral ICD placements and left-sided pleurodesis. HRCT revealed extensive bilateral cystic lung disease, predominantly in lower lobes. PET-CT showed hypermetabolic thyroid and cervical lymph nodes. FNAC confirmed Pulmonary Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis with rare thyroid involvement. Differential diagnoses like lymphangioleiomyomatosis and lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia were excluded. Systemic manifestations, including hypothalamic hypogonadism, diabetes insipidus, and hypothyroidism, supported a multisystemic LCH diagnosis. Thyroid involvement in LCH is exceedingly rare, with few cases reported, highlighting the importance of systemic evaluation and multidisciplinary collaboration in diagnosing and managing such complex cases.

Conclusions: This case highlights the diagnostic complexity of LCH with atypical lower lobe involvement and rare thyroid manifestations.

Pulmonary LCH with thyroid involvement is rare, necessitating consideration in unexplained systemic symptoms and abnormal imaging. A multidisciplinary approach and long-term follow-up are essential for disease monitoring and prevention of recurrence.

N. Nagpavan, T. Prasanna Kumar

Department of Respiratory Medicine, M. S. Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) is the leading cause of high mortality in intensive care units (ICUs) worldwide. An effective marker for prognosis in ARDS is particularly important given the absence of effective treatment strategies aside from small tidal volume ventilation. Previous studies identified an association between the neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio (NLR) and prognosis in critical patients. In this study, we explored the prognostic and predictive value of the NLR in ARDS patients.

Methods: We retrospectively included 70 ARDS patients. Clinical characteristics and experimental test data, including the NLR, were collected from medical records at 24 hours after the ARDS diagnosis. Subgroup stratification was performed according to different factors and continuous factors were divided according to median values.

Results: The NLR in survivors was significantly lower than that in non-survivors (P<0.001). We took the median NLR value as the cut-off point and further divided all patients into a high NLR group (NLR >14) and a low NLR group (NLR ≤14). We found that NLR >14 was associated with shorter overall survival (P=0.005).

Conclusion: Our study showed that a high NLR (>14) at 24 hours after an ARDS diagnosis independently predicted a poor prognosis in ARDS patients. This marker could potentially be used in clinical practice due to its convenience.

Keywords: Acute respiratory distress syndrome, biomarker, neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio, prognostic marker, retrospective study

Ayushi Sinha, Rajat Bajaj, Mrinal Sircar

Department of Pulmonology and Critical Care, Fortis Hospital, Noida, Uttar Pradesh, India

Background: Non-small-cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is a prevalent form of lung cancer, accounting for 85% of cases. Approximately 25% of NSCLC cases harbour oncogenic alterations, offering potential targets for therapy. ROS-1 rearrangement, a less common oncogenic driver, is identified in 0.9-2.6% of NSCLC cases. These rearrangements are more frequently observed in women, non-smokers, and younger individuals. This case report presents an unusual occurrence of ROS-1 rearranged NSCLC in an elderly male smoker, who demonstrated a positive response to Selpercatinib, a highly selective RET inhibitor. At the time of treatment, Selpercatinib was considered a second-line therapy for RET+ NSCLC. However, following the LIBERTTO trial, it has received FDA approval as a first-line treatment option.

Case: A 67-year-old male with a substantial smoking history presented with RET+ adenocarcinoma of the lung. Initial imaging revealed a mass lesion in the upper mediastinum and metabolically active lesions in the right lung. Biopsy confirmed metastatic adenocarcinoma. Unexpectedly, genetic testing identified an ROS-1 rearrangement. The patient received initial chemotherapy, but later transitioned to Selpercatinib. This treatment led to significant tumor regression and improved his life span.

Conclusion: The successful treatment of this unusual case with Selpercatinib aligns with the findings of the LIBERTTO trial, which led to its FDA approval as a first-line treatment for RET+ NSCLC. This case suggests that Selpercatinib may also be effective in treating NSCLC patients with ROS-1 rearrangements, particularly those who are elderly and have a history of smoking.

N. Aditya Reddy, Medha Bargaje, Pramod Chaudhari, Anubhuti Singh

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Bharati Vidyapeeth Medical College and Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Chronic Respiratory Diseases (CRDs) is one of five NCDs that contribute to highest mortality and morbidity globally. CRDs are frequently associated with multiple comorbidities. Pneumococcal and Influenza infection cause significant increase in recurrent hospitalisations, disease burden, mechanical ventilation and mortality in CRD patients, particularly among those with multiple comorbidities. Several studies and international guidelines recommend influenza and pneumococcal vaccines. This study aims to determine factors influencing acceptance of vaccines in CRD patients.

Methods: A cross sectional study was conducted over period of 6 months from June 2024 to November 2024 in Department of Respiratory Medicine, BHRC, Pune. 54 patients were enrolled in the study. All clinically diagnosed patients of chronic respiratory diseases who are above 18 years were included in the study. All participants provided informed consent.

Results: Out of 54 patients, 58% were Male. Mean Age was 63.85. The main comorbidities were Hypertension (44%), Diabetes Mellitus (46%) and heart disease (11%). Influenza and Pneumococcal vaccine coverage was 43% and 18% respectively. Of these, 84% of influenza vaccinated group and 100% of pneumococcal vaccinated group were previously recommended by doctors to obtain vaccine. Vaccine coverage was higher in ex-smokers (66.6%), education level above high school (38%) and history of prior exacerbations, hospitalisations (56%). Vaccinated group had higher proportion of pre-existing comorbidities (72%) compared to non-vaccinated group. Majority of influenza vaccinated group had also received pneumococcal vaccine.

Conclusion: Physicians and Pulmonologists may have to communicate the benefits of vaccination more effectively to patients with chronic respiratory diseases. Pneumococcal and Influenza vaccine inclusion under national immunisation programmes may improve acceptance rates.

Keywords: Acceptance rate, chronic respiratory diseases, influenza, pneumococcal

Krunal Sidapara

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Sheth L. G. General Hospital, Ahmedabad, Gujarat, India

Introduction: Endobronchial tuberculosis (EBTB) is a form of tuberculosis that affects the airways, leading to inflammation, obstruction, and symptoms such as coughing and wheezing. It can complicate pulmonary tuberculosis and may be challenging to diagnose due to its subtle presentation.

Case Report: I am presenting the case of a 14-year-old male patient admitted with complaints of fever with chills for the last 2 days, bilateral chest pain for the last 2 days, and generalized weakness for the last 2 days. Past and family histories did not reveal anything significant. A chest X-ray (PA view) of this patient suggested right middle zone consolidation. CRP was elevated, and total WBC counts were also raised, so a possible diagnosis of pneumonia was suspected. A sputum sample was tested using the TrueNAT method, and the result was negative. A sputum sample was also sent for culture and sensitivity, which showed normal flora. The patient was started on empirical antibiotic therapy but did not improve symptomatically after one week of treatment. HRCT Thorax was done, which showed consolidation with surrounding nodular opacities in the lateral segment of the right middle lobe. Bronchoscopy showed narrowing in the right middle lobe of the right bronchial tree. BAL was performed and sent for BAL CBNAAT, which tested negative for Mycobacterium tuberculosis. A biopsy was also taken and sent for HPE, which revealed moderate to dense chronic active nonspecific inflammation. Despite inconclusive results from all investigations, the patient was clinically diagnosed with endobronchial tuberculosis and started on anti-tuberculosis treatment. On follow-up after 15 days, the patient showed symptomatic improvement, and after 2 months, the patient’s chest X-ray also showed improvement.

Conclusion: Endobronchial tuberculosis is a rare yet critical manifestation of a common disease that requires heightened clinical vigilance. Its non-specific symptoms often mimic other respiratory conditions, leading to delayed diagnosis and potential complications, such as airway stenosis. Prompt diagnosis via bronchoscopy and timely treatment are essential to prevent long-term airway damage.

Introduction: Fibrosing Mediastinitis or sclerosing mediastinitis, is an uncommon, benign and progressive condition characterised by an invasive proliferation of fibrous tissue within the mediastinum. It affects persons aged between 13 and 65 years, with a strong predilection for young females. We report an unusual case of a 22-year-old male with fibrosing mediastinitis presenting as superior mediastinal mass causing dysphagia, cough and hoarseness of voice.

Case Report: 22 years male, presented with dry cough, dysphagia, exertional dyspnea, hoarseness of voice and weight loss since 1 year. Immunohistochemistry and ZN staining was negative. Serum IgG4 was within normal limits.

Conclusions: Fibrosing Mediastinitis should be considered in the differential diagnosis when a young patient presents with symptoms of mediastinal compression.

Mohd Aateer Shaikh, Radha Munje, Sanjay Gour

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Indira Gandhi Government Medical College, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Sjögren’s syndrome, a systemic autoimmune disease, can rarely present with motor-predominant polyneuropathy mimicking Guillain–Barré syndrome (GBS). We describe a case of Sjögren’s syndrome initially manifesting as acute motor polyneuropathy resembling GBS, later progressing to chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (CIDP). This rare overlap highlights the need for detailed clinical evaluation and diagnostic studies, including electrophysiology and autoimmune markers, to ensure timely diagnosis and treatment. Early immunomodulatory therapy is crucial to managing these complex, overlapping conditions effectively.

Case Report: A 19-year-old female, presented with progressive limb weakness over 3 months (ascending paralysis), low-grade fever for 5 months (high-grade for 15 days), breathlessness at rest, generalized body swelling, and intermittent vomiting. She had been hospitalized 3 months earlier for similar symptoms and was diagnosed with GBS based on MRI and nerve conduction studies, treated with intravenous steroids and plasmapheresis. On admission, she had hypotension, tachycardia, hypoproteinemia, cardiomegaly with pleural effusion, and metabolic acidosis. Investigations revealed albuminocytologic dissociation in CSF, ANA positivity, and SSA/SSB antibodies. A lip biopsy confirmed Sjögren’s syndrome. Diagnosed with CIDP secondary to Sjögren’s, she was treated with steroids, diuretics, and physiotherapy, resulting in clinical improvement at discharge. This case highlights the overlap of autoimmune and immune-mediated neuropathies, requiring multidisciplinary management.

Conclusions: Early recognition of atypical neurological manifestations in autoimmune diseases like Sjögren’s syndrome is crucial. Timely diagnosis and multidisciplinary management can significantly improve outcomes in rare overlaps like CIDP.

This case illustrates the rare occurrence of CIDP in a patient with Sjögren’s syndrome, initially presenting as GBS. It emphasizes the need for a thorough clinical evaluation, including electrophysiological studies, serological testing, and tissue biopsy, to accurately diagnose overlapping autoimmune and immune-mediated conditions. Early initiation of immunomodulatory treatment, such as steroids, is essential in managing complex cases to prevent progression and improve clinical outcomes.

Shraddha Acharya, Basava S J, N. Huliraj, M. K. Raghavendra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Kempegowda Institute of Medical Sciences, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Background: Multiple myeloma and plasmacytoma are disorders characterised by abnormal proliferation of plasma cells.Plasmacytoma is typically a localised tumour composed of plasma cells, whereas Multiple Myeloma is marked by the uncontrolled proliferation of plasma cells in the bone marrow, leading to widespread systemic damage affecting various organs.In our case study, we highlight this unique overlap of plasmacytoma with Multiple Myeloma.

Case Report: A 77year old man presented with right side shoulder pain,chest pain and exertional breathlessness that had persisted for one month.High resolution computed tomography (HRCT) Thorax revealed a heterogeneous enhancing lesion in the right upper lobe.A CT-guided biopsy was performed, and histopathological examination showed CD-138 positive plasma cells,suggestive of Plasmacytoma.Serum Protein electrophoresis revealed the presence of a M spike and elevated beta-2 microglobulin levels.A bone marrow aspiration was performed, which had features consistent with Multiple Myeloma. Chemotherapy was initiated based on the working diagnosis of multiple myeloma with plasmacytoma.

Conclusions: Patients with plasmacytoma typically present with focalized bony pain, and backpain being one of the most common symptom associated with multiple myeloma.However, backpain was absent in this patient.Imaging studies, such as MRI and CT scans, are crucial for the diagnosis in such cases.In patients with Multiple myeloma, histopathological evaluation and bone marrow aspiration are essential for confirming the diagnosis and guiding further management.

The case report underscores the importance of early evaluation of patient with unexplained shoulder pain, as the final diagnosis may be Multiple Myeloma.Early diagnosis and prompt initiation of chemotherapy, along with supportive care can significantly improve the prognosis and quality of life for patients with Multiple Myeloma.

P. Smitha, B. P. Rajesh

Department of Respiratory Medicine, JJM Medical College, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Mercury is a heavy metal known to cause harmful effects on various organ systems. It exists in 3 forms - Elemental mercury, Organic mercury, and Inorganic mercury compounds. Mercury toxicity is hazardous when inhaled, intravenously administered or ingested and can result from both occupational and non-occupational exposure. The common manifestations are fever, gastrointestinal distress, respiratory complications, neurological abnormalities, nephrotoxicity and dermatological manifestations.

Case Report: A 38 years old female came with complaints of non-productive cough for 10 days with no significant past history and comorbidities. On examination Vitals-WNL, RS- B/L rhonchi. Patient reassessed with history and gave history of taking medications for infertility from the local traditional folk practitioner two tablets twice daily with betelleaf juice and cowmilk from past 1 months. Composition of medications showed Swarna (Gold), PARADA (Mercury), Gandhaka (Sulphur) in 1:8:16 ratio. HRCTthorax showed high density opacity with classical diffraction. CBC, RFT, LFT – WNL. Blood mercury level 46mcg/L, Urine mercury level – 14mcg/L increased. A multidisciplinary approach made to rule out other systemic manifestation but no obvious findings. Patient started with Dimercaprol injection 150 mg twice daily im for 11 days and repeat blood and urine mercury level monitored showed improvement.

Conclusions: A rare yet serious medical illness occurred with unintentional ingestion of mercury containing medications. This case study illustrates the complexities involved in diagnosing and treating rare manifestations of mercury toxicity. The presentation of symptoms depends significantly on the form of mercury, the method of exposure and the amount involved.

This case report demonstrates the diagnostic and therapeutic challenges in managing a patient with mercury poisoning. The patients’ recovery relied on an interdisciplinary approach and timely medical interventions, highlighting the significances of coordinated care in managing toxicological emergencies.

Background: The anterior mediastinum is a site of diverse pathological entities ranging from benign and malignant neoplasms to infectious and inflammatory conditions each presenting unique diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. Fungal infections of the mediastinum are rare, they can occur in the context of immunosuppression or as an unusual presentation of systemic fungal disease. These infections are often misdiagnosed due to their nonspecific clinical and radiological manifestations, leading to potential delays in appropriate treatment.

Case Report: 39 year old male presented with h/o cough of 4 months duration, followed by left sided pleuritic chest pain for 3 months, hoarseness of voice, loss of weight and loss of appetite, features of superior vena caval obstruction like dilated veins over chest and left upper limb. Resham Agarwal, D. Pragati Rao

Department of Respiratory Medicine, M S Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Acute exacerbations of COPD (AECOPD) are a leading cause of hospitalization and mortality. Identifying early and accessible biomarkers to predict clinical outcomes like length of hospitalization (LoH), requirement for Non-Invasive Ventilation (NIV), mechanical ventilation (MV), and in-hospital mortality are essential. This study evaluates the predictive role of Eosinopenia, NAPR, NLR in AECOPD patients for early identification of high-risk patients.

Methods: 50 patients hospitalised for AECOPD were included in the study. Inflammatory biomarkers (eosinopenia, NAPR, NLR) were measured on admission. Clinical outcomes—LoH, NIV requirement, need for Mechanical Ventilation, and in-hospital mortality—were recorded. Cox regressions determined the associations between NPAR, NLR, eosinopenia with all-cause mortality and other clinical outcomes. Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve analysis estimated the predictive performances of these biomarkers for the clinical outcomes.

Results: Eosinopenia predicted a 30% higher risk of mortality (p = 0.02). Higher NAPR correlated with prolonged LoH and increased intubation rate (OR = 1.8, 95% CI: [1.3-2.5], p = 0.02). NLR ≥ 13.2 predicted in-hospital death with the sensitivity of 100% (95% CI 35.9-100%) and specificity of 92.6% (95% CI 88.8-95.4%).

Conclusion: NAPR, NLR and Eosinopenia are reliable tools for predicting clinical outcomes in AECOPD. They are simple, cost-effective, and will guide early interventions improving patient outcomes. This will result in the reduction of the financial burden caused by COPD exacerbations on patients and the healthcare system.

Background: Tuberculosis is a chronic infectious condition still rampant in many parts of India. Vandana Das1, Sapna Madas2, Monica Barne2, Monika Dhawale2, Ashfaque Shaikh2, Prasad Shinde2, Asmita Gaikwad2, Abhishek Sabale2, Sundeep Salvi2

1Chest Research and Training Pvt. Ltd., Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Preserved Ratio Impaired Spirometry (PRISm) is a novel spirometry index with significant implications for diagnosing and managing respiratory conditions. It is defined by a normal FEV1/FVC ratio but reduced FEV1 and/or FVC.

Objective: This study aims to evaluate the prevalence and impact of PRISm, along with associated factors, in community health screening camps in urban slums and rural areas of western India.

Methods: We organized community health screening camps under the Pollution Awareness & Wellness Assessment (PAWAN) initiative. Participants completed a paper-based questionnaire to collect demographic information, respiratory symptoms, and risk factors such as smoking and environmental exposures. Health screenings included measurements of height, weight, BMI, blood pressure, oxygen saturation, blood sugar, hemoglobin levels, and pre-bronchodilator spirometry. PRISm was identified based on FEV1/FVC ≥0.70 with FEV1 <80% predicted and/or FVC <80% predicted.

Results: Out of 3862 participants, 1759 (45.54%) reported respiratory symptoms or a family history of asthma/allergies. Spirometry was performed on 1230 individuals, revealing a 9% prevalence of obstructive airway disease (OAD) and a 24% prevalence of PRISm among the remaining 1114 subjects. Detailed phenotypic characteristics of PRISm, OAD, and normal lung function groups are outlined in Table 1, which highlights differences in age, gender, smoking status, lung function, and associated health conditions. PRISm with FVC <80% predicted exhibited more severe lung function impairment. Increased prevalence of hypertension and anemia was noted in PRISm groups compared to normal and OAD groups.

Phenotypic characteristics between normal, preserved ratio impaired spirometry and obstructive airway disease

PRISm: Preserved ratio impaired spirometry, OAD: Obstructive airway disease, FVC: Forced vital capacity, FEV1: Forced expiratory volume in 1 s

Conclusion: In our screening camps, 66.6% of individuals with respiratory symptoms had normal spirometry, 24% had PRISm, and 9.4% had OAD. PRISm was linked to heightened respiratory symptoms and small airway evidence. One-third of PRISm cases had FEV1 <80% predicted, indicating potential early or future OAD. PRISm was also associated with symptoms such as breathlessness, cough, wheezing, and health conditions like asthma, hypertension, anemia, and diabetes. These findings underscore the need for further research to understand PRISm’s implications and to develop targeted interventions for affected populations.

Sathiya Palani, Khyati Shamaliya, Grinish Tamakuwala

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Government Medical College, Surat, Gujarat, India

Background: Pulmonary alveolar microlithiasis (PAM) is a rare hereditary disease of abnormal phosphate transport associated with accumulation of calcium phosphate crystas within the alveolar airspaces of the lung with predominance to mid and lower zone. The disease is often discovered incidentally in asymptomatic patients and tends to progress slowly, often resulting in respiratory insufficiency in middle age. Attempts to treat PAM empirically have been uniformly disappointing, and lung transplantation remains the only remedy for end- stage disease. The discovery that mutation in the Sodium phosphate co-transporter gene SLC34A2 cause PAM.

Case Study: A 27 Years old male Patient came to our tertiary hospital With C/o breathlessness on exertion for last 3 month, chest pain on & off for one month and low grade fever with fatigue for 1 month. He was a smoker. There was no known past and 1family history of respiratory disease. Patient was vitally stable and apart from RS finding (BL crepts) all the clinical examination was normal. Blood biochemistry including Calcium, phosphorus concentration, hepatic, renal and parathyroid functions was found as normal. Radiological investigation CXR shows infiltrates as fine sand like calcific micronodules also called “sandstorm lung “ diffusely involving both lungs, most marked in middle and lower zones. Chest HRCT shows soft dense calcification involving peripheral pleura along septal thickening and diffuse GGO & “Black Pleura Sign “overall all findins s/o Pulmonary Alveolar Microlithiasis. PFT showed a mild restrictive pattern. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid examination showed no microliths. Histopathological examination of a transbronchial lung biopsy revealed numerous concentric calcified concretions.

Conclusion: Pulmonary alveolar microlithiasis is a rare lung disease with an autosomal recessive trait with mutation of SLC34A2 gene. Characteristic chest radiography and CT finding along with lung biopsy confirms the diagnosis. There is no effective medical therapy. The long -term prognosis is poor and respiratory failure is the usual cause of death. Need of lung transplantation is warranted at some stage of progression of the disease.

S. Madas1, M. Barne1, V. Das2, M. Dhawale1, A. Shaikh1, P. Shinde1, A. Gaikwad1, A. Sabale1, S. Salvi1

1Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 2Chest Research and Training Pvt. Ltd., Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Small airways obstruction (SAO) is a common feature of obstructive lungdiseases. SAO is the earliest manifestation of COPD even before symptoms occur and lung function decline. In Asthma SAO is a distinct phenotype that is difficult to treat. We aimed to study the prevalence of small airways obstruction on Spirometry and study the variables associated with it in a population-based seƫng among subjects with respiratory symptoms.

Methods: As part of the Pollution Awareness and Health Assessment (PAWAN) project, community-level health camps were organized to raise pollution awareness and evaluate lung health. An interviewer administered lung health questionnaire was administered to collect demographic information and potential risk factors for lung diseases. Spirometry was performed using NDD spirometer according to ATS/ERS standards in subjects reporting respiratory symptoms or clinical examination.

Results: Among 3,862 participants, 1,396 (36.1%) reported respiratory symptoms such as cough, breathlessness, and wheezing. A family history of atopy was reported by 11.6% of participants, while 4.7% reported a past history of asthma or tuberculosis. Spirometry was conducted in 1,230 participants (33.4%) based on the presence of symptoms, medical history, or findings from clinical examinations by physicians. Among those whounderwent spirometry, 56.7% showed evidence of small airways obstruction, defined asFEF25-75 <65% predicted. The prevalence of small airways obstruction was significantlyassociated with hypertension [OR=1.8 (1.4, 2.5)], respiratory symptoms[OR=2.4 (1.9, 3.1)], afamily history of atopy [OR=2.2 (1.6, 3.0)], past H/O asthma or tuberculosis [OR=5.6 (3.2, 9.8)], multimorbidity (defined as presence of Anaemia / HTN/Diabetes / Skin or Heart condition /Kidney disease/any cancer) [OR=2.7 (2.0, 3.6)], anaemia [OR=1.4 (1.1, 1.7)], diabetes [OR=1.8 (1.3, 2.5)] and female gender [OR=1.9 (1.5, 2.4)].

Conclusion: The prevalence of small airways obstruction (SAO) is high among subjects havingrespiratory symptoms and is strongly associated with hypertension, female gender, atopy, andpast asthma or tuberculosis highlights the need for awareness, early detection, and effectivemanagement to prevent chronic respiratory disease progression.

Keywords: Allergy and airways diseases (Asthma and COPD), small airways obstruction (SAO)

Introduction: Rheumatoid arthritis is a systemic inflammatory disorder that most commonly affects the joints, causing Progressive, symmetric, erosive destruction of cartilage and bone, which is usually associated with Autoantibody production, primarily affecting joints, but also has significant extra-articular manifestations. Pulmonary complications include Parenchymal disease-Interstitial lung disease; Pleural disease-Pleural effusion, Pneumothorax, Bronchopleural fistula, Trapped lung syndrome, Airway obstruction, Cricoarytenoid arthritis, Bronchiectasis, Follicular bronchiolitis, Obliterative (constrictive) bronchiolitis, Nodules- Rheumatoid nodules, Caplan syndrome, Vascular disease- Rheumatoid vasculitis, Pulmonary hypertension. While spontaneous pneumothorax in RA is uncommon, it may result from the rupture of subpleural rheumatoid nodules. This report describes a rare presentation of this complication in a 60-year-old female.

Case Report: A 60-year-old female with a 25-year history of seropositive RA presented to the Out patient department with Dyspnoea on exertion and right-sided pleuritic chest pain since three months. Her RA had been moderately controlled with hydroxychloroquine and leflunomide. She had no history of smoking or known pulmonary disease. On examination, she was having Respiratory rate: 20-22 breaths/min, Oxygen saturation: 95 % on room air, Decreased breath sounds and hyper resonance over the right hemithorax. Chest X-ray: Revealed a right-sided pneumothorax. High-resolution CT scan revealed: Few thick walled peripherally distributed cavitatory nodules in bilateral lower lobes with Moderate amount of pneumothorax with e/o few thin septation in right pleural cavity with underlying partial collapse of right lung parenchyma and resultant contralateral shift of mediastinum and heart towards left side due to rupture of subpleural cavitatory rheumatoid nodule. Laboratory tests: Rheumatoid factor (RF): Elevated, Anti-CCP antibodies: Positive, CRP and ESR Normal.

As patient was vitally stable, no immediate intervention was done. Watch for respiratory distress was advised. Patient’s PFT showed Obstructive pattern with Good Post Bronchodilator Reversibility and hence started on Inhaler. Patient was Continued on hydroxychloroquine and leflunomide after multidisciplinary review.

Conclusion: This case underscores the need for early imaging in RA patients presenting with respiratory symptoms. Prompt recognition and management can prevent morbidity and improve patient outcomes. Regular follow-up and imaging are crucial in patients with known pulmonary nodules.

Introduction: The prevalence of multimorbidity in India is approximately 32% (Patel et al. Beyond additive effects, multiple coexisting conditions often interact with each other that leads to a compounded impact on patients’ health- related quality of life (HRQoL). We aimed to study the burden of multimorbidity, lung health and its association with various factors in the adult general population.

Methods: As part of the Pollution Awareness and Health Assessment (PAWAN) project, community health camps were conducted to raise awareness about pollution and assess lung health. Lung condition and its association with various symptoms and risk factors

Conclusion: The study highlights a high burden of acute and chronic health conditions, with 26% of participants reporting lung-related issues. Aim/Objective: The aim of this case report was to describe a case of Bronchial artery embolization for post-tuberculosis cystic bronchiectasis and to provide a review of the post embolization symptoms and the patient’s quality of life following the embolisation.

Multiple episodes of hemoptysis lead to multiple hospitalisations over the course of 3 yrs but to no avail. Then the patient was advised bronchial artery embolization. After proper counselling and evaluation he underwent bronchial artery embolization which improved his quality of life and the patient is asymptomatic since then.

Results: After Bronchial Artery Embolization, there is a significant improvement of patient’s quality of life and reduction of symptoms.

Conclusions: Bronchial Artery Embolization leads to significant improvement in patient’s quality of life.

Ajal Shaji

Aster Medcity, Kochi, Kerala, India

Introduction: Thoracoscopy first emerged as a diagnostic tool in the early 20th century when therapeutic pneumothorax became the surgical treatment of choice for tuberculosis. Currently, the role of medical thoracoscopy in the treatment of undiagnosed pleural effusions is increasingly being recognized.

Methods: We analyzed prospective data of 50 thoracoscopic procedures performed at our center on patients with undiagnosed exudative pleural effusions in 6 months. Patients were subjected to medical thoracoscopy based on ultrasonographic stratification and a computed tomography (CT) thorax. Medical thoracoscopy was performed after an intercostal block under conscious sedation with midazolam (2 mg) and fentanyl (50 mcg) and local anesthesia with lignocaine 2% (10–15 ml), through a semirigid thoracoscope.

Results: A total of 50 patients (28 males and 22 females) underwent medical thoracoscopy during the study period. The mean age was 56.96 ± 14.84 (range, 22–88). The final diagnosis by thoracoscopy was bacterial empyema in 8 patients, tuberculosis in 13 patients and malignancy in 19 patients. Medical thoracoscopy was successful without subsequent intervention in 48, two patients underwent a second procedure, in the form of decortication. There were no major procedure-related complications that required intervention.

Conclusion: Medical dogma teaches: “the sun should never rise nor fall on a pleural effusion.” Although this is an overly simplistic view, the basic principle that a new pleural effusion needs prompt and thorough evaluation still holds. Medical thoracoscopy is a minimally invasive and cost-effective procedure in patients with exudative pleural effusions who have been stratified carefully using radiological methods. Thoracoscopy is recommended for the patient with an undiagnosed pleural effusion in whom the diagnosis of malignancy or tuberculosis is suspected, and in whom at least one pleural fluid cytology and one pleural fluid marker for tuberculosis (adenosine deaminase or interferon) have been negative.

Keywords: Pleural effusion, thoracoscopy, tuberculosis

Anoop Ponnachan

Grant Government Medical College and Sir JJ Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The increasing global burden of tuberculosis (TB) and the emergence of multidrug-resistant (MDR) TB have necessitated the use of second-line anti-TB drugs. According to the WHO World TB Fact Sheet, the estimated proportion of new TB cases with MDR/RR-TB decreased from 4.1% in 2015 to 3.2% globally. Linezolid, an antituberculosis drug from the oxazolidinone class, acts as a protein synthesis inhibitor[1] and has been found to be highly effective in MDR-TB cases. Initially, resistance to Linezolid was observed in 1.9% of Mycobacterium tuberculosis strains. A 2019 study from India reported high sensitivity (97%) and low resistance (3%) to Linezolid in MDR-suspected patients.[2] However, a recent study from Mumbai indicated 6.7% resistance in MDR-TB cases.[3]

Objective: This study aims to evaluate Linezolid resistance in patients attending a tertiary care center in western India.

Methods: This cross-sectional study was conducted at a tertiary healthcare center in Mumbai, India. Data were collected from diagnosed and follow-up cases of MDR and pre-XDR TB with drug susceptibility testing (DST) reports. Patients visiting the outpatient department were included in the study.

Results: Out of 629 patients (314 males, 315 females) who underwent Linezolid DST, 72 (11.2%) were found to be resistant. Of these, 25 (34%) had a history of TB exposure, and 15 (20%) had prior exposure to anti-tuberculosis treatment (ATT). Interestingly, the majority of cases showed no prior exposure to Linezolid, suggesting primary resistance.

Conclusions: Global meta-analyses estimate Linezolid resistance at approximately 4%.[4] Previous studies from India have reported resistance rates of 3% in Gujarat (2019)[2] and 6.7% in Mumbai (2020).[3] Compared to these findings, our study reveals a significantly higher resistance rate of 11% in Mumbai during 2022–2024.

While Linezolid remains an effective drug for MDR-TB treatment, the increasing resistance observed in this study underscores the need for routine DST for all diagnosed TB cases in Mumbai, India, to ensure appropriate and effective treatment strategies.

Keywords: India, Linezolid, MDR, Mumbai, Resistance, XDR

Background: Asthma is characterized by variable symptoms of wheeze, shortness of breath, chest tightness and cough, and by variable expiratory airflow limitation.
