Sushmita Vinod

Saveetha Medical College and Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Prognostic research in exacerbations requiring hospitalization has been limited, and there appears to be little common ground between predictors of mortality in stable disease and during AECOPD. The study aims to compare the prognostic value of the DECAF score, modified DECAF score, BAP 65 score and the 2008 score in predicting in-hospital mortality of patients with AECOPD.

Methods: Analytical prospective study conducted among 70 patients admitted in a state of acute exacerbation of COPD according to GOLD guidelines supported by spirometric evidence of airflow obstruction, in a tertiary care hospital.

Results: The sensitivity of 2008 score, DECAF and modified DECAF score was 100% in our study whereas the sensitivity of BAP-65 was 83.3%. The specificity of BAP-65 is highest when compared to all other scores and it was around 94.8%. Comparing the AUROC curve among the 4 studied scores, we found that the second highest AUROC curve was that of the BAP-65 score (0.948).

Conclusions: The ideal scoring system should be sensitive, specific, easy to perform and readily available on admission to facilitate the quick diagnosis and treatment. BAP 65 score satisfies all these requirements and emerges as a superior tool in predicting the mortality of AECOPD.

BAP-65 score can be used as a tool for evaluating and predicting the patient mortality based on minimum number of invasive tests and initial status of presentation of the patient. The BAP-65’s components are virtually impartial. This emphasizes that this score is consistent and reliable- qualities that are essential for any potential clinical tool. We find that compared to the BAP-65 score had a greater specificity, higher AUROC, and thus, a better predictor of in-hospital mortality in AECOPD patients.

Bharat Anil Toshniwal1, Damkondwar Omprasad Bhagwat

Consultant Chest Physician Interventional Pulmonologist and Allergy Specialist, Toshniwal Chest Hospital and Shwas Chest Hospital, Nanded, Maharashtra, India, 2Associate Professor, Department of Community Medicine, Parbhani Medical College, Parbhani, Maharashtra, India

Context: Allergic diseases are a growing global health concern, especially in rapidly urbanizing areas. Marathwada, a region in Maharashtra, India, presents a unique setting to examine the influence of environmental and demographic factors on allergic sensitization.

Aim: To investigate the prevalence and patterns of allergen sensitivities in the Marathwada region using skin prick testing and to examine correlations with demographic factors such as age and gender.

Settings and Design: A cross-sectional study was conducted at a centralized healthcare facility in Marathwada. Participants presenting with allergic symptoms underwent skin prick testing (SPT).

Statistical Analysis Used: Data was collected using a structured questionnaire and analyzed with Microsoft Excel 2021. Descriptive statistics were used to determine allergen prevalence and demographic correlations.

Results: Among 513 participants (54.39% female), the most represented age group was 21–30 years (32.4%). Positive SPT reactions were observed in 71.53% of participants. Mite allergens such as Dermatophagoides pteronyssinus, D. farinae, and Blomia tropicalis showed the highest positivity rates. There was a notable correlation between SPT positivity and elevated serum IgE levels across all age groups.

Conclusions: The study reveals a high prevalence of allergen sensitivity, particularly to mites, in the Marathwada region. These findings underscore the importance of region-specific allergen profiling to inform clinical management and public health strategies.

Keerthana Gnair

Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Extubation timing is crucial in managing mechanically ventilated patients, often based on a spontaneous breathing trial (SBT). However, SBT failure may lead to extubation failure and prolonged ventilation. Currently, no standardized tool reliably predicts SBT outcomes. This study evaluates MV-RDOS as a predictive tool for SBT failure and compares its accuracy with the Rapid Shallow Breathing Index (RSBI). MV-RDOS, if validated, could improve ICU protocols by minimizing SBT failures and enhancing patient care.

Methods: Patients intubated for more than 48 hours were included if they met standard readiness-to-wean criteria. MV-RDOS and RSBI were assessed before the SBT and at 2, 15, and 30 minutes (end) of the trial. Sensitivity, specificity, and AUC were calculated to assess the predictive value of MV-RDOS and its comparison with RSBI.

Results: Among 62 patients (mean age 63.05 ± 15.12 years), A MV-RDOS ≥ 2.06 before SBT predicted failure with 79% sensitivity and 88% specificity (AUC 87%). At 2 minutes, a cutoff ≥ 2.22 had 79% sensitivity, 98% specificity (AUC 90.8%); at 15 minutes, a cutoff ≥ 2.19 achieved 89% sensitivity, 98% specificity (AUC 94.2%); and at 30 minutes, a cutoff ≥ 2.56 had 89% sensitivity, 100% specificity (AUC 90.2%), with all p-values <0.001. At 15 minutes, MV-RDOS predicted success in 42 of 44 normal cases and failure in 17 of 18 abnormal cases. At 30 minutes, it predicted success in 40 of 42 normal cases and failure in 17 of 20 abnormal cases. MV-RDOS showed a significant association with RSBI but demonstrated higher sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy, particularly at 15 and 30 minutes, supporting its reliability.

Conclusions: MV-RDOS outperformed RSBI in detecting early respiratory distress and predicting SBT outcomes. It offers a reliable, sensitive, and feasible approach to refine weaning protocols, reduce unnecessary weaning attempts, and improve extubation success. Multicenter validation is necessary to confirm its utility.

MV-RDOS is a valuable tool for early detection of respiratory distress and predicting SBT outcomes, compared to RSBI. It may be helpful in weaning decisions in ICUs.

Keywords: ICU care, mechanical ventilation, MV-RDOS, RSBI, SBT failure, weaning protocol

Aditi Padki, Priti Patil, Shraddha Kulkarni

Tulip Healthcare, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: ACCP and AACVPR recommends pulmonary rehabilitation as appropriate for patients disabled with respiratory symptoms due to chronic lung disease. Irrespective of the severity of the disease, Pulmonary Rehabilitation (PR) can prove beneficial in improving functional capacity and overall health of patients. Telerehabilitation can also be considered as an option to classic centre-based rehabilitation in patients with barriers like transportation, fixed schedules and financial burden.

Methods: Total no. of patients enrolled for pulmonary rehab in the year 2024 were retrospectively evaluated. A 6-minute walk test and Pulmonary function test were taken in these patients, pre and post rehabilitation. Changes in the outcome measures were assessed post PR to analyse the health condition of the patient.

Results: Out of the 21 patients enrolled for Rehabilitation with chronic lung diseases, 66.7% were female. The average age of patients was 63.33 years. The average no. of rehab sessions completed by patients was 14, with 30 being highest and 04 lowest. Diagnosis included both obstructive (61.9%) and restrictive (38.1%) lung diseases. The average difference in 6-minute walk distance pre and post rehab was 52m and the average improvement in FVC post rehab was 7%. 4 patients out of 21 were on long term oxygen therapy and 2 of them were able to stop oxygen post rehab. Out of the 21 enrolled, nearly 33% (N=07) opted for telerehabilitation.

Conclusions: This analysis reinforces the need for pulmonary rehabilitation in patients suffering from chronic lung diseases for enhancing functional capacity and exercise tolerance. Further research is warranted to investigate the long-term impact of PR on various patient outcomes, including quality of life, healthcare utilization, and disease exacerbations.

Keywords: Pulmonary rehabilitation, tele pulmonary rehabilitation, walk test

Introduction: Birt-Hogg-Dubé Syndrome (BHDS) is a rare autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in the (FLCN) Folliculin gene. It is characterized by pulmonary cysts, spontaneous pneumothorax, renal cysts or tumors, and cutaneous lesions such as fibrofolliculomas. Patients with BHDS are at an increased risk of developing Chromophobe renal carcinoma. Early diagnosis and management are critical to prevent complications like recurrent pneumothorax and renal malignancies.

Case Report: Presenting Complaint: 61 years old Male patient presented with dyspnea for 4 days, later diagnosed as bilateral pneumothorax with a larger left-sided pneumothorax requiring an intercostal drainage (ICD).

Birt-Hogg-Dubé syndrome weaves a high-stakes tapestry of recurrent pneumothorax, RCC risk, vigilant care demands, and the weight of significant morbidity and mortality, thereby urging definitive intervention.

Rahul Kuruvilla, G. N. Srivastava, Vinod Kumar

Heritage Institute of Medical Sciences, Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh, India

Introduction: COPD is defined as a heterogeneous lung condition characterized by chronic respiratory symptoms due to abnormalities of the airways (bronchitis, bronchiolitis) and/or alveoli (emphysema) that cause persistent, often progressive, airflow obstruction. It often coexists with cardiac conditions like pulmonary hypertension, heart failure, arrhythmias, and ischemic heart disease, which worsen prognosis. Heart failure affects 20-70% of COPD patients and increases mortality, highlighting the need for early diagnosis and intervention.

Methods: A cross sectional study was conducted on patients satisfying inclusion criteria. Informed consent was obtained. Patients were evaluated in the OPD, and demographic details, history, and symptoms were recorded. Complete blood tests, chest X-ray, spirometry, and echocardiography were performed. Spirometry results were used to assess COPD severity, graded according to the latest GOLD guidelines, following which cardiac changes secondary to COPD and its association with severity of COPD is assessed by echocardiography.

Results: Total 25 patients were enrolled in this study. Echocardiographic findings were normal in 8 out of 25 subjects (32%). Abnormal echocardiographic findings included Tricuspid Regurgitation (TR) in 17 (68%), Pulmonary Hypertension (PAH) in 14 (56%), Cor Pulmonale in 6 (24%), and RA & RV dilatation in 7 (28%). The most common echocardiographic finding was TR, observed in 17 out of 25 cases (68%), among whom PAH was present in 14 cases (82.6%). The prevalence of mild, moderate, and severe PAH in these cases was 7/14 (47%), 4/14 (31%), and 3/14 (21%), respectively.

Conclusion: Our study revealed that echocardiography is a valuable tool for the early identification of cardiac comorbidities in COPD patients. It highlighted a significant prevalence of pulmonary hypertension, tricuspid regurgitation, cor pulmonale, and right atrial and ventricular dilatation, particularly in those with severe and very severe stages of COPD.

Keywords: Cardiac comorbidities, COPD, echocardiography, pulmonary hypertension, tricuspid regurgitation

Rahul Kuruvilla, G. N. Srivastava, Vinod Kumar

Heritage Institute of Medical Sciences, Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh, India

Introduction: Granulomatosis with polyangiitis is a potentially lethal ANCA associated small vessel vasculitis characterised by triad of upper respiratory tract, lung and kidney involvement. Lung involvement in GPA occurs in 25 -80% of cases.The most common involvement are lung nodules and masses, very often multiple. As there are various clinical presentations the diagnosis can be challenging, and the illness is difficult to distinguish from other diseases such as infection or malignancy.

Case Report: Hospital based case study was done on 52 year old female presented with complaints of shortness of breath, fever and cough of 2 months CT biopsy showed post inflammatory changes. PET CT showed metabolically active multiple variable sized irregular nodules and mass in bilateral lungs. Metabolically active soft tissue component in right maxillary sinus and diffuse FDG avidity in mucosa of right nasal cavity was seen. CECT THORAX showed ill-defined heterogeneously enhancing lesion in bilateral upper lobe, right middle and bilateral posterior segment of right lower lobe lung parenchyma of size 64×50mm. PR3-ANCA (C-ANCA) value was 76.35 AU/ml. CT guided biopsy of lung lesion showed inflammatory cells in histopathology. Patient was managed with methotrexate pulse therapy, prednisolone and supportive measures following which patient showed significant improvement in symptoms and reduction lung mass size.

Conclusions: A lung mass in ANCA-associated vasculitis is rare and can mimic malignancy or infection. Early diagnosis through biopsy and imaging, followed by timely immunosuppressive therapy, ensures effective treatment and better outcomes.

AAV should be included in the differential diagnosis of lung masses, especially in patients with known systemic vasculitis. Prompt diagnosis and tailored immunosuppressive therapy can prevent irreversible organ damage and improve patient outcomes.

K. T. Deviprasad, Manu Chopra, Rahul Tyagi, Kislay Kishore, Aseem Yadav, M. C. Jyothis

Department of Respiratory Medicine, AICTS, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: We present a case report of an adult male, a case of thymic neuroendocrine tumor with endobronchial lesion who presented with features of obstruction collapse with respiratory failure requiring 100% FiO2. He was managed with therapeutic bronchoscopy allowing for patient to be weaned off oxygen support and allowing to be fit for a life saving surgery.

Case Report: A 42 year old male, a known case of metastatic thymic neuroendocrine tumour (NET), diagnosed in 2017, on chemotherapy, presented with rapidly progressive breathlessness and hypoxemia. On evaluation, PET CT revealed FDG avid mass lesion with irregular margin (4.5 x 6.1 x 4 cc) in middle mediastinum and a FDG avid perihilar lesion (3 X4.2 X 3.9cc) with collapse of distal RML. Video-bronchoscopy showed a fleshy endobronchial lesion completely occluding the right main bronchus. Endobronchial biopsy of the same revealed well-defined NET, Grade I. Patient was declared unfit after assessment by onco-surgeon and anaesthesiologist in view of severe hypoxemia and high FiO2 requirement. We performed therapeutic bronchoscopy as a high risk procedure was done using electrocautery fulguration and cryo-canalisation of the endobronchial lesion. Minor complication was faced in the form of minimal bleeding which was controlled using local measures. There was signification resolution of obstructive symptoms and hypoxemia and he was weaned off oxygen support. The intervention ensured patient could be taken up for life-saving surgery and also improved his quality of life.

Conclusions: Therapeutic bronchoscopy using cryotherapy and electrocautery can be used as a life-saving intervention to relieve endobronchial obstruction in malignant lesions of airway leading to positive outcomes.

Preetha Senthilkumar

Sri Ramachandra Institute of Higher Education and Research, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is the third leading cause of mortality. Non-pharmacological management, such as vaccination, smoking cessation, and pulmonary rehabilitation (PR), play a crucial role in managing COPD. PR combines exercise training, education, and psychological support to improve quality of life (QOL) and reduce disability. This study assesses the effectiveness of an 8-week hybrid (in-person and telemonitored) PR program on exercise capacity and QOL in stable COPD patients.

Methodology: This prospective observational study, conducted at a tertiary care centre in Chennai, involved 62 COPD patients meeting specific inclusion criteria. Participants completed an 8-week hybrid PR program with 24 sessions (3 sessions per week): six in-person and the remaining virtual, remotely monitored. COPD Assessment Test (CAT), St. George Respiratory Questionnaire (SGRQ), 6-minute walk distance (6MWD), BODE index, and spirometry were performed before and after intervention and results were compared.

Results: The 6MWD improved by 19.61 meters, from 223.58 to 243.19 meters (p<0.001). CAT and SGRQ scores decreased significantly, from 21.17 to 19.53 and 55.4 to 42.24, respectively (p<0.001). FEV1 increased from 0.96 to 1.34 liters (p<0.042). The BODE index decreased from 5.11 to 4.45 (p<0.002).

Conclusions: The improvements in 6MWD and reduction in CAT and SGRQ scores reflect enhanced exercise capacity and QOL respectively. A reduction in the BODE index suggests better disease outcomes with PR. Statistically significant improvements in 6MWD, CAT and SGRQ scores were seen in COPD GOLD grades 2 and 3, while only SGRQ scores improved in GOLD grade 4, highlighting the benefits of early PR introduction in COPD.

The 8 week hybrid PR program effectively improved exercise capacity, QOL, lung function and prognosis in COPD patients, demonstrating its potential as a valuable intervention in COPD management.

Keywords: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, exercise capacity, pulmonary rehabilitation, quality of life

Introduction: Air pollution, both ambient and indoor, is a major public health issue, causing 6.7 million premature deaths annually. In India, 0.98 million deaths are attributed to ambient air pollution and 0.61 million to indoor pollution. IAP accounts for 17.8% of total deaths, with unclean fuels being a major contributor. In India, there are several other sources of IAP which are usually not studied.

We aimed to understand the health impact of sources of IAP other than unclean fuels.

Methods: Through our Pollution Awareness and Wellness Assessment Initiative (PAWAN) project we conducted a questionnaire based cross sectional study in Pune City. Sources of IAP captured were burning of mosquito coils, incense sticks, dhoop, and loban at least once a day inside the house. We also checked BP, height, weight, random blood glucose and hemoglobin. 1230 participants underwent spirometry.

Results: We administered the health-related questionnaire to 3862 participants of which 1230 underwent spirometry. Average age was 44 +/- 15 years and 60% were females. 93% of the participants resided in urban slums. 41.7% used mosquito coils, 28.7% burnt dhoop batti or loban or camphor and 70.5% burnt incense sticks at least once a day. Saroj Meena, Rohan Malakar

National Institute of Tuberculosis and Respiratory Disease, New Delhi, India

Background: Tuberculosis (TB) is a major cause of mortality and morbidity worldwide, with India accounting for 25% of the global TB burden. Anti-tubercular drugs (ATD) can cause multiple adverse reactions, including hepatitis, flu-like syndrome, and optic neuritis. Cutaneous Adverse Drug Reactions (CADRs) can range from mild itching to severe conditions like toxic epidermal necrolysis (TEN), which may require treatment discontinuation. Drug rash with eosininophilia and systemic syndrome (DRESS) is a less frequent but severe hypersensivity reaction, usually reported with anticonvulsant, allopurinol, NSAIDs, and antibiotics. The prevalence of DRESS ranges from 1 in 1000–10,000 people, with a mortality rate of 10–20%. It is characterized by fever, skin eruption, hematological abnormalities, and systemic organ involvement with a long latency period of 2–8 weeks. Around 60% of tuberculosis patients experience adverse drug reactions, with one-third being Drug Hypersensitivity Reaction (DHR). Combination treatment with four drugs increases the risk of simultaneous MDHS. Some cases of MDHS associated with anti-tuberculosis drugs have been reported, and some patients with MDHS have been included in studies. We describe an unusual case of DRESS syndrome in a young female with drug-sensitive TB. The syndrome was triggered by multiple anti TB medications, presenting challenges in formulating a treatment regimen for the patient.

Aim and Objective: To study and evaluate the adverse drug reactions caused by anti-tubercular drugs.

Methods/Case Study: We present a 32-year-old female patient from Delhi. She had complaints of fever for 3 months, productive cough for 3 months. Loss of weight and Loss of appetite for same duration. She had no history of vomiting, pain abdomen or icterus. The patient was diagnosed with PTB sensitive to Rifampicin and Isoniazid by LPA and started treatment with first-line Anti-tubercular drugs from outside hospital on April 2023. After 17 days, the patient developed fever, pruritus, deranged LFT values, leukocytosis and redness on her torso, which subsequently spread to her limbs. We present the case of a 32-year-old female patient from Delhi, who experienced severe adverse reactions to first-line ATDs. The patient reported a three-month history of fever, productive cough, weight loss, and loss of appetite. She had no history of vomiting, abdominal pain, or jaundice. In April 2023, the patient was diagnosed with pulmonary tuberculosis (PTB) sensitive to Rifampicin and Isoniazid through Line Probe Assay (LPA) and commenced treatment with first-line ATDs at an external hospital. Seventeen days into the treatment, the patient developed fever, pruritus, and elevated liver function test (LFT) values, leukocytosis, and a rash that started on her torso and spread to her limbs. She was hospitalized, and first-line ATDs were discontinued. An induction regimen was initiated, gradually introducing one drug at a time, starting with Isoniazid. After two days of Isoniazid therapy at 150mg, the patient developed an erythematous rash with pruritus. Anti-allergic medications and oral steroids were administered for five days, and Isoniazid was discontinued. Upon resolution of symptoms, Rifampicin was reintroduced incrementally. However, after one day of 150mg Rifampicin therapy, the patient developed another erythematous rash with pruritus. This was again managed with anti-allergic medications and steroids. Pyrazinamide was then introduced incrementally. The patient tolerated a 600mg dose for three days without issues, but similar reactions occurred at higher doses. Pyrazinamide was withheld, and symptoms were managed with anti-allergic drugs and a short course of steroids. Ethambutol was also reintroduced incrementally, but on the second day, the patient developed pruritus and an erythematous rash. This was managed similarly. Throughout this period, the patient’s SGOT and SGPT levels were elevated to more than three times the upper limit of normal. A modified ATT regimen, including Streptomycin, Levofloxacin, and Ethambutol, was attempted. However, the patient again developed erythematous rash and pruritus, leading to the discontinuation of the modified ATT and management with steroids and anti-allergic. DRESS syndrome or ATT-induced hypersensitivity syndrome was suspected due to presence of fever, rashes, and elevated liver enzymes and persistent lymphocytosis. Multiple attempts at sequential reintroduction of ATDs were made at different hospitals, but rashes developed each time. One year after the primary diagnosis, the patient was referred to our hospital and admitted to the department of tuberculosis and respiratory diseases. A repeat sputum LPA confirmed PTB sensitivity to Rifampicin and Isoniazid. Her ATT had been stopped for the past month when she came to us. We initiated a Bedaquiline-based all-oral longer regimen, including Bedaquiline, Levofloxacin, Linezolid, Clofazimine, and Cycloserine after a pre-treatment evaluation. Given the patient’s history of drug-induced skin rash, we decided to introduce the drugs one by one with a gap of three days between each. The patient tolerated Bedaquiline, Levofloxacin, Linezolid, and Clofazimine without issues. However, upon introduction of Cycloserine, the patient developed an erythematous skin rash on the torso and trunk, along with pruritus and fever. Cycloserine was with-held and symptoms were managed with steroid and antihistaminics. Ethionamide was added in replacement of Cycloserine. Patient developed itching and erythematous rash after 1 day of starting Ethionamide. Hence Ethionamide was stopped and inj Amikacin added. After one day patient developed rash, so it was stopped. Delaminid was added in to the regime. This time patient tolerated the drug without any skin reactions. Patient’s final regime comprises of Bedaquiline, Delamanide, Levofloxacin, Linezolid, Cycloserin. ECG and relevant blood investigations were performed on regular interval and occurrence of any skin reaction has been monitored closely. Due to long standing in-adequately treated disease process, patient developed sudden onset hypoxia and left sided chest pain. CXR revealed left sided Pyo-pneumothorax. It was managed with Intercostal Chest Drain with water seal bag. We have suspected HLA association in hypersensitivity reaction when multiple drugs are used. We have sent HLA typing for this patient, although no correlation was found in our case. Patient didn’t show any adverse reactions, improved clinically after starting the individualized treatment regime. Her total leukocyte count reduced to 10000 from 39000 and she is now in room air. Her appetite has improved and physical and mental wellbeing is restored. Her ICD is still working and pus collection is >100ml/day.

Conclusion: ATD induced drug reaction is a very common adverse reaction that may occur during the tenure of treatment of Tuberculosis. We should look for the adverse reactions more closely and meticulously.

Sharvary Rathod, Priti Ghadage, Alpa Dalal, Tarang Kulkarni

Jupiter Hospital, Thane, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Difficult-to-treat asthma (DTA) remains a major clinical challenge, characterized by persistent symptoms despite optimal therapy. This study investigates the factors contributing to DTA, aiming to enhance management strategies and patient outcomes.

Methods: 78 patients aged 18-70 with poorly controlled asthma were recruited in this one-year observational study in the Pulmonary Medicine Department of Jupiter Hospital. Inclusion criteria focused on patients with poor asthma control despite maximal treatment, while excluding those with other chronic respiratory diseases or cognitive impairments.

Spirometry: 61% patients had not undergone spirometry, limiting comprehensive asthma management

GINA assessment : In this cohort, 46% patients were in step 3 having moderate asthma and 32% were in step 4 and 5 requiring high dose therapies for severe asthma.

Amol Suryawanshi, Ketaki Utpat, Unnati Desai

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, TNMC and BYL Nair Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Patients with tuberculosis though rare can present with respiratory emergencies in certain circumstances like massive hemoptysis, respiratory failure and pulmonary thromboembolism being few of them. Stigma associated with tuberculosis is still common in many societies contributing to delays in treatment seeking and treatment disparities.

Case Report: A 38 years old male known diabetic was admitted in Chest Medicine RICU with type 1 respiratory failure. He hailed from Rajasthan and worked in Mumbai. His co-workers gave history of acute onset of symptoms for 5-6 days with complaints of dyspnea MMRC grade III – IV, cough with expectoration associated with hemoptysis, intermittent fever. He was admitted with the provisional diagnosis of bilateral pneumonia with type 1 respiratory failure with ARDS and initiated on antibiotics, NIV and later HFNC. The radiology was atypical for ARDS and hence a detailed history was sought from the patient when he was little better. He gave history of TB therapy and DM in the past. His relatives who later were available gave history of prolonged ICU care in the past which was treated with ATT but no reports or documents were available. His coworkers were not aware of this history as the patient feared stigmatization inspite of previous complete TB therapy.

Clinical Examination:

His Vitals were

Pulse rate -104/min

Respiratory rate-34/min

SpO2-90% @ HFNC (40 L/Min flow, 40% FiO2)

RS- BSBE bilateral crepitation.

He was evaluated with sputum studies for CBNAAT which came MTB detected medium rifampicin resistance detected. His chest x-ray showed bilateral consolidatory changes [Figure 1]. A computed tomography of chest with pulmonary angiography done post stabilization showed no pulmonary thromboembolism however consolidation with air bronchogram, cavitation and multiple adjacent centrilobular nodules in bilateral lung field, multiple sub centimetric pre and paratracheal, subcarinal and pre vascular lymph nodes largest measuring 1.7 x 0.8 cm, multiple enlarged lymph nodes in bilateral cervical levels II, III, IV, V and in bilateral axillary region largest measuring 3 x 2.2cm in right axilla [Figure 2a-c]. (a-c) Representative axial CT scans

-Tab. BDQ 400 mg OD for 14 days f/b Tab. BDQ 200 mg OD alternate day

-Tab. Linezolid 600 mg OD

-Tab. Levofloxacin 1000 mg OD

-Tab. Clofazimine 100 mg OD

-Tab. Cycloserine 1000 mg OD

Within three days as he was tolerating drugs and stable. He was discharged home with NTEP referral to his hometown and drugs given for a week to continue therapy as he wished to go back to his home town.

Conclusions: Tuberculosis should be kept in mind as a possible etiology in respiratory failure in presence of history, radiological features like cavitation and presence of risk factors like DM. Every attempt should be made to achieve quick diagnosis with microbiological confirmation. Basic ICU care in a case of respiratory failure should be given to all presenting with the same as no patient comes with the labelled diagnosis. In pulmonary tuberculosis, super added secondary bacterial infections or complications like hemoptysis, thromboembolism or pleural complications cause respiratory failure rather than the primary lung parenchymal involvement due to TB disease. Over diagnosis should be avoided as empirical ATT can cause serious adverse events. On stabilization patients can be discharged home and rarely need prolonged institutional care in TB hospitals.

Amit Sharma

National Institute of Tuberculosis and Respiratory Diseases, Sri Aurobindo Marg, New Delhi, India

A 35-year-old lady presented with cough, expectoration and fever for last 2-3 months. Her past history was significant as she had received Anti-Tubercular Chemotherapy 2 times in the past year. Initially she was sputum positive and Line Probe Assay (LPA) showed Isoniazid (H) resistance with Rifampicin (R), Fluoroquinolone (FQ) and second-line injectables (SLI) sensitive. As per guidelines, she was started on a FQ, Levofloxacin (Lfx), R, Ethambutol (E) and Pyrazinamide (Z) which she took for 6 months. Patient improved during treatment but developed cough, expectoration and fever within 15 days of stopping treatment. She was again sputum positive for AFB with same resistance pattern as before (H mono-resistance). She was again started on LRZE for 6 months. However, at the 5th month of treatment, patient developed fever, cough and expectoration. Sputum was again positive for AFB and LPA showed this time H and FQ resistance. R and SLI were both sensitive. Patient’s Chest X Ray had deteriorated and clinically also, she had deteriorated. After detailed analysis, patient was declared a Clinical Failure started on Bedquiline (BDQ), Linezolid (LNZ), Clofazimine (Cfz), Cycloserine (Cyc), Delamanid (Dlm). The patient improved on this regime and after 7 months of treatment now, both her smear and culture is continuously negative and patient is asymptomatic with significant radiological improvement. This case report demonstrates that clinico-radiological failure of ATT in PTB patients may not always be accompanied with microbiological evidence of failure by Drug Susceptibility testing (DST). In this case, DST only revealed H resistance before first and second course of ATT and H and FQ polyresistance at around the end of second course. Yet clinico-radiological features highly favored a treatment failure and second line drugs were initiated such as BDQ and Dlm. The patient responded very well to this regime and she became clinically, radiologically and microbiologically negative.

Conclusions: A judicious and diligent decision making in such complex cases may be life saving for the patient.

Mahavir Bagrecha1, Vikrant Deshmukh2, Sanjay Sathe2, Mahavir Bagrecha2, Swapnil Kadam2, G. Gananathan2, Carlton Periera2, Vaishali Gupte2, Gurmeet Thakur2, Manjushree Naik Patil2

1Cipla Ltd., Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The present survey aimed to understand clinical perceptions and the role of a combination of bilastine and montelukast in the management of allergic rhinitis (AR).

Methods: A digital questionnaire-based survey was conducted from May to October 2024.

Results: Among 445 clinicians (ENT, Chest, Consulting Physicians), most (43.12%) were aged 31-40 years. In practice, 40% reported an AR prevalence of 21-30%, and 37.98% noted that 10-20% of AR patients had concomitant asthma. Sneezing was the most reported AR symptom (67.19%). For diagnosing patients with moderate-to-severe or persistent AR 56.40% of clinicians recommended a skin prick test. For managing mild intermittent/persistent AR, 45.39% preferred antihistamines, while 53.71% favored a combination of antihistamines and leukotriene receptor antagonists for moderate intermittent/persistent AR. Approximately 87% of clinicians preferred the bilastine-montelukast combination for AR management, citing its non-sedative properties, superior nasal congestion control, cardiac safety, and compatibility with renal or hepatic impairments. Around 61% supported its use for various AR patient profiles, including those with asthma, mild to moderate AR with any cardiac or renal dysfunction, moderate to severe AR not controlled with INCS, mild to moderate intermittent AR, and persistent AR. The ideal duration of this combination was reported to be 3-4 weeks by 44% of clinicians. Most HCPs expressed satisfaction with this combination’s efficacy (88.8%) and safety (92.3%). The most reported adverse events were gastrointestinal disturbances and fatigue (30%), followed by headache (22%). Neuropsychiatric events were noted by 7% of clinicians.

Conclusion: The survey demonstrates a combination of bilastine-montelukast was preferred by most clinicians in the management of AR across diverse patient profiles and was found to be effective and well-tolerated.

Keywords: Antihistamine, AR, bilastine montelukast combination, LTRA

Ronak Panwala, Kundan Nivangune, Kamlesh Patel

Lupin Limited

Introduction: >7% of Indian adults are estimated to have COPD, and it is the second leading cause of deaths in India. Amongst various single/dual/triple combination inhalation therapies, Single inhaler triple therapy (SITT) is relatively new concept. This study was conducted to understand COPD burden and usage patterns of triple therapy in management of COPD by Indian respiratory physicians.

Methods: Structured Questionnaire was administered to HCPs through personal interview. Descriptive statistics were applied.

Results: 608 HCPs completed survey. Participant HCPs reported high burden of COPD patients:~50% HCPs reported >40 COPD patients/month. 37% HCPs responded proportion of newly diagnosed COPD patients to be <30%, 38% responded proportion to be 30-50%. Majority HCPs(>50%) preferred COPD treatment per GOLD COPD report, 21% preferred based on experience. 66% HCPs opined their initial pharmacotherapy was guided more as per symptom burden and exacerbation history, rather than airflow obstruction. ~20% HCPs reported Group E COPD patients to be >50% of all patients. 45% HCPs preferred SITT more, 13% preferred MITT, 40% HCPs showed no particular preference. Severe exacerbations, frequent exacerbations, EOS>300 were reported as leading factors to use triple therapy. ~10% HCPs reported concomitant Asthma also an important criterion. 50% HCPs prescribed triple therapy for 3 months, 33% & 16% HCPs prescribed triple therapy for 6 & >6 months respectively.

Conclusion: This study based on survey questionnaire amongst 608 HCPs showed high burden of COPD in Indian respiratory physicians’ practice. It demonstrated the usage of triple therapy (including SITT) in patients with severe/frequent exacerbations, patients with high EOS count. Future real-world studies should be done to investigate patient selection, time-point of initiation, outcomes during triple therapy and after step-down to dual therapy.

Keywords: COPD, GOLD, SITT, triple therapy

Ronak Panwala, Kundan Nivangune, Kamlesh Patel

Lupin Limited

Introduction: Acebrophylline is an airway mucus regulator with anti-inflammatory, mild bronchodilatory, pulmonary surfactant secretory activities, and is widely used in patients with Asthma, COPD, and other respiratory disorders. N-acetylcysteine is a mucolytic agent with antioxidant properties, and also has been found to reduce the incidence of exacerbations in COPD patients. This survey-based study was conducted to identify practice patterns for combination of Acebrophylline + NAC.

Methods: Structured Questionnaire was administered to HCPs through personal interview. Descriptive statistics were applied.

Results: 248 HCPs completed the survey. 75% of participant HCPs reported mucus hypersecretion to be prevalent in 20-60% of patients suffering from asthma/COPD, despite using standard inhalation therapies. 95% HCPs believed mucus hypersecretion to be an independent risk factor for poor outcomes in chronic respiratory disease. 71% HCPs responded that mucoregulators/mucolytics should be integral part of therapy for majority of patients. 60% HCPs reported usage of Acebrophylline+NAC in 30-70% of their COPD patients. Molecular properties such as mucoregulatory (~60% HCPs), mucociliary clearance (35% HCPs), synthesis & release of pulmonary surfactant (~25% HCPs) were leading benefits perceived by HCPs while using Acebrophylline. 85% of HCPs opined that regular use of mucolytic/antioxidant such as NAC can reduce exacerbations and can improve health status. 80% HCPs believed that NAC could also improve efficacy of antibacterial drugs. 44% HCPs predominantly preferred Acebrophylline+NAC in Group B, followed by 28% & 27% HCPs in Group E and Group A COPD patients respectively. Apart from COPD, HCPs reported use of this combination in chronic bronchitis, bronchiectasis, Asthma. 75% HCPs recommended Acebrophylline+NAC two times a days whereas 25% HCPs recommended once a day. Regarding duration of treatment, 45% of HCPs usually prescribed this combination for 2-4 weeks, whereas 28% of HCPs prescribed the combination for 1-3 weeks.

The current survey suggests wide use of add-on therapies such as Acebrophylline + NAC to manage chronic mucus hypersecretion. Whereas mucolytic property can translate in early relief to patients’ symptom, use of such therapy for prevention of exacerbations need longer duration of treatment and follow-up. This study reported 2-4 weeks of duration of treatment with Acebrophylline + NAC combination. Studies can be done to investigate benefit of short (i.e. early post-exaceberbation period) and long term treatment with these agents.

Conclusion: Finding from this 248 Indian Respiratory physicians survey suggests chronic Mucus hypersecretion to be widely prevalent in patients with obstructive airway disease, and pharmacotherapeutic agents such as Acebrophylline + NAC are widely used in practice. >90% of HCPs perceived combination of Acebrophylline + NAC to be better than either agent alone. Prospective studies could be planned to identify right patient profiles, to measure magnitude of patients benefits symptomatically and for lung function, and to assess optimal duration of treatment.

Keywords: Acebrophylline, antioxidant, COPD, mucus, N-acetylcysteine

Putta Sravya Krishna, Divya Balan

Department of Respiratory Medicine, KMC, Manipal, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Lung cancers are divided into sclc and nsclc. Sclc is a neuroendocrine tumor that accounts for 15% of all lung cancers. Sclc occurs predominantly in smokers. Some of the common sites for metastasis include bone brain liver adrenals. Renal metastases is rare.

Case Study: A 56-year-old man presented with complaints of breathing difficulty, cough and pain abdomen for 15 days. Patient had history of significant weight loss. Ultrasound abdomen and pelvis revealed a right renal mass of 16*16 mm. Fnac of mass was suggestive of metastatic deposits from neuroendocrine carcinoma. CT thorax was suggestive of a focal lesion of 14mm in the apical segment of the right upper lobe. Bronchoscopy revealed endobronchial growth in right upper lobe bronchus and biopsy of the lesion was suggestive of neuroendocrine carcinoma of lung.

Carry Home Message: This case is a rare case of neuroendocrine carcinoma of lung presenting as renal mass. Due to its unusual presentation, where the metastatic lesion is bigger than the primary lesion, it can be mistaken for a primary Renal cell carcinoma.

Conclusion: A Thorough evaluation including biopsy of primary and metastatic lesion is of importance in establishing the diagnosis as it will define the further course of treatment.

Keywords: Biopsy, lung, metastases, neuroendocrine carcinoma, renal cell carcinoma

Kavitha Nair

Nizams Institute of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis is a chronic respiratory disease marked by recurrent infections and persistent bronchial inflammation. A prominent feature is heightened neutrophil activity, leading to enzyme-mediated airway damage and accelerated lung function decline. The neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio (NLR), a cost-effective and accessible biomarker, offers a measure of inflammation and immune balance. This study explores the association between NLR, measured at hospital admission, and the severity of bronchiectasis exacerbations.

Methods: A prospective hospital-based study was conducted over 12 months in the Department of Pulmonary Medicine at NIMS, Hyderabad. Adult patients hospitalized for bronchiectasis exacerbation were enrolled. NLR was calculated using absolute neutrophil and lymphocyte counts from blood samples taken within 24 hours of admission. The severity of exacerbation was classified based on the type of respiratory support required, including oxygen therapy and mechanical ventilation (non-invasive or invasive).

Results: A total of 108 patients participated in the study, with a mean age of 47.9 years; 67 (62%) were male. The average BMI was 18.54 ± 4.71 kg/m², and pulmonary hypertension was the most common comorbidity (27.8%). The predominant cause of bronchiectasis was idiopathic (42.6%), followed by post-tubercular sequelae (24.1%). The mean NLR was 7.74 ± 7.48. Patients requiring mechanical ventilation had significantly higher NLR (12.21 ± 10.74) compared to those needing oxygen only (7.01 ± 5.41) or no support (4.64 ± 3.58). A statistically significant positive correlation was observed between NLR and mechanical ventilation requirement (rpb = +0.37, p < 0.01).

Conclusion: NLR correlates positively with disease severity and mechanical ventilation requirements in bronchiectasis exacerbations.

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, exacerbation, mechanical ventilation, NLR

Jeeva Babu

Amala Institute of Medical Sciences, Thrissur, Kerala, India

Introduction: Pulmonary embolism (PE) can present with severe respiratory distress and may be complicated by coexisting haematological disorders, such as autoimmune haemolytic anaemia (AIHA) and immune thrombocytopenia (ITP). This case highlights the diagnostic and therapeutic challenges in a young male patient with PE and concurrent AIHA and ITP.

Case Report: A 37-year-old male, previously healthy, presented with bilateral pneumonia, respiratory failure, and significant shortness of breath. Investigations revealed bilateral pleural effusions, elevated D-dimer, and severe pulmonary artery hypertension. CTPA confirmed PE. Initial treatment included anticoagulation and antibiotics, but the patient showed no improvement, developing symptomatic anaemia and thrombocytopenia. Thrombolysis was performed, and autoimmune workup indicated positive direct Coombs test, leading to the diagnosis of AIHA and ITP. Treatment was escalated with IVIG, rituximab, and steroids.

Conclusion: The case underscores the importance of a multidisciplinary approach to managing complex cases involving PE and haematological disorders. Timely diagnosis and tailored immunosuppressive therapy were crucial in improving the patient’s condition.

Keywords: Autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, immune thrombocytopenia, multidisciplinary approach, pulmonary embolism, thrombolysis

Jeeva Babu

Amala Institute of Medical Sciences, Thrissur, Kerala, India

Background: Rigid bronchoscopy is traditionally preferred for the removal of foreign bodies (FB) lodged in the airways. However, flexible bronchoscopy has shown a high success rate in ideal cases with optimal modality. This study aims to evaluate the effectiveness of flexible bronchoscopy using various accessories for FB removal.

Methods: This retrospective analysis reviewed 3064 bronchoscopic procedures performed over 15 years at a tertiary hospital in central Kerala. Among these, 52 procedures were conducted for FB removal. The fiberoptic bronchoscope (Olympus BF-1T 150) and pediatric rigid bronchoscope (Karl Storz) were utilized. Accessories included toothed and cup forceps, dormia basket, tripod forceps, snare, and cryobiopsy probes.

Results: The incidence of FB aspiration was 1.7% (52/3064). Of the 52 patients, 36 had organic FBs, and 16 had inorganic FBs. Organic FBs included blood clots (47.2%), peanuts (16.7%), chicken bones (8.3%), mucus plugs (5.6%), green peas (2.8%), arecanut (2.8%), garlic (2.8%), sitaphal seeds (2.8%), and ayurveda tablets (2.8%). Inorganic FBs included hairpins (31.2%), teeth (25%), pen tips (12.5%), pen caps (12.5%), LED bulbs (6.3%), nose pins (6.3%), and whistles (6.3%). For organic FBs, toothed forceps were most commonly used (52.8%), followed by tripod forceps (38.9%), cryobiopsy forceps (5.5%), and dormia basket (2.8%). For inorganic FBs, toothed forceps were used in 68.8% of cases, followed by dormia basket (18.8%) and tripod forceps (12.4%). Seventeen patients had life-threatening blood clots requiring emergency intervention.

Conclusion: Flexible bronchoscopy with a multi-modality approach using different accessories is effective for diagnosing and removing FBs in the lower respiratory airways in adults, achieving a high success rate (90%) comparable to rigid bronchoscopy. Rigid bronchoscopy is recommended if flexible bronchoscopy fails, if the FB is centrally located, firmly embedded in scar tissue, or for removing sharp objects to minimize mucosal trauma, especially in pediatric patients. Cryobiopsy is particularly effective for organic FB retrieval. The Fogarty balloon catheter is a valuable aid in preventing distal displacement of FBs during retrieval.

Tamal Bhattacharyya

Department of Respiratory Medicine, IIMSAR and Dr. B. C. Roy Hospital, Haldia, West Bengal, India

Introduction: COPD1 & cardiovascular disease frequently coexist, leading to poor outcomes than each condition alone. Complication includes Pulmonary artery hypertension(PAH), Ischemic heart disease, Ventricular Dysfunction. ECG & Echocardiography provides rapid, non-invasive, cost effective method to detect early cardiovascular complication & management.

Methods: This hospital based cross sectional study involved 114 diagnosed COPD patients from OPD & IPD of Department of Respiratory Medicine & classified by GOLD2 A,B,E classification & mMRC3 grading. Cardiovascular complications were assessed by ECG & echocardiography.

Results: Out of 114 patients ECG findings : IHD-37.72% (p=0.002) ; P-Pulmonale-32.46% (p=0.002); Right Bundle Branch Block-28.07% (p=0.017); Left Anterior Hemiblock-13.16% (p=0.044); P-Pulmonale-32.46% (p<0.001). In Echocardiography : Tricuspid Regurgitation gradient in 35.9%, p<0.001, mean 30.16, SD=8.17; PAH in 44.7% (mild-22.81%, moderate-12.28%, severe-9.65%). ROC analysis showed PASP4 cut off 40.5(sensitivity 87.5%, p<0.011) & TAPSE/PASP5 ratio cut off 0.5(sensitivity 82.65%).

Conclusion: PAH is highly prevalent in advanced COPD significantly elevating mortality and morbidity risks. Early ECG & Echocardiography are crucial for prognosis & management. Routine cardiovascular screening for all COPD patients is recommended.

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) is a common clinical condition presenting in a physicians or pulmonologist practice.While AEs are a chapter in most clinical guidelines, the drug related pharmacokinetics and metabolism are rarely elaborated upon. Isoniazid (INH) acetylating status and its variability amongst different regions and parts of the world is well known, however never elaborated much in day-to-day TB guidelines.

Case Report: There are 3 case reports: (1) A 58 year old lady, history of CA rectum presented with complaints of dyspnea,cough with expectoration and fever of 1 month duration. (2) A 63 year old lady, known case of Interstitial lung disease presented with complaints of increased dyspnea, cough with expectoration and fever of 2 weeks duration. (3) A 62 year old, known case of meningitis post multiple excisional surgery presented with complaints of dyspnea,dry cough, fever and cervical swelling.

Anitha Mohanraj Barney1, Barney Isaac2, Balamugesh Thangakunam2, Sumita Danda1, Debashish Danda3, Venkata Raghava Mohan4, Christhunesa Soundararajan Christudass5, Devasagayam J. Christopher2

1Department of Clinical Genetics, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 3Department of Clinical Immunology and Rheumatology, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 4Department of Community Health, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 5Department of Neurological Sciences, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (IPF) is a chronic, progressive Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD) that has a significant impact on the health-related quality of life (HRQoL). There are generic and disease-specific questionnaires to assess this, but no literature from India exists.

Objective: To estimate the impact of IPF on HRQoL and determine its predictors in a cohort of patients attending a tertiary care centre in Southern India.

Methods: Consecutive consenting patients diagnosed with IPF by the ILD MDD between September 2023 and August 2024 were self-administered the St. George’s Respiratory Questionnaire (SGRQ) and the King’s Brief Interstitial Lung Disease (KBILD) questionnaire to assess the HRQoL.

Results: In all, 70 patients with IPF were recruited into the study. The median total SGRQ was 35.9 (23.9 – 57.8), and the median total KBILD was 54.5 (46.4 – 65.6). Based on SGRQ (>30), 63% (44/70) and based on KBILD (≤50), 34% (24/70) had a poor quality of life. The SGRQ was significantly lower in those not on antifibrotics across all domains, with a trend towards near significance in the activity domain. The GAP (Gender, Age, Physiology) index had a significant association with the KBILD - breathlessness and activity domain (p-value 0.01) and the total score (p-value 0.02).

Conclusion: This is the first Indian study reporting HRQoL in IPF. IPF impacts HRQoL drastically, with a predominant impact on physical wellness and a lesser effect on psychological well-being. KBILD is associated with the GAP index. A larger study is required to identify independent factors associated with HRQoL.

Acknowledgement: The research work presented in this article is part of a proposed PhD thesis of The Tamil Nadu Dr. M.G.R. Medical University, Chennai - 600 032.

Keywords: Health-related quality of life (HRQoL), Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (IPF), Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD), King’s Brief Interstitial Lung Disease (KBILD) questionnaire, St. George’s Respiratory Questionnaire (SGRQ)

Anitha Mohanraj Barney1, Barney Isaac2, Devasagayam J. Christopher2, Balamugesh Thangakunam2, Venkata Raghava Mohan3, Christhunesa Soundararajan Christudass4, Debashish Danda5, Sumita Danda1

1Department of Clinical Genetics, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 3Department ofCommunity Health, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 4Department of Neurological Sciences, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 5Department of Clinical Immunology and Rheumatology, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: The etiology of IPF, the prototype idiopathic interstitial pneumonia with poor prognosis, remains poorly understood. While genetic and environmental factors have been proposed and studied, there is a dearth of data from India on the genetic predisposition of IPF. This study aims to fill this crucial gap.

Objective: We conducted an exploratory whole exome sequencing analysis to identify the genetic variants associated with IPF among Indian patients.

Methods: Consecutive consenting patients attending the pulmonary medicine outpatient service at a tertiary care hospital in southern India from February to June 2024 who met diagnostic criteria for IPF and were recruited. Their clinical, demographic, radiological, and physiological parameters were captured. A blood sample was taken for whole genome sequencing, and the results were analysed.

Results: In all, 15 IPF patients with a mean age of 63 were recruited. There was male predominance (93%). Seven patients harboured variants in TERT, TTR, MUC5B, DSP, ECT2, and CFAP74 genes. In 2 patients whole exome sequencing identified a missense benign variant c.3004G>A (p.Val1002Met) and c.11224T>G ( p.Ser3742Ala) in the MUC5B gene. Both these variants show susceptibility to pulmonary fibrosis, and neither patient reported cough. WES picked up a frameshift variant c.4687_4688del (p.Leu1563GlufsTer63) in the DSP gene, which could cause arrhythmogenic right ventricular dysplasia – 8 in a patient in which IPF was incidentally diagnosed while being evaluated for cardiac arrhythmia.

Conclusion: This exploratory research gave insights into the underlying genetic predispositions for IPF in India. A larger Indian study is warranted.

Acknowledgement: The research work presented in this article is a part of a proposed PhD thesis of the Tamil Nadu Dr. M.G.R. Medical University.

Keywords: CFAP74, DSP, ECT2, Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis, MUC5B, TERT, TTR, Whole Exome Sequencing

Alina Varghese, Awnish Shubham, Alpa Dalal, Dipanjan Haldar

Jupiter Lifeline Hospital, Thane, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pulmonary cryptococcosis, caused by Cryptococcus neoformans, is a serious opportunistic infection leading to isolated or disseminated disease, particularly in immunocompromised individuals. Lymphoma is a major haematological malignancy linked to cryptococcal infections due to factors like T-cell depleting agents, glucocorticoid use, and immune dysregulation. Below unveils a case of Pulmonary cryptococcosis in a DLBCL patient:

Case: A 77 year old male known hypertensive, presented with right sided chest swelling and pleuritic chest pain associated with cough and other constitutional symptoms. Right sided pleural tapping was done and he was diagnosed with DLBCL (CD20 & Pax5 positive)- Triple hit lymphoma(c-myc,bcl-2,bcl-6 positive). He was treated with 5 cycles of dose adjusted R EPOCH along with radiation therapy. Patient did clinically well, with interval PET imaging showing significant metabolic and morphologic resolution of soft tissue mass and lymphnodes. However, a follow-up PET scan after 7 months of chemotherapy surprisingly revealed new 4-5mm nodules along with a metabolically active subpleural opacity in the left lower lung (2.6*1.6cm -SUV max -12.23) while the primary lesions showed full regression. He was evaluated further and a CT guided biopsy of left lung lesion was done which revealed - Areas of necrosis with many cryptococcal yeasts, with surrounding epitheloid cell granulomas. PAS/GMS stain & Fungal culture revealed cryptococcus neoformans. Bacterial culture and TB tests-Negative. CNS involvement of cryptococcosis was also ruled out. The patient was started on oral fluconazole therapy-400mg daily. He responded well to treatment with good radiological response.

Conclusion: Early identification and maintaining a low threshold of suspicion along with aggressive approach in immunocompromised patients is crucial for early diagnosis and management to prevent fatal outcomes.

This case highlights the importance of close monitoring for infections among immunocompromised patients through regular imaging and investigating through invasive approach.

Mahima Jaisinghani, Sneha Tirpude, Ram Chopra

Ruby Hall Clinic, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Organizing pneumonia (OP) has an incidence of 1.97-7/100,000 and typically presents subacutely without severe disease. It is an inflammatory, fibroproliferative lung condition with multifocal, diffuse, cavitatory, or nodular patterns.

Case Reports: This report presents four cases with varying manifestations, all diagnosed through histopathological analysis, highlighting the diverse presentations of this clinico-pathological syndrome [Table 1].

Details of the clinical symptoms, radiology, histopathology and treatment of the cases

Organizing pneumonia (OP) affects individuals without a specific age or sex predilection. Radiologically, the typical presentation is diffuse, patchy, peripheral consolidation surrounded by ground-glass opacities (GGO), with the Atoll sign seen in 20% of cases. However, all four cases presented here show localized, unilateral involvement, a less common form of the disease. Histopathologically, OP is characterized by organized fibrin balls in alveoli and respiratory ducts, with patchy distribution and the absence of vitreous membranes, inflammatory granulomas, eosinophilic pneumonia, and bronchiolitis with abscess formation. The first case describes secondary OP as an extraintestinal manifestation of Crohnrespiratory ducts, with patchy distribution and the absence of vitreous membranes, inflammatory granulomas, eosinophilic pneumonia, and bronchiolitis with abscess formatiThis case involved acute fibrinous organizing pneumonia. In the second case, a patient presented with a mass lesion with spiculated margins, raising suspicion of malignancy. While OP is typically bilateral, this case showed the rare single nodule presentation. Diagnosing COP requires excluding both infection and malignancy. The third case involved a recurrence of OP in a diabetic patient, eight years after initial treatment, at a new site. This recurrence required another course of corticosteroids, and delayed initial treatment, along with evidence of cholestasis, was associated with multiple relapses. The fourth case was a 62-year-old smoker who presented with a unifocal mass, another rare presentation of COP. Despite smoking being a known risk factor for many respiratory diseases, it is not considered a risk factor for Cryptogenic organizing pneumonia.

Organising pneumonia can have varied presentations and clinicians need to be wary of those.

Pravin Taware, Arjun Srinivasan, Pattabhiraman Vallandaramam, Mahadevan Sivaramakrishnan, Arun Raja

Royal Care Superspeciality Hospital, Neelambur, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Foreign body aspiration in the airway is a rare occurrence in adults, and such foreign bodies can be either organic or inorganic. We present a case series of five adult patients who aspirated sapota seeds and the challenges faced during their bronchoscopic management at a tertiary care hospital.

Objectives: The study aims to discuss and document the unusual cases of sapota seed aspiration, highlight the challenges encountered during bronchoscopic removal, and present the solutions employed for successful extraction.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective review of patient records with foreign body aspiration who underwent bronchoscopic removal at a tertiary care referral center between November 2016 and July 2023.

Results: Among 50 retrieved foreign bodies via bronchoscopy during the study period, 28 cases (56%) were in adults, and five of these adults had aspirated sapota seeds. All five patients were females with a mean age of 64.6 years. All had delayed presentations. Presenting complaints consisted of cough and dyspnea in all patients, wheezing in one (20%) with post-obstructive pneumonia in two patients (40%) and hyperinflation in two patients (40%). Granulation tissue and mucosal reactions were observed in all cases. The sapota seeds were lodged in different locations within the bronchial tree with the left side being the most common (n=3,60%). Four cases were managed with a rigid bronchoscope (80%) with one managed with flexible under local anaesthesia. A rigid bronchoscope and various accessories, such as forceps, dormia basket, Fogarty catheter, cryoprobe, electrocautery snare and knife were employed for successful retrieval. In 2 cases (40%), the seed was removed in toto along with a rigid barrel and in the remaining 3 cases(60%) seed was broken and removed in a piecemeal fashion using cryo-probe and forceps.

Conclusion: Sapota seeds are unusual foreign bodies that we occasionally encounter in adults. The size and physical structure poses unique challenges in its retrieval. A skilled team is crucial for successful extraction. Rigid bronchoscopy is a safer option for chronically impacted sapota seeds. Rigid and flexible bronchoscopes are complementary to each other. The use of various bronchoscopic accessories is essential for effective retrieval and at times breaking of the seed may be needed for successful extraction.

Pravin Taware, Wasim Khot, Manali Nilekeri, Namrata Jasani, Samir Garde

Gleneagles Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Melioidosis is an infectious disease caused by a gram-negative bacterium Burkhloderia pseudomallei which is ubiquitous in rice farming areas. It is an endemic disesase in West Coastal Maharashtra, but remained under-reported.It causes significant morbidity and mortality if diagnosis and treatment is delayed. Important risk factors are diabetes, chronic kidney disease, chronic lung disease, immunosuppressive therapy etc.Influenza is usually a self-limiting disease but can cause morbidity and mortality in high risk groups. Both melioidosis and Influenza A commonly present with pneumonia. Secondary bacterial pneumonia is a known complication of Influenza A infection.However, melioidosis is not commonly reported as a cause of bacterial pneumonia complicating influenza A infection.Here we report a rare case of concurrent pulmonary melioidosis and influenza A.

Case Report: A 58 year-old male rice farmer with background history of asthma and type 2 DM presented with high grade fever, nonproductive cough, throat pain, dyspnea and severe headache. He was febrile on admission.He was evaluated with CT thorax a week before hospitalization.CT thorax was showing ground glass opacities in bilateral lower lobe of lungs suggestive of viral or atypical infection for which he received empirical antibiotic and antiviral.On admission viral RTPCR panel showed H1N1 influenza A detected. He was continued to have fever and headache.CT brain revealed no abnormality.In view of persistent fever and cough, a repeat CT thorax was done. CT chest revealed a large consolidation with cavitation along with GGO in right lower lobe with differential of TB versus fungal etiology. Sputum aerobic culture showed no growth hence decision of bronchoscopy was taken. BAL was collected from lower lobe of right lung. BAL Mycobacterial evaluation showed no MTB detected. Aerobic culture showed no growth till 3 days of incubation.MDT with ID specialist and microbiologist decided to incubate the culture for further few days. After 5 days of total incubation, culture showed cream colored colonies of B. pseudomallei. He was treated with 3 weeks of ceftazidime and 3 months of Trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole. Patient improved symptomatically. CT thorax after 12 weeks of treatment showed only a small fibrotic band in right lower lobe.

Conclusion: Melioidosis is an emerging infection, especially among immunocompromised patients in coastal areas. The clinical picture is often similar to tuberculosis which is seen commonly in the same subset of patients. It is important to consider this uncommon infection in our differential diagnoses when faced with an appropriate clinical scenario in patients with co-infection of Influenza a infection.

Vipin Patel

School of Respiratory Medicine, Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose Medical College, Jabalpur, Madhya Pradesh, India

Introduction: Lymphoid interstitial pneumonia is a rare form of diffuse cystic lung disease with very less cases worldwide and is frequently associated with connective tissue disorder, infection and sometimes idiopathic. When symptomatic, the most frequent complication is dyspnoea on exertion and nonproductive cough. LIP affects women more frequently than men (M:F -1.00-2.75). Men are more likely to develop idiopathic LIP while women are more likely to develop LIP related to an autoimmune disorder. Some patients may have an indolent or even an asymptomatic course while others may have complications such as associated infections, pulmonary fibrosis and transformation into lipoma. Diagnosis is based on the history, physical examination, imaging tests and lung biopsy. The management of LIP should be focused on controlling the underlying disease process. LIP is a steroid-responsive disease with symptomatic and/or radiographic stabilization in 50-60% of the patients.

Case: A 78-year-old male presented to our Respiratory Medicine OPD with complaints of dyspnoea on exertion for 12 months and cough associated with expectoration. Patient did not have any known comorbidities and was a chronic bidi smoker for 40 years. On examination patient had fine crypts on auscultation, with no evidence of clubbing. Patient was diagnosed with COPD in a public health setup and was on bronchodilators with incomplete resolution of symptoms. HRCT chest was done which was suggestive of diffuse patchy lower lobe predominant cysts and thickened peri-broncho-vascular markings with intervening normal lung parenchyma. Patient was negative for antiretroviral disease and serology was negative for Anti SS-A and Anti SS-B. PFT was suggestive of restrictive lung disease with impaired DLCO. Bronchoscopy was done and transbronchial lung biopsy was suggestive of dense interstitial lymphoid infiltrate with type two cell hyperplasia and intraalveolar macrophages. Diagnosis of LIP was confirmed and patient was started on oral prednisolone at 1mg/kg. Patient is currently under regular follow up and has symptomatic relief.

Conclusion: LIP is a rare entity and is a benign lymphoproliferative disorder. Here we presented a case of a 78/year old male who was initially diagnosed with COPD but was later confirmed to have idiopathic LIP. Patient responded well to immunosuppressive therapy and is currently under regular follow up.

Coccidioidomycosis is a disease endemic to the Southwestern United States and Mexico, with cases in non-endemic regions such as India primarily being imported. Establishing a history of exposure is essential for accurately diagnosing coccidioidomycosis, particularly in non-endemic locations.

Case Report: This study reports on a 76-year-old male patient from India who presented to our hospital with symptoms of chronic cough, malaise and loss of weight, revealing a mass lesion with a small cavity in the upper lobe of the right lung. Prior to his admission, he had spent four months in Tracy, California. Diagnosis of pulmonary coccidioidomycosis was established through histopathological analysis of a CT-guided lung biopsy, serological testing for Coccidioides and confirmed by growth on fungal cultures.

Conclusions: Accurate differentiation from tuberculosis, other dimorphic fungi, and malignancies is crucial for appropriate management.

Sharadruthi Akula

MGM Insititute of Health Sciences, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis remains a major global health challenge, with pleural effusion being one of its common extrapulmonary manifestations, typically presents with constitutional symptoms such as fever, weight loss, and cough, often accompanied by unilateral fluid accumulation in the pleural cavity. While pleural fluid analysis, including adenosine deaminase levels and cytology, is a cornerstone for diagnosis, some cases exhibit atypical features, such as ambiguous imaging findings or inconclusive fluid analysis which pose significant diagnostic challenges, delaying timely intervention.

Thoracoscopy, a minimally invasive procedure, has emerged as a valuable tool for evaluating undiagnosed pleural effusions. By allowing direct visualization of pleural surfaces and enabling targeted biopsies, it provides a higher diagnostic yield compared to traditional methods.

Case Report: A 55 yrs old male presented with breathlessness, dry cough, chest congestion, weight loss since 15 days. Chest xray suggestive of right sided pleural effusion. Pleural fluid, sputum, bronchoalveolar lavage, GeneXpert and cultures were negative. Further evaluation through thoracoscopic pleural biopsy tissue MGIT confirmed the presence of mycobacterium tuberculosis

Conclusion: Atypical presentations of tubercular pleural effusion require advanced diagnostic techniques like thoracoscopy. This procedure ensures diagnosis by enabling direct visualization and biopsies, allowing for prompt initiation of anti-tubercular therapy.

In regions with a high TB burden, thoracoscopy should be considered early in evaluating unexplained pleural effusion. Its use improves clinical outcomes, reduces morbidity, and prevents complications associated with delayed treatment, making it an indispensable diagnostic tool.

Thoracoscopy is valuable for diagnosing atypical tubercular pleural effusion, ensuring timely and accurate treatment. Its early use improves outcomes, prevents delays, and is essential in high TB prevalence regions.

Komal Shah1, Bhavik Shah2

1Department of Respiratory Medicine, Dr. Vasantrao Pawar Medical College Hospital and Research Centre, Nashik, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Critical Care, Ashoka Medicover Hospital, Nashik, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Invasive pulmonary aspergillosis (IPA), traditionally seen in immunocompromised individuals, is increasingly found in immunocompetent patients with atypical presentations. This prospective study aimed to examine the clinical features, diagnostic approaches, and treatment outcomes of IPA, focusing on posaconazole as an alternative to voriconazole.

Methods: Conducted over six months at Dr. Vasantrao Pawar Medical College, Nashik, the prspective observation study included 18 IPA patients diagnosed via positive bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL)/serum galactomannan, Aspergillus smear/culture, or PCR. Demographic data, immune status, risk factors, clinical presentation, radiological findings, and treatments were analyzed. Outcomes included mortality rates, treatment response, hospital stay, and complications.

Results: The study revealed diverse IPA presentations, with half of the patients (9/18) showing atypical forms, such as secondary IPA following bacterial sepsis (5 cases), endobronchial mass, unstable angina, concurrent tuberculosis, and even a case of IPA after deep cleaning in an immunocompetent individual. Common risk factors included immunocompromised states and sepsis. Main symptoms were fever, cough, and breathlessness, with 40% of patients also reporting weight loss and decreased appetite. Radiological findings primarily showed consolidations with ground-glass opacities (GGOs), while only 25% presented with cavitary lesions. Diagnosis was confirmed by BAL galactomannan (14), fungal cultures (5), histopathology (2), and PCR (1). Treatment mainly included posaconazole, with voriconazole/caspofungin used for critically ill patients. Outcomes included 5 complete resolutions, 9 partial recoveries under ongoing treatment, and 3 deaths.

Concluson: This study underscores the importance of BAL galactomannan testing and fungal cultures in non-resolving pneumonia cases, as IPA can present atypically. Early diagnosis and prompt treatment with azoles like posaconazole significantly improve outcomes, emphasizing the need to consider IPA in the differential diagnosis.

Arpit Gupta

Vedantaa Institute of Medical Sciences, Dahanu, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) remains a significant health burden, particularly in developing countries, often presenting with diverse clinical manifestations. Polyserositis, characterized by the simultaneous involvement of multiple serous membranes, is a rare and severe manifestation of TB. This report discusses a unique case of TB with polyserositis in a young male with a history of chronic alcohol and tobacco abuse, highlighting its clinical challenges and diagnostic approach.

Case Report: A 24-year-old male farmer with a six-year history of chronic alcoholism and five years of tobacco abuse presented to the emergency department in February 2024. The patient complained of non-productive cough, high-grade fever with chills, dyspnoea (MMRC grade 4), pleuritic chest pain, significant weight loss (6 kg), and loss of appetite for two weeks. On examination, the patient appeared pale and sick, with a temperature of 101.4°F, pulse rate of 108/min, and blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg. Auscultation revealed decreased air entry over the left lung field and evidence of a pericardial rub. Radiological investigations, including a chest X-ray and CECT thorax, showed gross left-sided pleural effusion and pericardial effusion. Ultrasound (USG) of the thorax confirmed the findings, and USG abdomen revealed ascites with a cirrhotic liver. Laboratory findings indicated grossly deranged liver function tests (SGOT/PT: 1323/2016 IU/L), hypoalbuminemia (2.5 g/dL), and elevated PT/INR (25 sec/1.97). Analysis of pleural, pericardial, and ascitic fluid showed raised ADA levels (82/64/58 U/L) with lymphocytic predominance. Cartridge-Based Nucleic Acid Amplification Test (CBNAAT) confirmed Mycobacterium tuberculosis with rifampicin sensitivity.

Conclusion: Tuberculosis should be considered a differential diagnosis in patients presenting with polyserositis, especially in endemic regions. Early identification and intervention are crucial to prevent severe complications. Comprehensive imaging and laboratory analyses, including ADA levels and CBNAAT, are indispensable for diagnosis.

This case highlights the complexity of TB with polyserositis, emphasizing the importance of a multidisciplinary approach to timely diagnosis and management. Early treatment can significantly improve patient outcomes in such severe TB manifestations.

Ronit Kumar, Arpit Gupta, S. V. Ghorpade, Vinod Mutkule

Vedantaa Institute of Medical Sciences, Dahanu, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome complicating viral pneumonia occasionally presents with the complications like thrombocytopenia. ARDS is defined by a PaO2/FiO2 ratio ≤300 mmHg, with severe ARDS classified as ≤100 mmHg. This case series explores the role of thrombocytopenia correction in improving prognosis in patients with ARDS and viral pneumonitis.

Methods: Three patients were included: a 60-year-old female with viral pneumonitis and severe ARDS with thrombocytopenia (PaO2/FiO2 = 90 mmHg), a 17-year-old male with severe ARDS and thrombocytopenia (PaO2/FiO2 = 85 mmHg), and a 56-year-old male with severe ARDS and thrombocytopenia (PaO2/FiO2 = 75 mmHg). All presented with fever, cough, severe shortness of breath, and bilateral reticular opacities on chest X-rays. HRCT confirmed ARDS and viral pneumonitis. Laboratory findings included thrombocytopenia, leukopenia, and normal liver and kidney function tests. Dengue NS1/IgG/IgM was negative for all the three patients. Management included intensive oxygen therapy, antivirals, corticosteroids, and platelet transfusions.

Results: The 60-year-old female and 17-year-old male showed significant clinical improvement after thrombocytopenia correction and were discharged in stable condition. In contrast, the 56-year-old male succumbed to severe ARDS due to delays in platelet correction.

Timely correction of thrombocytopenia paired with intensive care approach helped improve oxygenation (PaO2/FiO2 >150 mmHg) and clinical outcomes in two patients. Delay in platelet correction despite intensive oxygen therapy was a key factor in the poor prognosis of the third patient, underscoring the importance of early intervention.

Conclusion: Thrombocytopenia correction is a crucial therapeutic component in managing ARDS and viral pneumonitis. Early platelet transfusions can significantly improve PaO2/FiO2 ratios, clinical recovery, and survival rates.

Keywords: ARDS, PaO2/FiO2 ratio, platelet transfusion, respiratory distress, thrombocytopenia, viral pneumonitis

Introduction: methemoglobin is a form of hemoglobin in which iron in hb is in an oxidised form (FERRIC) instead of ferrous making it difficult to bind with oxygen, usually methhemoglobin present in small quantities in human but once methb increase beyond 3 %,it is know as methemoglobinemia, classified into hereditary and acquired. Hereditary forms are rare causes of hypoxia and cyanosis.

Case Description: A 22 yrs old female primigravida was refered to pulmonology OPD from OBG OPD for evaluation of bluish discoloration of hands she gives H/O of bluish discoloration of hands, feet, tongue, lips present since childhood and increased after pregnancy
