Laboratory test normal expect for PCV-47%, ABG –PaO2-72, CXRAY-normal. Blood drawn showed chocolate brown color. Cardiology reference was sought adviced 2d echo which was normal. The clinical picture with no cardiovascular disease or pulmonary and the discrepancy between PaO2 and SpO2 raised suspision of abnormal HB. HB electrophoresis sent-normal Meth hemoglobin -43.5 %haematologist opinion was sought adviced for ascorbic ACID 500 mg TID orally.

Conclusion: Congenital methemoglobinemia is usually missed as a cause of cyanosis especially in adult it is crucial to consider as differential diagnosis when assessing cyanotic patients mainly if they have discrepancy between PaO2 and SpO2.

Kriti Agarwal

Grant Medical College and JJ Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Bedaquiline has become a cornerstone in the treatment of multidrug-resistant and extensively resistant tuberculosis. However, emerging reports of bedaquiline resistance raise concerns about its long-term efficacy and need for ongoing surveillance to guide treatment strategies. Understanding the prevalence and determinants of bedaquiline resistance in pulmonary tuberculosis (TB) is critical to maintaining the effectiveness of this important drug.

Methods: A cross-sectional study was conducted at a Tertiary Care Hospital, involving 1831 patients diagnosed with drug-resistant TB. Resistance to bedaquiline was assessed using molecular diagnostic methods (e.g., PCR sequencing, line probe assays) and mycobacterial culture-based drug susceptibility testing. Demographic, clinical, and microbiological data were collected to identify factors influencing resistance.

Results: Patients were enrolled in a period of 2 years, from January 2022 to December 2023. Out of total 1831 patients, only 257 were further tested for DST for bedaquiline. Out of 257 tested, 51(19.84%) patients had bedaquiline resistance in which 24(47.0%) were males and 27(52.9%) were females. Majority (80.39%) were young (less than 40 years of age) and only 10(19.6%) patients had history of Bedaquiline exposure or contact, rest were primary resistance.

Conclusion: The prevalence of bedaquiline resistance in pulmonary TB patients, especially those with MDR-TB and XDR-TB, is a growing concern and warrants intensified surveillance efforts. The findings highlight the need for more comprehensive diagnostic tools, regular resistance testing, and tailored treatment strategies to mitigate the spread of drug-resistant TB and need for further studies to assess impact.

Keywords: Bedaquiline, pulmonary tuberculosis, drug susceptibility, extensively drug-resistant tuberculosis, multidrug-resistant tuberculosis, prevalence

Anitha Mohanraj Barney1, Barney Isaac2, Balamugesh Thangakunam2, Sumita Danda1, Debashish Danda3, Venkata Raghava Mohan4, Christhunesa Soundararajan Christudass5, Devasagayam J. Christopher2

1Department of Clinical Genetics, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 3Department of Clinical Immunology and Rheumatology, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 4Department of Community Health, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India, 5Department of Neurological Sciences, Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Background: Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (IPF) is a chronic, progressive Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD) that has a significant impact on the health-related quality of life (HRQoL). There are generic and disease-specific questionnaires to assess this, but no literature from India exists.

Objective: To estimate the impact of IPF on HRQoL and determine its predictors in a cohort of patients attending a tertiary care centre in Southern India.

Methodology: Consecutive consenting patients diagnosed with IPF by the ILD MDD between September 2023 and August 2024 were self-administered the St. George’s Respiratory Questionnaire (SGRQ) and the King’s Brief Interstitial Lung Disease (KBILD) questionnaire to assess the HRQoL.

Results: In all, 70 patients with IPF were recruited into the study. The median total SGRQ was 35.9 (23.9 – 57.8), and the median total KBILD was 54.5 (46.4 – 65.6). Based on SGRQ (>30), 63% (44/70) and based on KBILD (≤50), 34% (24/70) had a poor quality of life. The SGRQ was significantly lower in those not on antifibrotics across all domains, with a trend towards near significance in the activity domain. The GAP (Gender, Age, Physiology) index had a significant association with the KBILD - breathlessness and activity domain (p-value 0.01) and the total score (p-value 0.02).

Conclusion: This is the first Indian study reporting HRQoL in IPF. IPF impacts HRQoL drastically, with a predominant impact on physical wellness and a lesser effect on psychological well-being. KBILD is associated with the GAP index. A larger study is required to identify independent factors associated with HRQoL.

Acknowledgement: The research work presented in this article is part of a proposed PhD thesis of The Tamil Nadu Dr. M.G.R. Medical University, Chennai - 600 032.

Keywords: Health-related quality of life (HRQoL), Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF), Interstitial lung disease (ILD), King’s brief interstitial lung disease (KBILD) questionnaire, St. george’s respiratory questionnaire (SGRQ)

Introduction: Pulmonary sporadic lymphangioleiomyomatosis is a female dominant disease associated with smooth muscle cell proliferation in lung parenchyma, airway cells as well as lymphatic system. It is estimated to have an incidence of 1 in 100000 patients in US and Europe,however disease is potentially underdiagnosed.LAM may be mistaken for other pulmonary disorders due to similarities in initial presentation.LAM has two specific types .sporadic and congenital.

Case Report: A 65 yr old female patient k/c/o bronchial asthma presented with ℅ sudden onset chest pain and breathlesness, cxr showed left sided pneumothorax, chest tube put and ventillatory support given.pt had h/o left nephrectomy 7 yrs back, biopsy of the specimen shows angiomyolipoma of the left kidney with hilar node.later patient’s hrct taken showed multiple thin walled cyst suggestive of leiomyomatosis. serum VGEF Levels >800ng/ L made definitive diagnosis of lymphangioleiomyomatosis. Patient general conditions improved and chemical pleurodesis done via chest tube to avoid further pneumothoraces.patient pulmonary function test done shows restrictive changes and hypoxemia on exercise testing. Later patient discharged with bronchodilators and tab sirolimus.

Conclusion: Lymphangiomyomatosis is a rare disease in women mostly young, presents with spontaneous pneumothorax and exertional dyspnoea. The definitive treatment for this disease requires lung transplant. If patients are not transplant candidates, can be managed pharmacologically with sirolimus. Recurrent pneumothoraces can be prevented with pleurodesis and by avoiding air travel and preventing barotruama. The goal of this case report is to improve the diagnosis of this condition by considering LAM as a differential diagnosis for female patients (esp of reproductive age group), that present with exertional dyspnoea or spontaneous pneumothoraces. Subsequently, this should lead to further research and increased treatment options for LAM patients.

Ancy Nisha

Sri Ramachandra Institute of Higher Education and Research, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Pleurodesis is the obliteration of the pleural space by fusion of the visceral and parietal pleurae with fibrous tissue.

Methods: The study was done as an observational study among 68 subjects. Patients above 18 years, with recurrent exudative pleural effusion, and secondary spontaneous pneumothorax were included in the study. Demographic features, historical information, pleural fluid measurements, and blood sample assessments were collected. Pleural fluid was extracted with an intercostal drainage tube or thoracoscopy procedure. Following a thorough evacuation and a drainage rate of less than 100ml per day, a chest X-ray was performed to verify that the lung had fully expanded. Group 1– 10 ml of 2% lignocaine injected intrapleurally via chest tube and followed by 10 mg/kg of powdered Doxycycline slurry tablets in 100 ml normal-saline. Group 2 – 10 ml of 2% lignocaine injected intrapleurally and followed by povidone iodine pleurodesis – 20 ml of 10 % povidone iodine diluted in 80 milli litre of normal saline. The ICD was clamped for 4 hours. Response was weighed in terms Paladine’s criteria of “complete-response (CR), partial-response (PR) and failure (F)”. The study also assessed post procedural complications.

Results: In the study the subjects with lung malignancy were 72.06%, other malignancies were 5.88% and pneumothorax accounted for 22.06%. The povidone iodine group had a complication-free rate of 55.88%, whereas the Doxycycline slurry group had a complication-free rate of 79.4%. The Povidone iodine group had 76.4% success pleurodesis compared to Doxycycline slurry group which had 79.4% success rate. The partial response rate among the povidone iodine group was 5.8% compared to 3% in Doxycycline slurry group.

Conclusion: Considering doxycycline pleurodesis is a cost-effective option with success rate non inferior or slightly better to the povidone iodine group with lesser complications.

Keywords: Doxycycline slurry, pleural effusion, pleural sclerosing agent, pneumothorax, povidone iodine

Shivani Acharya, Aayushi Gaudani, Bineet Ahluwalia, Sanjeev Mehta

Lilavati Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Plastic bronchitis is a rare and serious respiratory condition characterised by the production of thick, plastic-like bronchial casts that obstruct the airways causing difficulty breathing. It has a prevalence of fewer than 600 cases worldwide. It has been associated with congenital heart disease, viral infections, hematological malignancies.

Case: A 12-year-old female came to us with a history of acute onset cough with thick mucus and hemoptysis. CT scan of chest showed a mucus plug in the left lung lobar collapse.

Bronchoscopic evaluation revealed a mucous cast, which stained positive for Masson’s trichrome and Oil Red O and composed of fibrin and mucin on histopathology. MRI lymphangiography showed a right-sided aortic arch, an aberrant left subclavian artery coursing posterior to the esophagus and the left carotid artery coursing anterior to the trachea, suggesting the presence of a vascular sling associated with pulmonary lymphatic perfusion syndrome. The patient was diagnosed as a case of plastic bronchitis treated with antibiotics and mucolytics. She experienced similar symptoms six years later. Bronchoscopic evaluation revealed casts similar to those seen previously. Cardiac MRI and CT pulmonary angiography were performed, confirming the presence of a vascular sling causing tracheal compression with a possibility of lymphatic duct obstruction. Additionally, both the patient and her mother had associated lipedema, a condition of abnormal accumulation of fat in the lower body, exacerbated by lymphatic dysfunction. She was treated symptomatically and was asked to resume duties and follow up if required.

Conclusion: Ours is a very rare case likely caused by a vascular sling, leading to lymphatic obstruction and resulting in a mucus plug of plastic bronchitis.

Kiran Bhadrashetty

SDM Institute of Medical Sciences and Hospital, Dharwad, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome (HPS) is a rare autosomal recessive disorder with a prevalence of 1-9 in 1,000,000. It is characterized by triad of oculocutaneous albinism, bleeding diathesis, and accumulation of ceroid-lipofuscin pigment in various organs, resulting in systemic complications in the form of pulmonary fibrosis, granulomatous colitis, immunodeficiency, corpulmonale and vision problems.

Case Report: A 39 year male, who is ex-smoker and tobacco chewer and works at paper factory came to our pulmonology OPD with complaints of cough with expectoration and dyspnea on exertion since 2 months and fever since 2 days. On examination his pulse rate was 134 beats/min, BP was 110/70 mmHg, respiratory rate was 20 cpm, spo2 of 82% on room air. Respiratory system revealed bilateral fine inspiratory crepitations. Physical examination revealed decreased pigmentation of skin and hair. Chest x-ray showed B/L opacities. CT scan of chest was s/o multifocal areas of consolidation and surrounding ground glass opacities in bilateral lung fields suggestive of bronchopneumonia and mediastinal lymphadenopathy. ABG showed type 1 respiratory failure. Bronchoscopy was performed and BAL culture and sensitivity showed growth of enterococcus species and sensitive antibiotics were given. ANA Screening was positive (2+) with nuclear speckled pattern. ANA profile was negative and complement levels were within normal limits. Patient was treated with oxygen therapy, IV antibiotics, steroids, antivirals and other symptomatic measures. With background oculocutaneous albinism with type 1 respiratory failure and CT chest findings, HPS was suspected. Whole genome sequencing was sent. Genome sequencing evaluation showed likely compound heterozygous of PVS1, PMS2 suggesting HPS-1.

Conclusion: HPS is a rare autosomal recessive disorder, characterized by multisystem involvement. Pulmonary fibrosis is highly prevalent in 3 out of 10 genetic types of HPS (HPS-1, HPS-2 and HPS-4). Thus, genotyping of individuals with HPS is clinically relevant. HPS pulmonary fibrosis shares some clinical features with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF), including dyspnoea, cough, restrictive lung physiology and computed tomography (CT) findings of fibrosis. Pulmonary fibrosis is the most serious complication and main reason of death of patients with HPS. It usually presents in the 3rd or 4th decades of life and accounts for premature death in 50% of HPS patients, generally by the 5th decade. The incidence and severity of fibrosis is greatest in patients with HPS1 and HPS4.

Introduction: Lung neuroendocrine tumors (NETs) are an uncommon pulmonary neoplasm characterized by neuroendocrine differentiation & relatively indolent clinical behavior. GI tract is the most frequently involved site, while lung is the second most common. Lung NETs are the most common primary lung neoplasm in children, typically presenting in late adolescence. This report highlights a simple case of lung carcinoid in a 23 years old male, showcasing importance of early diagnosis and apt choice of intervention!

Case Report: 23 years old male presented with complaints of fever with chills on & off, hemoptysis, vomiting, abdominal pain since 1 month with repeated episodes occurring on and off since 2 years. Conclusion: This case report highlights the need of early diagnosis including imaging, intervention and histopathological analysis. It highlights importance of early diagnosis and choice of intervention to be done for appropriate management.

Krupa Rani

Guntur Medical College, Guntur, Andhra Pradesh, India

Introduction: Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is an autoimmune disease that can affect multiple organs, including the lungs. However, hydropneumothorax is a rare and potentially life-threatening manifestation of SLE that must not be overlooked.

Case Report: A 17-year-old female presented with a 20-days history of dry cough, dyspnea, left-sided chest pain, fever, and back pain. She also had skin lesions for the past year. On examination, her vital signs were stable, and a discoid rash with scarring alopecia and diffuse erythematous scaly lesions were noted, which had appeared one year ago following her first abortion due to a molar pregnancy. Nine months later, she experienced a flare-up of alopecia and skin lesions after a second abortion. She had been married for four years and had a healthy 18-month-old daughter from a non-consanguineous marriage. Laboratory results revealed anemia (hemoglobin 7.5 g/dL), a white blood cell count of 4,900, and an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) of 30. Chest X-ray showed a homogenous opacity in the left lower lung zone, obscuring the left costophrenic angle, with an air-fluid level, suggesting hydropneumothorax. The patient underwent left tube thoracostomy for treatment. Further investigations revealed a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) profile. The patient was treated with systemic corticosteroids and symptomatic care. She showed significant improvement and is currently stable with regular follow-up.

Conclusion: Hydropneumothorax is a rare but potentially life-threatening complication of SLE, emphasizing the need for early diagnosis and treatment.

In summary, SLE patients presenting with hydropneumothorax require prompt recognition, diagnosis, and treatment to prevent morbidity and mortality.

Introduction: Spontaneous pneumothorax can result in many lung diseases conditions, of which lymphangioleiomyomatosis (LAM) is an unusual cause. LAM is a multi system disorder, predominant in premenopausal women. It can be inherited disease associated with tuberous sclerosis complex syndrome or sporadic. LAM is complicated by spontaneous pneumothoraces in upto 70% of patients with high rate of recurrence. mTOR inhibitors are the treatment of choice in LAM.

Case Report: 30 years old female, resident of Goa, school teacher by profession, non smoker presented with acute onset breathlessness and pneumothorax but hemodynamically stable. On chest xray cyst in the right mid zone with pneumothorax with shift of mediastinum towards opposite side. HRCT chest showed right sided pneumothorax. Patient underwent right VATS adhesiolysis with bullectomy with pleurectomy and lung tissue sent for histopathological examination. Patient was diagnosed as a case of Lymphangioleiomyomatosis requiring thoracoscopic bullectomy with pleurectomy. On follow up patient was started on tab sirolimus 2mg/day, drug levels were monitored (patient was on carbamazepine-drug inducer). Pulmonary rehabilitation was advised.

Conclusion: This case shows an interesting presentation of spontaneous pneumothorax and highlights the importance of evaluating for LAM in young females with TSC. This case also shows the role of surgical intervention in LAM-cystic lung diseases.

Nisha Chandra

P. D. Hinduja Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculous meningitis (TBM) is a form of tuberculosis that is treated with anti-tubercular therapy and steroids. For paradoxical CNS tuberculosis unresponsive to steroids, Infliximab, a TNF-alpha inhibitor, offers a promising alternative.

Case Report: An 18-year-old female with no prior history of tuberculosis presented with fever, headache, and dizziness. Brain MRI showed leptomeningeal enhancement with multiple ring-enhancing lesions, suggestive of TBM. CSF analysis showed high protein, low glucose, normal ADA, lymphocyte predominant, TB genexpert MTB detected low and Rifampicin sensitive and was initially treated with isoniazid, rifampicin, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol along with intravenous steroids. Two months later, her culture revealed resistance to isoniazid and pyrazinamide, and her regimen was modified with addition of moxifloxacin and linezolid. Despite this, 2 months later her symptoms and MRI findings worsened, which was thought to be paradoxical worsening and hence IV steroids were reintroduced which lead to initial improvement in symptoms and deteriorated on shifting to oral steroids. Three attempts were made to switch her intravenous to oral steroids which failed, she suffered seizures with persistent headaches hence a trial of thalidomide 100mg BD was started. Meanwhile, 7 months in to treatment she developed severe hydrocephalus, underwent an initial EVD insertion followed by VP shunting 3 days later. Following unsuccessful trials with IV and high dose oral steroids, thalidomide and surgeries, we advised infliximab (5 mg/kg) 3 doses, which resulted in symptomatic improvement allowing her to transition from intravenous to oral and eventually stopping her steroid therapy.

Conclusion: Paradoxical reactions in TBM can be steroid-dependent, necessitating exploration of alternative treatment options to effectively manage these challenging scenarios.

This case highlights the challenges in managing paradoxical worsening in TB meningitis, particularly in steroid-dependent scenarios and underscores the potential role of biologics like Infliximab which allowed successful tapering and cessation of corticosteroids.

Nisha Chandra

P. D. Hinduja Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Cystic fibrosis (CF) is an autosomal recessive disorder with pulmonary and extrapulmonary symptoms. It affects 1 in 40,000 to 100,000 individuals in India, but remains severely underdiagnosed.

Case Report: 29 year old male with a history of seasonal cough and cold since age 2 and low BMI from birth. At age 8, he developed chronic cough with thick expectoration and was treated with inhalers. He later developed bilateral otitis media at age 15. CT thorax done during the period was suggestive of bilateral bronchiectasis. Between the ages of 19 and 23, he received three courses of empiric anti-tuberculosis treatment from local doctors, but his symptoms persisted, and was repeatedly treated for pneumonia. In 2019, he sought care at our hospital. Investigations revealed sputum culture positivity for Pseudomonas, TB, NTM cultures negative with normal serum immunoglobulin levels. Upon enquiring, the patient also reported to have fatty stools. Given his pulmonary and extrapulmonary manifestations, CF was suspected and a delF508 RTPCR test was advised which was negative. A sweat chloride test was recommended but could not be done initially due to unavailability of the test and then the COVID-19 pandemic. He continued to experience recurrent exacerbations and his condition worsened, with increasing breathlessness, cough, and fatigue. In 2024, during follow-up, stool analysis revealed multiple fat globules and low fecal elastase levels, suggesting severe pancreatic exocrine insufficiency. Given these findings and the persistence of symptoms, whole exome sequencing was performed, which confirmed a compound heterozygous mutation, ultimately establishing the diagnosis of CF.

Conclusion: Diagnosis of CF is essential as it has implications in genetic counselling, family planning and prenatal testing.

This case highlights the importance of considering CF in adults with recurrent respiratory and extrapulmonary symptoms, and the value of comprehensive diagnostic testing, including advanced genetic analysis.

Kumari Sulochana 1,2

1Chettinad Academy of Research and Education, Kelambakkam, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Bharati Hospital and Research Centre, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD) is a progressive condition characterized by inflammation or fibrosis of the interstitial space, resulting in reduced exercise tolerance and diminished quality of life (QoL). Pulmonary rehabilitation (PR) is important to ILD management, improving symptoms & QoL. However, poor compliance with traditional in-person PR programs, often due to travel difficulties and time constraints, limits their effectiveness. This study evaluates the impact of telerehabilitation on compliance and dropout rates in ILD patients undergoing PR.

Methods: An RCT was conducted with 46 ILD patients divided into two groups. The intervention group (n=25) participated in a 6-week telerehabilitation program delivered via Google Meet and WhatsApp, including tailored exercise sessions, and health education. The control group (n=21) underwent a 6-week conventional institution-based PR program. Compliance rates, dropout rates, and clinical outcomes were compared to assess telerehabilitation’s effectiveness.

Results: The telerehabilitation group demonstrated significantly higher compliance rates (85% vs. 65%) and lower dropout rates (10% vs. 30%) compared to the institution-based PR group. The telerehabilitation patients showed improved accessibility to PRP and satisfaction with the program. Additionally, clinical outcomes such as exercise tolerance (6MWT) and symptom management showed comparable improvements in both groups.

Conclusion: Telerehabilitation offers a promising approach to improving ILD management by reducing barriers to PR, fostering better adherence, and enhancing patients’ QoL.

Keywords: Compliance rate, exercise tolerance, interstitial lung disease, pulmonary rehabilitation, telerehabilitation

Kumari Sulochana 1,2

1Chettinad Academy of Research and Education, Kelambakkam, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India, 2Bharati Hospital and Research Centre, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The burden of chronic respiratory diseases (CRDs) in India exceeds 100 million cases and shows signs of growing further. Pulmonary rehabilitation (PR) is proven to be an effective, evidence-based intervention for improving the health outcomes of patients with CRD. However, the adoption and implementation of PR programs (PRPs) in India remain limited. Despite the strong evidence supporting PR as a beneficial and cost-effective intervention, poor referral and uptake rates by suitable CRD patients are global issues. This study aims to evaluate the effectiveness, and challenges of PR programs (PRP) in India, focusing on identifying key barriers faced by the healthcare professionals in initiating and sustaining these programs.

Methods: Following validation, a cross-sectional survey was administered to healthcare professionals to evaluate their receptiveness to pulmonary rehabilitation in India. The survey comprised seven domains to evaluate HCPs’ knowledge, Attitudes, and practices and the challenges they faced in carrying out the PRPs.

Results: A total of 155 HCPs (M: F = 2:3) participated in the survey. While over 75% of respondents were familiar with PRPs, only 65.8% were actively involved in delivering at least one form of PR. Younger clinicians demonstrated higher levels of engagement, with a mean knowledge score of 4.48±1.77. Most PRPs (75.5%) are concentrated in urban areas, leaving rural regions underserved. Patient adherence remains a significant challenge, with only 10% completing ≥75% of prescribed sessions and 62.7% dropping out after 25-50%. Key barriers include inadequate infrastructure, limited availability of facilities, and the financial burden on patients.

Conclusion: The study highlights barriers such as limited awareness, inadequate infrastructure, and insufficient training. To improve patient compliance and expand PRPs, particularly in rural areas, it is crucial to enhance infrastructure, increase professional training, and reduce the financial burden on patients. Expanding PRP reach, improving adherence, and raising awareness among healthcare providers and patients are key to enhancing respiratory care quality in India.

Kusha Gupta

Institute of Respiratory Diseases, Sawai Man Singh Medical College, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Paravertebral tumors can originate from the vertebrae or the paravertebral soft tissues, and can invade the epidural/intercostal spaces, mediastinum, pleura, and retroperitoneum. An MRI is needed to assess the anatomical location of paravertebral lesions, and an image-guided biopsy is required to determine the histological nature of the mass. Hence, paravertebral tumors should be considered as one of the differential diagnoses in a case of suspected lung mass.

Case Report: A 32 year old female presented with complaints of chest pain, shortness of breath for 1 month. On evaluation, routine investigations were within normal limits. Chest xray was suggestive of a well defined homogenous opacity in the right upper zone. Following which, a CECT thorax was done which revealed a pleural based soft tissue density lesion causing upliftment of the right main bronchus. Fibreoptic bronchoscopy was done which did not reveal any mass lesion. MRI chest was done which revealed a well defined encapsulated mass lesion in right paravertebral region protruding into the thoracic cavity. A CT guided biopsy was then performed which showed benign spindle cell mesenchymal neoplasm. Subsequently, immunohistochemistry was performed and a paravertebral soft tissue origin was confirmed. Hence, the patient was referred to neurosurgery department for further management.

Conclusion: Paravertebral tumors can protrude into the thoracic cavity, causing respiratory symptoms and can easily be misdiagnosed as a lung mass on a chest X-ray and sometimes even on a CECT thorax. Hence, it should be considered as a differential while dealing with a case of suspected lung mass.

Shabina Faruqi

GGMC and Sir JJ Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Asthma is a heterogeneous lung condition characterized by chronic airway inflammation. Asthma is a clinical diagnosis; however, symptoms and spirometry do not provide accurate representation of underlying airway inflammation. Biomarkers like blood eosinophil counts (BEC) and serum immunoglobulin E (IgE) can support the diagnosis of asthma. Fractional exhaled nitric oxide (FeNO) is a simple, non-invasive test for quantifying airway inflammation.

Aim: To establish correlation between FENO, IgE, BEC, spirometry (FEV1, FEF 25-75), asthma control measured by asthma control test (ACT) and number of exacerbations in previous year.

Methods: Study design: Cross-sectional analysis conducted in a tertiary care setting on patients with clinical diagnosis of asthma, aged 18-65 years. Parameters analyzed: (1) FeNO (2) BEC (3) IgE (4) Spirometry (FEV1, FEF25-75) (5) ACT (6) Number of exacerbations in previous year. Statistical analysis: Correlations analyzed using Pearson’s method. Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curves generated to calculate the area under the curve (AUC) and assess predictive capabilities [Table 1].

Results:

Showing correlation between FENO and other variables

FeNO: Fractional exhaled nitric oxide, ACT: Asthma control test, BEC: Blood eosinophil counts, IgE: Immunoglobulin E, FEV1: Forced expiratory volume in 1 s, FEF: Forced expiratory flow

A strong positive correlation was observed between FeNO and BEC (r=0.825, p<0.0001) and FeNO and serum IgE (r=0.497, p<0.0001). The findings show FeNO’s exceptional predictive capacity for BEC (AUC=0.94) and moderate capacity for IgE (AUC=0.71), suggesting its significant role in evaluating eosinophilic airway inflammation. The weak correlations with spirometric indices emphasize FeNO’s specificity for inflammatory processes, distinguishing it from measures of airflow limitation.

Conclusion: FeNO is a powerful biomarker for eosinophilic airway inflammation. Higher FeNO levels are associated with poor asthma control and increased risk of exacerbations.

Keywords: ACT, Asthma, eosinophils, FeNO

Pranay Bhad

Grant Government Medical College and Sir JJ Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India.

Introduction: Urinary incontinence (UI) is one of the priority health issues recognized by WHO. Urinary incontinence (UI) is defined as a loss of bladder control and is characterized by the complaint of any involuntary urine leakage. Evidence suggests that the prevalence of UI is higher in subjects with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) than in age-matched controls in both sexes. UI is classified as stress, urge, and mixed. However, the prevalence of UI in individuals with chronic respiratory diseases is mostly unexplored in clinical research and often underestimated in clinical practice.

Methods: This study was based among the patients in Outpatient Department of Pulmonary Medicine Department at JJ Hospital, Mumbai. 100 patients with various chronic respiratory diseases (COPD, Asthma, Cystic Bronchiectasis, ILD, Post TB OAD etc.) were evaluated for Urinary Incontinence (UI) by administration of Urinary Incontinence Questionnaire and ICIQ-UI Short Form. Whereas, cough was evaluated using Cough Severity Index (CSI) and Dyspnoea by mMRC scale.

Results: Out of 100 patients, 50 were male 50 were female. Of the 50 females 32 (64%) had UI and 21 (42%) out of 50 males showed UI. Out of which Stress incontinence was prominent in females (66.66%) while males showed nearly equal no. of stress and urge incontinence. According to ICIQ-UI SF scale, 54% patients with UI had a urine leakage several times a day and 24% once a day, whereas 2% patients with severe leakage all the day.

There was a direct relation between cough and UI. 66.66% of patients unintentionally lose urine while coughing and sneezing. Among the patients with diagnosed UI (47 out of 53) i.e. 89% had a CSI score >20 (0 to 20-10%, 20 to 30-36%, 30 to 40-54%). 11% UI patients (6 out of 53) had a CSI score >20 but with symptoms of UI. In terms of Dyspnoea, symptoms of UI were more in patients with Dyspnoea grade II (46%) and III (24%) (mMRC) scale, grade IV had 9% patients only. Non UI patients were with lesser grades of Dyspnoea (82%-Grade I and II).

Conclusions: Most patients with urinary incontinence had a mMRC score of II or III. Almost all (89%) patients with urinary incontinence had a CSI score of more than 20.

Pranav Modi, Himanshu Pophale, Pankaj Magar, Suhas Kulkarni, Unmesha Pawar

Smt. Kashibai Navale Medical College and General Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Background: HIV and tuberculosis (TB) are interconnected global health challenges, with TB being the leading cause of death among people living with HIV, particularly in low-and middle-income countries. In 2022, TB accounted for 187,000 deaths among HIV-positive individuals. Due to weakened immune systems, HIV-positive individuals are significantly more susceptible to developing active TB.

Method: This study examines clinical and demographic data from 50 HIV-positive patients co-infected with TB, focusing on antiretroviral therapy (ART) initiation, types of TB, treatment completion rates, and CD4 counts. This retrospective study analyzed 52 patients diagnosed with Mycobacterium tuberculosis (MTB) in HIV-positive individuals across all age groups and sexes. The subjects were followed from January 2021 to June 2024 at a tertiary care hospital. MTB diagnosis was based on clinical and microbiological evidence, while HIV diagnosis was confirmed using the enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA).

Results: The study on 52 TB-HIV co-infected patients revealed significant findings. The age range of patients was 20-50 years, with a slight male predominance (28 males, 24 females). Pulmonary TB was observed in 62% of patients, while 38% had extrapulmonary TB. ART was initiated before ATT in 48.1% of patients, while 51.9% began ART after ATT. CD4 counts ranged from 24-474 cells/mm³, with lower counts linked to poorer outcomes. Treatment outcomes showed a cure rate of 59.6 % (n-31), 17.3% (n-9) experiencing relapse, and 23.1% (n-12) succumbing to the disease.

Conclusion: These findings emphasize in assessing for HIV risk factors and to undergo HIV testing in all TB patients. Conversely, all HIV-positive cases should be screened for TB. Also the importance of initiating ART prior to ATT to improve TB treatment outcomes in HIV-positive patients. Monitoring CD4 counts and addressing immune suppression early is critical to enhancing treatment adherence and reducing mortality.

Introduction: A tracheoesophageal fistula (TEF) is a congenital or acquired communication between the trachea and esophagus. Case Presentation: A 46-year-old smoker male presented to us with complaints of chest pain, cough on swallowing, hemoptysis for 2 weeks and dysphagia for two months. The patient also gave a history of loss of appetite associated with loss of weight.

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Adrenal crisis occurs when there is decreased secretion of steroid hormones from the adrenal glands due to various etiologies. Rifampicin may increase the metabolism of cortisol by inducing cytochrome CYP3A4 activity and precipitate an adrenal crisis.

Case: A 61-year-old gentleman presented with complaints of cough with expectoration for 9 months, Loss of appetite and weight for 1 year. Chest Xray showed left sided pleural effusion and BAL xpert confirmed diagnosis of pulmonary TB outside. He was initiated on programmatic regimen of 2HRZE, followed by 4HRE. He developed abdominal pain, loss of consciousness, hypotension, lethargy, and weakness 2 days after starting ATT. He required ICU admission and he required inotropic support with which he improved. ATT which was tentatively stopped was restarted and he had a similar episode once again after restarting ATT. On presentation he stable and examination was unremarkable. Blood counts, serum electrolytes, liver and renal parameters were normal. Chest x ray showed bilateral pleural effusion and pleural fluid analysis showed exudative lymphocytic rich with low ADA. CT thorax revealed bilateral nodular lesions, multiple mediastinal and intrabdominal lymphadenopathy, bilateral pleural effusion, bilateral adrenal enlargement. Serum cortisol (8am) was 1.2 mcg% (range: 5.27-22.45), ACTH – 485pg/ml (range: 0-46), aldosterone 21.4 pg/ml (range: 25.2-392) and direct renin 16.8 mIU/ml (4.4-46.1). CT guided biopsy from left paraaortic LN showed non necrotizing focal granulomatous inflammation, special stains for AFB and fungal were negative. He was discharged on oral prednisolone 30mg/day and fludrocortisone 25mcg/day along with ATT. He was followed after 2months and improved well. He was planned for lifelong steroid replacement therapy.

Conclusion: Current TB treatment guidelines should incorporate the diagnosis and treatment of adrenal tuberculosis regarding dose and duration of steroid replacement therapy.

Treating physicians should be cautious on initiating rifampicin containing ATT for TB in those with adrenal involvement.

M. Sugumar Christopher, Balamugesh T., Barney Isaac

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis is a more heterogeneous and complex disease. Different clinical phenotypes and endotypes were identified previously. Single clinical variable may not be useful to assess the severity of the disease. This study aimed to identify the different clinical phenotypes and to assess their severity to implicate the knowledge to develop more personalised treatment.

Methods: It is a cross-sectional study conducted in adult patients with bronchiectasis over the period of one year. QOL was assessed by using BHQ score and disease severity was measured by FACED and BSI scores.

Results: Among 125 patients (54.4% male) five clusters were identified: Pseudomonas colonisation (16%), other organism colonisation (7%), daily sputum production (38%), intermittent sputum production (27%) and dry bronchiectasis (12%). The different aetiologies identified were post tuberculosis sequelae (44%), Idiopathic (34 %), ABPA (8%) and other causes (14%).

Our study found that most common cause of bronchiectasis is post-TB sequelae. Among the all-phenotypes average FACED and BSI scores were highest in Pseudomonas colonisation group and QOL is better in dry bronchiectasis group.

Conclusion: Cluster analysis, using clinical, microbiological, functional and radiological variables, identified five clinical phenotypes easily detected according to the presence of chronic infection with P. aeruginosa or other pathogens and sputum productivity. Patients belonging to these clinical phenotypes show differences in quality of life and mortality prediction.

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, BSI, FACED scores, phenotypes

Ajay Dhruw, Kamlesh G. Vithalani

Department of Respiratory Medicine, PDU Medical College, Rajkot, Gujarat, India

Introduction: Rifampicin is a crucial drug in the treatment regimens for tuberculosis. Usually it is well tolerated. Apart from minor side effects like occasional hepatotoxicity, abdominal discomfort, nausea and vomiting, very rarely it can cause life threatening acute renal failure or thrombocytopenia. Rifampicin induced thrombocytopenia was first reported in 1970. It is usually reversible if detected early and treated appropriately. The Tuberculosis Research Centre (TRC), Chennai of the Indian Council of Medical Research has been conducting controlled clinical trials for treatment of tuberculosis since 1956. Since 1974, more than 8000 patients with pulmonary and extra-pulmonary tuberculosis have been treated with rifampicin-containing treatment regimens. Here we report a case of thrombocytopenia caused by daily administration of rifampicin after interruption of the drug for two months in view of its rare occurrence.

Case Report: A case of a 60-year-old female presented to our hospital with complaints of low-grade evening rise fever, dyspnea on exertion, cough with expectoration, anorexia and weight loss since the last 2 months. CXR was suggestive of left apical Koch’s lesion. Sputum examination was positive for AFB smear. Patient was started on ATT (4FDC) as per weight band. After 1 month of ATT, patient developed bleeding per vaginum and rash on both legs. On examination, there was purpura over both legs. Blood examination revealed a Platlet count of 6000/cumm. ATT was put on hold, and patient was started on prednisolone, Patient also received platelet transfusion. After 5 days of therapy, the PC was 2.19 lac/cumm. Therefore, patient was again started on ATT. After 3 days of taking ATT, patient again developed purpuric rash over both legs. Blood reports revealed PC of 2000/cumm. ATT was put on hold, and patient received prednisolone and platelet transfusion until platelet count was normalized. ANA screening was positive for auto antibodies. It was suspected that patient was having rifampicin induced auto immune thrombocytopenia. Therefore, patient was started on ATT regimen not containing rifampicin. Patient did not develop any symptoms after stopping rifampicin.

Conclusions: Serious adverse reactions during rifampicin therapy are usually reversible if detected early and treated appropriately. The clinician must be aware of these rare complications which are life threatening, but if detected early are completely reversible. It has been recommended that rifampicin-induced thrombocytopenia is an absolute contraindication to further therapy with rifampicin. However, Bhasin and co-workers has suggested that rechallenges should be done before finally withdrawing rifampicin. In our opinion, when it is necessary to re-start rifampicin, one should always check the platelet counts frequently and this should be treated under supervision with coverage of steroids.

Aditya Gaikwad

Smt. Kashibai Navale Medical College and General Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Objective: This study aims to explore the correlation between Body Mass Index (BMI) and Peak Expiratory Flow Rate (PEFR) in patients attending a tertiary care hospital. The relationship between these two variables has significant clinical implications, particularly in understanding respiratory function in individuals with varying body weight statuses.

Methods: A cross-sectional study was conducted involving 130 adult patients, aged 18 to 65 years, who visited the hospital’s pulmonary medicine department. BMI was calculated using the standard formula, while PEFR was measured using a portable peak flow meter. Patients were grouped according to their BMI categories: underweight, normal weight, overweight, and obese. Data analysis was performed to assess the correlation between BMI and PEFR.

Results: The mean BMI of the participants was 27.5 ± 5.1, with a significant proportion categorized as overweight or obese. The overall PEFR values showed a significant inverse correlation with increasing BMI. Specifically, individuals in the obese category exhibited the lowest PEFR values, while those in the normal weight category had higher peak flow rates. Analysis revealed a moderate negative correlation between BMI and PEFR, suggesting that higher BMI is associated with reduced expiratory flow rates.

Conclusion: This study indicates a significant negative correlation between BMI and PEFR, with higher BMI leading to a decrease in respiratory function. These findings highlight the importance of weight management in improving pulmonary health. Future studies with larger sample sizes and longitudinal data are recommended to further investigate the underlying mechanisms of this association and its long-term health implications.

Roshan Katara

Shri M. P. Shah Medical College and Hospital, Jamnagar, Gujarat, India

Introduction: This case study presents a 55-year-old male patient initially suspected of having community-acquired pneumonia (CAP), but later diagnosed with pulmonary infarction (PI) resulting from pulmonary embolism (PE). The case highlights the importance of considering PI in the differential diagnosis of patients presenting with symptoms similar to CAP.

Case Report: A 55-year-old male presented with a 10-day history of right-sided localized chest pain, dry cough, and grade 2 MMRC dyspnea. He reported high-grade fever for 5 days. Initial examination showed no tachypnea or tachycardia. Laboratory tests revealed leukocytosis (TC 11k) and elevated D-dimer (1637 ng/mL). Chest X-ray showed right lower zone peripheral opacification, while ultrasound indicated right lower lung consolidation with minimal pleural effusion. ECG was normal, but troponin I was elevated (2.97 ng/ml). Initially treated for suspected CAP, further investigation with CT pulmonary angiography revealed PE with complete occlusion of the right descending artery and right lower lobe pulmonary infarct. Echocardiography showed no right atrial or ventricular dilation, apical akinesia, or pulmonary hypertension. Given the patient’s clinical stability and absence of hypoxia or hemoptysis, thrombolysis was not performed. Treatment with oral anticoagulants was initiated. Follow-up at 7 months showed resolution of chest X-ray opacities.

Conclusion: Pulmonary infarction can mimic CAP in its initial presentation. A high suspicion index and appropriate diagnostic workup are essential to differentiate between these conditions and provide optimal treatment, thereby preventing potentially devastating pulmonary complications. Pulmonary infarction, community-acquired pneumonia, pulmonary embolism, D-dimer, anticoagulation.

This case underscores the importance of considering pulmonary infarction in patients presenting with CAP-like symptoms, especially when accompanied by elevated D-dimer levels. Timely and appropriate investigations, including CT pulmonary angiography, are crucial for accurate diagnosis and management.

NKP Salve Institute of Medical Sciences and Lata Mangeshkar Hospital, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Complicated pneumonia refers to pneumonia that is associated with complications affecting the pleura or other structures. This is a case of Pneumonia complicated with Parapneumonic effusion associated with Hepatic Abscess.

Case Report: A 58-year-old male was admitted with complaints of right-side chest pain, fever and cough with expectoration. Chest X-ray was done and a diagnosis of Right-sided CAP was made. Sputum C/S was negative and BAL C/S showed Klebsiella Growth. Antibiotics were modified accordingly. The patient didn’t respond to antibiotics, counts were raised, O2 requirement increased and breath sounds were reduced on the Right side, chest X-ray was done s/o parapneumonic effusion. The patient improved symptomatically and clinically over the course of the treatment and was maintaining saturation on room air, hence was discharged.

Conclusion: This case highlights the importance of considering secondary infections or complications in patients with pneumonia who do not respond to appropriate antibiotic therapy. As seen in this patient, the coexistence of parapneumonic effusion and hepatic abscess emphasises the need for a thorough evaluation, including imaging and culture studies, to identify and treat associated infections promptly. Early intervention with targeted therapy, drainage procedures, and supportive care can significantly improve patient outcomes and prevent further complications.

Mrunal N. Gaikwad1, Aniket M. Mathesul2, Nitin S. Gaikwad3, Ashwin Ramesh4, Dr. Shruti Mudale5

1Department of Public Health, Maharashtra University of Health Sciences, Nashik, Maharashtra, India, 2Department of Community Medicine, MIMER Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 3Department of Respiratory Medicine, MIMER Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 4Department of Critical Care Medicine, Bharti Vidyapeeth, Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India, 5Department of Microbiology, BJ Medical College and Sassoon General Hospital, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Evidence-Based Medicine (EBM) and healthcare ethics are essential for ensuring high-quality, patient-centered care. However, in resource-limited settings like Maharashtra, India, challenges such as knowledge gaps, resource constraints, and insufficient training hinder their effective implementation. This study evaluates the knowledge, attitudes, and practices (KAP) of EBM and healthcare ethics among doctors in Maharashtra.

Methods: A cross-sectional survey was conducted among 195 doctors from primary, secondary, and tertiary healthcare facilities. A validated KAP questionnaire was distributed electronically to collect data on EBM knowledge, attitudes towards evidence-based practices, and ethical considerations in patient care. Data analysis included descriptive & inferential statistics to identify correlations between sociodemographic factors and KAP outcomes. Ethical approval was obtained, adhering to principles of confidentiality and informed consent.

Results: The study revealed gaps in knowledge, with only 38.4% of respondents aware of the Nuremberg Declaration, while 63.1% correctly identified “primum non nocere.” Positive attitudes towards EBM were evident, with 71.2% recognizing its role in improving patient outcomes, though 26.1% preferred experience-based medicine. Only 25.1% consistently applied EBM in clinical practice, with barriers including time constraints, limited resources, and insufficient training. Ethical dilemmas were frequently encountered, with 24.1% reporting weekly challenges and relying more on informal advice than formal ethics committees.

Conclusion: The study underscores the need for comprehensive interventions to address knowledge gaps, improve attitudes, and ensure the consistent practice of Evidence-Based Medicine and healthcare ethics. By implementing structured training programs, enhancing access to evidence-based resources, and strengthening institutional ethical frameworks, healthcare professionals can be better equipped to deliver high-quality, patient-centered care, ultimately improving clinical outcomes and fostering ethical medical practices.

Keywords: Attitudes, evidence-based medicine, healthcare ethics, knowledge, Maharashtra, practices.

Anisha Rajvanshi, Jigneshkumar Patel, Zarir Udwadia

PD Hinduja National Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Histoplasmosis is often underdiagnosed and misidentified in India as tuberculosis, due to overlapping clinical features. This report highlights the difficulties in diagnosing histoplasmosis and presents the successful management of a case initially confused with tuberculosis.

Case Report: A 35-year-old female presented with persistent throat pain, unresponsive to usual antibiotic course. She had no complains of fever, cough or weight loss. In view of persistent throat pain, a biopsy of the posterior pharyngeal wall revealed inflammatory granulation tissue. Chest CT scan was advised after the biopsy which demonstrated centrilobular nodules and left upper lobe cavity, suggestive of an infectious process. She had history of pulmonary tuberculosis, treated in 2007 for 9 months. Despite negative sputum TB Gene Xpert and TB culture, she was treated empirically with anti-TB medication, but her symptom of sore throat persisted. At this point she presented to our institute and underwent a bronchoscopy. Bronchoscopy revealed irregular bronchial mucosa, and biopsy samples showed intracytoplasmic organisms consistent with Histoplasma capsulatum on GMS stain. Diagnosis of histoplasmosis was confirmed through urine antigen testing and the patient was started on IV amphotericin B. On detailed history taking patient revealed travel to endemic areas in USA few weeks before her symptoms developed. She was discharged on oral itraconazole and showed significant clinical and radiological improvement at one-month follow-up.

Conclusion: This case highlights the importance of considering histoplasmosis as a key differential diagnosis for tuberculosis.

Vedant Zalke

NKP Salve Institute of Medical Sciences and Lata Mangeshkar Hospital, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB), particularly pulmonary TB, is a major global health challenge. This study evaluates changes in inflammatory biomarkers (CRP/HSCRP, NLR, TLC, RDW, ESR) in pulmonary TB patients at diagnosis and after the intensive phase of anti-tubercular therapy, and their correlation with radiological outcomes.

Methods: This prospective cohort study included 35 pulmonary TB patients (>18 years) diagnosed clinically, radiologically, or microbiologically. Inflammatory markers (CRP/HSCRP, ESR, NLR, RDW) and chest X-rays (by Modified Chest X-ray scoring system) were assessed at diagnosis and after the intensive therapy phase. Data were analyzed using SPSS v24 with descriptive statistics and significance tests (p<0.05).

Results: Among 35 patients (65% males, 35% females; mean age 40 years), CRP was higher in males. After 2 months, markers significantly decreased: CRP/HSCRP (25.1 ± 4.5 to 6.2 ± 2.1 mg/L), ESR (75.3 ± 12.1 to 32.8 ± 8.5 mm/hr), NLR (5.2 ± 1.4 to 2.1 ± 0.7), and RDW (16.5 ± 1.2% to 14.8 ± 0.9%) (all p<0.001). Radiological improvement occurred in 71.4% (10 severe to moderate, 8 moderate to mild, 7 complete resolution). However, 28.6% showed no improvement despite reduced markers, suggesting residual or delayed resolution.

Conclusion: Inflammatory markers (CRP/HSCRP, ESR, NLR, RDW) are effective for monitoring pulmonary TB treatment. Reductions in these markers correlated with radiological improvement, but 28.6% showed no radiological improvement despite marker decline, suggesting the need for extended follow-up and integration of inflammatory markers in routine clinical monitoring.

Keywords: Inflammatory markers, modified chest xray scoring system, pulmonary tuberculosis

S. M. Rahul

Belagavi Institute of Medical Sciences, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Pulmonary alveolar microlithiasis is a rare, progressive lung disease characterized by the widespread presence of tiny, calcium-rich deposits called microliths within the alveoli. These microliths can lead to respiratory impairment over time. PAM was first described in 1918 by the Norwegian physician Harbitz, and the condition is sometimes referred to as “Harbitz’s syndrome.

Case Description: A 35year old female who by occupation is farmer presented to casualty with insidious onset breathlessness which gradually progressed and on 15ltrs of o2 on NRBM with no relieving factors and also complaints dry cough since 10 days. All the symptoms developed following exposure to fertilizer. Following chest x ray bilateral diffuse infiltrate were noted. HRCT thorax findings were diffuse hyper dense calcification, sub pleural calcification with black pleura sign suggestive of pulmonary alveolar microlithiasis. Following bronchoscopy BAL microliths were observed. No similar family history. No other comorbidities.

It’s a rare genetic disorder seen mostly in middle aged female patients. It can be either sporadic or familial in occurrence. Can be suspected with acute history and diagnosed mainly on radiological basis. Currently the treatment recommended is sequential bilateral double lung transplant.

Conclusion: Pulmonary alveolar microlithiasis a rare inherited disease caused by mutations in the SLC34A2 gene. It requires integrated clinical, radiological, and histopathologic approach. The disease often presents with non-specific symptoms or can be asymptomatic in its early stages. As the condition progresses, it can lead to severe complications, including respiratory failure. Management varies from severity, ranging from supportive therapies to use of Disodium Etidronate (DE) to lung transplantation in advanced stages. Early diagnosis and careful monitoring are crucial to improving patient outcomes and slowing disease progression.

Ajal Shaji

Aster Medcity, Kochi, Kerala, India

Introduction: The word allergy is derived from the Greek words:”allos” meaning “other” or “different” and “ergon” meaning “work” or “action”. Allergic diseases are systemic disorders caused by an impaired immune system.

Case Report: 50 years old female, presented with complaints of swelling over epigastric region which she noticed few weeks back. She was planned for Laparoscopic epigastric hernia repair on 12/10/2024. After induction of anaesthesia, patient developed hypotension and desaturation. Possibility of anaphylactic shock was considered, surgery was deferred and she was shifted to ICU. A Pulmonology consultation was sought. From her history, we realized that she has undergone 3 prior surgeries during which she has received multiple anesthetic agents like xylocaine, propofol, scoline, atracurium, hydrocort, vecuronium, fentanyl. During this time, she was given Inj propofol, Inj Atracurium and Inj piptaz.

From history we realised that she has already received inj propofol and atracurium in the past. So the likely culprit is inj Piptaz. We were planning to proceed with an intradermal drug allergy testing next. But due to recent anaphylaxis, we first did a histamine challenge test to make sure her mast cells are active. The test came out to be positive and she was taken up for intradermal allergy testing using 26 drugs next day. The panel included commonly used anaesthetic agents and few antibiotics. She was found allergic to Piptaz, cis atracurium, ciprobid, ketamine. The test did not show positive result to antibiotic ceftum. Hence as part of finding an alternative drug which can be given to the patient peri and post operatively, we did an oral drug challenge with tab ceftum which she tolerated. We also did an IV drug challenge with cefuroxime the next day. She tolerated it as well. Hence Cefuroxime was proved to be safe in this patient by intradermal, intravenous and oral challenge. She underwent laparoscopic hernia repair and was discharged.

Conclusion: Taking a detailed clinical history is very important in drug allergy cases. 80% of patients with drug allergy in childhood outgrow them as age advances.

Patients with a history of pencillin allergy are more likely to be treated with less effective, more toxic, or more expensive antibiotics, leading to increased cost, antibiotic associated infections, longer hospital stays and increased mortality. Therefore a proactive effort should be made to delabel pencillin allergy whenever possible.

Ajal Shaji

Aster Medcity, Kochi, Kerala, India

Introduction: Thoracoscopy first emerged as a diagnostic tool in the early 20th century when therapeutic pneumothorax became the surgical treatment of choice for tuberculosis. Currently, the role of medical thoracoscopy in the treatment of undiagnosed pleural effusions is increasingly being recognized.

Methods: We analyzed prospective data of 50 thoracoscopic procedures performed at our center on patients with undiagnosed exudative pleural effusions in 6 months. Patients were subjected to medical thoracoscopy based on ultrasonographic stratification and a computed tomography (CT) thorax. Medical thoracoscopy was performed after an intercostal block under conscious sedation with midazolam (2 mg) and fentanyl (50 mcg) and local anesthesia with lignocaine 2% (10–15 ml), through a semirigid thoracoscope.

Results: A total of 50 patients (28 males and 22 females) underwent medical thoracoscopy during the study period. The mean age was 56.96 ± 14.84 (range, 22–88). The final diagnosis by thoracoscopy was bacterial empyema in 8 patients, tuberculosis in 13 patients and malignancy in 19 patients. Medical thoracoscopy was successful without subsequent intervention in 48, two patients underwent a second procedure, in the form of decortication. There were no major procedure-related complications that required intervention (adenosine deaminase or interferon) have been negative.

Conclusion: Medical dogma teaches: “the sun should never rise nor fall on a pleural effusion.” Although this is an overly simplistic view, the basic principle that a new pleural effusion needs prompt and thorough evaluation still holds. Medical thoracoscopy is a minimally invasive and cost-effective procedure in patients with exudative pleural effusions who have been stratified carefully using radiological methods.

Keywords: Pleural effusion, thoracoscopy, tuberculosis

Bharat Toshniwal, Dushyant Khedikar, Jafferhusein Sura, Archana Muralidharan, Lavanya M, Rahul Jaiswal, Samir Gami, Sandeep Athavale, Seemab Shaikh, Sowmya Ghosh, Sweta Lohiya, Gurmeet Thakur, Vaishali Gupte, Anand Pendakur

Toshniwal Chest Hospital Maternity and Nursing Home, Nanded, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Allergic Rhinitis (AR) is increasing in India, significantly impacting patients’ quality of life and daily activities. Combining an antihistamine with montelukast offers a complementary effect in reducing AR symptoms. This study evaluated the effectiveness and tolerability of the fixed-dose combination of Montelukast and Fexofenadine (FDC-M/F: 10 mg + 120 mg) in real-world settings.

Methods: A retrospective, open-label, multicentre study, included patients aged >15 years, with symptoms suggesting AR (mild-intermittent/persistent, or moderate intermittent) or Total Nasal Symptom Score (TNSS) of 5 or more, provided they hadn’t used antihistamines in the past week or FDC – M/F in the last 4 weeks. Medical records were collected from the database of 12 sites across India from June 2022-June 2023. The primary variables assessed included changes in TNSS, Total Ocular Symptom Score (TOSS), Physicians Global Assessment (PGA), and Patients Global Assessment Scale (PGAS) from baseline to 4-6 weeks. All statistical analyses were conducted using R statistical software (R 4.4.0, R Core Team, 2024, Vienna Austria) with a two-tailed overall significance level set at 0.05.

Results: A total of 329 medical records met the enrolment criteria. Significant improvement in TNSS (baseline 6.66 to 0.95, p <0.001) and TOSS (baseline 1.54 to 0.23, p <0.001) were observed with FDC M/F. The mean treatment duration was 5 weeks (interquartile range: 4-6 weeks). The combination was well-tolerated, and most patients were satisfied with the treatment.

Conclusion: The combination of fexofenadine, a long-acting H1 antagonist, and montelukast, a potent LTRA, provides dual action on early-and late-phase allergic reactions in AR. This Indian multicentre study supports the effectiveness of FDC-M/F in mild intermittent/persistent AR and highlights the need for randomized trials to assess its side effect profile.

The FDC M/F may be an attractive treatment option for mild-intermittent, mild-persistent AR.

Keywords: Allergic rhinitis, FDC, fexofenadine, Montelukast, TNSS, Total Nasal Symptom Score

Introduction: Empyema necessitans is a rare complication of empyema, defined by the extension of a thoracic empyema through surrounding soft tissues towards the chest wall, sometimes forming skin disconnections. This condition is particularly associated with Mycobacterium tuberculosis, especially in immune-compromised individuals.

Case Description: We report the case of a 42-year-old male with a history of HIV and pulmonary tuberculosis who presented with shortness of breath, fever, and progressive swelling in the right hemithorax. Niralam Kaur

Government Medical College, Patiala, Punjab, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) remains a major health concern in developing nations, with an increasing global incidence, including its extrapulmonary forms. Extrapulmonary TB constitutes 15–20% of all TB cases, while cutaneous tuberculosis is a rare manifestation, accounting for only 1.5% of these cases. Mycobacterium tuberculosis, M. bovis, and occasionally the Calmette-Guérin bacillus (BCG) can cause Cutaneous TB. Lupus vulgaris, the most common form of Cutaneous TB, presents as progressive, firm plaques with central clearing and active margins. Due to its paucibacillary nature and nonspecific clinical features, diagnosing Lupus Vulgaris is often delayed or misdiagnosed.

Case Report: A 35-year-old male presented with a persistent, reddish, firm plaque on the left malar region for four months. Systemic examination was unremarkable, with no past history of TB or contact history of TB. Chest X-ray and sputum workup were normal. Dermatology consultation ruled out other differential diagnoses. Histopathological analysis of a skin biopsy revealed mild hyperkeratosis, epidermal acanthosis, and ill-formed granulomas within dense lymphocytic infiltrates in the dermis, confirming lupus vulgaris. Anti-tubercular therapy (ATT) comprising Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Pyrazinamide, and Ethambutol was initiated. Remarkable lesion resolution and clinical improvement were observed following treatment.

Conclusion: Cutaneous TB, though rare, is challenging to diagnose due to its nonspecific and paucibacillary presentation, often mimicking conditions like sarcoidosis, discoid lupus erythematosus, fungal infections, or basal cell carcinoma. While caseous necrosis in granulomas is diagnostic, its absence does not rule out TB. In this case, clinical and histopathological findings supported the diagnosis and guided treatment.

Diagnosing lupus vulgaris is complex and demands vigilance, especially in endemic regions like India. Prompt recognition and treatment are essential to ensure favorable outcomes and minimize complications.

SSIMS and RC, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis is a public health problem worldwide, especially in developing countries like India. About two-thirds of patients continue to have sequelae and pulmonary impairment despite adequate microbiological cure. Our study aimed to assess the overall clinical status and lung function of treated post-pulmonary tuberculosis patients.

Methods: This was a hospital-based cross-sectional study conducted over a period of 12 months (2023-24) at Davanagere, Karnataka. They were administered structured questionnaire seeking socio-demographic data, respiratory symptomatology and subjected to chest x ray, sputum smear examination and spirometry. Patients with smear positive for acid fast bacilli, active haemoptysis, cardiac comorbidities and those not willing to give consent were excluded.

Results: Of the 70 recruited participants, males constituted 61.4% (n= 43) and 38.6% (n=27) were females. Mean age was 54.6 ±12.8 years. On spirometry, obstructive pattern seen in 51.4%, restrictive pattern 21.4%, mixed pattern 12.8% and normal finding in 14.3%.

Conclusion: Given the significant proportion of persistent respiratory symptoms, radiographic and spirometric abnormalities, post-tuberculosis patients should be carefully evaluated and followed-up regularly to control exacerbations and reduce premature mortality.

Jigar Khanpara

Lilavati Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Severe aortic stenosis (AS) is a critical cardiac condition that can be exacerbated by systemic infections, leading to life-threatening complications. Mycoplasma pneumonia, though a common cause of atypical pneumonia, rarely triggers severe cardiogenic and septic shock. This case highlights the complexities of managing such patients and the pivotal role of veno-arterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (V-A ECMO) as a bridge to definitive surgical treatment.

Case Description: A 55-year-old female with severe AS presented with acute fever, dyspnoea, and hypotension. Investigations confirmed Mycoplasma pneumonia as the aetiology of severe sepsis. The patient rapidly deteriorated, developing refractory septic and cardiogenic shock despite optimal medical therapy, including antibiotics, vasopressors, steroids, and mechanical ventilation. Echocardiography revealed critically reduced cardiac output due to severe AS. Patient underwent cardiac arrest and was emergently initiated on V-A ECMO for circulatory and respiratory support [E-CPR]. After stabilization, she underwent successful aortic valve replacement. Postoperatively, ECMO was weaned off, and the patient recovered but had single organ residual dysfunction, landed on permanent haemodialysis.

Conclusion: This case demonstrates the necessity of prompt multidisciplinary management in patients with severe AS complicated by infections. V-A ECMO can provide critical support, serving as a bridge to definitive interventions such as valve replacement surgery.

Mycoplasma pneumonia in the context of severe AS can precipitate catastrophic complications. Early diagnosis, timely initiation of advanced support modalities like V-A ECMO, and coordinated surgical intervention are crucial to achieving favourable outcomes. This report emphasizes the importance of integrating ECMO in the management algorithm for critically ill patients with dual cardiac and infectious pathologies.
