Vinay C. Dharmadhikari

S. S. Institute of Medical Sciences and Research Centre, Davangere, Karnataka, India

Introduction: A pneumothorax occurs when air accumulates between the visceral and parietal pleura. Classified as spontaneous, iatrogenic or traumatic. Further classified as primary or secondary, based on underlying lung parenchyma. Secondary spontaneous pneumothorax (SSPs) occur in the presence of underlying lung disease, most commonly COPD, Tuberculosis. SSP has high chance of recurrence (>50%) even in the first episode and can lead to more physiologic imbalance and can also be life-threatening.

Case: Case 1: 48 year old male, K/C/O COPD came with C/O Breathlessness on exertion, right chest pain since 8 days with H/O hospitalisation 1 month ago for similar complaints and right ICD was inserted. Chest Xray was done s/o right pneumothorax. Right ICD was done, good resolution of pneumothorax, then Chemical pleurodesis with Povidine iodine was done through ICD.

Case 2: 56 year old male with K/C/O COPD came with c/o Breathlessness on exertion and right chest pain since 2 days. Similar complaints 2 months ago, right ICD procedure was done. CXR-s/o pneumothorax with bilateral emphysematous changes. Right ICD inserted, Pleurodesis with Povidine iodine through ICD was done. Case 3: 61 year old male with H/O pulmonary TB 22 years ago. Came with c/o breathlessness on exertion since 2 days with left chest pain. H/O similar episode 20 days ago, Left ICD was inserted. CXR done-s/o post tb sequalae with pneumothorax. Left ICD inserted, pleurodesis done through ICD with povidine Iodine. In all 3 cases-1 month,6 month follow up no further recurrence of pneumothorax.

Conclusion: In a resource limited setting with no medical thoracoscopy facility pleurodesis through ICD tube with povidine iodine can be considered with probable good resolution of pneumothorax with reduced risk of recurrence.

Patients with recurrent secondary spontaneous pneumothorax should be managed promptly and an early intervention helps in better outcomes.

Anoop Ponnachan

Grant Government Medical College and Sir JJ Group of Hospitals, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tubercular pleural effusion is a common presentation in regions with high tuberculosis prevalence and is typically managed with antitubercular therapy (ATT).[1,2] However, the overlap of symptoms between tuberculosis and malignancies poses diagnostic challenges,[2,3] particularly in resource-limited settings. Here, we present a unique case initially diagnosed as tubercular pleural effusion and treated with ATT, later revealed to be renal cell carcinoma with metastasis. This case highlights the importance of thorough diagnostic evaluation and maintaining a broad differential diagnosis when treating pleural effusions, even in TB-endemic areas.

Case: 65 year old male urban Mumbai resident electronic repair agent, presented to OPD with symptoms of cough, evening rise in temperature and chest pain since last 7 days. Clinical examination revealed decreased breath sounds and stony dullness on the right hemithorax. Chest X-ray and CT thorax demonstrated a large pleural effusion with multiple pulmonary nodules. Diagnostic thoracocentesis yielded exudative fluid with high protein, lymphocytosis, and elevated adenosine deaminase (ADA) levels, and microbiological confirmation (MTB detected very low) supporting a diagnosis of tubercular pleural effusion. After multiple pleural tap around 3L pleural fluid was aspirated. Further evaluation, including abdominal imaging, revealed bilateral renal mass consistent with malignancy, confirmed by histopathology as renal cell carcinoma (RCC).[4] CT highlighted metastatic pulmonary involvement. The patient was initiated on anti-tuberculosis therapy (ATT) alongside palliative care for the malignancy.

Conclusion: The coexistence of tuberculosis and malignancy poses diagnostic challenges due to overlapping clinical and radiological features. Immunosuppression associated with malignancy and metastasis may predispose patients to opportunistic infections like tuberculosis.[5] Early and accurate differentiation is crucial for guiding treatment, as both conditions require distinct management strategies.

Coexistence of TB and immunodeficiency is very common. Workup for HIV and Malignancy should me diagnosed with a wider and more open differentials will help in managemt of other life threatening conditions.

Gaikwad Naresh, C. Korday, A. Dandawate, N. Inamdar, S. Agarwal

HBT Medical College and Dr. R N Cooper Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Disseminated tuberculosis remains a diagnostic dilemma in infancy due to the paucibacillary nature of the disease. A high index of suspicion, thorough family history, and extensive investigations may be the only way to reach a diagnosis.

Case: A 2-month-old full term female child with 3kg birth weight was admitted with fever, cough, poor feeding, tachypnoea, and bilateral crepitations. X-Ray chest showed bilateral lung infiltrates with lymphocytic leukocytosis on CBC suggesting viral bronchopneumonia. She was treated with HHHFNC, intravenous fluids and antibiotics. CT abdomen done for developing abdominal distension showed only mildly dilated bowel loops. Father was treated for pulmonary cavitatory TB two years ago. TB workup in the mother and child was negative. The child improved, gained adequate weight and was discharged. 3 months later, the child was readmitted with right sided divergent squint suggestive of 3rd nerve palsy. Follow up chest X-Ray showed bilateral micronodular infiltrates suggesting miliary tuberculosis. MRI Brain revealed a ring enhancing lesion in the midbrain with vasogenic edema and eccentric scolex suspicious of neurocysticercosis/tuberculoma. MR spectroscopy was planned, however, the parents refused further hospital stay. The child was given AKT, steroids, and anti-helminthic therapy but compliance at home was questionable. During this time the father developed bronchiectasis and became hypoxic on room air. Tragically, the parents never followed up and the child succumbed three weeks later.

Conclusion: Tuberculosis presents in a diverse evolving manner even in young infants creating diagnostic dilemmas. Microbiological diagnosis is a challenge in pediatric tuberculosis. One may have to rely on the clinical and radiological route.

It is imperative to be vigilant especially in screening family contacts while managing pediatric tuberculosis. Stringent adherence to treatment and attitude change in the family is extremely important to prevent devastating consequences in our fight against tuberculosis.

Afshan Sadaf

Deccan College of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Pulmonary Aspergillosis can be in the form of Invasive Aspergillosis, Aspergilloma, Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis and chronic necrotizing Aspergillus pneumonia. Fungal entities are very rare pathogens with <1% of all pleural effusions of fungal etiology.

Case Description: A 68 year old male presented to respiratory clinic with complaints of productive cough and low grade/intermittent fever for fifteen days. Known diabetic on treatment with oral antihyperglycemic agents and a chronic smoker with a very recent cessation. Patient was initiated on an empirical trial of Antitubercular Treatment despite sputum negativity. After one month of treatment, due to no improvement in patient’s symptoms the trial was abandoned. On presentation to our hospital, a detailed blood workup/imaging was ordered. HRCT Chest revealed a right sided pyopneumothorax with pleural calcifications. An ICD was placed, the fluid was drained and sent for detailed evaluation.

Conclusion: Aerobic culture and sensitivity test to check for growth of bacteria was negative but fungal growth had been observed. Growth of fungal hyphae on cultures done on pleural fluid remains the gold standard for diagnosing an Aspergillus infection.

The diagnosis of Aspergillosis as a cause of pleural effusion is not one that is usually made unless there is a high index of suspicion based on the clinical presentation.

Afshan Sadaf

Deccan College of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: (1) Pneumonia is a common respiratory disorder, which is a leading cause of disability adjusted life years worldwide. (2) Non resolving pneumonia includes: Incomplete clearance of radiological infiltrates in 4 weeks. <50% clearance in chest infiltrates, who improved symptomatically. Non resolution of radiological infiltrates in an expected period of time with 10 days of antibiotics.

Methods: A hospital based prospective study. Sample size – 50, Investigations done after labelling patient as non-resolving pneumonia are ultrasound guided FNAC and bronchoscopy, and subjected to both the diagnostic procedures (cytology/microbiological examination). Inclusion/exclusion criteria are specified. Interpretation/Statistical analysis is performed.

Results: Out of 50 cases, distribution of consolidation is as follows. Medial – 11, Lateral-16 and Mixed – 23

Discussion: Yield obtained in medial consolidation is more by bronchoscopy whereas in lateral consolidation it is more by FNAC.

Conclusion: Sensitivity of FNAC for total cases is found as 76% which is better compared to 70% in bronchoscopy. Comparison of FNAC and bronchoscopy in medially and laterally located consolidation shows marked variation. P value using chi square test is 0.00001.

Keywords: Bronchoscopy, FNAC, non-resolving consolidation

Debdeep Sen, Arup Halder, Raja Dhar

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) is a common disorder with far-reaching health implications. Understanding the influence of gender, age, BMI and comorbidities on the apnea-hypopnea index (AHI) is important for phenotyping and judging long term outcome. This study examines data from sleep studies conducted over one year to identify key correlates of OSA severity.

Methods: retrospective analysis was performed on 239 patients (149 males, 90 females) who underwent sleep studies at a tertiary care hospital. Patient demographics, body mass index (BMI), mean oxygen saturation, and AHI were analyzed. Relationships between AHI and variables such as age, gender, BMI, and comorbidities were evaluated.

Results: Males exhibited a significantly higher mean AHI (49.85) compared to females (36.87) with p<0.001. The females have a higher BMI (33.32) than males (30.35). There was no correlation between AHI and age or the number of comorbidities. However, an increase in AHI was associated with a decrease in mean oxygen saturation. Mean saturation in males and females were comparable.

Conclusion: The findings emphasize gender disparities in OSA severity, with males experiencing more severe sleep-disordered breathing. The lack of correlation between AHI and age or comorbidities suggests that other factors may influence OSA severity. The significant association between AHI and reduced oxygen saturation underscores the need for timely diagnosis and management.

This study highlights gender differences in OSA and the detrimental impact of severe OSA on oxygenation.

Keywords: Apnea-Hypopnea Index, gender differences, obstructive sleep apnea, oxygen saturation, sleep study

Srija Meddela

Mediciti Institute of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Sarcoidosis is a multisystemic granulomatous disease with an indetermined etiology. Sarcoidosis is caused by the human immune system reacting to unknown substances (infectious pathogens, chemicals or dust, etc.) in genetically susceptible people. The diagnosis of sarcoidosis mainly depends on the pathological manifestations of granuloma, which consist of multinucleated giant cells at the core and peripheral immunocytes. The lungs and intrathoracic lymph nodes are commonly involved, although other organs can be affected, including the skin, eyes, bone and heart. The incidence rate of extrathoracic sarcoidosis is lower & frequency of bone involvement was between 3% and 13%.

Case: A 40 yr old male with no known comorbidities presented with pain in left upperlimb and lowerlimb since 1 year. MRI LS spine showed multiple hypointense lesions in lumbar & thoracic vertebrae and concomitant CT thorax confirmed multiple peribronchovascular nodules in both lungs. FDG-PET-CT scan of wholebody showed FDG uptake in soft tissue density nodules in bilateral perihilar regions, mediastinal lymphnodes and left supraclavicular lymphnode. Routine investigations were normal. Serum ACE was raised and ionized calcium levels increased. Histopathological examination of left supraclavicular lymphnode showed granulomatous lymphadenitis without necrosis. Patient started on steroids responding well to treatment.

Conclusion: Skeletal involvement of sarcoidosis is rare. PET-CT scan & histopathological examination aids in differentiating other causes reaching the diagnosis.

This case report shows the case history of a person who visited orthopaedician multiple time because of musculoskeletal pains, on evaluation accidentally found lesions in vertebrae, PET-CT was done to rule out metastasis but to the surprise biopsy of lymphnodes showed granulomatous disease. Rare presentation of pulmonary sarcoidosis with extrapulmonary involvement of musculoskeletal system is described in this report.

Kharak Singh Arora

Multispeciality Hospital, Alwar, Rajasthan, India

Background: While mucolytic agents are essential in managing chronic respiratory conditions, comparative data between erdosteine and N-acetylcysteine (NAC) remains limited. This study compared the effectiveness of these agents in improving pulmonary function outcomes.

Methods: This prospective, randomized, double-blind study enrolled 148 patients with chronic respiratory disease, receiving either erdosteine (300 mg twice daily) or NAC (600 mg once daily) for 12 weeks. Primary outcomes were changes in FEV1 and FVC, with secondary outcomes including 6MWD, SGRQ scores, and mMRC dyspnea scale.

Results: Of 132 study completers (erdosteine n=67, NAC n=65), the erdosteine group showed greater improvements in FEV1 (0.28 ± 0.11 L vs 0.19 ± 0.09 L, p=0.008) and FVC (0.32 ± 0.14 L vs 0.24 ± 0.12 L, p=0.015). Exercise capacity improved more with erdosteine (Δ6MWD: 42.6 ± 18.4 vs 31.2 ± 16.8 meters, p=0.024), as did quality of life (ΔSGRQ:-8.4 ± 3.2 vs-6.1 ± 2.9, p=0.018). Both treatments showed similar safety profiles and adherence rates.

Conclusion: Erdosteine demonstrated superior efficacy in improving pulmonary function, exercise capacity, and quality of life compared to NAC. These findings support erdosteine as a preferred option for patients requiring mucolytic therapy, especially when enhanced pulmonary function and exercise capacity are primary treatment goals.

Keywords: Erdosteine, exercise capacity, mucolytic therapy, N-acetylcysteine, pulmonary function, quality of life

M. Eswar Narayan, Raja Dhar, Amrita Bhattacharya, Arup Halder, Shyam Krishnan

C K Birla Group, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Background: Mycoplasma pneumoniae is a common cause of atypical pneumonia, often presents with varied respiratory and extrapulmonary manifestations, posing diagnostic challenges. This study aims to phenotype patients presenting to the hospital with respiratory symptoms to predict Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection based on clinical features, prior illnesses, and laboratory and radiological findings.

Methods: Eight cases (all inpatients) of Mycoplasma pneumoniae confirmed via PCR testing were analysed. Patients, aged 6–46 years, included adults (37.5%) and children (62.5%), all of whom were fully immunized and belonged to a single demographic area. Clinical history, physical findings, and radiological and laboratory data were reviewed.

Results: All patients presented with fever and cough (100%), while dyspnoea (37.5%), diarrhoea (25%), ear pain (12.5%), and sore throat (12.5%) were also observed. Desaturation was noted in 25% patients. Prior illnesses included atopy (50%), bronchial asthma (25%), hypertrophied adenoids (12.5%), tuberculosis (12.5%), and tonsillitis (12.5%). Radiological findings included bilateral lung involvement (37.5%), minimal bilateral pleural effusion (12.5%), and unilateral pleural effusion (25%), which was exudative in nature. Targeted management with macrolides (azithromycin) and Broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics resulted in clinical and radiological improvement in all cases.

Discussion and Conclusion: The clinical and radiological features of Mycoplasma pneumoniae often overlap with other forms of pneumonia. Awareness of this condition and empirical coverage with appropriate antibiotics in regions with high prevalence are crucial for effective management.

Keywords: Antibiotic management, atypical pneumonia, Mycoplasma pneumonia, PCR, phenotypic predictors, pleural effusion

Aravindan Karthikeyan

JLN Main Hospital, Bhilai, Chhattisgarh, India

Introduction: Jharco-Levin Syndrome is an exceptionally rare genetic disorder characterized by congenital skeletal anomalies, distinct craniofacial features, and developmental delays. Adult presentation with severe scoliosis leading to respiratory failure is extremely uncommon, posing diagnostic and management challenges.

Case: We report a case of a 37-year-old male who presented with progressive breathlessness and generalized weakness for one month. On examination patient had generalized oedema, scoliosis towards left side with ABG analysis suggestive of type 2 respiratory failure. Radiological imaging confirmed the presence of scoliosis of thoracic spine with spondylo-thoracic dysostosis suggestive of the diagnosis Jharco-Levin Syndrome. Patient was managed conservatively with non-invasive ventilation. Patient’s general condition improved clinically and hence discharged with the advice of domiciliary CPAP therapy.

Conclusion: This rare case of Jharco-Levin Syndrome presenting in adulthood with scoliosis and respiratory failure highlights the importance of clinical vigilance and integrated care to manage complex presentations effectively.

Keywords: Jharco-Levin syndrome, multidisciplinary management, rare genetic disorder, respiratory failure, scoliosis

Aditi Singh

Old NRH IGGMC, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Cardiac tamponade as the initial manifestation of malignancy, whether primary or metastatic, is uncommon. In developing countries, tuberculosis is the leading cause of pericardial effusion, often mimicking other causes, including malignancy. This case highlights a patient initially misdiagnosed with tubercular pericardial effusion, only to be later diagnosed with disseminated malignancy.

Case: A 57-year-old female with a history of cardiac tamponade secondary to clinically diagnosed tubercular pericardial effusion, on antitubercular therapy, presented with exertional dyspnea, chest pain, and abdominal pain for 15 days. Chest radiography showed left-sided gross pleural effusion, and pleural fluid analysis indicated an exudative pattern with low ADA and negative cytology. Pleural fluid GeneXpert was negative for MTB complex. Pericardial fluid ADA was mildly elevated (46.5 IU/L), but PCR for MTB was negative. CECT revealed bilateral pleural effusion, intraabdominal lymphadenopathy, omental nodularity, and minimal ascites. Histopathology from an omental biopsy showed metastatic poorly differentiated epithelial malignancy. PET CT showed hypermetabolic lesions in the left lung, pericardium, and peritoneum, with widespread metastatic nodal involvement.

Conclusion: Consider alternative diagnoses when ADA levels are borderline. A multimodal diagnostic approach is essential. Cardiac tamponade, though rare, can be the initial presentation of undiagnosed malignancy.

This case emphasizes the importance of considering malignancy in the differential diagnosis of pericardial effusion, especially when tuberculosis is suspected but diagnostic markers are not definitive. A comprehensive diagnostic approach, including imaging and biopsy, is crucial for accurate identification. Cardiac tamponade, though uncommon, can be the first manifestation of an undiagnosed malignancy, underscoring the need for vigilance in such cases.

Chakshu Chhabra

Mahatma Gandhi Medical College, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India

Pulmonary alveolar proteinosis (PAP) is a rare diffuse lung disease characterized by abnormal surfactant accumulation in alveolar spaces, leading to hypoxemic respiratory failure. Secondary PAP, commonly caused by chronic silica exposure, presents unique diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. We report a case of a 40-year-old stone grinder with progressive dyspnea and cough. High-resolution computed tomography revealed bilateral ground-glass opacities with a crazy-paving pattern, and bronchoalveolar lavage fluid analysis showed PAS-positive macrophages, confirming silica-induced PAP.

The patient underwent bilateral whole lung lavage (WLL) under general anesthesia with one-lung ventilation. The procedure significantly improved oxygenation, with follow-up imaging showing resolution of opacities. This case highlights WLL as an effective treatment for secondary PAP and underscores the importance of early diagnosis and multidisciplinary management to improve outcomes.

Keywords: Bronchoalveolar lavage, crazy-paving pattern, diffuse lung disease, pulmonary alveolar proteinosis, respiratory failure, silica exposure, whole lung lavage

Debajyoti Ghosh, Somenath Kundu

Institute of Post Graduate Medical Education and Research, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis is a common but often inadequately treated chronic lung disease, frequently associated with recurrent exacerbation causing functional impairment and poor quality of life. This study was done to describe etiology, severity, management of bronchiectasis in patients attending tertiary care hospital.

Methods: This is a cross-sectional observational study done in 85 adult patients with non-cystic fibrosis bronchiectasis diagnosed under Respiratory Medicine department of IPGME&R from July2023 to December 2024. Details of each patient like clinical features, exacerbation history, investigations, treatment received, FACED and BSI Score for assessing prognosis and SGRQ for assessing quality of life were collected and analyzed.

Result: Out of 85 patients 43 were male (50.6%) and 42 were female (49.4%). Mean age of patients was 43.84±14.3 (SD). 27 patients had mMRC grade II (31.8%) and 37 patients (43.5%) had mMRC-III dyspnea. 41 patients had hemoptysis (48.2%), 15 had frequent exacerbations (17.7%). Post-tuberculosis, Post-infective and Idiopathic were the three common etiologies with 29 (34.1%), 16 (18.8%), 15 (17.6%) patients respectively. Radiologically 37 (40.6%) had ≥3lobes involvement and 43 (50.6%) had cystic dilatation. Frequent exacerbation phenotype were significantly associated with mMRC dyspnea grading (chi-square 17.45, p0.001), ≥3 lobes involvement (13.79, p<0.001), colonization with P. aeruginosa (8.19, p0.004) and BSI Score (r 0.385, p<0.001). Poor QoL assessed by SGRQ were significantly associated with mMRC grade (r 0.543, p<0.001), history of hospitalization (0.222, p0.04), ≥3 lobes involved (0.256. p 0.018), cystic dilatation pattern (0.214, p0.05), FEV1% (0.449, p<0.001), FACED Score (0.449, p<0.001), BSI Score (0.416, p<0.001).

Conclusion: Our study shows that the etiology of bronchiectasis is multifactorial and the outcome can be predicted by multiple clinico-radiological characteristics.

Bronchiectasis is a heterogenous disorder which needs further studies to improve quality of care for the patients.

Keywords: Bronchiectsis, BSI, Exacerbation, FACED

Richa Mittal

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, KEM Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Background: Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) and asymptomatic airflow obstruction (AFO) are significant public health concerns, often underdiagnosed in individuals without symptoms. This study aimed to assess the prevalence of latent airflow obstruction using spirometry in asymptomatic patients at a tertiary care hospital in Mumbai.

Methods: This retrospective study analyzed spirometry records from 100 patients who underwent Pulmonary Function Test for Pre Anaesthetic Risk assessment and presented with no respiratory complaints. The study was conducted by taking the spirometery records of three years (2021–2024), from the pulmonary medicine department, the study evaluated pulmonary function and categorized abnormalities into obstructive, restrictive, and mixed patterns. Chi-square tests were applied to assess associations with factors like allergy status, smoking, age, and BMI.

Results: Abnormal spirometry patterns were identified in 46% of the asymptomatic patients, indicating a considerable prevalence of underlying pulmonary dysfunction. Obstructive patterns were the most frequent, accounting for 31% of cases, followed by restrictive patterns at 12%, and mixed patterns at 3%. Small airway obstruction with good reversibility, potentially indicative of asthma, was observed in 10 patients. Among the 24 patients diagnosed with Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD), the majority were in the mild (47.62%) or moderate (38.10%) stages, while severe and very severe stages were less common (14.28%). Restrictive patterns were predominantly mild (80%). Significant associations were noted between airway obstruction severity and allergy status (p=0.04) and smoking (p=0.04), while no significant correlations were observed with gender, age, or BMI.

Conclusions: A substantial prevalence of latent airway obstruction in asymptomatic individuals underscores the utility of spirometry as a screening tool. The findings highlight the need for targeted interventions, particularly in high-risk groups such as smokers and individuals with allergies. Smoking cessation programs and early diagnostic efforts can improve patient outcomes and potentially mitigate disease progression.

Keywords: Allergy status, asymptomatic airflow obstruction, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, pulmonary function tests, restrictive patterns, small airway obstruction, smoking cessation, spirometry

Linta Sajan

Aster Medcity, Kochi, Kerala, India

Introduction: Langerhans cell histiocytosis (LCH) is characterised by proliferation of Langerhans cells. Lung involvement is called as Pulmonary LCH. Clinical features are dry cough, dyspnea, chest pain and systemic features such as fatigue, weight loss, fever. Some patients are asymptomatic or come for evaluation after incidental finding on chest imaging and spontaneous pneumothorax. It occurs exclusively in smokers.

Case Description: A 54 years old male who is a known case of diabetes mellitus presented with history of fever since 6 months. Evening rise of temperature present with night sweats. No history of loss of weight, apetite, contact with tuberculosis. He gives history of smoking of 30 pack years. He has taken multiple courses of antibiotics. Due to persistent fever he came to our centre for further evaluation. Chest Radiograph PA view showed multiple nodules in bilateral lung. A Computed tomography of Thorax showed multiple nodules of varying sizes, some showing cavitation in bilateral lung with mediastinal lymphadenopathy and emphysematous changes. He was evaluated with Sputum CBNAAT and diagnostic flexible bronchoscopy. All test results were negative. Patient underwent whole body Positron emission tomography scan which revealed metabolically active nodules in lung. He underwent VATS lung biopsy. Histopathological examination showed features of eosinophilic granuloma. Immunohistochemistry showed CD1a, S100 + langerhan cells. He was started on oral corticosteroids. On follow up repeat CT Thorax showed decrease in number of nodules.

Conclusion: Presence of CD1a, S100 + on immunohistochemistry is gold standard for diagnosis.

Pulmonary LCH should be kept in mind as a diagnosis of exclusion in patient presenting with bilateral lung nodules and cavities.

K. V. Kavin

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Endometriosis is characterized by presence of endometrial-like glands and stroma outside uterine cavity. Endometriosis affects approximately 6%–10% of reproductive aged women. Endometriosis involving lung parenchyma, diaphragm and pleura produces range of clinical and radiological manifestations, collectively known as thoracic endometriosis syndrome (TES).

Case: 24year old married nulliparous women presented with complaints of cough, breathlessness for the past 1 year with history of recurrent right sided pleural effusion and ICD insertion with empirical ATT initiation elsewhere and no improvement. Her chest-X-ray showed right massive pleural effusion with contralateral mediastinal shift. Pleural fluid analysis showed exudative lymphocytic effusion. Medical thoracoscopy and pleural biopsy histopathology showed foci of cell aggregates positive for ER and CD10 with evidence of haemorrhage, suggestive of endometriosis. VATS decortication with pleurodesis was done in view of non-expansion of lung with histopathology suggestive of similar picture as above. Leuprolide for hormonal suppression was initiated.

36 year old married nulliparous women with history of recurrent right haemorrhagic pleural effusion and empirically ATT twice 8 years ago and bilateral hydropneumothorax 2 years ago, now presented with breathlessness, chest pain and cough for 4 days. CT thorax showed bilateral hydropneumothorax. Left ICD insertion and pleurodesis was done. CT abdomen showed cystic lesion in left ovary with features of pelvic and extra pelvic endometriosis. Hormonal therapy was initiated in concurrence with gynecology.

Conclusion: Women of reproductive age with complaints of chest pain, dyspnea, cough around the time of menstrual cycle should be evaluated for TES. A timely diagnosis may reduce the likelihood of disease progression.

Microembolization through pelvic veins and peritoneal-pleural migration of endometrial tissue through diaphragmatic defects are the most likely explanations for pathogenesis of thoracic endometriosis. Fertility preservation utilizing hormonal therapy is currently the standard of care and VATS surgery if medical management fails or is contraindicated. Postoperative hormonal suppression may further reduce recurrence of TES.

K. V. Kavin

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Pulmonary tumor thrombotic microangiopathy (PTTM) is a fatal disease process in which pulmonary hypertension (PH) develops in setting of malignancy. It is a disease process in which tumorcells embolize to the pulmonary vasculature with formation of fibrin clots and fibrocellular proliferation of intimal layer of blood vessels. Elevated pulmonary artery pressures are observed and death results from right heart failure. Patient outcomes remain poor with rapid decline in clinical condition often occurring within weeks. It is predominantly seen in gastric adenocarcinoma followed by breast and lung.

Case: 55/F, case of Invasive ductal carcinoma left breast with multiple bone metastasis underwent surgery followed by chemoradiation. PET CT done 2 years later showed disease progression with multiple bone metastasis. Presented with complaints of breathlessness. ABG showed hypoxemic Respiratory failure. CTPA showed features of PH with RV strain. ECHO showed moderate PH. RHC suggestive of Pre-capillary PH. Pulmonary capillary wedge blood samples for cytospin study were inconclusive. She was treated for OAD exacerbation as she had wheeze and therapeutic anticoagulation. She was initiated on Riociguat. Despite above treatment, her hypoxemia worsened. All other possible differentials were ruled out, she was suspected to have PTTM and was started on Imatinib. Despite the above treatment, patient succumbed to her illness.

Conclusion: In carcinoma patients presenting with respiratory symptoms and unexplained PH, clinical suspicion of PTTM should be considered. Early diagnosis of PTTM can prolong the survival of those patients.

PTTM is a rapidly progressive pulmonary disease with extremely poor prognosis. PTTM is a difficult diagnosis to establish and most cases are diagnosed post-mortem. High index of clinical suspicion, especially among patients with history of carcinoma is essential for antemortem diagnosis and treatment of PTTM. Early diagnosis may prolong the survival of PTTM patients.

Mamata Medical College, Khammam, Telegana, India

Introduction: Chylothorax is a rare diagnosis which is characterized by the accumulation of lymphatic fluid in the pleural cavity, presenting with a multitude of diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. Our patient’s condition is further complicated by the presence of parathyroid adenoma which profoundly affects calcium homeostasis and contributes to a range of systemic issues.

Case Description: A 33year old male presented with complaints of dyspnea, cough, chest pain, pain abdomen since 2 months. Aggravated since past 3 days. O/E :vitals were stable except RR 27/min. G/E :chest movements decreased on right side, trachea deviated to left, percussion dull note on right side. O/E : breath sounds were absent on right side.

Chest X ray and USG Chest: Right moderate pleural effusion. Pleural fluid is milky white, protein-6 g/dl, sugar-120 mg/dl, lymphocytic, with triglycerides-1320 mg/dl; cholesterol : 111 mg/dl. Diagnosed as chylothorax & patient was managed with chest tube insertion. CT thorax: expansile lytic lesion involving left 6th rib on the anterolateral portion, multiple lytic lesions involving sternum. CT abdomen revealed ill defined iso dense lesions in liver. PET-CT: no FDG uptake; but a left inferior parathyroid adenoma was noted. Serum PTH and calcium levels were elevated. 99m-TC Parathyroid scintigraphy confirmed left inferior parathyroid adenoma. MR Lymphangiography revealed dilated thoracic duct throughout its course with stenosis at venous drainage site (level of T2), possibly due to compression by the parathyroid adenoma. Patient underwent left inferior parathyroidectomy and histopathology confirmed it as parathyroid adenoma.

Conclusion: As chylothorax is an uncommon cause of pleural effusion with broad differentials and significant mortality and morbidity, early recognition of underlying rare causes and multidisciplinary approach is essential for management.

This case highlights the importance of considering parathyroid adenoma in the differential diagnosis of chylothorax, particularly in patients with elevated calcium and PTH levels.

Neha Rane

N. K. P Salve Institute of Medical Sciences and Research Centre and Lata Mangeshkar Hospital, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Non-resolving pneumonia refers to presumed community-acquired pneumonia (CAP) that slowly/incompletely resolve despite antibiotics. In these cases, in-depth workup including CT scan and bronchoscopy maybe warranted to aid in correct diagnosis and direct treatment accordingly.

Methods: It is a retrospective study done in department of respiratory medicine of tertiary care hospital. We reported 5 cases with right lower zone non resolving pneumonia. Patients were further evaluated with CT-thorax and flexible bronchoscopy to diagnose underlying cause.

Results: In case of non resolving pneumonia, diagnosing underlying etiology is the most important before treating the patient. Of the 5 patients diagnosed with non resolving pneumonia, 2 had vegetative foreign body impaction, 1 had endobronchial growth in right lower lobe bronchus, 1 had repeated right lower zone pneumonia and was diagnosed with tracheo-esophageal fistula in a treated case of CA larynx and 1 had newly diagnosed uncontrollable DM with worsening of pneumonia even after sensitive antibiotics treatment.

Conclusion: Timely diagnosis and treatment of non resolving pneumonia is crucial. In depth history taking and advanced diagnostic modalities helps in diagnosing and treating these cases.

Prachi Jakhar, Mahesh K. Mishra

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Mahatma Gandhi Medical College, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India

Background: Malignant pleural mesothelioma (MPM) is a rare and aggressive neoplasm primarily linked to asbestos exposure, with a long latency period between exposure and disease manifestation. However, cases of MPM without any known asbestos exposure have also been reported.

Case: We describe a 70-year-old male, a teacher by profession and a lifetime smoker, who presented with right-sided chest pain, shortness of breath, generalized weakness, and weight loss over two months. The patient had a history of pulmonary tuberculosis and a family history of carcinoma of the buccal mucosa. Clinical examination revealed decreased chest movement and diminished breath sounds on the right side. Imaging studies, including chest X-ray, CT, and PET-CT, indicated pleural effusion and pleural thickening.

The patient underwent medical thoracoscopy, which revealed multiple gray-white nodules on the pleura. Biopsies confirmed the diagnosis of MPM. Despite the absence of asbestos exposure, other potential risk factors such as genetic predisposition or exposure to other carcinogens were considered.

Conclusion: This case underscores the importance of considering MPM in patients presenting with pleural effusion and pleural thickening, even in the absence of known asbestos exposure. Early diagnosis through thoracoscopy and biopsy can significantly improve management and potentially enhance survival rates in MPM patients.

Keywords: Asbestos exposure, early diagnosis, malignant pleural mesothelioma, pleural effusion, thoracoscopy

S. Kishore Kanna

Rajarajeswari Medical College and Hospital, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is a chronic progressive condition marked by inflammation and airflow limitation. Platelets and red cells plays an important role in pathogenesis of COPD and pulmonary hypertension by mediating thrombotic, inflammatory, and immune processes in the lungs. Blood indices like platelet-to-lymphocyte ratio (PLR), platelet distribution width (PDW), Plateletocrit (PCT), Red cell Distribution Width (RDW), Mean platelet volume (MPV) and platelet count (PLT) have emerged as potential biomarkers for predicting severity of pulmonary hypertension (PH). This study compares these indices and clinical profiles between COPD and Non-COPD patients.

Methods: This cross-sectional study included 100 participants, categorized into COPD (n=75) and Non-COPD (n=25) groups. Laboratory parameters like platelet and red cell indices, and pulmonary function parameters like FEV1, FVC, DLCO were analysed. PH severity (MILD, MODERATE, SEVERE) was assessed by 2D-echo and statistical significance was determined using t-tests, chi-square tests, and one-way ANOVA.

Results: COPD patients had lower lymphocyte counts (1352.6 ± 483.9 vs. 2253.4 ± 867.6), higher PLR (253.8 ± 86.6 vs. 126.5 ± 79.4), elevated RDW (15.4 ± 0.9 vs. 11.8 ± 1.4), lower PDW (10.2 ± 1.6 vs. 11.2 ± 1.6), and higher PLT (318720.0 ± 99905.2 vs. 261500.0 ± 112661.7) when compared to Non-COPD patients. PLR (p = 0.040), RDW (p = 0.021), DLCO (p = 0.025) increased with severity of PH, while PDW, MPV, PCT, and PLT did not predict PH.

Discussion: PLR, RDW, PDW, PLT were able to differentiate between COPD and Non-COPD patients and PLR, RDW predicted the severity of PH. Hemogram is a useful investigation in a low-resource setting in COPD patients.

Conclusion: Blood markers particularly RDW, PLR are effective in predicting the severity of PH in COPD patients and can be used in early suspicion and diagnosis of pulmonary hypertension in COPD patients.

Keywords: COPD, pulmonary hypertension

Clinicians should maintain a high index of suspicion for malignancy in cases of organizing pneumonia with atypical presentation or recurrent pleural effusion. Early diagnosis and targeted therapy can significantly improve patient outcomes.

Mohan Srinivas Kunapareddy

Shri Vasantrao Naik Govt. Medical College, Yavatmal, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Diagnosis of COPD is confirmed by Spirometry, which is defined as a Post – Bronchodilator Ratio of the forced expiratory volume in the First Second and the Forced Vital Capacity (FEV1/FVC) below 0.7. However, now widely accepted that FEV1 measurement alone does not effectively represent the functional impairment and subsequent symptomatology experienced by individual patients. A hlistic patient assessment including multiple parameters is required for efficient COPD Phenotyping. CAT is good measurement of Health Status and symptoms burden in COPD Patients.

Methods: It is a cross sectional observational study done in a tertiary care hospital From January 2023 to November 2024, in population of COPD and healthy smoker attending present tertiary care center.

Results: Significantly, higher proportion of cases with the Higher (or) Very higher level of disease impact based on CAT score had higher prevalence of moderate/severe Airflow obstruction in COPD based on spirometry and vice versa. Increase in frequency of exacerbations showed significant rise in CAT Score and vice versa.

Conclusion: This study demonstrated a strong association between CAT Score and the severity of Airflow obstruction in stable COPD Patients. Patients with higher CAT Scores were more likely to have severe airflow limitation, suggesting that CAT is an effective tool for assessing the Impact of COPD on patient health.

Keywords: CAT score, COPD, spirometry

Kadiri Raviteja

Nizam’s Institute of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Pleural effusions can result from increased fluid production or decreased reabsorption, categorized as transudates or exudates based on Light’s criteria. Common causes include tuberculosis, malignancy, and parapneumonic. The RAPID score, which factors in age, urea, purulence, infection source, and albumin, predicts mortality risk and helps guide management decisions.

Methods: This hospital-based prospective observational study, conducted over 18 months in the Pulmonary Medicine Department at NIMS, Telangana, lnvolved 83 participants. After obtaining informed consent, clinical data will be gathered, and relevant investigations are performed. Routine blood tests included urea, creatinine, liver function tests (LFT), and a hemogram. Pleural fluid undergone biochemical analysis (protein, glucose, ADA, LDH), microbiological analysis (AFB smear, GeneXpert, gram staining, fungal elements, and culture sensitivity), and pathological examination (cell count and cytopathology).

Results: A total of 83 participants, with a mean age of 46.1 years, were enrolled in the study. The majority were male (71.1%), and the most common pulmonary diagnosis was pleural effusion (60.2%). The remaining participants had empyema (24.1%) or hydro pneumothorax (15.7%). Most effusions were right-sided (54.2%), and dyspnea was the most common presenting symptom. Diabetes was the most prevalent comorbidity (26.5%). Diagnostic aspiration was performed in 82 participants, with 60% undergoing both diagnostic and therapeutic aspiration. Surgical interventions included pig-tail insertion, ICD, decortication, pneumonectomy, and thoracoscopy. The RAPID score identified 51.8% of participants as low risk, while the CURB-65 score categorized 74.7% as low risk. Mortality rates were higher in participants with moderate and severe RAPID scores. Significant variables included age and albumin, with comorbidities correlating with increased mortality.

The RAPID score, a validated prognostic tool, was used to predict 3-month mortality. In this study, 51.8% of patients were low risk, 32.5% moderate risk, and 15.7% high risk. Mortality rates were low across risk categories, but the small sample size and lack of statistical significance in the findings limit conclusions. Radiologically, most participants exhibited unilateral or bilateral pleural effusions, with consolidation and ground-glass opacities being common findings. Microbiological cultures were negative in 91.6% of cases, likely due to prior antibiotic treatment. Positive cultures included E. coli, Enterococcus faecalis, and Candida, highlighting challenges in pleural fluid isolation and diagnosis.

Conclusion: The RAPID score was not a reliable tool for stratifying non-malignant exudative pleural effusions. However, the CURB-65 score was effective in predicting mortality. The RAPID score did not show statistical significance for mortality prediction, whereas CURB-65 provided a useful measure for mortality risk in these patients.

Keywords: Age, albumin, CURB-65 score, diagnostic and therapeutic aspiration, diagnostic aspiration, exudative, hydro pneumothorax, microbiological analysis, RAPID score, thoracoscopy pleural effusion

Introduction: Eosinophilic COPD patients exhibit greater left atrial dilation, mild left ventricular hypertrophy, and elevated mean pulmonary artery pressure (mPAP) and pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR), predisposing them to pulmonary hypertension (PH) more than non-eosinophilic COPD patients. Shibani Chandiramani

MGM Kamothe, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The chest wall plays a crucial role in respiration, and traumatic injuries to the ribs or sternum can lead to significant structural and functional impairment. Sometimes these injuries are so severe or these injuries are beyond known injuries like flail chest. This can lead to physiological impairment of respiration-i. e full inspiration or full expiration can not occur and give way in between leading to respiratory failure and putting the patient on prolonged ventilation. Hence immediate recognition and Early identification is vital to reduce the risk of complications such as hypoxia, pneumonia, and prolonged respiratory insufficiency.

Aim: The aim of this review is to highlight the importance of early identification of failing chest wall following trauma. Specifically, it seeks to address the clinical signs, diagnostic strategies, and management approaches that can be employed to detect this condition in its early stages, thereby improving patient outcomes and minimizing the risk of life-threatening complications, hospital stay and financial burden.

Conclusion: Traumatic cheat wall fails to fulfill the biomechanical task required for harmonius respiratory function. Hence timely surgical restoration of bony chest wall using titanium implant result early (immediate) establishment of normal respiratory homeostasis. Early recognition of failing chest wall post-trauma is essential for effective management and prevention of respiratory complications leading to prolonged ventilatory support, if unrecognized these patient are difficult to extubate and develop Ventilator association complications lwading to death. Timely use of imaging techniques, such as chest X-rays and CT scans, alongside clinical examination, is crucial in diagnosing this condition. Interventions, including pain control, respiratory support, and, when necessary, surgical stabilization of the chest wall using titanium inplant are key to improving respiratory function and reducing morbidity. A proactive and multidisciplinary approach to management significantly enhances recovery and reduces mortality in trauma patients with chest wall failure.

Shibani Chandiramani

MGM Kamothe, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Chest trauma is a significant cause of morbidity and mortality, particularly in cases of flail chest, a severe condition characterized by multiple rib fractures leading to paradoxical chest wall movement. Early identification and timely intervention are crucial for improving patient outcomes.

Objective: This abstract aims to discuss the importance of early identification of chest trauma, with a focus on flail chest, and to highlight the diagnostic tools and clinical assessment techniques that can aid in prompt diagnosis and management.

Results: Early clinical signs and symptoms, such as respiratory distress, chest pain, and paradoxical chest wall movement, are essential clues for suspecting flail chest. Physical examination findings, including decreased breath sounds, subcutaneous emphysema, and hypotension, further support the diagnosis. Chest radiography and computed tomography (CT) scans are valuable imaging modalities for confirming the diagnosis and assessing the extent of lung injury.

Conclusion: Early identification of chest trauma, particularly flail chest, is vital for initiating appropriate management strategies and improving patient outcomes. A high index of suspicion, combined with careful clinical assessment and timely diagnostic imaging, can facilitate prompt diagnosis and intervention.

Rida Khan, Amita Nene

Bombay Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB) remains a significant health challenge in India, with extrapulmonary tuberculosis (EPTB) comprising 15–25% of cases. The paucibacillary nature of EPTB complicates diagnosis, often necessitating molecular tests and culture methods to identify drug resistance patterns.

Methods: This hospital-based prospective study evaluated the diagnostic accuracy of Gene Xpert (CBNAAT) and liquid culture (MGIT) in diagnosing EPTB and detecting rifampicin resistance. Over one year, 88 confirmed EPTB patients were studied using samples from pleural fluid, lymph nodes, ascitic fluid, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), and spinal tissue.

Results: CBNAAT detected Mycobacterium tuberculosis in 47.72% of cases, identifying rifampicin resistance in 11 patients. Liquid culture yielded a higher positivity rate (65.9%). While liquid culture demonstrated superior sensitivity, CBNAAT provided faster results with higher specificity.

Conclusion: CBNAAT is invaluable for rapid diagnosis and rifampicin resistance detection, offering results within two hours—crucial for timely treatment. Liquid culture, with better sensitivity, complements CBNAAT. The combined use of both methods ensures accurate and efficient diagnosis of EPTB.

Rida Khan, Amita Nene

Bombay Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pneumocystis pneumonia (PCP) is an opportunistic infection commonly seen in immunocompromised individuals, particularly those with HIV. While high-resolution CT (HRCT) scans typically show diffuse ground-glass opacities (GGO), subtle signs like the ‘dark bronchus’ sign can offer crucial diagnostic clues when other findings are ambiguous.

Case: A 35-year-old male with newly diagnosed HIV presented with worsening dyspnea and a persistent dry cough. Initial chest X-ray was unremarkable. HRCT imaging revealed diffuse GGO, with an unusual finding: air-filled bronchi appeared darker against the hyperdense GGO background, a feature known as the ‘dark bronchus’ sign. PCR testing of induced sputum confirmed the diagnosis of PCP. The patient was promptly treated with Co-trimoxazole and adjunctive corticosteroids, leading to significant clinical improvement. Follow-up HRCT showed resolution of GGO, aligning with the patient’s recovery.

Conclusions: The ‘dark bronchus’ sign, a subtle yet important HRCT finding, can assist in the early diagnosis of PCP, especially when conventional imaging features are nonspecific. Recognizing this sign can facilitate timely intervention, improving outcomes in immunocompromised patients.

The ‘dark bronchus’ sign is a valuable imaging marker for diagnosing PCP in HIV-positive patients. Its early recognition can guide clinicians in making an accurate diagnosis, leading to appropriate treatment and improved clinical outcomes.

Introduction: The global threat of MDR TB underscores the the prompt and rapid identification of resistant MTB strains. Benefits of rapid testing of Rifampicin resistance by molecular technique (Gene Xpert MTB/RIF) at the time of diagnosis include increased cure rates, decreased mortality, reduced development of drug resistance and a reduced likelihood of failure and relapse.

Methodology: Samples of those patients whose Gene Xpert showed ‘MTB detected’ were sent for AFB smear and AFB MGIT culture. During the study period a total of 140 patients were enrolled. All results were tabulated and Statistical analysis was done.

Results: Sensitivity and specificity for the detection of Rifampicin resistance in “very low” bacterial load was 100% & 87.5% respectively. In all cases with Gene Xpert MTB detected medium & high, there was complete agreement with DST. The overall sensitivity, specificity, PPV, NPV and Diagnostic accuracy of Gene Xpert were 100%, 96.88%, 91.9%, 100% and 97.69% respectively.

Conclusion: GeneXpertMTB/RIF has significantly contributed to the early diagnosis and management of DR-TB because of its rapidity and high sensitivity. But it shows low specificity in detection of Rif resistance in ‘very low’ bacterial load.(should be confirmed with a “gold standard” culture-based DST to avoid in inappropriate treatment with toxic second-line anti-tuberculosis drugs).

Introduction: Igg4 disease is a multi system inflammatory disorder with non specific symptoms, making diagnosis challenging. This case report highlights the diagnostic challenges and pitfalls encountered in a patient with IGG4 disease emphasizing the need for increased awareness and a multidisciplinary approach to accurately diagnose and manage this enigmatic disease

Histopathology-nonspecific inflammation. But looking into the description there were interstitial infiltrates of lymphocytes, plasma cells neutrophils, with fibrosis. IHC confirmed IGG4 disease and pt started on prednisolone 40mg. Pt improved, steroids slowly tapered and stopped.

Divya Wankhede

IGGMC and MAYO Hospital, Nagpur, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) is an immune-mediated disorder caused by hypersensitivity to Aspergillus fumigatus, commonly seen in individuals with asthma or cystic fibrosis. It manifests as wheezing, mucus impaction, and pulmonary infiltrates. However, approximately 7% of ABPA cases occur in individuals without asthma, presenting diagnostic and therapeutic challenges. Diagnosis relies on clinical features, serological markers, and imaging findings. This report presents a case of ABPA in a non-asthmatic individual, emphasizing diagnostic complexities and treatment strategies.

Case Description: A 47-year-old male sanitary worker involved in chlorinating water wells presented with breathlessness, low-grade fever, and chronic cough with whitish expectoration for one month with a history of systemic hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and chronic alcoholism. Diagnostic investigations revealed significantly elevated total IgE (4280 IU/mL), Aspergillus-specific IgG (191 mgA/L), and IgE (0.40 kUA/L). Peripheral eosinophilia was mild at 520 cells/µL. Imaging studies demonstrated bilateral bronchiectasis, ground-glass opacities. These findings satisfied the revised ISHAM (International Society for the Human and Animal Mycology) criteria for ABPA.

Conclusion: This case underscores the importance of recognizing ABPA in non-asthmatic patients with persistent respiratory symptoms and occupational Aspergillus exposure. Early diagnosis and a multidisciplinary approach are essential to prevent complications. Greater awareness and research on atypical ABPA presentations are imperative to refine diagnostic and therapeutic strategies.

Arvind Kumar Swarnkar

IIMSAR DR. B. C. Roy Hospital, Haldia, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Magnesium is an essential cation involved in cellular metabolism, cardiac function, and muscle contractility, with normal serum levels ranging between 1.5-2.3 mg/dl. This study examines the relationship between serum magnesium levels and asthma severity and control in adult patients.

Methods: A hospital-based cross-sectional study was conducted on asthmatic patients aged over 18 at the IIMSAR Haldia respiratory medicine department. Asthma control was assessed using a standardized questionnaire, and serum magnesium level were measured. Patients were categorized based on asthma symptom control (well-controlled, partly controlled, and uncontrolled) and severity (mild, moderate, and severe).

Results: Among 60 patients, 32 were in the wellcontrolled group, 23 partly controlled, and 5 uncontrolled. The mean serum magnesium levels were 2.1 1 + 0.15 mg/dl, 1.97 + 0.17 mg/dl, and 1.60 + 0.05 mg/dl for well-controlled, partly controlled, and uncontrolled groups, respectively. A decline in symptom control correlated with a significant drop in serum magnesium levels. Similarly, patients categorized by severity showed a progressive decline in serum magnesium level 2.09 + 0.15 mg/dl (mild), 1.81 + 0.12 mg/dl (moderate), and 1.59 + 0.06 mg/dl (severe). These findings confirm that magnesium levels decrease as asthma severity increases.

Conclusions: This study highlights the prevalence of hypomagnesemia in asthma patients and its association with disease control and severity. Findings align with previous studies by Yuvarajan, Saidoka, and Daliparty, which also reported a positive correlation between serum magnesium levels and asthma symptom control and severity.

Serum magnesium levels have a significant positive correlation with asthma control and severity. Low magnesium levels are particularly evident in uncontrolled and severe asthma, suggesting its potential role as a biomarker for monitoring and managing asthma.

Keywords: Asthma, magnesium, hypomagnesemia

Isha Sharma

TB and Chest Hospital, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Multidrug-resistant tuberculosis (MDR-TB) occurs due to Mycobacterium tuberculosis resistant to isoniazid (H) and rifampicin (R), while rifampicin-resistant TB (RR-TB) involves resistance to rifampicin alone. Both present significant challenges to TB eradication. In 2023, 3.2% of new cases and 16% of previously treated TB cases were MDR/RR-TB, with only 44% of the estimated 400,000 cases treated. Treatment success for MDR/RR-TB remains low at 68%, though it has improved from 50% in 2012. Poor outcomes are linked to lengthy treatments, adverse drug reactions, and risk factors such as extensive lesions, HIV, diabetes, and delayed sputum conversion.

Methods: A prospective observational study was conducted at a tertiary care hospital in Southern Rajasthan. MDR-TB patients diagnosed, referred, or under follow-up were included. Data on demographics, clinical history, comorbidities, and radiological findings were collected. Sputum samples were tested for drug susceptibility. Patients were categorized into good outcomes (cure or treatment completed) and adverse outcomes. Treatment adherence was ensured through visits and telephonic follow-ups. Statistical analysis was conducted using SPSS software, with Pearson’s Chi-square and Fisher’s exact tests applied.

Results: Adverse outcomes were more common in males (30.48%) than females (15.24%). Other risk factors included age >45 years (23.80%), rural residence (21.90%), BMI <18.5 (30.47%), prior treatment for DSTB, high mycobacterial load, prolonged illness (>12 months), far-advanced or bilateral disease, and delayed treatment initiation.

Conclusions: The study highlights significant predictors of poor outcomes in MDR-TB treatment. Addressing these factors can optimize treatment regimens and improve adherence.

Targeted management of predictors and improved adherence strategies are essential to enhance MDR-TB treatment success.

Keywords: Adverse outcomes, MDR-TB, PMDT guidelines, predictors, Rajasthan, RR-TB, treatment outcomes, tuberculosis

TB and Chest Hospital, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Aspergillus species are opportunistic fungi usually associated with infections in immunocompromised individuals. However, Aspergillus granuloma in immunocompetent hosts is rare, posing significant diagnostic challenges due to its non-specific clinical, radiological, and histopathological features. Early diagnosis is crucial to prevent treatment delays.

Case: A 66-year-old male, previously healthy, presented with a 2-month history of cough with minimal whitish expectoration, low-grade fever, exertional dyspnoea (Grade 1), and generalized weakness. He had a 10 pack-year smoking history, no prior tuberculosis (TB), and a family history of TB. Chest X-ray showed left-sided pleural effusion, leading to a suspicion of TB. However, pleural fluid analysis (ADA 28.9 U/L, protein 4.9 g/dL, sugar 95 mg/dL, lymphocyte predominance, CBNAAT negative) was inconclusive. Given his smoking history, contrast-enhanced CT revealed multiple peripheral lung nodules with central calcifications and adjacent consolidation, suggesting chronic granulomatous lesions of fungal origin. Bronchoscopy showed blackish patches in the left bronchi, and bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) revealed septate hyphae. Fungal culture showed growth of Aspergillus Fumigatus. The patient had significant exposure to damp, fungal-infected walls at home. Aspergillus granuloma was diagnosed, and itraconazole 200 mg BD was initiated for four months. Follow-up showed marked clinical and radiological improvement, including resolution of pleural effusion.

This case highlights the diagnostic complexity of pulmonary Aspergillus granuloma in immunocompetent hosts, often mimicking tuberculosis or malignancy. A high index of suspicion, along with histopathological and microbiological confirmation, is essential to avoid misdiagnosis and complications.

Pulmonary Aspergillus granuloma should be considered in the differential diagnosis of lung lesions in immunocompetent individuals. Prompt diagnosis and antifungal therapy ensure favorable outcomes.

Key Takeaway: Broad differential diagnosis and appropriate diagnostic tools are crucial in accurately diagnosing pulmonary aspergillosis in immunocompetent patients

Ishani Bansal

TB Hospital, Patiala, Punjab, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis (TB), caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis, predominantly manifests as pulmonary TB. Extrapulmonary TB often spreads hematogenously, affecting multiple systems. The skeletal system is the third most commonly involved site after pulmonary TB and lymphadenopathy, accounting for 10–35% of extrapulmonary cases. The spine is most frequently affected, followed by large joints like the hip and knee. Joint TB has an insidious course and is rarely diagnosed before arthritis develops.

Case Description: A 50-year-old male presented with a two-month history of intermittent low-grade fever (100°F) relieved by antipyretics, followed by swelling and persistent pain in the left wrist for 7–8 months. He self-medicated with analgesics. There was no history of trauma, weight loss, appetite loss, or tuberculosis in the patient or family. Examination revealed left wrist swelling, erythema, tenderness, and a discharging sinus with watery secretions. General physical examination was unremarkable.

Investigations showed a normal CBC with PBF, negative Mantoux test and normal chest x-ray. Wrist X-ray was inconclusive, while MRI revealed diffuse erosion, subluxation, synovial effusion, and thickening, suggestive of inflammatory/infective etiology. Biopsy results confirmed TB (ZN staining: AFB+, CBNAAT: MTB detected, Rifampicin-sensitive, necrotizing granulomatous synovitis). Anti-tubercular therapy (ATT) was initiated, resulting in significant improvement.

Joint TB begins as synovitis, progressing to bony erosion and joint destruction. Its nonspecific presentation mimics other pathologies, such as septic arthritis or bone tumors, often delaying diagnosis. MRI facilitates early detection, while microbiological and histopathological studies confirm TB.

Conclusion: Tuberculosis of the wrist joint is rare and frequently misdiagnosed. Early diagnosis and timely initiation of ATT are essential to prevent joint damage and reduce morbidity.

Topiwala National Medical College, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Cyst with a bronchiectasis may be present with a variety of non pulmonary manifestations. We present a case with systemic manifestations.

History: A 59-year-old male driver presented with chronic respiratory symptoms, including dyspnea on exertion (grade 2-3) and copious mucoid expectoration for the past 3-4 years. The patient reported recurrent exacerbations with multiple hospitalizations, suggesting a progressive course. His medical history included Diabetes Mellitus, and he was non-smoker. Strikingly, the patient also exhibited disproportionate short stature, noted since childhood, with similar findings in his elder sister and youngest daughter, hinting at a potential familial pattern.

Clinical examination

Disproportionate short stature (Fig 3)

Vitally stable grade 2 clubbing + and Respiratory system-B/L crepitations+

Investigations

Radiological Findings:

CT Thorax and CXR revealed classical features of cystic bronchiectasis (Fig 1, Fig2) explaining the chronic pulmonary symptoms.

Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis (ABPA) Workup:

Elevated serum total IgE: 536 IU/mL

Aspergillus-specific IgE: Negative, ruling out ABPA despite high IgE levels.

USG Abdomen: Revealed a simple renal cyst in the left kidney, adding a renal component to the systemic involvement.

Endocrine Evaluation: Showed decreased Insulin-like Growth Factor 1 (IGF-1) with a normal sex hormone panel. The patient’s upper segment to lower segment ratio was 0.8), confirming disproportionate short stature.

The patient was managed with optimal supportive care for cystic bronchiectasis, including bronchodilators, mucolytics, and aggressive chest physiotherapy.

Referral to an endocrinologist was made for further evaluation and management of the growth hormone-related deficiency.

The renal cyst was monitored as an incidental finding without immediate intervention.

Differential Diagnosis of Cystic Lung Diseases:

Cystic Bronchiectasis (confirmed by radiology and clinical history)

Cystic Fibrosis (unlikely due to age and absence of gastrointestinal involvement)

Pulmonary Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis (less likely given no smoking history)

Lymphangioleiomyomatosis (LAM)

Short Stature:

Growth hormone deficiency (suggested by low IGF-1)

Familial short stature (supported by family history)

Skeletal dysplasia (unlikely, normal skeletal survey)

Final Outcome and Summary: This case presents a rare presentation of cystic bronchiectasis with a background of familial short stature and a renal cyst. The patient’s pulmonary symptoms were controlled with standard treatment, while his short stature raised the possibility of an underlying growth hormone deficiency, further supported by decreased IGF-1 levels. A multidisciplinary approach involving pulmonologists, endocrinologists, and radiologists was essential to provide holistic care.

Key Learning Points:

1. Systemic evaluation in pulmonary disease: Cystic bronchiectasis in the presence of familial short stature and renal cyst highlights the importance of looking beyond the lungs in cases with unusual systemic features

2. Multidisciplinary approach: Collaborative care across specialties is crucial for managing complex cases that span multiple systems

3. Chronic pulmonary management: Early recognition and intervention are vital in preventing progressive lung damage and improving patient outcomes.

Sanket Gadam, Avya Bansal, Amita Nene

Bombay Hospital and Medical Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS) is a rare but potentially life-threatening hypersensitivity reaction to certain medications. This condition involves widespread skin rashes, fever, and systemic organ involvement, often triggered by anticonvulsants or anti-tubercular drugs.

Methods: We present a case of a 26-year-old female with pulmonary tuberculosis on first-line anti-TB therapy (HRZE) who developed generalized tonic-clonic seizures and subsequent complications, including high-grade fever, eosinophilia, and a maculopapular rash. Diagnostic evaluations included blood tests, cultures, imaging, CSF analysis, and a skin biopsy.

Results: The patient was diagnosed with disseminated TB and treated accordingly. However, despite discontinuing anti-TB therapy due to suspected drug-induced hepatitis, her symptoms persisted. A dermatology consultation and skin biopsy confirmed the diagnosis of DRESS, with inj. levetiracetam identified as the causative agent. Stopping levetiracetam led to rapid clinical improvement, including resolution of rashes, normalization of eosinophil counts, and subsidence of fever spikes.

Conclusion: This case highlights the importance of considering DRESS in patients presenting with systemic symptoms, rash, and eosinophilia, particularly when associated with anticonvulsant therapy. Early recognition and prompt discontinuation of the offending drug are critical for favorable outcomes.

Anant Verma

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Bone and joint tuberculosis accounts for 10%–11% of all extrapulmonary tuberculosis (EPTB) cases, representing about 1%-3% of all tuberculosis cases. The sacroiliac joint is affected in 2%-5% of cases with osteoarticular tuberculosis. The delay in diagnosis, averaging 17 months from the onset of symptoms to a confirmed diagnosis of skeletal tuberculosis, is primarily due to a lack of suspicion and limited clinical experience.

Case Study: A 20 years old boy, was referred from a private orthopaedician for evaluation and opinion on a right hip swelling which he was suspecting to be tubercular. Detailed history revealed patient had right hi pain since 2 years. With time he developed swelling on lateral aspect of right hip and constitutional symptoms of tuberculosis which ultimately lead to diagnosis.

Discussion: The diagnosis of sacroiliac joint infection is often delayed. Pain over the affected joint is the main symptom. In the early stages, the plain radiographs are normal. The CT and MRI are of great value in detecting early joint erosions, cavitation and abscess. Definitive diagnosis is obtained by fine needle aspiration or open biopsy. AFB staining, CBNAAT, culture in solid and liquid media and chronic granulomatous lesions on histopathology confirms the diagnosis.

Conclusion: The delay in diagnosis can be prevented by having a high degree of suspicion of tuberculosis in developing country like ours for non-resolving joint. Definitive investigations and timely diagnosis can save patient from major surgeries and permanent disability.

Cipla Ltd

Introduction: Appropriate combination of drug and device is essential for determining the choice of inhaler for each patient. Vilanterol/Fluticasone-Furoate, a once-daily ICS/LABA, is currently available as unit-dose-DPI. Recently, the combination has been developed to be delivered via the Ciphaler® (multidose-discrete-DPI). Comparison of performance of the two devices is therefore warranted.

Aim: To evaluate the in-vitro performance of Voltido (Vilanterol/Fluticasone-Furoate)-Ciphaler® (Cipla-Ltd.) with Voltido-Rotacaps using Revolizer® (Cipla-Ltd).

Methods: The performance of the two devices was compared using random samples for fine particle dose (FPD), mass median aerodynamic diameter (MMAD) and geometric standard deviation (GSD). The values were measured using Next Generation Impactor (Copley-Scientific) at 76 L/min for Ciphaler® and 42 L/min for Revolizer® (flow rate at which 4 kPA pressure drop is seen). The mean of three readings taken for each parameter was calculated. The batch-to-batch consistency was also ascertained for three batches.

Results: FPD, MMAD and GSD for fluticasone furoate and vilanterol delivered with Voltido-Ciphaler and Voltido-Rotacaps® with Revolizer® were comparable.

The dose delivered with Voltido Ciphaler® was found to be consistent in all 3 batches at 76 LPM [Table 1].

Fine particle dose, mass median aerodynamic diameter and geometric standard deviation of fluticasone furoate and vilanterol for both the devices

FPD: Fine particle dose, MMAD: Mass median aerodynamic diameter, GSD: Geometric standard deviation

Discussion: As per the findings, drug delivered by both the devices were comparable, which implies that switching the patients from unit dose to multidose DPI would not affect the delivered dose. This in vitro study helps in establishing the similarity between drug delivery with Revolizer ® and Ciphaler ® and the dose consistency of newly developed Voltido Ciphaler®.

Conclusion: The FPD, MMAD and GSD of vilanterol and fluticasone delivered by both the devices were comparable at specified flow rates, suggesting similar drug delivery into the lungs under ideal conditions using correct inhaler technique.

Salil Bendre, Kumar Doshi, Kavita Mehta, Anjali Kamath

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Nanavati Max Superspeciality Hospital, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Diffuse Panbronchiolitis (DPB), predominantly documented in Japan, is a rare idiopathic inflammatory condition characterized by increasing suppurative and obstructive airway illness, which, if neglected, results in bronchiectasis, respiratory failure, and mortality.[1] This disease entity has very rarely been reported from India.[2]

Case Report: A 32-year-old male with a history of childhood asthma and sinusitis presented with complaints of cough and expectoration for one week, low-grade fever, and progressively worsening breathlessness for 3 days. Examination revealed a saturation of 90% on room air, tachypnoea, and bilateral rhonchi, consistent with airway obstruction. Outpatient treatment with oseltamivir, antihistamines, and nebulised bronchodilators showed no symptomatic relief. He was given intravenous and inhaled corticosteroids, cephalosporin and macrolide antibiotics and inhaled bronchodilators. His flu panel was negative and sputum culture showed no growth. Counts were 5350 with normal differential counts. High-resolution Computed Tomography (HRCT) of the chest showed diffuse bronchial wall thickening, multiple linear branching centrilobular nodules, and patchy ground-glass opacities (GGOs), raising suspicion of diffuse pan-bronchiolitis. IV steroids and macrolides were continued, and within a week, the patient showed significant clinical response. A repeat imaging revealed significant resolution of the centrilobular nodules and ground glassing. Bronchoscopic biopsy was contemplated but deferred due to significant clinical improvement. The patient was discharged on tapering doses of oral steroids. Regular follow-up was advised to monitor symptoms and prevent recurrence.

Conclusion: This case study underscores the importance of considering DPB in the differential diagnosis for worsening airway obstruction, particularly among individuals with a history of asthma and sinusitis. Timely diagnosis and swift intervention with steroids and macrolides produced considerable clinical and radiological improvement, demonstrating the efficacy of this treatment approach. This case contributes to the limited reports of DPB in India, improving understanding of its manifestation and management in diverse populations.

Introduction: A chylothorax, also known as chylous pleural effusion, is characterized by the accumulation of bacteriostatic chyle in the pleural space.

Case Report: A 61year old male presented with complaints of breathlessness, cough since one week with unquantifiable weight loss. Examination revealed grade 2 clubbing, with palpable left supraclavicular lymph nodes and reduced intensity breath sounds on right side. Routine blood investigations were unremarkable. Chest radiograph confirmed the diagnosis of right pleural effusion. Diagnostic thoracocentesis yielded thick cream-colored pleural fluid, analysis revealed low ADA, exudative, lymphocyte predominant. Ultrasound soft tissue neck showed left cervical and supraclavicular necrotic lymphadenopathy, FNAC abandoned due to close proximity to the vasculature. Patient then underwent thoracoscopy, which suggested pus in the right pleural cavity -? Chylothorax, biochemical analysis confirmed the presence of chyle. Pleural biopsy suggested acute on chronic inflammation. CECT revealed right lobar consolidation, multiple randomly distributed nodules with nodular interlobular septal thickening in bilateral lung parenchyma, mediastinal lymphadenopathy. FOB+BAL+TBLB+TBNA suggested congested and oedematous mucosa of the right bronchus intermedius with irregularity noted, suggestive of -? Neoplastic aetiology. BAL analysis showed 100% lymphocytes, negative for malignancy, CBNAAT was negative. TBNA Smears studied were positive for malignancy, favoring metastatic carcinoma. PET CT performed suggested hypermetabolic focus in the collapsed lower lobe of right lung suspicious for primary neoplasm with metastasis to mediastinal and bilateral supraclavicular lymph nodes; hypermetabolic lung and skeletal metastasis. Histopathological examination from transbronchial lung biopsy was reported as features favoring adenocarcinoma.

Conclusions: It is imperative to note that pleural effusion, especially in elderly individuals, must be dealt with utmost care, bearing in mind the possibility of an underlying malignancy.

The presence of chylothorax necessitates further evaluation to determine the cause. Management of a chylothorax requires a multidisciplinary approach employing medical therapy and possibly surgical intervention.
