Department of Respiratory Medicine, Post Graduate Kempegowda Institute of Medical Sciences and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Background: COPD is now the top three leading causes of death across the world and 90% of these deaths occur in low and middle income nations.While Expiratory flow limitation occurs when an increase in transpulmonary pressure causes no increases in respiratory expiratory flow .It is common in patients of COPD and is a major determinant of dynamic hyperinflation and exercise limitation This study aims to evaluate the role of EFL in assessing the severity of COPD and compare the performance with spirometry.

Methods: It is a cross sectional study performed on a group of stable COPD patients.Forced oscillation technique and spirometry were performed on a group of 60 patients and EFL index was calculated by differences between inspiratory and expiratory phases of Xrs at 5Hz(ΔX5),respiratory system resistance(Rrs), X5(reactance 5Hz), AX, FEV1, FVC, FEV1/FVC, FEF (25-75).Grading of COPD patients was done based on spirometry according to GOLD 2024.Lastly, the EFL index was correlated with the spirometry values in COPD patients.

Results: On the basis of median value of ΔX5,60 stable COPD patients were classified into low EFL index and high EFL index group.A correlation was derived between the spirometry severity values with that of EFL index.ΔX5 correlate negatively with FEV1 , FVC , FEF 25-75, R5, R5-R20 and positively with AX.

Discussion: While spirometry requires forced expiration technique , FOT can be done based on Tidal volume breathing.It is a more sensitive technique in detecting early or mild airway obstruction, especially in peripheral airways.

Conclusion: EFL index is a valuable tool to assess severity in COPD patients , identify the patients at risk and can aid in treatment.

Keywords: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, expiratory flow limitation index, forced oscillation technique, spirometry

Aditya V. Patil, G. S. Gaude, Bhagyashri Patil, Jyothi Hattiholi, S. Gautam, Kiran Kumar Pujar, Guruprasad Antin, K. Ningappa

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, KAHER, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Bronchiectasis, characterized by irreversible airway dilation and chronic respiratory symptoms, is increasingly diagnosed as a comorbidity in Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD). Despite global recognition and guidelines like GOLD 2023 emphasizing its impact on COPD progression, data specific to the Indian context remain sparse. The coexistence of these conditions has significant implications, including higher morbidity and reduced quality of life, necessitating targeted research to identify prevalence and risk factors.

Aim: This study aims to evaluate the prevalence of bronchiectasis in COPD patients and analyse the associated predisposing factors.

Methods: This cross-sectional study included 135 COPD patients diagnosed per GOLD 2023 criteria. Systematic sampling was employed, and detailed clinical histories were obtained, including exacerbation frequency, prior tuberculosis treatment, and sputum analysis. Bronchiectasis was confirmed through HRCT thorax based on established radiological criteria. Risk factors such as age, gender, BMI, pulmonary function, and Pseudomonas aeruginosa colonization were assessed. Quality of life was measured using the Bronchiectasis Health Questionnaire. Statistical analysis utilized chi-square tests and multivariate logistic regression, with p < 0.05 deemed significant.

Results: Preliminary data revealed a prevalence of 24.1% bronchiectasis among COPD patients. Factors such as advanced age, male gender, and repeated exacerbations emerged as significant risks. The coexistence of these conditions correlated with worse respiratory function and diminished quality of life, emphasizing the need for early diagnosis and intervention.

Conclusion: The study highlights the considerable burden of bronchiectasis in COPD patients in the Indian population. Routine screening and targeted management strategies are essential to improve outcomes and reduce exacerbations in affected individuals.

Aditya V. Patil, Ningappa Karlingannavar, G. S. Gaude, Bhagyashri Patil, Jyothi Hattiholi, S. Gautam, Kiran Kumar Pujar, Guruprasad Antin

Department of Respiratory Medicine, Jawaharlal Nehru Medical College, KAHER, Belagavi, Karnataka, India

Background: Mycobacterium abscessus is a rapidly growing non-tuberculous mycobacterium that is commonly found in the environment. It can lead to severe infections in both immunocompromised and immunocompetent individuals. This case highlights the challenges in diagnosing and managing disseminated M. abscessus infections in an otherwise healthy adolescent.

Case Study: A 17-year-old female presented with cough, breathlessness, fever, generalized weakness, loss of appetite, and weight loss over the past month. Initial investigations were inconclusive, but non-tuberculous mycobacteria (NTM) cultures eventually revealed M. abscessus. The patient was initially treated with anti-tubercular therapy (ATT) but developed hepatitis, necessitating a change in treatment. After discontinuation of ATT, she was started on a regimen including levofloxacin, amikacin, and azithromycin. Despite this, the patient developed a left-sided pneumothorax and continued to show clinical deterioration.

Sputum culture revealed Streptococcus agalactiae and M. abscessus. The M. abscessus strain was resistant to macrolides and sensitive to aminoglycosides. Consequently, treatment was adjusted to include tigecycline, amikacin, clofazimine, and linezolid, leading to significant clinical improvement.

Conclusion: This case underscores the importance of accurate diagnosis and tailored antibiotic therapy in managing M. abscessus infections, highlighting the need for vigilance in cases where conventional treatments fail.

Ajay Lanjewar, Pravin Satkar, Shrinidhi Datar

Department of Medicine, Mahatma Gandhi Mission Institute of Health Sciences, Sevagram, Maharashtra, India

Background: Blood clots, mucus, food particles and foreign bodies can obstruct the endotracheal tube for a mechanically ventilated patient. Immediate consequences of such an event are hypoxia, poor ventilation and if severe and sustained, hemodynamic collapse. Blood clots are seen to obstruct airways in cases of tuberculosis, cavitary respiratory lesions, mitral stenosis, trauma and malignancy (1) It is essential to diagnose and manage this condition as it can be life-threatening.

Case Description: We discuss a case of a middle-aged man who had a case of bronchial Bronchial Asthma. The patient was on bronchodilator medications. He came with a history of acute onset breathing difficulty and dry cough. On examination, he had tachypnoea, tachycardia, hypoxia and bilateral wheeze on chest auscultation. All signs and symptoms were supportive of the diagnosis of acute respiratory failure. His symptoms did not respond to Non-Invasive ventilation. Therefore, he was intubated and mechanical ventilation was initiated. We started him on intravenous methylprednisolone, salbutamol and ipratropium bromide nebulisation which resolved his bronchospasm. He was being planned for weaning off the mechanical ventilation. Pressure Support ventilation was initiated, he had spontaneous breaths and he generated good tidal volume. We observed during the hospital stay when he was defecating using a bedpan, he had episodes of respiratory distress. Chest auscultation during these episodes showed decreased respiratory sounds. Similar episodes were repeated. One day, he got self-extubated. We observed that the distal end of the endotracheal tube had a blood clot. The blood clot was surrounded by a narrow space sufficient enough to allow a suction catheter. On retrospection, the resident doctor recollected that during suctioning the Endotracheal secretions were stained with blood. In this particular case, it was difficult to predict complete Endotracheal Tube obstruction. Even in the presence of a blood clot, the suction catheter could go beyond the distal end of the ET Tube. It was also noted later that between the episodes of sudden spasm after defecation plateau pressure on the ventilator were elevated. This cause of elevation of plateau pressure on the ventilator was because of straining while defecating and positive intra-abdominal pressure.

Background: Individuals with lung cancer are frequently mistaken for having pulmonary tuberculosis, resulting in delays in accurate diagnosis and exposure to unsuitable treatments. Akshay Dalmia

RNT Medical College, Udaipur, Rajasthan, India

Introduction: Actinomycosis is a rare, chronic bacterial infection caused by Actinomyces species, gram-positive bacilli found in the normal human flora. Pulmonary actinomycosis, accounting for 15-20% of cases, arises from aspiration of oropharyngeal or gastrointestinal material, often mimicking tuberculosis or malignancy.

Case: A 43-year-old male presented with a 6-month history of cough with blood-streaked sputum, low-grade fever, and weakness. He was a chronic smoker and alcohol user but had no weight loss or anorexia. Examination revealed decreased breath sounds over the left interscapular region. Chest X-ray showed left hilar opacity; CECT thorax revealed a left upper lobe consolidation with necrosis, cavitation, and lymphadenopathy. Bronchoscopy showed a yellowish necrotic endobronchial growth causing partial obstruction . BAL cell block and biopsy revealed filamentous organisms persistent with pulmonary actinomycosis. The patient was treated with intravenous piperacillin-tazobactam followed by oral amoxicillin-clavulanic acid, with marked clinical and radiological improvement.

Conclusion: This case underscores the importance of maintaining a high index of suspicion for pulmonary actinomycosis in patients with persistent lung lesions. Accurate diagnosis and timely treatment can prevent complications and mismanagement, emphasizing the need for awareness among clinicians about this rare but treatable condition.

Introduction: ABPA is a chronic condition caused by hypersensitivity to A.fumigatus, affecting the airways and linked to respiratory diseases.

Althafurahiman Ali

Srinivas Institute of Medical Sciences and Research Centre, Mangalore, Karnataka, India

Introduction: COPD is the 4th leading cause of death around the world according to WHO, causing 3.5 million deaths around the globe only in 2021. COPD is more prevalent and associated with worse health outcomes in rural areas compared to urban areas, highlighting the disparities in awareness and access to facilities such as spirometry.

Methods: In this descriptive, cross-sectional study, we compared the GOLD grades of 61 COPD patients with their NLR and PLR ratios to find any association. We also compared the forced mid-expiratory flow value along with the NLR and PLR ratio.

Results: In this study, we found a positive correlation of the NLR and PLR ratio with the GOLD grades of COPD. This correlation is statistically significant in the case of the NLR ratio, with a p-value of 0.0019. In the case of forced mid-expiratory flow, we found a negative correlation with the NLR ratio. As the forced mid-expiratory flow decreases, the NLR ratio increases.

Conclusion: This study concludes that the NLR and PLR ratio has a positive correlation with GOLD grades, which is usually determined after PFT evaluation. Hence in those places where there is no access to the PFT, NLR and PLR ratios can be used to categorize the severity of the patient and fill the gap in the access to healthcare to an extent.

Keywords: COPD, GOLD grades, mid-expiratory flow, NLR ratio, PLR ratio

Amit Panjwani

King Hanad American Mission Hospital, Goverment Hospitals, A’ali, Bahrain

Introduction: Septic pulmonary embolism (SPE) is a type of non-thrombotic pulmonary embolism that is associated with a clinical challenge due to its rarity of occurrence, varied presentation and need for distinctive diagnostic and management options. It accounts for less than 3% of pulmonary embolisms. Despite its fatal potential, there is paucity of data on patients with SPE in Kingdom of Bahrain and is limited to case reports.

Aims: This study was undertaken to illustrate the clinical, microbiological, and radiological features of patients with SPE and to understand treatment outcomes in this rare cohort of patients from this island nation.

Methods: We retrospectively analyzed 14 patients of SPE encountered in academic tertiary care referral hospitals over a 5-year period.

Results: There was male preponderance with more than half of patients admitted in intensive care unit. Staphylococcus aureus was isolated in 57% of cases. Bilateral nodules in peripheral location were the most encountered radiological abnormality. Most of these nodules had feeding vessel sign (93%), and cavitation. Infectious vegetations of the right-side heart were detected in 36% of cases. All patients received parenteral antimicrobial therapy while drainage at extrapulmonary sites was done in 36% cases. The survival rate was 79%. None of the factors associated with increased mortality in previous studies impacted in-hospital mortality in the current study.

Conclusion: Staphylococcus aureus is the most common culprit in patients of SPE. Prompt initiation of appropriate antimicrobial therapy along with surgical intervention, where indicated, helps in achieving a favorable outcome after treatment of SPE.

Introduction: Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) is a complex hypersensitivity reaction in response to colonization of the airways with Aspergillus fumigatus that occurs almost exclusively in patients with asthma or cystic fibrosis. Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia (TPE) is caused by an immune hyper-responsiveness to microfilariae trapped in the lungs. Although both present as pulmonary infiltrates with peripheral eosinophilia, the management of these diseases is largely different. Simultaneous occurrence of ABPA and TPE is rarely reported in the literature. We here describe 3 cases with the co-existence of these two entities.

Results: The features of the cases and various modes of management are depcited in Table 1.

Clinical, lab features and management of the cases

DEC: Diethyl-carbamazine, A. fumigatus: Aspergillus fumigatus

Conclusions: Although infrequent, co-existence may be possible, especially in filarial endemic countries.

Understanding the overlap between TPE and ABPA is crucial for accurate diagnosis and management, as the treatments for these conditions are vastly different. Addressing both diseases can avoid suboptimal outcomes, such as the progression of individual diseases or unnecessary immunosuppressive therapy.

K. C. Anil Kumar, C. L. Nagaraja, B. L. Shashi Bhushan

Bangalore Medical College and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Small cell carcinoma accounts for approximately 25% of bronchial carcinoma cases. It tends to grow more aggressively and often metastasizes earlier than other types of carcinoma. This case report details the unique presentation of the cancer which mimicked two illness complicating the diagnosis. The case first mimicked as Bronchopenumonia and later as pulmonary embolism. This case highlights the importance of diagnostic imaging for early diagnosis of small cell carcinoma.

Case: An 80-year-old male presented at emergency department with acute breathlessness, hemoptysis and chest pain of one-day duration. He had history of exertional breathlessness for 6 years and 30-year smoking history, with grade-3 clubbing. On Examination, the patient was afebrile and hypoxic maintaining saturation with 6L Oxygen, with reduced breath sounds in the left infra-scapular area and bilateral fine inspiratory crepitations in interscapular and infrascapular regions. The chest radiograph showed a wedge-shaped opacity in the left middle zone. Routine blood investigations were normal, and sputum culture grew Acinetobacter baumannii, prompting the initiation of antibiotic treatment. Persistent symptoms necessitated Contrast-enhanced CT, which initially suggested pulmonary thromboembolism involving the right pulmonary artery and segmental arteries. Further inspection revealed an occult mass in the apicoposterior segment of left upper lobe and superior segment of left lower lobe infiltrating segmental bronchi, descending aorta, left pulmonary and interlobar arteries indicative of bronchogenic carcinoma. Multiple malignant mediastinal lymph nodes and lesions in the adrenal gland suggested metastasis. Additional findings included extensive centriacinar emphysematous changes, cardiomegaly dilated main pulmonary artery, and calcified hydatid cysts in the liver. A CT-guided biopsy of the left upper lobe mass confirmed small cell carcinoma. The patient was referred to the Oncology department for further management.

Conclusion: This case underscores the challenges of diagnosing small cell carcinoma due to its atypical presentation and the potential for diagnostic pitfalls, such as misinterpretation of imaging findings as pulmonary embolism. A high index of suspicion and thorough evaluation, including diagnostic imaging and biopsy, are crucial for early detection and appropriate management of this aggressive malignancy.

Aravind Ram

Sri Ramachandra Institute of Higher Education and Research, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Asthma is a chronic respiratory condition that significantly impacts both physical and psychological health, with poor medication adherence often complicating management. The Asthma APGAR tool was developed to improve asthma care by focusing on activities, persistent symptoms, triggers, asthma medications, and response to therapy. This study evaluates the impact of the APGAR tool on asthma control among patients with bronchial asthma in primary care settings.

Methods: A quantitative design was employed, involving 60 patients with bronchial asthma, aged 20–60 years, diagnosed for over one year, and on stable maintenance therapy for at least a month. Exclusion criteria included acute exacerbations, active tuberculosis, and those requiring systemic corticosteroid therapy. Data were collected using a three-section tool: demographic variables, clinical variables, and the Asthma APGAR assessment. The study was conducted with ethical approval from the institutional ethics committee of Sri Ramachandra Hospital, Chennai.

Results: Of the 60 patients, 44 (73%) had inadequate asthma control, while 16 (27%) demonstrated adequate control. Significant associations were observed between APGAR scores and family history of asthma, as well as asthma duration. Demographic findings showed that 38% of patients were aged 20–30 years, with 60% male, 55% having primary education, and 47% living in semi-urban areas. The clinical analysis revealed 42% had asthma for 2–5 years, 30% had hypertension, and 58% were non-smokers. The average score for medication adherence was 14.9 ± 3.45.

The study highlighted that 73% of patients had inadequate asthma control, emphasizing the need for improved patient education. The Asthma APGAR tool contributed to better documentation, adherence to treatment, and enhanced communication between patients and healthcare providers. The study supports the positive role of patient-centered approaches in asthma management, consistent with previous research showing the tool’s effectiveness in improving asthma outcomes. Future studies should assess the long-term impact of the APGAR tool on clinical outcomes and adherence.

Conclusion: The study confirmed the Asthma APGAR tool’s reliability in screening and managing asthma in the general population. It emphasized the importance of a collaborative care model, involving active patient participation in asthma management. Despite limitations, such as the lack of direct patient outcomes and the study’s homogenous participant pool, the APGAR tool showed promise in improving asthma care in primary care settings. It was well-received by both healthcare professionals and patients, providing a valuable addition to asthma management practices. Future research should explore its long-term effects and effectiveness in diverse populations.

Aravind Ram

Sri Ramachandra Institute of Higher Education and Research, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Bronchogenic cysts are congenital anomalies that are often asymptomatic. Diagnosis with CT using characteristics of a round, well-circumscribed, unilocular mass. Resection of cysts includes through either thoracotomy or VATS procedure. Alternatively, TBNA of mediastinal cysts has been advocated in the management.

Case Description: A cohort of four patients compromising of two male and two female with prevailing symptoms featured shortness of breath, accompanied by a persistent cough in three cases and one patient had dysphagia as major compliant. Chest X-ray, followed by CT thorax done, revealing a right paratracheal lesion in three instances, subsequently confirmed as bronchogenic cysts. EBUS was used to unveil the cystic nature through the presence of an anechoic shadow in all and air-fluid level in one instance. EBUS guided aspiration of cystic lesion was executed in all cases yielding a straw-colored clear aspirate devoid of any positivity for bacterial culture or cytological examination. No post-aspiration complications were encountered, with successful discharge of two patients on the same day as the procedure. Bronchogenic cysts are diagnosed with chest X-rays irregularities, but CT or MRI affirm their cystic essence. EBUS, a minimally invasive tool, offering complete cyst drainage and preventing re-accumulation. In our four-patient cohort, post-EBUS complications were conspicuously absent, with two cases even discharged on day of procedure. No recurrence has been documented on follow up in any of our patient till date. EBUS shines over surgery, highlighting efficacy and speedy recovery rates.

Conclusion: EBUS has its advantages in the management of bronchogenic cyst. The appearance on ultrasound of an anechoic or hypoechoic structure without colour flow signal helps us to rule out nodal, vascular, or soft tissue structures. Cyst wall thickening, septations within the cyst, and hyperechoic shadows within the cyst may help to identify infection as well as complex cyst.

Aravind Ram

Sri Ramachandra Institute of Higher Education and Research, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Obesity is a growing global health concern with significant implications for respiratory health. Spirometry, a vital tool for assessing lung function, measures parameters such as forced expiratory volume (FEV) and forced vital capacity (FVC) to diagnose and monitor respiratory conditions. In non-smoking adults, obesity can influence spirometry results due to its impact on lung mechanics, including reduced chest wall compliance, altered respiratory muscle function, and increased airway resistance. Understanding the relationship between obesity and spirometry outcomes is crucial for accurately interpreting pulmonary function tests and tailoring appropriate clinical interventions.

Methods: This study evaluates the effect of obesity on spirometry test results among non-smoking adults. A cross-sectional design was employed, involving participants aged 18–65 years who were categorized into normal weight, overweight, and obese groups based on body mass index (BMI) as per WHO standards. Participants with a history of smoking, chronic respiratory diseases, or other medical conditions affecting pulmonary function were excluded. Pulmonary function tests were conducted using standardized spirometry equipment following ATS/ERS guidelines. Key spirometric parameters, including forced vital capacity (FVC), forced expiratory volume in one second (FEV1), and the FEV1/FVC ratio, were measured. Height, weight, and BMI were recorded for all participants. Data analysis was performed using statistical software to compare spirometric outcomes across BMI groups. The relationship between obesity and spirometric parameters was assessed using correlation and regression analyses, adjusting for age, sex, and other potential confounders. Ethical approval was obtained, and all participants provided informed consent.

Results: The study included 100 non-smoking adults, equally divided into obese (BMI >30 kg/m²) and normal-weight (BMI 18–25 kg/m²) groups. The mean FEV1/FVC ratio was slightly higher in obese participants (85.32 ± 6.27) compared to normal-weight individuals (83.08 ± 8.58), but this difference was not statistically significant (P = 0.139). Conversely, the mean FVC was significantly lower in obese patients (1.92 ± 0.92) than in normal-weight participants (2.43 ± 0.92) with a P value of 0.008, indicating a significant impact of obesity on FVC. Similarly, the mean FEV1 was reduced in obese individuals (1.64 ± 0.79) compared to normal-weight subjects (2.01 ± 0.78), with a statistically significant P value of 0.018. However, no significant differences were observed in the mean MEF25-75 (P = 0.197) and PEF (P = 0.068) values between the two groups. These findings suggest that obesity significantly affects FVC and FEV1, while its impact on other spirometric parameters is less pronounced.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that obesity significantly impacts specific pulmonary function parameters, particularly FVC and FEV1, which showed a statistically significant reduction in obese patients (P < 0.05). However, no significant changes were observed in the FEV1/FVC ratio, MEF25-75, or PEF. The findings indicate a diphasic relationship between BMI and spirometric values, with obesity playing a crucial role in reducing FVC and FEV1. These results highlight the need for targeted interventions to address respiratory health in obese individuals.

K. Aswathi

Bangalore Medical College and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Even though Deep Vein thrombosis has been associated with tuberculosis, the true incidence is unknown with studies reporting values between 3%-4%. The true incidence may be higher because of non specific or asymptomatic clinical presentations of deep vein thrombosis.

Case: A 28 year old male presented to casualty with c/o cough with expectoration, dyspnea and fever since 2 months. On examination, patient’s saturation at room air was 84% and RR was 26 cpm. He was febrile and BP was 100/60 mm hg and pulse rate was 120 bpm. He had right calf pain also. Sputum CBNAAT was sent and patient was diagnosed to be sputum MTB detected, rifampicin sensitive. Patient was started on ATT under NTEP.D-Dimer was raised with value 3.96 mcg/ml., B/L DVT screening was done which showed deep vein thrombosis of right popliteal vein. Patient was started on IV anticoagulation. Emergency CTPA was done and it showed no evidence of pulmonary thromboembolism. Patient became symptomatically better and was hence discharged home on ATT and oral anticoagulants.

Conclusions: This case shows that an integrated approach is required towards a pulmonary tuberculosis patient, so that diagnosis such as deep vein thrombosis is not missed. Active history taking with leading questions should be encouraged to avoid missing DVT and if found to be DVT positive, an emergency CT pulmonary angiogram is advisable to rule out pulmonary thromboembolism.

Awnish Shubham, Alina Vargheese, Tarang kulkarni, Alpa Dalal

Jupiter Hospital, Thane, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pulmonary mucormycosis is a rare and aggressive fungal infection affecting immunocompromised individuals and is associated with high morbidity and mortality. We present four cases of pulmonary mucormycosis in immunocompromised patients treated at our centre.

Case 1: A 66 year old male with uncontrolled diabetes and long term corticosteroids for COVID19 infection presented with cough , fever, hoarseness of voice and weight loss. CT scan chest revealed left upper lobe consolidation with central breakdown. BAL fungal stain and endobronchial biopsy revealed mucormycosis. He was successfully treated with amphotericin B and subjected to left upper lobectomy.

Case 2: A 27 year old male kidney transplant recipient on immunosuppressant presented with cough with rusty sputum ,weight loss and pleuritic chest pain. CT scan revealed peripheral round opacity in right middle and lower lobe. CT guided biopsy showed aseptate hyphae of mucormycosis. His immunosuppression is reduced and managed successfully with amphotericin B followed by posaconazole and lobectomy.

Case 3: A 63 year male with diabetic ketoacidosis was transferred on mechanical ventilation with complaints of cough, fever and hemoptysis. HRCT thorax revealed bird’s nest sign in right upper lobe. Bronchoscopy revealed black mucosal lesions and biopsy confirmed mucormycosis. Despite starting on liposomal amphotericin B, he died of septic shock.

Case 4: A 31 year old diabetic female with diabetic ketoacidosis presented with fever , abdominal pain and cough. CT imaging suggested fungal pneumonia. BAL and biopsy confirmed mucormycosis. Liposomal amphotericin B started, but her clinical condition deteriorated and she died.

Conclusion: Pulmonary mucormycosis is diagnostic and therapeutic challenge. A multidisciplinary approach combining early detection, aggressive antifungal therapy and surgical intervention are vital for reducing mortality in high risk population.

Army Institute of Cardio Thoracic Sciences, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pulmonary paragonimiasis, caused by Paragonimus species, is a food-borne parasitic lung disease often contracted through consuming raw or undercooked freshwater crab or crayfish. Due to overlapping clinical, radiological and pleural fluid features, it can be misdiagnosed as tuberculosis leading to delayed and inappropriate management.

Case Description: We report a 56-year-old male smoker with coronary artery disease (post-PTCA, September 2023) who presented in February 2024 with left-sided pleuritic chest pain. Imaging revealed left-sided pleural effusion with segmental collapse consolidation and ground-glass opacities. Diagnostic pleurocentesis showed an exudative, neutrophil-predominant effusion with low glucose and ADA (28), treated as empyema. Bronchoscopy showed no infection, but BAL revealed raised galactomannan and 12% eosinophils, prompting antifungal therapy. In August 2024, he presented with fever and right-sided pleuritic chest pain. Imaging revealed right-sided effusion with resolution of the left-sided effusion. Pleurocentesis showed an exudative, lymphocyte-predominant effusion with high ADA (105). Thoracoscopy revealed necrotizing granulomas, but infective workup was negative. He was started on ATT but remained symptomatic. Persistent eosinophilia (AEC-2600/cmm), raised IgE, and crab consumption history raised suspicion of paragonimiasis, confirmed by serology. Treated with praziquantel, his symptoms resolved, and follow-up radiographs showed complete resolution.

Conclusion: pleuropulmonary paragonimiasis represents a rare but critical differential in patients presenting with exudative effusion with high ADA with histologic picture of tuberculosis Pulmonary paragonimiasis should be considered in patients from endemic areas with tuberculosis-like symptoms and imaging findings. A positive dietary history, negative TB cultures, and lack of response to TB therapy warrant revision of the diagnosis.

Bharti Kumari

Army Institute of Cardio Thoracic Sciences, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) affects millions globally, severely impacting quality of life by impairing breathing, physical activity, and daily functioning. Malnutrition, affecting 25–65% of COPD patients, is a significant yet underrecognized factor influencing disease progression. Nutritional deficiencies, particularly in protein and essential micronutrients, weaken diaphragmatic and skeletal muscle function, reducing lung capacity and exercise tolerance. This study investigates the role of nutritional education as a critical component of COPD management, emphasizing its potential to alleviate symptoms, improve clinical outcomes, and enhance patients’ overall quality of life.

Methods: This prospective observational study was conducted at a tertiary care hospital, AICTS Pune, involving pre- and post-interventional assessments. Sixty COPD patients participating in a tele-rehabilitation program were included. Lung capacity and exercise tolerance were assessed using FEV1 and the 6MWT before and after intervention. Disease severity and prognosis were evaluated using the BODE (Body mass index, Obstruction, Dyspnea, Exercise capacity) and ADO (Age, Dyspnea, Obstruction) indices. A structured nutritional education program, supplemented with advice on energy intake and muscle mass building, was implemented to assess its impact on patient outcomes.

Results: The study cohort (n=60, mean age 65.87 ± 5.40, 54% male) demonstrated statically significant improvements post-intervention. BODE scores decreased from 4.36 to 2.54 (p = 4.19×10-4), and ADO scores declined (p = 0.008). Spirometry revealed an increase in FEV1% from (1.05→1.68L) 51.64% to 68.57% (p = 4×10-4), while 6MWT scores improved by a mean of 50.2m (p = 0.0001).

Conclusion: Nutritional education is a pivotal non-pharmacological intervention in COPD management. Incorporating nutritional counselling into routine COPD care significantly improves lung capacity, exercise tolerance, and quality of life, reduces disease burden, and potentially slows disease progression.

Carry Home Message: A well-balanced diet and targeted nutritional therapy, combined with rehabilitation and exercise, should be promoted to enhance outcomes in COPD patients.

Diptendu Dey, Arup Halder, Raja Dhar, Shyam Krishnan, Beauty Biswas, Aditya Satpati, Pradip Mondal

CMRI Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Unexplained exertional breathlessness in young individuals often presents a diagnostic challenge. Dysfunctional breathing (DB) is characterized by abnormal breathing patterns that can occur in the absence of significant organic disease. This study aims to emphasize the role of CPET in diagnosing DB and explore the lung physiology of these patients.

Methods: Five patients (mean age: 23.2 years, gender-all females) presented with exertional shortness of breath over 6–12 weeks, associated with mostly with 3 to 4 of the following symptoms-chest discomfort, palpitations, generalized weakness, and mood instability. Clinical examination & investigations (chest X-ray, ECG, and echocardiography) were unremarkable. Subsequently, CPET was performed to evaluate unexplained breathlessness. Body box plethysmography with DLCO was conducted to assess the lung physiology of these patients.

Results: CPET findings in 5 patients revealed oscillatory breathing patterns, sufficient ventilatory reserve, elevated VE/VCO2 slope, and the absence of oxygen desaturation. Body plethysmography revealed in all 5 patients: Elevated residual volume (162%–296% predicted), Increased RV/TLC ratio (40.39%–50.21%), Reduced VA/TLC ratio (0.70–0.74).

Conclusions: Dysfunctional breathing emerges as a key contributor to unexplained exertional breathlessness, with CPET playing a pivotal role in diagnosis especially oscillatory breathing pattern is highly indicative of this condition.

Consistent lung physiology abnormalities might indicate two possible factors:

The presence of an undiagnosed obstructive airway component by Low VA/TLC ratioAn unusual breathing habit maintaining high residual lung volume (162-296%).

The presence of an undiagnosed obstructive airway component by Low VA/TLC ratio

An unusual breathing habit maintaining high residual lung volume (162-296%).

CPET is invaluable in diagnosing DB, with oscillatory breathing patterns serving as an important feature. The physiology for DB mandates further research to diagnose Undiagnosed airway disease and High Residual lung volume is associated with DB.

Keywords: CPET, Dysfunctional breathing, exertional breathlessness, lung physiology, oscillatory breathing

Guna Jefferson

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Nocardia, a genus of Gram-positive bacteria, typically causes opportunistic infections in immunocompromised hosts but can also affect immunocompetent individuals.

Case: This case involves a 59-year-old female presenting with left-sided chest pain, dry cough, weight loss, and fever over several months. Initial CT revealed consolidation in the left lower lobe with minimal pleural effusion; however, pleural fluid analysis was inconclusive. After empirical antibiotic treatment, her symptoms recurred, and a repeat CT showed a hypodense collection in the left posterior costophrenic recess, extending through the 10th intercostal space into the posterior chest wall, quadratus lumborum, and paraspinal muscle. Ultrasound-guided drainage was performed, revealing pus suggestive of aerobic actinomycetes. It was likely that she had nocardiosis and as Nocardia are prone to cause brain abscesses, CNS imaging was conducted even though the patient did not exhibit neurological symptoms, leading to the discovery of an abscess in the right anterior striatocapsular region. Pus culture confirmed Nocardia asiatica. The patient was started on intravenous ceftriaxone and cotrimoxazole, with a planned total therapy duration of one year. Follow-up MRI showed a reduction in abscess size, and she reported improvement after one month.

Conclusion: Pulmonary Nocardia infections have the potential to disseminate, most often to the brain. Nocardia can also spread contiguously from the lungs to adjacent structures, causing complications such as empyema, empyema necessitans, mediastinitis, pericarditis, and superior vena cava syndrome. Therefore, the differential diagnosis for this presentation, even in immunocompetent individuals, must include less common entities such as disseminated Nocardia.

Guna Jefferson

Christian Medical College, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India

Introduction: Amyloidosis can present in the lungs in three forms: nodular pulmonary amyloidosis, diffuse alveolar-septal amyloidosis, and tracheobronchial amyloidosis. Diffuse alveolar-septal involvement is a rare form of pulmonary amyloidosis.

Case: A 65-year-old male presented with cough, breathlessness (Grade II MMRC), loss of appetite, weight loss, and frothy urine for three years. His chest X-ray showed reticular opacities in the bilateral mid and lower zones. An ECG revealed a left anterior fascicular block, and spirometry indicated a restrictive pattern. HRCT thorax demonstrated diffuse reticulations, irregular interlobular septal thickening, and ground-glass opacities predominantly in the subpleural regions of both lungs. 24-hour urine protein test was high (784 mg/24 hr). 2D echo showed thickened left and right ventricular walls, a dilated left atrium, Grade II LV diastolic dysfunction, and an apical-sparing pattern on longitudinal strain, all suggesting cardiac amyloidosis. Urinary Bence-Jones protein was positive. The HRCT thorax was discussed in a multidisciplinary team meeting, leading to the conclusion that the CT features were consistent with diffuse alveolar-septal type amyloidosis. Bone marrow aspiration and biopsy revealed diffuse interstitial plasmacytosis and lambda light chain restriction. Immune fixation electrophoresis showed only lambda light chains, with elevated kappa (38.94) and lambda light chains (683.60), resulting in a kappa/lambda ratio of 0.056. He was diagnosed with lambda light chain myeloma and started on a chemotherapy regimen of cyclophosphamide, bortezomib, and dexamethasone (CyBORD). After 28 days of follow-up, he was doing well.

Conclusion: Knowledge of pulmonary amyloidosis is crucial due to the nonspecific findings in chest CT scans. This case underscores the importance of multidisciplinary team meetings in diagnosing diffuse parenchymal lung diseases.

Introduction: Immunoglobulin light chain (AL) amyloidosis is an uncommon disease. Approximately 20% of multiple myeloma patients will develop AL amyloidosis. Persistent pleural effusions occur in 1 to 2% of patients with systemic amyloidosis and are usually associated with poor prognosis.

Case Description: A 57 years old male presented with breathlessness, right sided chest pain, dysphagia for 6 months. So the final diagnosis as multiple myeloma with AL systemic amyloidosis affecting pleura, gastric mucosa, heart, started on VPD protocol with Bortezomib, Pomalidomide, Dexamethasone.

Jerry Jose

Little Flower Hospital and Research Centre, Angamaly, Kerala, India

Introduction: Establishing cause of ILD is challenging. Obtaining proper medical history is important. Here we discuss about a 74-year-old male with worsening breathlessness of 1 month and diagnosed as Nitrofurantoin induced ILD based on history and by exclusion of other causes.

Case Report: 74-year-old male, ex-smoker with worsening breathlessness and cough of 1 month. He had a history of fall 3 years back following which he had vertebral fracture, paraparesis. He was tachypnoeic, with saturation of 76% in room air. He had bilateral fine end inspiratory crepitations throughout the lung field. With NIV he was stable and was maintaining a saturation of 95 % with 60 % FiO2. His chest X ray showed bilateral reticular shadows with ill-defined cardiac and diaphragmatic borders. ECHO showed moderate pulmonary hypertension with good systolic and diastolic function. HIS HRCT chest was suggestive of Non-specific interstitial pneumonia with no evidence of active infection. He was further evaluated with RA, ANA profile, ACE and were negative. On detailed medical history he gave history of recurrent urinary tract infection for the last 3 years as he was catheterised and he used to take oral nitrofurantoin almost every month. He was diagnosed as Nitrofurantoin induced ILD after excluding other causes. He was started on intravenous methylprednisolone and by third day weaned off from NIV support and maintained 93 % with 2L/Mi. Repeat CXR showed clearance. 2 months following discharge he was clinically improved with saturation of 97 % in room air. Proper medical history will help in diagnosis of ILD.

Conclusion: Clinical and radiographic features of drug induced ILD are often difficult to distinguish from other causes of ILD. Drug induced lung injury should always be kept in mind. Appropriate medical history will help in establishing the cause in most cases of ILD.

Jyothi Kini

M S Ramaiah Medical College, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: COPD is one of the leading causes of morbidity and mortality worldwide. COPD has considerable effects on cardiac functions. Most of the increased mortality associated with COPD is due to cardiac involvement. Spirometry is the gold standard test to diagnose COPD and to determine the severity of disease. 2D echocardiogram with PAAT is used to determine mean pulmonary artery pressure and derive pulmonary hypertension, to correlate disease severity and relation with mean pulmonary artery pressure

Primary Objective: To determine correlation of CAT score with stages of pulmonary hypertension.

Secondary Objective: To determine pulmonary hypertension with measurement of pulmonary artery acceleration time and mean pulmonary artery pressure in COPD patients.

Materials and Methods: Total 33 subjects were enrolled into study from 1/05/2023 to 30/04/2024 and were subjected to mMRC scale, CAT scoring, PFT , GOLD grading, 2D echocardiogram ,PAAT and HRCT thorax.

Results: In our study 84.8% of subjects had mild pulmonary hypertension. There was no correlation of mPAP with mMRC score, CAT score and GOLD grade. There was a strong negative correlation between PAAT and mPAP which was statistically significant. Specificity of the PAAT for mPAP assessment was 100%, sensitivity was 92.86%, Accuracy was 93.94%. PPV of 100% and NPV OF 71.43%.

Discussion: GOLD grade 1 and 2 have pulmonary hypertension: 82% and 90% respectively. Patients with low CAT score can have pulmonary hypertension.

Conclusion: All patients with a diagnosis of COPD irrespective of the stage of disease should undergo PAAT and mPAP estimation, which is cheap, noninvasive, repeatable, highly sensitive, and highly specific diagnostic test. This helps in optimizing the COPD treatment with pulmonary rehabilitation and reduce morbidity and mortality from cardiac complications. To improve correlation we need larger study group and need to include subjects with normal FEV1 and HRCT finding consistent with COPD.

Keywords: CAT score, COPD, mMRC scale, PAAT

Kundan Nivangune

Medical Affairs, Lupin Ltd., Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The burden of asthma in India is significant with high mortality and poor adherence to treatment. Also, real-world data on the effectiveness and safety of fluticasone-formoterol is limited in India. This study evaluated the effectiveness and safety of Formoterol fumarate (6 µg) and Fluticasone propionate (250 µg) administered via dry powder inhaler (DPI) or metered-dose inhaler (MDI) in Indian asthma patients.

Methods: This 24-week prospective, multicentre study (CTRI/2023/08/056250) evaluated Formoflo 250 Transcaps (DPI) and Formoflo 250 Transhaler (MDI) in adults (18–65 years). The primary endpoint was the change in trough forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1) at weeks 12 and 24. Secondary endpoints included changes in trough forced vital capacity (FVC), Asthma Control Test (ACT), and Asthma Quality of Life Questionnaire (AQLQ) scores. Safety was assessed through adverse events (AEs) and asthma exacerbations, with statistical analyses performed on the modified intention-to-treat (mITT) population.

Results: A total of 503 patients were enrolled, with 495 included in the mITT analysis and all 503 in the safety analysis. Trough FEV1 improved significantly from 1993.0 ± 423.6 mL at baseline to 2305.2 ± 441.3 mL at Week 24 (P<0.0001) with mean change from baseline to end of study of 310.8 ml ± 118.4 ml (300.1 to 321.4). Similarly, trough FVC increased from 2495.6 ± 531.4 mL to 2774.9 ± 533.4 mL (P<0.0001). The mean ACT score improved from 10.3 ± 2.5 at baseline to 21.9 ± 3.2 at Week 24, with 78.4% of patients achieving excellent asthma control (ACT > 20). The mean AQLQ scores also improved significantly from 3.5 ± 1.0 to 5.9 ± 1.0 (P<0.0001). AEs were reported in 7.0% of patients, primarily mild, with no serious AEs or fatalities. These findings demonstrate consistent effectiveness and safety of Formoflo DPI and MDI formulations in the real-world settings in India.

Conclusion: The Formoterol-Fluticasone combination significantly improved lung function, asthma control, and quality of life, with comparable effectiveness and safety between DPI and MDI in Indian asthma patients.

Keywords: Asthma management, dry powder inhaler (DPI), formoterol-fluticasone combination, metered-dose inhaler (MDI)

G. Madhavan

Department of Respiratory Medicine, SLMCH, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Objective: To evaluate the performance of existing screening questionnaires and scoring scales in identifying clinically relevant obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) patients.

Methods: This observational study was conducted at Sri Lalithambigai Medical College & Hospital over six months, involving seventy-three subjects referred to the sleep clinic. Participants completed the STOP-Bang questionnaire and the Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS), with OSA diagnosis confirmed through polysomnography (PSG) or home sleep apnea tests (HSAT). Data analysis included correlation, logistic regression, and sensitivity/specificity assessments using SPSS 26.0.

Results: The mean age of subjects was 52.3 years, with a majority being male (61.6%). The mean BMI was 30.1 kg/m², and the mean neck circumference was 38.5 cm. ESS and STOP-Bang scores were positively correlated with AHI (r = 0.45 and r = 0.60, respectively, p < 0.001). Higher STOP-Bang scores were significantly associated with more severe OSA. Sensitivity and specificity analyses revealed that STOP-Bang (cutoff ≥5) had higher sensitivity (90%) compared to ESS (cutoff ≥11) at 85%.

Conclusion: The STOP-Bang questionnaire demonstrated superior performance in screening for OSA compared to ESS, with higher sensitivity and stronger correlation with AHI. These findings support the use of STOP-Bang as a primary screening tool in clinical settings to identify patients at high risk for OSA, facilitating timely diagnosis and management.

Mandar Shinde

Dr. Vasantrao Pawar Medical College, hospital and research centre, Nashik, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Aspergillus is a ubiquitous fungus, isolated from construction sites, soil, and hospital dust and is present in the surroundings almost everywhere. Repeated inhalation of its spores can either trigger immune response in sensitive individuals (e.g. ABPA) and/or can have infective presentation as aspergilloma. Aspergillus infection can occur in either immunocompetent patients with underlying lung disease or in immunocompromised hosts [critically ill individuals managed at the intensive care unit (ICU); neutropenia; chronic use of corticosteroids; immunosuppressants; cancer; COPD; HIV infection; chronic renal disease; liver disease; DM and occupational exposures], with or without underlying lung disease. In this case report, a case of 45 years old male with type 2 DM who developed aspergillus pneumonia will be presented.

Case: A 45-years-old male, farmer, presented with complaints of chronic cough (dry), fever with chills (on and off), generalized weakness, reduced appetite and weight loss since 4-5 months. He is a known case of type 2 DM for past 10 years, is non-compliant on medications & a chronic alcoholic since 25 years. His routine lab investigations were done. RBS, FBS, PPBS and HbA1c were deranged. Chest radiograph s/o left middle zone inhomogeneous opacities. HRCT thorax was s/o left middle lobe and superior segment of left lower lobe consolidation. Bronchoscopy was done. BAL fluid C/S, CBNAAT, gram stain were all negative. BAL fluid galactomannan was 1.23 and BAL fluid fungal culture was suggestive of Aspergillus spp. Patient was started on Tab. Posaconazole 300 mg OD for 8 weeks, at the end of which there was complete resolution of clinical and radiological findings.

Conclusion: Fungal infections should be suspected and investigated thoroughly in patients having risk factors causing immunocompromised state, with management of the fungal infection along with controlling/eliminating the risk factor(s) responsible for immunocompromised state.

Pooja Patil, Sanjiv Zangde

Shankarrao Chavan Government Medical College, Nanded, Maharashtra, India

Pulmonary mucormycosis, a severe fungal infection caused by mucormycetes, has emerged as a critical concern in immunocompromised populations, particularly during the COVID-19 pandemic. This presentation aims to elucidate the latest insights into the pathogenesis of pulmonary mucormycosis, emphasizing the factors that facilitate infection, including host immunosuppression, hyperglycemia, and environmental exposure. We will explore the unique morphological and biological characteristics of mucormycetes that contribute to their virulence and the host’s inflammatory response. Additionally, we will discuss advancements in diagnostic techniques, including imaging modalities and molecular methods, that enhance early detection and improve patient outcomes. Treatment strategies will be reviewed, highlighting the role of antifungal therapies, surgical interventions, and supportive care in managing this life-threatening condition. Furthermore, we will address the challenges in treatment, such as drug resistance and the need for timely intervention. By synthesizing recent research findings and clinical experiences, this presentation aims to provide a comprehensive overview of pulmonary mucormycosis, fostering awareness and guiding future research efforts in this critical area of infectious diseases.

Pramod Chavan

Kokilaben Dhirubhai Ambani Hospital, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Tuberculosis is a major health problem, with an estimated eight million new cases and two million deaths occurring every year worldwide. However, 40-60% of patients with Pulmonary Tuberculosis suspected clinically or radiologically may fail to produce any sputum can be diagnosed with Fiberoptic Bronchoscopy.

Aims: To assess significance of Fiberoptic Bronchoscopy in diagnosis of radiologically suspected but sputum negative and unable to expectorate patients.

Methods: A prospective study was conducted in 80 patients at Saijyot Multispeciality Hospital Koparkhairane Navi Mumbai between 2023-2024. Among the 80 patients 44 were Sputum negative and 36 were unable to expectorate. This data was analyzed and results were given in percentage.

Results: Out of 80 patients, 45% patients were diagnosed as Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) positive for Pulmonary Tuberculosis, 20% were Bacterial Pneumonia, 35% were no growth on microbiological examination in Fiberoptic Bronchoscopy.

Conclusion: Fiberoptic Bronchoscopy has an important role in accurate and early diagnosis of sputum negative and unable to expectorate patients, in radiologically suspected Pulmonary Tuberculosis patients.

Keywords: BAL, fiberoptic bronchoscopy, pulmonary tuberculosis

Prarabdh Modi, Shahid Patel, Girija Nair, Manas Bhanushali, Tanay Sinha, Nikhil Sarangdhar

D Y Patil Hospiatl, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Retrospective analysis of the bronchoalveolar lavage in patients undergone bronchoscopy at tertiary care hospital may yield valuable insights about changing paradigms in the trends of various diseases diagnosed by interventional pulmonology techniques.

Objective: To analyse retrospective bronchoalveolar lavage reports in patients and to assess the prevalence of various lung diseases diagnosed by bronchoscopy over a period of 8 years.

Methodology: Retrospective data from 1st January 2017 to 31st December 2024 was collected and analysed from our bronchoscopy registers and medical records. Disease stratification into infectious (Tuberculosis, non-tubercular mycobacterial infections, Pneumonias) and non- infectious (malignancy, Interstitial lung diseases and others) causes .The trend were analysed year wise for each diagnosis.

Results: Total 2123 bronchoscopy reports were found over 8 years. Out of these 793 are positive for bacterial culture, 229 for tuberculosis (Drug Sensitive and Drug resistant),83 for NTM , 70 for malignancy and 25 were others. 923 BAL reports were normal. An interesting trend observed was a rise in Non-tubercular mycobacterial infections in 2020 and 2021 and bacterial culture and sensitivity. The prevalence of tuberculosis and malignancy remained otherwise stable. The drop in all diagnoses during the year 2020 could be attributed to the COVID-19 lockdown.

Conclusion: Bronchoscopy remains a valuable tool for diagnosis and management of a wide variety of pulmonary diseases, both acute like infections and chronic such as ILDs and others.In our study 43.4 % of bronchoalveolar lavage reports were normal showing that bronchoscopy also helps greatly to rule out certain pulmonary diseases, mainly infections and malignancy.

Nandakishore Bommeri

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Adichunchanagiri Institute of Medical Scicences, Mandya, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Pulmonary sequestration is a rare condition where a portion of lung tissue develops abnormally and is not connected to the normal airway system. Instead, it has its own blood supply separate from the rest of the lung.[1] This condition was first identified by Pryce in 1946 and makes up a small percentage of congenital lung abnormalities i.e., about 0.15%-6.40%.[2]

Case: A 47 year old non-smoker male with no co-morbidities presented to outpatient clinic with history of recurrent cough since 20 years and 1st episode of heamoptysis. TLC was 19520 cells/mm3. Sputum culture yielded Heavy growth of Pseudomonas aeroginosa. CECT Thorax showed brochiolectatic cavitatory changes with air fluid levels in bilateral lung fields and a segment of left upper lobe receiving anomalous arterial supply. The CT aortogram revealed an intralobar pulmonary sequestration in the lingular segment of the left upper lobe. The anomalous artery originates from the abdominal aorta at L1, traversing the diaphragm to supply this sequestered lung tissue [Figures 1-4].

CECT Thorax showing brochiolectatic changes with air fluid levels in bilateral lung fields and a segment of left upper lobe receiving anomalous arterial supply. CT aortogram revealed an intralobar pulmonary sequestration in the lingular segment of the left upper lobe. The anomalous artery originates from the abdominal aorta at L1, traversing the diaphragm to supply this sequestered lung tissue

Conclusions: Pulmonary sequestration is a rare congenital anomaly characterized by a lack of connection to the airway, rendering it typically non-functional.

Diagnosis of pulmonary sequestration is uncommon during adulthood, however it warrants to keen high index of suspicion for the diagnosis of sequestration in patients with bronchiectasis with or without hemoptysis & recurrent infections. Often, intralobar sequestrations need to be resected to prevent recurrent infections and considerable shunting of blood. In the past few years, endovascular embolization and coiling have emerged as a therapeutic alternatives.

PD Hinduja National Hospital and Medical Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

A 20-year-old male presented to the chest clinic with fever, and pleuritic chest pain for the past six weeks. Patient had no significant past medical or surgical history. The patient had no history of recent travel, exposure to tuberculosis, or immunosuppressive therapy. Digital chest X-ray-PA view revealed a peripheral wedge-shaped opacity in the right upper lobe and that was confirmed by CECT-Thorax. 2DECHO, D-dimer, CTPA, cardiac profile ruled out thrombo-embolic condition. Priyanka Chhabaria

Heritage Institute of Medical Sciences, Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh, India

Introduction: Asthma is a heterogeneous disease characterized by airway hyperresponsiveness and chronic airway inflammation and usually presents with variability in respiratory symptoms (cough, shortness of breath, wheeze, chest tightness) over time and in intensity along with variable expiratory airflow limitation. Various tools have been developed and validated to measure symptom control- GINA symptom control tool I, Asthma Control Test (ACT), Asthma Control Questionnaire (ACQ).

Aim: Comparison of the level of asthma control using ACT and ACQ tools with GINA asthma symptom control.

Objectives: To compare the ACT and ACQ tools with GINA criteria in detecting asthma control.

Methods: It is cross sectional study conducted at HIMS, VARANASI. All patients with age> 20 years presenting with symptoms of asthma and Diagnosed with criteria according to recent GINA guidelines are included in study. Baseline data were collected and control status was determined using the GINA symptom control tool, ACT, and ACQ.

Results: Total of 60 patients were enrolled in the study. The sample comprised of 69% males and 3 I% females. There was a moderate-strong correlation of GINA with ACT (p= 0.82, p < 0.001), and ACQ (p= 0.6; p < 0.001).

Conclusion: We conclude that the GINA symptom control tool correlates well with ACT and ACQ for classifying well controlled asthma and used as tool for asthma control.

Keywords: ACQ, ACT, Asthma, GINA

Raghupathy Anchala

Indian Institute of Public Health, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Introduction: Social determinants of health play a key role in adherence to treatment among patients suffering with MDRTB. The aim of the study was to explore the unknown determinants that may play a role in the first month default among MDRTB patients.

Methods: A cross-sectional study was carried out among newly registered MDRTB patients who didn’t follow up with the treatment one month later at TB Units (TU) in slums of Pune district, to identify social determinants associated with defaulting from treatment. As part of the sampling strategy, the initial defaulters identified within the study’s time frame (July- Sep 2021) and one year earlier in the selected Tuberculosis units (TUs) were included. Patient’s address were retrieved from Nikshay registers from different TUs and patients were contacted either through a phone call or interviewed in their home.

Results: Among 74 diagnosed and registered MDRTB patients in the TUs, 14 patients were reported cured, 12 patients died as a result of MDRTB, 20 patients were currently undergoing MDR TB treatment, 18 patients shifted to private hospitals for further treatment, and 10 patients were reported as missing. 64% of men (24 out of 37) reported being able to manage the amount of medication pills, whereas only 28% of women were able to cope with the pill burden (p=0.004). Patients with family income more than Rs 10,000 per month were likely to shift to private practitioners when compared to low income category after treatment initiation (p= 0.010).

Discussion: In addition to previously known social determinants such as income and gender, our study has shown that pill burden among female MDRTB patients plays an important role in initial treatment default among MDRTB patients.

Conclusion: Social determinants of health such as family income, gender, and pill burden play an important role in the first month default among MDRTB patients.

Keywords: First month defaulters, MDRTB, pill burden, social determinants

Rajeev Ranjan, Utkarsh Singh

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Bharati Vidyapeeth, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: The effects of Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) and the impacts of Diastolic Heart Failure with Preserved Ejection Fraction (HFpEF) worsen the overall condition of a patient and complicate treatment as the two tend to occur concomitantly, this study aims to analyze how such patients respond to treatment along with the evaluation that the use of combination therapy is more effective than providing therapy.

Methods: This study adopts a one-year approach, examining 106 patients who suffer from COPD at the Chest Hospital Chapra OPD. The patients were subjected to diagnosis-controlled research in which they were divided into two groups after the diagnosis. The first group of patients was treated only for COPD under GOLD standards while the second group was treated for COPD and HFpEF. A minimum follow-up of three months was conducted to assess the effectiveness of the treatment, with a focus on clinical improvement, symptom relief, and the overall efficacy of the medical care given.

Results: Both sides of the group that received dual treatment for COPD with HFpEF had better results than those treated for only COPD. Respiration, exercise capability, and quality of life factors improved. The dual-treatment group also had a statistically significant lower number of acute exacerbation and hospital admissions during the entire follow-up. On the contrary, patients in the group that only received treatment for COPD made little advancement and continued suffering from chronic breathlessness and the events of exacerbation.

Conclusion: This study demonstrates the need for a conflict-resolving approach in the presence of the comorbidities of COPD and HFpEF. The necessity of addressing the comorbidity of pulmonary and cardiac dysfunction is substantiated when both problems are tackled together which translates into better outcomes. In the context of the management of COPD where HFpEF is also present, future recommendations need to explore the possibilities of using approaches of dual management to improve the treatment of patients and reduce the burden of these concurrent diseases.

Keywords: COPD, diastolic heart failure, GOLD standards, HFpEF

V. P. Rasheeka

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Amrita Hospital, Faridabad, Haryana, India

Introduction: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is strongly linked to tobacco smoking, with considerable epidemiological evidence focused on smokers despite recent research highlighting the effect of indoor air pollution on development of COPD. A large population in many parts of the developing world still uses coal and indigenous gas stoves which generates tremendous amount of indoor air pollution. Therefore, a growing need exists to differentiate between the various epidemiological, clinical, radiologic and prognostic characteristics of Tobacco smoke (TS) associated COPD and indoor air pollution associated COPD (AP-COPD).

Methods: 120 patients with COPD (GOLD diagnostic criteria) were studied. 60 patients had significant exposure to smoked tobacco. 60 patients were never smokers but had been exposed to significant indoor air-pollution for at least 5–10 years, mostly for domestic cooking exposure.

Results: 92.5% (37/40) in the smoking group were males. 85% (34/ 40) in the BMF group were females. The average age at presentation for TS group was 54 years and 69 years in AP-COPD group. In the TS group the average FEV1 value (% age predicted) was 54% and 62% in AP-COPD group. Both the groups had an average exacerbation of 2/year, over the 2 years of study period. AP-COPD group had a higher prevalence of associated Bronchiectasis on CT scans (53% vs 18%).

Conclusions: Majority of AP-COPD patients are females, who are unknowingly exposed to large a corpus of inhaled smoke via indigenous stoves and wood fire used for cooking. AP-COPD group had a late age of first presentation, first presentation in exacerbation and poor post discharge follow up. This special subgroup of COPD patients’ needs to be identified and treated early, for more favourable outcomes.

Rohan Khoobchandani

Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India

Introduction: Cryptococcosis is a fungal infection caused by Cryptococcus neoformans and Cryptococcus gattii, which thrive in environments with bird droppings, soil, and decaying organic matter. It primarily affects the lungs but can spread to other organs, particularly the central nervous system. While more common in immunocompromised individuals, it can also occur in immunocompetent hosts.

Aim: To describe a case of severe pulmonary cryptococcosis in an immunocompromised individual.

Methods: We report a case of a 63-year-old male, previously undiagnosed with type 2 diabetes and chronic liver disease, who presented with a 2-week history of dry cough and fever. Chest X-ray showed left lower zone homogenous opacity and CT thorax revealed a large lobulated mass lesion in the left lower lobe. Bronchoscopy and bronchoalveolar lavage confirmed cryptococcal infection with narrow-based budding yeast cells.

Results: The patient was started on antifungal therapy, including amphotericin B, 5-flucytosine, and fluconazole. Despite 2 weeks of medical treatment, he had persistent fever and no radiological improvement in repeat CT chest. Consequently, left lower lobectomy was performed. The postoperative period was uneventful, and the patient completed 4 weeks of amphotericin B and 5-flucytosine, before being discharged on oral fluconazole.

Discussion: Diagnosis of cryptococcosis involves isolating the pathogen from blood, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), sputum, or tissue samples, as well as antigen detection and microscopic examination. In this case, underlying diabetes and liver disease may have contributed to the poor response to medical therapy, necessitating surgical intervention.

Conclusion: The induction phase of cryptococcosis treatment typically lasts 2 weeks but may extend to 4-6 weeks, depending on infection severity. Surgical intervention, such as resection of cryptococcomas, may be needed if there is no improvement with medical management.

Sabari Harish

Indira Gandhi Medical College, Puducherry, India

Pulmonary synovial sarcoma is a rare lung tumor with an incidence of 0.5% when compared to other lung malignancies. It is more common in males. Clinical diagnosis of this tumor is a challenging task. Hence to confirm the diagnosis we need histopathological examination and immunohistochemical staining. TLE1 is a specific and sensitive IHC marker for synovial sarcoma and helpful to differentiate from other tumors which are histologic mimics of synovial sarcoma. To establish the diagnosis other primary lung malignancies should be ruled out. Till now only a few cases have been documented in the literature. Considering the rarity of this tumor we report a case of a 66-year-old female with a huge right lung mass with ipsilateral pleural effusion that was diagnosed as primary pulmonary synovial sarcoma by USG guided lung biopsy.
