Saikat Sarkar

Pulmocare Research and Education Foundation, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Sarcomatoid mesothelioma is a rare, aggressive subtype of mesothelioma characterized by rapid progression, poor prognosis, and resistance to conventional therapy. It is underreported in India, with an estimated 2,213 cases from 2012 onwards. The disease has a long latency period of 20 to 50 years and presents with non-specific symptoms, often leading to delayed diagnosis.

Case Description: A 34-year-old male surgical assistant presented with pleuritic left-sided chest pain, loss of appetite, and unintentional weight loss. Imaging revealed a posterior mediastinal mass and liver lesions. After extensive testing and a PET scan, a radial EBUS-guided biopsy was attempted but abandoned due to bronchial hyperreactivity. A metabolic biopsy confirmed the diagnosis of sarcomatoid mesothelioma. The patient was referred for palliative therapy with permetrexed, cisplatin, and bevacizumab.

Conclusion: Sarcomatoid mesothelioma is the rarest and most aggressive subtype of mesothelioma, with a dismal prognosis. The lack of clinical data in India often leads to delayed diagnosis and poor outcomes. Despite advances in therapy, there is an urgent need for research into clinical biomarkers for early detection and screening of at-risk populations to prevent it from becoming a public health problem in developing countries.

Sangay Chopel Bhutia1, Pulak Kumar Jana2

1PGT, NRS Medical College and Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India, 2Deparment of Respiratory Medicine, NRS Medical College and Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Amyloidosis is a rare heterogenous disorder caused by extracellular deposition of misfolded proteins resulting in organ dysfunction and eventually death. It predominantly affects male population (male to female ratio 3:2) and mean age being 67 years. It can be localized or systemic. Systemic amyloidosis has incidence of approximately 10 cases per million per person’s year and pulmonary involvement is seen in 50% of cases. Pulmonary amyloidosis can present as nodular, diffuse alveolar septal, Tracheobronchial and amyloid deposition in diffuse alveolar septal pattern is the least common variety seen only in 3% cases of pulmonary amyloidosis and has poor prognosis.

Case: A 72-year, female presented with the complaint of insidious onset with gradual progression of shortness of breath along with dry cough for one year and pain abdomen since past 1 month. She had past history of Anti tubercular drugs intake for 1 month and left on her own. Examination revealed pallor, Tachypnoea and tachycardia with SP02 of 94% in room air. Accessory muscle of respiration was working and auscultation of chest reveled bilateral diffuse rhonchi. Xray and CT chest revealed presence of bilateral random nodules distributed all the lobes of lung and an area of pleural thickening. CT guided Tru cut biopsy was done from the lesion but during procedure patient developed hemoptysis and bleeding from the puncture site, oxygen saturation dropped to 80% which was managed on site. The tissue sample was sent for HPE and revealed it to be Amyloid deposition. Possible secondary causes for amyloidosis were ruled out and the diagnosis of Primary amyloidosis involving Lung was made.

Conclusion: Pulmonary Amyloidosis is a rare disorder that has the potential to cause significant morbidity and mortality. It should be considered as one of the differentials along with other common diagnosis as malignancy, infections, and vasculitis when multisystem involvement is seen or when non resolving pneumonia is encountered. The diagnosis of amyloidosis should be based on tissue biopsy from easily accessible sites as abdominal fat or minor salivary glands due increased risk of hemorrhage with an organ biopsy. Knowledge about pulmonary amyloidosis is important due to nonspecific findings in CT chest and poor prognosis and treatment depend upon the pattern of involvement.

Shemsya Shajahan

Armed Forces Medical College, Pune, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Lung Transplantation has become a viable treatment option in patients with various advanced stage lung disease. Since the inception of first lung transplant the number of lung transplant procedures performed have rapidly increased, the current trend shows the increase in acceptable donors as well as recipient and relatively lower absolute contraindications. Lung transplantation offers the potential to prolong and improve quality of life for patients.

Description: Referring a patient for lung transplant is a multifaceted procedure. Ideally, patients should be referred before they meet criteria for wait-listing to provide an opportunity to introduce the concept of lung transplant, its requirements, and expected outcomes. The right patient selection and referral at the right time is significantly associated with positive outcomes and less complications. Lung transplant evaluation in a busy OPD can be time consuming and may deter physicians from performing a detailed evaluation for LT referral and listing at an early stage. We hypothesized that assessing the same via a web-based software can provide accurate results in less time a step-based approach. Till now in India there is no such study done despite having a huge population with advanced lung disease. We developed a web based ‘lung transplant eligibility calculator’ which can be used to do a comprehensive evaluation and assess the eligibility of patients for referral/listing according to ISHLT criterion. This will ensure early and correct assessment, thus improving outcomes. Ethical clearance was taken from Institutional Ethical committee, AFMC, Pune (IEC No: IEC/2024/516) and the study was registered with CTRI (CTRI registration no- CTRI/2024/06/068913). Patients of COPD, ILD and Bronchiectasis with end- stage lung disease attending respiratory OPD at a tertiary care centre in Maharashtra were included.

Results: We have included 23 candidates till now of which 52% patients were found to be eligible for referral and 43% patients were eligible for listing according to ISHLT criterion.

Conclusion: Referral for lung transplantation is a complex, multifactorial process that significantly impacts patient outcomes. Early identification and prompt referral of eligible patients will streamline and accelerate the process for lung transplantation. A scientific study will give statistical data on average time period required in referring and listing patients eligible for lung transplantation.

Keywords: Bronchiectasis, COPD, end stage lung disease, ILD, lung transplant eligibility calculator, lung transplantation, web based software

Shubham Kumar, Raja Dhar, Beauty Biswas, Shyam Krishnan, Aditya Satpati, Arup Halder

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: Large airway obstruction necessitates urgent intervention to ensure airway patency and prevent life-threatening complications. Endobronchial amyloidosis (EA), a rare cause of large airway obstruction due to amyloid deposits in the bronchial tree with unique diagnostic challenges.

Methods: Three cases of endobronchial amyloidosis diagnosed at a tertiary care institute in Kolkata, were retrospectively reviewed. Clinical data, imaging findings, bronchoscopy observations, and histopathology results were analyzed. Diagnosis was confirmed via endobronchial biopsy with Congo red staining for amyloid deposits on histopathology.

Results:

Case 1: A 49-year, male presented with dry cough, exertional dyspnea, and fever. HRCT thorax revealed right upper lobe consolidation with hilar and paratracheal lymphadenopathy. Bronchoscopy showed a diffuse endobronchial mass-like growth occluding 70% of the RUL bronchial lumen at its maximum. Rigid bronchoscopy with laser debulking reduced the obstruction to 10%, restoring airway patency. Histopathology confirmed localized EA

Case 2: A 69-year, female presented with dry cough, exertional dyspnea, stridor, and intermittent hemoptysis. HRCT showed narrowing at the left main bronchus. Rigid bronchoscopy with tumor debulking reduced the obstruction from 80% to 20%. To maintain patency, a self-expanding metallic stent was placed. Histopathology confirmed localized EA.

Case 3: A 62-year, female with chronic dry cough, monophonic wheezing (right parasternal area), and worsening dyspnea over three months was found to have segmental atelectasis of the right middle lobe on HRCT thorax. Bronchoscopy revealed a friable vascular lesion partially occluding 60% of the right middle lobe bronchus. Multiples biopsies were taken from RML and the complete mass was removed endoscopically and bleeding was managed with argon plasma coagulation. Histopathology confirmed localized Endobronchial amyloidosis.

Conclusion: Endobronchial amyloidosis is a rare but significant cause of large airway obstruction. Early diagnosis through bronchoscopy and histopathology, combined with timely intervention to restore airway patency, is critical for managing symptomatic patients.

Shubham Kumar, Raja Dhar, Shyam Krishnan, Beauty Biswas, Aditya Satpati, Arup Halder

CK Birla Hospitals, CMRI, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Introduction: This case series highlights complex endobronchial infections, emphasizing the pivotal role of advanced interventional pulmonology techniques in diagnosis and management.

Case: A 45/male with fever and hemoptysis and a CT Thorax suggestive of a lingula and left lower lobe mass lesion with High Attenuated Mucus (HAM) underwent diagnostic bronchoscopy which revealed necrotic material in the lingula and left lower lobe. Cryoprobe debulking done and histopathology confirmed aspergillosis, suggesting that the mass lesion was actually HAM. A 58/male, initially being treated for sarcoidosis, presented with fever, altered sensorium, with right upper lobe cavitary lung and brain lesions on imaging. Radial EBUS biopsy and CSF analysis confirmed pulmonary and CNS histoplasmosis. A 39/male with past H/O Nasal Rhinosporidiosis, now presented with haemoptysis and dyspnoea. CT thorax was suggestive of right main bronchial obstruction which was diagnosed as Rhinosporidiosis on bronchoscopic biopsy. Rigid bronchoscopy with a microdebrider restored airway patency. A 34/male with severe kyphoscoliosis and respiratory failure underwent bronchoscopy revealing necrotic slough like material in right lower lobe, which was biopsied and was diagnosed as endobronchial tuberculosis. A 65/female with COPD and bronchiectasis presented with hemoptysis. Bronchoscopy showed necrotic sloughed off mucosa in RLL bronchus. BAL AFB was positive with CBNAAT negative raising suspicions of MOTT, but eventually revealed nocardiosis. A 60/male with a right upper lobe cavitary lesion and mediastinal lymphadenopathy with inflamed nodular mucosa in RUL on bronchoscopy was diagnosed with pulmonary mucormycosis using EBUS-TBNA and endobronchial biopsies. A 63/male with a suspected bronchocoele showed friable vascular tissue during bronchoscopy, which histopathology confirmed as squamous carcinoma.

Conclusion: This series underscores the critical role of interventional pulmonology in diagnosing and managing rare and complex pulmonary infections.

Sri Aishwarya

Sri Lalithambigai Medical College and Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Objective: Pulmonary tuberculosis (TB) remains a major health challenge globally, with hematological manifestations being frequently observed in TB patients. This study aims to determine the frequency of various hematological abnormalities in newly diagnosed pulmonary tuberculosis cases based on complete blood counts.

Methods: This retrospective cross-sectional study was conducted at Sri Lalithambigai Medical College, Chennai, on 230 newly diagnosed pulmonary tuberculosis patients. Data was collected from 1st September 2023 to 30th March 2024, using consecutive sampling. Detailed medical histories, physical examination findings, and complete blood counts (CBC) including erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) were retrieved from the hospital’s electronic medical records system. The data was entered into SPSS version 23.0 for analysis. The results were presented as mean±SD for continuous variables and frequency and percentages for categorical variables, using tables and figures.

Results: The mean age of the patients was 34.36 ± 6.41 years, with a male to female ratio of 2.52:1. Anemia was present in 82.6% (n=190) of the patients, leukocytosis in 46.2% (n=106), and leucopenia in 20.4% (n=47) of the cases. Thrombocytosis was found in 26.2% (n=60) of the patients, and raised ESR was observed in 99% (n=228) of the patients. There was no significant association between age and gender with the hematological manifestations. However, thrombocytosis was significantly more common in male patients (p-value = 0.008).

Conclusion: Hematological abnormalities, especially anemia, leukocytosis, and raised ESR, are highly prevalent in patients with newly diagnosed pulmonary tuberculosis. Thrombocytosis was more frequent in male patients. These findings emphasize the need for thorough hematological screening in pulmonary tuberculosis cases for early detection and management.

Keywords: Anemia, erythrocyte sedimentation rate-give the result with changes of 230 samples, hematological manifestations, leukocytosis, pulmonary tuberculosis, thrombocytosis

Sri Valli

Mediciti Institute of Medical Sciences, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

A 29-year-old female presented to the OPD with complaints of body aches, breathlessness, 3-day history of fever, left lower chest pain, breathlessness, giddiness, weakness, abdominal pain, and bloating. A chest X-ray revealed left paracardiac shadow on the left side, and she was treated with antibiotics and supportive care. Further evaluation with high-resolution computed tomography (HRCT) of the chest showed multiple bilateral lung nodules, soft tissue density with calcifications in the inferior lingula, adjacent traction bronchiectasis, and areas of ground-glass opacity, with suspicion of collapse. A PET-CT scan identified a hypermetabolic collapsed consolidation (2.8 x 2.2 x 2.7 cm) in the left inferior lingula, with FDG-avid ill-defined lesions and calcifications, raising concerns about malignancy. Minimal left pleural effusion was also noted. Above lesion biopsied under CT-guided. Histopathological examination revealed lung parenchyma with acute inflammation and lamellated calcified nodules, suggesting a reaction to dead parasites, rather than malignancy.

Conclusion: The imaging and biopsy findings, including calcified nodules and acute inflammation, were consistent with a parasitic infection, likely a granulomatous response to dead parasites. These findings indicated that the clinical presentation a parasitic etiology.

Sumit Verma

NSCB Medical College, Jabalpur, Madhya Pradesh, India

Introduction: Lung cancer has various presentations and is sometimes diagnosed as a tuberculous cavity or an abscess. The link between lung abscess and lung cancer has been known, but the presence of malignancy in lung abscesses often undiagnosed. Obstruction from lung cancer can predispose to the development of a lung abscess. Here we present a case of 73-year-old male who was diagnosed with tuberculous lung abscess which was later confirm to be squamous cell carcinoma of lung.

Case Presentation: A 73 year old male manual labour by occupation came to our outpatient department with complaints of chest pain and cough associated with haemoptysis since 6 month it was also associated with hoarseness of voice and difficulty in swallowing and loss of appetite and significant weight loss of 10kg over 2months, he was clinically diagnosed with tuberculous lung abscess by a local district hospital and was already taking anti tubercular treatment since 3 month, he was a chronic smoker smoking about 30 bidis per day for 30years. There were no palpable lymph nodes and no digital clubbing. Patient was subjected to sputum examination which was negative for AFB on direct smear and showed no growth of bacteria after 5 days of incubation. Patient was subjected to CECT chest which revealed large thick cavity with air fluid level communicating with right sub segmental bronchus along with metastatic lymphadenopathy and adrenal mets. Patient was subjected to bronchoscopy which revelled irregular mucosal growth in right main bronchus less than 2cm from carina, sample were taken for brush cytology, biopsy and histopathology which was suggestive of squamous cell carcinoma. Patient was started on chemotherapy and followed up periodically.

Conclusion: We reported a rare case, a 73-year-old male patient, with the diagnosis of Carcinomatous Lung Abscess. This case report was prepared with the aim of increasing awareness of malignancy in patients with a clinical presentation of abscesses, especially in old age.

Swetha Pasupuleti

Mahatma Gandhi Mission Institute of Health Sciences, Navi Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Scleroderma, a rare autoimmune connective tissue disorder, is characterized by skin fibrosis and internal organ involvement, including the lungs, heart, and kidneys. Pulmonary complications, such as interstitial lung disease (ILD) and pulmonary hypertension, are frequently observed. However, the association between scleroderma and lung malignancies, particularly squamous cell carcinoma (SCC), remains poorly documented. This report highlights a rare case of SCC in the lungs of a patient with scleroderma.

Case Description: A 58-year-old female with a history of diffuse cutaneous scleroderma and progressive ILD presented with a persistent cough, chest pain, and dyspnea lasting seven months. Imaging, including HRCT, revealed bilateral extensive consolidation, initially suggestive of tuberculosis. Despite six months of anti-tubercular therapy, empirical antibiotics, IV steroids, and supportive care, her symptoms persisted. Sputum GeneXpert and cultures were negative. Further evaluation through bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) cytology confirmed the presence of epithelial malignancy consistent with squamous cell carcinoma.

The co-occurrence of SCC in scleroderma raises concerns about an increased predisposition to cancer in this population. Chronic inflammation, immune dysregulation, and lung fibrosis associated with scleroderma may play a role in carcinogenesis. Additionally, ILD-related architectural lung changes may create a microenvironment conducive to malignancy. This case underscores the importance of maintaining a high index of suspicion for malignancy in scleroderma patients presenting with persistent or atypical respiratory symptoms. Early recognition and timely intervention are crucial for improving outcomes.

Conclusion: In conclusion, while lung cancer is not commonly associated with scleroderma, its possibility should not be overlooked, particularly in patients with refractory pulmonary symptoms. Clinicians must remain vigilant for malignancies, including SCC, in this unique patient subset to ensure appropriate management and better prognosis.

Yash Gholve

Dr. Vasantrao Pawar Medical College, Hospital and Research Centre, Nashik, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia is a potentially life threatening fungal infection seen in immunocompromised individuals. In 1980s, the worldwide epidemic of HIV dramatically increased the prevalence of PCP. Because of the progress of ART and the use of routine prophylaxis against PCP, the incidence of PCP in HIV infected population was reduced in most of the industrialized countries. However , PCP still remains to be the most common opportunistic infection among patients with AIDS. A case of pulmonary fungal infection in a newly diagnosed HIV patient is reported here. Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia was initially suspected due to typical radiological features, but the patient was eventually diagnosed with pulmonary aspergillosis.

Case Description: 30 year old male, newly diagnosed HIV patient came to opd with complains of dry cough weight loss & loss of appetite since 2 months. Routine labs were within normal limits. CXR PA s/o bilateral perihilar infiltrates. HRCT showed bilateral perihilar ground glass opacities and cystic changes. Bronchoscopy was done. BAL cultures, CBNAAT, PCP PCR were negative. BAL galactomannan was 1.74. Pt was started on Tab Posacanazole for 8weeks. Clinical and radiological improvement were seen within 4 weeks of treatment.

Conclusions: Pulmonary aspergillosis and pneumocystis jiroveci pneumonia are life threatening opportunistic infections that occur in immunocompromised hosts. Early diagnosis and treatment of these opportunistic infections is essential to the survival of immunocompromised patients.

Radiological findings can offer valuable suggestions regarding the underlying cause of pathological condition, but there are instances where these findings lack specificity and can lead to misinterpretation.

Rutvee Kakkad, Tarang Kulkarni, Alpa Dalal, Dipanjan Haldar

Jupiter Hospital, Thane, Maharashtra, India

Introduction: BOS is a chronic lung injury leading to narrowing and/or obliteration of bronchial lumen by granulation tissue or fibrosis giving rise to new onset fixed airflow obstruction in patients post- transplant. Development of BOS worsens lung function, contributing to increased morbidity and mortality post HCT.

Case 1: A 30-year-old patient, post Bone Marrow Transplant for Acute Lymphocytic Leukemia (ALL), with history of lichenoid GVHD, with no prior history of atopy, asthma or bronchitis, presented with increased breathlessness, cough, chest tightness and wheezing since 2 weeks. CT Chest showed mosaic attenuation and air trapping on expiratory phase. PFT demonstrated severe irreversible obstruction with significant air trapping. BOS was diagnosed based on the 2014 National Institutes of Health (NIH) consensus criteria. Patient was treated with daily inhaled Fluticasone and oral Montelukast with thrice weekly oral Azithromycin (FAM therapy) and twice daily oral Ruxolitinib. There was improvement in symptoms and serial Pulmonary Function Tests (PFT) after initiation of therapy.

Case 2: A 34-year-old Male, ALL, post HCT, with history of gastrointestinal tract GVHD, with no prior history of atopy, asthma or bronchitis, required hospitalization for fungal pneumonia. Patient was treated with oral antifungal therapy, but patient persisted to have breathlessness. Interval CT Chest revealed complete resolution of pneumonia and new pulmonary nodules with air trapping. Serial PFTs showed worsening lung functions. Patient started on FAM therapy and Ruxolitinib. Patient is clinically better, currently on follow-up.

Take-home message: Identification of high-risk patients, early diagnosis and timely management of BOS is crucial to improve clinical outcomes and quality of life post HCT.

Conclusion: There is increasing evidence on Janus Kinase inhibitors like Ruxolitinib for refractory chronic GVHD-BOS post HCT due to its immunomodulatory and steroid-sparing effect. With access to newer management strategies, prompt initiation of treatment is fundamental in improving outcomes post HCT.

Department of Pulmonary Medicine, Yashoda Hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

Background: Malignant pleural mesothelioma and empyema, although distinct lung conditions, can exhibit similar symptoms, such as chest pain and cough. This case highlights mesothelioma presenting as thoracic empyema.

Case: A 50-year-old male farmer, chronic smoker, presented with dry cough and left-sided chest pain for two months. Chest X-ray revealed a left-sided homogeneous opacity. Subsequent contrast-enhanced CT Chest suggested a loculated pleural effusion. A ultrasound-guided intercostal tube drained 100 ml of pus, which was analyzed and showed elevated ADA levels (50 U/L), neutrophilic predominance, and high LDH (1636 U/L). predominance, and high LDH (1636 U/L). Histopathology from the VATS biopsy indicated moderately differentiated adenocarcinoma. Immunohistochemical markers were positive for CK WT1, CK 5/6, and calretinin, while negative for CK7, CK20, and napsin, confirming malignant mesothelioma.

Conclusion: Accurate diagnosis is crucial as thoracic empyema and malignant pleural mesothelioma can mimic each other. VATS aids in this diagnostic process, as empyema-like presentations could suggest a poorer prognosis for mesothelioma patients.

Hitech Medical College, Bhubaneswar, Odisha, India

Background: Scrub typhus, caused by Orientia tsutsugamushi, poses a significant public health challenge in South-East Asia, contributing to 25% of acute undifferentiated febrile illness (AUFI) cases in India. The disease typically presents with fever, headaches, myalgia, and gastrointestinal symptoms but can lead to severe complications such as hepatitis, renal failure, meningoencephalitis, and acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS). Respiratory involvement in scrub typhus, particularly ARDS, is of special interest due to its rarity in tropical infections and its association with high morbidity and mortality. Understanding this manifestation is crucial for early diagnosis and effective management.

Aim and Objective: To study the respiratory manifestations of scrub typhus patients admitted to our tertiary care center between January and June 2024.

Method: A retrospective observational study was conducted after ethical approval. We included patients with acute febrile illness (AFI) who tested positive for scrub typhus IgM. Their clinical and demographic data, particularly respiratory symptoms, were analyzed.

Results: Among 42 patients with AFI, 14 (33.3%) had respiratory involvement. Common manifestations included cough, dyspnea, and sore throat (42.8%), pleural effusion (21.4%), ARDS (21.4%), pneumonitis (7%), and diffuse alveolar hemorrhage (7%) [Figure 1]. The median hospital stay was 12 days, with one mortality due to hemorrhage. Most patients recovered with doxycycline and azithromycin therapy, with some requiring steroids.

Respiratory manifestations of the patients with scrub typhus

Conclusion: Early diagnosis and appropriate antibiotic treatment can prevent severe respiratory complications in scrub typhus patients, improving outcomes.

Keywords: Chest infections, critical care, miscellaneous

Background: The type of inhaler device is an important determinant in adherence to the treatment of Asthma and Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD). Our objective was to study the adherence to BA-pMDI, MDI and DPI in Asthma and COPD patients and its impact on disease outcomes.

Methodology: In this multi-centric, observational study, we collected data from 390 Asthma and COPD patients using different inhaler devices across 23 sites in India. We measured adherence to inhaler devices using the Test of Adherence to Inhalers (TAI) and Morisky Medication Adherence Scale (MMAS-8). The impact of disease was measured using the Asthma Control Test (ACT) and COPD Assessment Test (CAT).

Results: Out of 390 patients, 59.7% had Asthma and 40.3% had COPD. 37.7% of patients used BA-pMDI, 29.5% used MDI and 32.9% used DPI. On the TAI scale, the Good/Intermediate adherence for BA-pMDI, MDI, and DPI users was 42.9%, 42.6%, and 63.3%, respectively. However, on the MMAS-8 scale, a significantly high proportion of BA-pMDI users showed Good/Medium adherence as compared to MDI and DPI users [91.2%, 65.2%, and 76.6%, respectively (p<0.001)]. The results for the impact of Good/Intermediate adherence according to TAI and MMAS scores on the ACT and CAT scale are shown in Table 1. BA-pMDI users had significantly fewer exacerbations and lower hospitalizations in last 3 months than MDI and DPI users (Exacerbations: BA-pMDI: 15.9%, MDI: 28.6% and DPI: 24.7%, p<0.05) (Hospitalization: BA-pMDI: 6.3%, MDI: 12.2% and DPI: 9.9%).

Impact of adherence to breath-actuated P metered-dose inhaler, metered-dose inhaler and dry powder inhaler on asthma control test and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease assessment test scales

MDI: Metered-dose inhaler, DPI: Dry powder inhaler, ACT: Asthma control test, CAT: Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease assessment test, MMAS-8: Morisky Medication Adherence Scale-8, TAI: Test of adherence to inhalers, BA-pMDI: Breath-actuated p metered-dose inhaler

Conclusion: The study showed that irrespective of the inhaler type, patients who showed Good/Intermediate adherence were more likely to have complete control and a low impact of Asthma and COPD on their lives. BA-pMDI users reported lower exacerbations and the number of hospitalizations as compared to MDI and DPI.

Acknowledgment: This study was supported by Cipla Ltd. India.

Keywords: ACT, adherence, asthma, BA-pMDI, CAT, COPD

Introduction: Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) is a complex allergic disorder caused by immune reactions against the offending fungus, Aspergillus fumigatus. ABPA most commonly complicates the course of patients with bronchial asthma or cystic fibrosis (CF). We aimed to evaluate the diagnostic practices of ABPA by physicians from multiple centers across India.

Methods: Pulmonologists from 23 centers within the Respiratory Research Network (RRN) participated in this observational registry study. Patients were categorized into 3 groups based on International Society for Human and Animal Mycology (ISHAM) 2013 criteria; Group 1: meeting ISHAM criteria, Group 2: meeting partial ISHAM criteria (HRCT positive or serological positive in any 2 parameters), and Group 3: physician diagnosed, not meeting ISHAM criteria.

Results: Out of 802 patients, 37.2% (299) were in group I, 50.2% (403) in group II, and 12.4% (100) in group III. 97.2% were non-smokers. A total of 742 out of 802 had a history of asthma and 73% (n= 587) were residing in urban areas.A significant difference was observed amongst the 3 ISHAM criteria groups for the serological parameters namely, Serum total IgE level (IU/mL), Serum A. fumigatus IgE levels (kUA/L), Serum A.fumigatus IgG levels converted, Total eosinophil count (per uL) (P=0.000, 0.724, 0.002, 0.004) respectively. Differences between 3 groups are presented in Table 1.

Clinical and laboratory findings in the 3 groups of patients

Conclusion: There is considerable variation in the diagnostic criteria for ABPA across India and greater awareness needs to be created about ISHAM criteria.

Introduction: A phantom/vanishing tumor represent interlobar pleural fluid collection mimicking a tumor on CXR, resolving with appropriate management. It occurs when transudation from pulmonary vessels exceeds pleural lymphatic absorption but in patients without prior heart failure, an infective cause should be considered.

Case Report: A 58-year-old male, LIC agent and Ex-smoker (~0.4pack-year) with DM, HTN, IHD (post-CABG) presented with hematemesis (2-3 episodes), cough with hemoptysis (1-2 tsp) for 4 days and fever for 2 days (upto 101°F) and 6-month H/O anorexia, mouth dryness, and dysphagia (solids>liquids). UGI scopy 2 months ago –s/o Severe erosive reflux esophagitis. 2DECHO- conc LVH LVEF-55%, mild PH (PASP 45 mmHg). On follow up 2 months later, repeat 2D ECHO -severe concentric LVH, LVEF 50%, mild PH. Bilateral pleural effusion present (R>L), Right pleural space thoracentesis done (~250 cc). Microbiologically inconclusive, Cytology- mesothelial hyperplasia, Blood ProBNP– 451. UGI scopy revealed columnar-lined healing epithelium with esophagitis. CT guided Thoracentesis from collection and biopsy yielded haemorrhagic fluid with negative microbiology and cytology. ANA blot- negative. The final diagnosis suggested benign/non-malignant cause.

Follow Up Imaging: 1 month apart [Figures 2 and 3].

Follow up radiograph after 1 month

Followup CT

Conclusion: Subpleural focus, ruptured in minor fissure, forming organised hematoma.

For radiographic pulmonary masses, use appropriate diagnostic modalities and antibiotics to avoid unnecessary and harmful errors.

V. Swathy Radhakrishnan, B. L. Shashibhushan, T. J. Basavaraju

Bangalore Medical Collage, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Pulmonary Aspergillosis is a mycotic disease usually caused by Aspergillus Fumigatus,a saprophytic airborne fungi.Aspergillus related lung diseases are classified into four different forms-whose occurrence depends upon immunological status of underlying lung disease -Allergic Broncho-Pulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA), Saprophytic infection (aspergilloma), Chronic necrotizing aspergillosis (semi invasive form) and Invasive aspergillosis in severely immunocompromised patients.

Case Description: A 55/F farmer by occupation presented to OPD with the complaints of breathlessness since 2 months, cough with expectoration since 2 months, History of loss of appetite present.On resentation Spo2 :96%, Pr 69 bpm, RR 22 cpm, Bp 190/100 mm hg. On examination trachea was pulled to left side,supraclavicular hollowing was present on left side and chest movements reduced over left hemithorax. On auscultation bronchial breath sounds heard in left infraclavicular, axillary, mammary areas. Chest Xray showed left lung collapse. Patient was empirically started on INJ CEFTRIAXONE. CT Thorax was done suggestive of complete collapse of left lung, with mucous plug in left main bronchus.Patient underwent FOB+BAL which showed cauliflower like growth seen in lower end of the trachea occluding tracheal lumen more in favour of neoplastic etiology and Biopsy was sent.HPE report showed fibrinous material admixed with colonies of hyphae suggestive of Pulmonary Aspergillosis.Patient was started on oral Voriconazole and became symptomatically better and discharged with a plan of outpatient followup.

Conclusions: This case emphasizes the importance of considering pulmonary aspergillosis in the differential diagnosis of lung masses, particularly in patients with pre-existing lung damage or risk factors such as smoking. Radiologic features of aspergillosis including cavitary lesions with fungal balls, can closely resemble malignancy, potentially leading to misdiagnosis.

Case Report: A 51-year-old male, non-smoker, who was working as an IT professional presented to us with history of attacks of chronic intermittent cough and copious sputum production over the last 7 years. It was also associated with recurrent pneumonia and hospital admissions. Had progressive dyspnea now increased to grade 3-4 mmrc dyspnoea scale. No significant history of hemoptysis, difficulty of swallowing, or chocking was reported. Systemic examination was unremarkable. He used inhalers and cough suppressants as required. He is married and is having 1 child. No other relevant family history.

Examination:

The patient was thin built and malnourished. Pan digital clubbing, bilateral pedal edema was present, Dyspneic, tachypneic, Spo2-86% room air, Bilateral midinspiratory coarse crackles noted, more in lower hemithorax areas, Other system examination were normal except for loud p2.

Chest X-ray:

Enlongated opacity along paravertebral region from c7 spine extending below diaphragm with tapering towards left near the fundus of the stomach.Small cystic changes with peribronchial thickening noted on right midzone,lower zone and left lower zone [Figure 1].

Immunoglobulin profile was normal except for Igg that was high

Abg showed type 2 respiratory failure

ANA profile was negative

Renal function test was deranged.

In view of elevated Igg and acute kidney injury, serum electrophoresis was sent which showed monoclonal M band.

Bal culture showed MDR pseudomonas.

Echo showed mild dilatation of RA, RV with severe PAH.

Management:

reatment of exacerbation with antibiotics, bronchodilators and mucolytics

Airway clearance and anti inflammatory treatment

Bipap and supplemental oxygenation atleast 15 hours per day

Pneumococcal and influenza vaccination

Pulmonary rehabilitation and nutrition.

Conclusion: This case highlights the importance of identifying rare congenital anomalies like H-type TEF and achalasiacardia in adults presenting with chronic respiratory symptoms. Delayed diagnosis led to severe bilateral bronchiectasis, respiratory failure, and cor pulmonale. The coexisting multiple myeloma further complicated the clinical picture. A multidisciplinary approach combining interventional, medical, and supportive therapies was key to stabilizing the patient and improving quality of life.

Mihika Sawale, Rishabh Raj, Mugdha Bhide

K. J. Somaiya Institute, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India

Background: Infectious diseases remain a significant cause of mortality worldwide, particularly in ICU settings where timely identification of pathogens is critical. Traditional culture-based methods are time-consuming and often fail to detect fastidious or atypical organisms, leading to delayed treatment. Next-generation sequencing (NGS) offers a promising alternative, enabling comprehensive, rapid, and unbiased pathogen identification. This study aimed to evaluate the diagnostic accuracy of NGS compared to culture-based methods in a tertiary care ICU in India.

Methods: A retrospective analysis was conducted on 187 patients admitted to the ICU with infectious etiologies. Paired samples from blood, urine, bronchoalveolar lavage fluid (BALF), cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), and other body fluids(pleural fluid, ascitic fluid, pus, peritoneal fluid, bile, and joint aspirate were subjected to both NGS and culture-based diagnostics. Sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value (PPV), and negative predictive value (NPV) were calculated using culture as the reference standard. The concordance rate was also calculated, including the matched positive results (both tests detect the same organism) and matched negative results (both tests yield negative findings). Subgroup analyses were performed based on each sample type.

Results: NGS exhibited a sensitivity of 75%, specificity of 59.6%, PPV of 62.23%, and NPV of 72.84%. NGS detected pathogens in 56.68% of cases compared to 47.06% by culture. It identified atypical organisms in 17 cases missed by culture. The highest sensitivity for NGS was observed in CSF (100%) and BALF (87.5%) samples, while the highest specificity was noted in pleural fluid (100%) and blood (87.5%) samples. The overall concordance between NGS and culture was 57.2%.

Conclusion: NGS offers a powerful diagnostic advantage by detecting a broader range of pathogens, including atypical organisms often missed by culture-based methods. Its superior sensitivity across various sample types highlights its potential as a complementary tool in ICU settings. However, the relatively lower specificity and operational challenges such as cost and standardization limit its standalone clinical application. Integrating NGS with conventional diagnostics could enhance pathogen identification and guide targeted antimicrobial therapy. However, further studies are needed to optimize its use and assess its impact on clinical decision-making and patient outcomes.

Aardra Prem

Bangalore Medical College and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India

Introduction: Young syndrome, also known as primary ciliary dyskinesia (PCD) with bronchiectasis, sinusitis, and azoospermia, is a rare autosomal recessive condition. This case report describes the clinical presentation, diagnostic workup, and management of a patient with Young’s Syndrome, emphasizing the importance of early recognition and multidisciplinary care.

Case: A 20 year old male, student, with no known comorbidities, presented with complaints of fever since 2 weeks, cough with expectoration since 4 years, breathlessness since 4 years and history of recurrent upper respiratory tract infection since childhood. On examination vitals were stable, Grade II clubbing was present and on auscultation bilateral inspiratory crepitations heard.

Chest X-ray showed bilateral lower zone bronchiectatic changes. HRCT Thorax showed Cystic bronchiectasis in bilateral lower lobe bronchial segments. Sputum Culture showed Enterobacter cloacae growth and CBNAAT MTB not detected.Bronchoscopy showed Ectatic segments in right middle and lower lobe. BAL gene xpert MTB not detected.ENT opinion sought for chronic upper respiratory symptoms and was adviced CT Paranasal sinuses- which revealed bilateral maxillary and ethmoidal sinusitis. Scrotal Ultrasonography done -showed prominent bilateral epididymis with tubular ectasia with no obstruction. Semen Analysis no spermatozoa seen. Serum Testosterone, LH, FSH were normal. Patient was diagnosed as a case of Youngs Syndrome based on clinicoradiological features.He was discharged with antibiotics,mucolytics ,adviced chest physiotherapy ,and instructed to Follow up in OPD .He was also explained on vaccination, Genetic Testing and anticipated fertility issues.

Conclusions: As Young’s Syndrome links respiratory and reproductive health, it is important not to miss out on its diagnosis and always be considered in differential diagnosis.

This case report underscores the need for awareness regarding this rare condition. A comprehensive diagnostic workup, multidisciplinary approach and timely intervention can alleviate symptoms, improve fertility prospects, and enhance overall well-being and quality of life.
